## 📚 Paper List

- **[ForagerSim: Evaluating Embodied Map-Based Reasoning in Large-Scale Dynamic Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26395)**  `arXiv:2610.26395`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Tomáš Musil, Martin Kotík, Michal Vavrečka, Karla Štěpánová, Sebastian Scherer, Martin Saska_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Animals build cognitive maps of vast environments, use them to perform map-based navigation, and might even be reusing the same circuitry used in spatial reasoning for creating and using maps of arbitrary task-spaces. In contrast, current embodied AI systems either rely on hand-engineered mapping pipelines, which break in the presence of unmodeled dynamics and are restricted to metric space, or on learned policies that rarely form map-like representations at all. Developing architectures that could learn to build and use map-like representations as flexibly as animals is, however, difficult because few embodied AI benchmarks actually require map-based reasoning. To address this, we present ForagerSim --- an interdisciplinary research platform and simulation environment focused on embodied tasks that require map-based reasoning, with scenarios ranging from mobile-robot search and rescue to animal foraging in both complex mazes and vast, potentially infinite open areas. ForagerSim is highly modular and allows modifying independent axes of difficulty related to perception (abstract 2D sensing to full RGB or LiDAR), scale (room-scale to multi-km scale), topology (open-fields to complex mazes), dynamics (smoke, debris, sensorimotor failures and others) and tasks (from simple 2D coverage to foraging-inspired well-alternation tasks). We compare reinforcement learning baselines with a simple engineered robotics stack and a human player on 8 example worlds which show how different difficulty axes affect existing methods. Our results show that, when sensing is simplified to 2D, learned policies can perform multi-object search and well-alternation tasks surprisingly well in simple topologies, but they fall short of human performance in larger or more complex-topology environments where map-based baselines perform well, suggesting that map-based reasoning is the bottleneck in these scenarios.
  </details>

- **[NestedVLA: Learning to Consolidate and Generate Skills for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23271)**  `arXiv:2610.23271`  `cs.RO` `cs.LG`  
  _Guodong Du, Wanyu Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision--Language--Action (VLA) models promise generalist robotic agents, yet consolidating skills learned across heterogeneous tasks and environments remains difficult: training data is fragmented across platforms, joint multi-task training suffers from interference, and model merging tends to introduce parameter conflicts or require architectural changes. We instead advocate a paradigm in which skills are first learned independently and then consolidated post hoc. To this end, we propose NestedVLA, which replaces parameter aggregation with parameter generation: a nested hypernetwork, conditioned on the target task, synthesizes task-adaptive action-expert parameters that compose with a frozen base VLA. Task-specific compressed VLM residuals are retained separately. This formulation enables flexible task-conditioned skill reuse while mitigating interference. Across three RL benchmarks (MetaWorld, ManiSkill, CALVIN), NestedVLA outperforms the strongest model-merging baseline by 41 pp on average, matches per-task fine-tuning within 1 pp on ManiSkill, and substantially closes the gap on CALVIN, pointing toward a storage-efficient route to skill consolidation. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[BiDex-100: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Bimanual Dexterous Manipulation Across 100+ Simulation Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25459)**  `arXiv:2610.25459`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Yong Wang, Kecheng Zheng, Xing Zhu, Chao Xu, Yujun Shen, Shengze Cai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bimanual manipulation with dexterous hands has attracted growing attention in research on robotic action models. However, few studies have developed benchmarks specifically tailored for dexterous manipulation, leaving the dexterous manipulation capabilities of existing models insufficiently characterized. In this paper, we present BiDex-100, a simulation benchmark for bimanual dexterous manipulation that combines broad task coverage, multi-dimensional evaluation, and high-DoF hand embodiments. Concretely, the benchmark comprises 106 tasks organized along five capability axes: generalization, instruction following, limited demonstrations, long horizon, and memory. Each task is paired with an out-of-domain variant featuring controlled visual and spatial changes, allowing systematic assessment of robustness. In addition, the benchmark supports two embodiments and provides 8,080 success-validated teleoperated demonstrations, totaling 6.25 million frames. We evaluate seven recent Vision-Language-Action models under a unified protocol, establishing baselines for capability-specific performance, robustness, and data efficiency. The website is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CLARO: Clutter Aware Robot Orchestrator for Open-Vocabulary Grasping using VLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24016)**  `arXiv:2610.24016`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Sungsu Kim, Vineet Bhat, Prashanth Krishnamurthy, Ramesh Karri, Farshad Khorrami_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robots that follow natural-language instructions unlock a wide range of autonomous applications in households, warehouses, and labs. Real-world environments are diverse and often heavily cluttered, requiring robots to plan over multiple steps, act, and replan as they work toward a goal. We present CLARO (CLutter-Aware Robot Orchestrator), a VLM agent that orchestrates a set of perception and manipulation skills: open-vocabulary object detection, segmentation, 6-DoF grasping, and non-prehensile pushing to locate and retrieve a language-specified target in clutter. CLARO maintains a memory of its past actions to support long-horizon execution, and emits manipulation targets (grasp poses/3D waypoints) allowing transfer across robot arms without task-specific training. We evaluate CLARO against a range of methods on a dynamic simulation environment and standard image-based benchmarks, demonstrating strong generalization across object classes, lighting, and clutter levels. CLARO attains an average success rate of 89.1%, a relative improvement of over 16% on the strongest competing method in high cluttered scenes. We further analyze performance along grounding and grasping axes to characterize where each method succeeds and fails. Finally, we deploy CLARO on a robot arm across diverse cluttered scenes, achieving a success rate of 88.8%, and demonstrating strong sim2real generalizability.
  </details>

- **[ICAT: Incident-Case-Grounded Adaptive Testing for Physical-Risk Prediction in Embodied World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16405)**  `arXiv:2604.16405`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhenglin Lai, Sirui Huang, Yuteng Li, Changxin Huang, Jianqiang Li, Bingzhe Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reference-image-conditioned video generators are increasingly used as neural simulators for embodied planning and policy learning, yet their ability to anticipate physical hazards is rarely evaluated. We introduce ICAT, which grounds test construction in incident reports and safety guidance through structured risk memories, constrained case generation, and human verification. The resulting benchmark contains 909 evaluation units with explicit initial conditions, triggering actions, and documented consequences. Across six generation systems, severe outcomes are frequently missing or attenuated even when the initial state is preserved and the risky action is executed: the strongest system achieves 23.5% conditional strict success under best-of-eight selection. Expert auditing, image-generator sensitivity analysis, matched safe-risky pairs, and alternative-outcome scoring provide complementary checks on this system-level finding. ICAT exposes a gap between reproducing risky actions and generating their documented consequences.
  </details>

- **[Where is Physical RSI capability from? An Observational Scaling Law of LLM-as-policy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26848)**  `arXiv:2610.26848`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Zekai Li, Jiayi Yu, Jinlu Zhang, Luyang Niu, Zekun Qi, Jun Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we systematically investigate the mechanisms, scaling behavior, and generalization of physical Recursive self-improvement. We introduce PhysRSI-eval to quantify physical RSI through a five-axis metric: First-Attempt Performance, Efficiency, Success, Budget and Improvement from failure. Different VLMs are evaluated on 18 manipulation tasks under a fixed interaction budget. The analysis of experimental results motivates the Physical Barrel Hypothesis: different capabilities may constrain different parts of improvement. The five-axis metrics also distinguish systems with close public benchmark averages and show that high scores need not come with early or efficient gains. We study observational scaling from two perspectives: predicting the relative performance of VLM agents from their public capabilities, and forecasting later progress from early physical feedback. Together, these analyses ask how quickly and how far an agent can improve. Moreover, we conduct workspace interventions and then analyze what improvement leaves behind. Removing history reduces late-round success, frozen workspaces remain useful on unseen layouts, and three cross-task cases achieve success earlier. The results suggest that scene-specific adaptation and reusable experience can coexist.
  </details>

- **[GROW$^2$: Grounding Which and Where for Robot Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30632)**  `arXiv:2606.30632`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Yuhong Deng, Yuyao Liu, David Hsu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can the robot use a plate to cut a cake if no knife is available? Tool use greatly expands robot capabilities, but to use tools creatively beyond their intended functions, the robot faces the challenge of $\textit{open-world affordance grounding}$: select an open-category object to act as a tool and localize its specific region of action. To this end, we introduce GROW$^2$ (GROunding Which and Where), which leverages object parts as a natural abstraction to split the grounding process hierarchically into semantic and geometric levels, thus bypassing the need for data-heavy, end-to-end training. Semantically, GROW$^2$ harnesses the commonsense reasoning of Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to parse a natural-language task instruction, select a suitable object as the tool, and identify task-relevant parts on the tool and the target object. Geometrically, vision foundation models then ground the selected parts into precise 3D regions from a single RGB-D image. Experiments on established benchmarks show that GROW$^2$ outperforms state-of-the-art baselines on affordance prediction benchmarks. Further, it achieves zero-shot generalization over open-category objects and outperforms baselines in both simulated and real-world robot tool use experiments.
  </details>

- **[VLA-Pro: Cross-Task Procedural Memory Transfer for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.29562)**  `arXiv:2605.29562`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Shengyu Si, Yuanzhuo Lu, Ruimeng Yang, Ziyi Ye, Zuxuan Wu, Yu-Gang Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have shown strong potential for general-purpose robotic manipulation, yet they still struggle to generalize to unseen tasks that necessitate transferring relevant experience across objects, scenes, and action patterns. This paper proposes VLA-Pro, a plug-and-play framework designed to enhance cross-task generalization by storing task-relevant procedural memories at training time and transferring these memories during inference. Specifically, VLA-Pro stores task-specific LoRA adapters as parameterized procedural memories during training. At inference time, VLA-Pro retrieves relevant procedural memories based on the current multi-modal context and dynamically fuses these memories for generating the current action chunk. Experiments on RoboTwin, RLBench, and real-world manipulation tasks show that VLA-Pro consistently improves cross-task generalization across multiple backbones, achieving up to a 207% relative improvement in simulation and increasing real-world success rate from 5.8% to 65.0%. These results suggest that procedural memory retrieval and adaptation provide an effective mechanism for transferring manipulation experience to novel tasks while preserving modularity and execution stability.
  </details>

- **[Reconstruction and Discrete-Event Simulation Assessment of an Analytical](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25967)**  `arXiv:2610.25967`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Mohsen Reda Mahmoud Moursi, Ahmed Stohy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This article presents an analytical-DES benchmark for intra-aisle robotic picking in automated warehousing. The investigated cycle combines horizontal shuttle travel, tote handling and item picking at storage locations. Simulation results are compared with the analytical formulation of Lerher et al. under the same rack geometry, shuttle-motion, handling and picking conditions, and the same definitions of cycle time and derived item throughput. Reproducing the published analytical tables requires the distance interpretation dES = 1.5 m and dTB = 0 m. The analytical-DES benchmark comprises 180 scenarios formed from three rack lengths, two paired motion profiles and n = 1 to 30 visited picking locations, with ten simulation replications per scenario. The six n = 1 cases are reported separately because no travel-between component is present. Across the remaining 174 scenarios, the mean absolute relative cycle-time discrepancy is 0.8655%, the median is 0.6758%, and the maximum is 7.0370%. The mean absolute error is 2.411 s and the RMSE is 2.744 s. The simulated scenario-mean cycle time is below the analytical value in all 174 principal scenarios. The results therefore show close numerical correspondence under the tested conditions, but not exact agreement, formal statistical equivalence, or validation against an operating warehouse.
  </details>

- **[PaceVLA: Adaptive Temporal Action Quantization in Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25961)**  `arXiv:2610.25961`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Hojin Jeon, Jimin Lee, Taekwan Kim, Nayoung Oh, Taeyoung Kim, Myungkyu Koo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fast and reliable manipulation is crucial for practical robot deployment. Although Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have recently shown remarkable progress as generalist robot policies, their execution often remains temporally inefficient. A key source of this inefficiency is that policies learn fine-grained temporal patterns from teleoperated demonstrations, requiring many low-level action steps. Temporal action quantization offers a simple way to accelerate execution by aggregating consecutive low-level actions into temporally coarser actions. However, applying action quantization without considering when fine-grained control is required introduces a speed-accuracy trade-off: accelerated actions may efficiently reduce redundant steps in some phases, while discarding critical motion details in precision-sensitive interactions. To address this, we propose PaceVLA, a unified framework for adaptive temporal execution in VLAs that integrates two coupled components: (1) mode-specific action decoders built on a shared action expert, supporting multiple temporal granularities and prediction horizons, and (2) a quantizability-guided mode selection scheme which decides whether coarse execution is appropriate, and a context-conditioned router that selects an execution mode at each inference step. Experiments across three multi-task simulation benchmarks and six real-world tasks show that PaceVLA improves execution efficiency while maintaining task success rate across multiple embodiments, including long-horizon and dexterous manipulation.
  </details>

- **[UMI-WM: Interactive World Modeling for Bimanual Manipulation from Wrist Cameras](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23913)**  `arXiv:2610.23913`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Jingxiang Guo, Haoyu Zhao, Xidong Zhang, Yuhang Zheng, Hao Wu, Chen Gao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Universal Manipulation Interface (UMI) pairs wrist-camera videos with camera trajectories and gripper openings, providing scalable, robot-free data for learning how bimanual actions change a shared scene. Existing interactive world models, however, typically rely on text or single-camera control and struggle to follow each arm's actions while maintaining consistency between the two wrist views. We introduce UMI-WM, an action-conditioned bimanual world model that jointly generates both wrist views from observation history, per-arm relative camera poses, and gripper widths, with cross-view consistency, action following, and visual fidelity. UMI-WM injects each arm's actions into its own wrist stream, while sparse hub tokens exchange scene information between the two streams. Camera-aware geometry conditioning and camera-guided noise further support per-view motion control and cross-view geometric exchange. We adapt the model for block-causal generation and distill the 30-step Teacher into a Student with four denoising steps. We train UMI-WM on self-collected open-world and in-studio UMI recordings combined with public manipulation data. To evaluate action following, we use an inverse dynamics model (IDM) to recover relative camera poses and gripper widths from generated videos, and introduce Bimanual Action Fidelity (BAF) to measure how closely these recovered actions match the conditioning actions. UMI-WM achieves the best results on all five reference-video fidelity metrics and the best or competitive performance on VBench and WorldArena. The Teacher and Student outperform all compared baselines in action following, while the four-step Student generates paired wrist videos 6.9 times faster than the Teacher.
  </details>

- **[BioProVLA-Agent: An Affordable, Protocol-Driven, Vision-Enhanced VLA-Enabled Embodied Multi-Agent System with Closed-Loop-Capable Reasoning for Biological Laboratory Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07306)**  `arXiv:2605.07306`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhaohui Du, Zhe Wang, Dongzhan Zhou, Minting Pan, Hongmei Fei, Xiwen Cao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Biological laboratory automation can reduce repetitive manual work and improve reproducibility, but reliable embodied execution in wet-lab environments remains challenging. Protocols are often unstructured, labware is frequently transparent or reflective, and multi-step procedures require state-aware execution beyond one-shot instruction following. Existing robotic systems often rely on costly hardware, fixed workflows, dedicated instruments, or robotics-oriented interfaces. Here, we introduce BioProVLA-Agent, an affordable, protocol-driven, vision-enhanced embodied multi-agent system enabled by Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models for biological manipulation. The system uses protocols as the task interface and integrates protocol parsing, visual state verification, and embodied execution in a closed-loop workflow. A Tailored LLM Protocol Agent converts protocols into verifiable subtasks; a VLM-RAG Verification Agent assesses readiness and completion using observations, robot states, retrieved knowledge, and success/failure examples; and a VLA Embodied Agent executes verified subtasks through a lightweight policy. To improve robustness under wet-lab visual perturbations, we develop AugSmolVLA, an online augmentation strategy targeting transparent labware, reflections, illumination shifts, and overexposure. We evaluate the system on a hierarchical benchmark covering 15 atomic tasks, 6 composite workflows, and 3 bimanual tasks, including tube loading, sorting, waste disposal, cap twisting, and liquid pouring. Across normal and high-exposure settings, AugSmolVLA improves execution stability over ACT, X-VLA, and the original SmolVLA, especially for precise placement, transparent-object manipulation, composite workflows, and visually degraded scenes. These results suggest a practical route toward accessible, protocol-centered, and verification-capable embodied AI for biological manipulation.
  </details>

- **[Causal Scene Narration: Inference-Time Text Restructuring for Vision-Language-Action Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.01723)**  `arXiv:2604.01723`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Yun Li, Yidu Zhang, Simon Thompson, Ehsan Javanmardi, Manabu Tsukada_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models for autonomous driving receive navigation commands and hazard notices as disconnected text fragments, forcing the model to discover on its own which environmental constraints are relevant to the current maneuver. We introduce Causal Scene Narration (CSN), which restructures VLA text inputs through intent-constraint alignment, quantitative grounding, and structured separation at inference time with zero GPU cost. To test CSN across different model states and safety layers, we evaluate it alongside training-time preference alignment (PL-DPO-NLL) and runtime supervision as experimental conditions. A multi-town CARLA evaluation (16 routes, 8 towns, N=5, 95% bootstrap CIs) shows that CSN yields relative improvements in CARLA Driving Score (DS, route completion weighted by infraction penalty) of +31.1% on original LMDrive and +24.5% on the PL-DPO-NLL variant. A controlled ablation separating information content from text organization reveals that information content carries most of CSN's gain across both model weights (+6.17 DS on original LMDrive; +6.88 DS on PL-DPO-NLL). While causal structure accounts for an estimated 39.1% (95% CI [6.6%, 71.4%]) of the gain on original LMDrive versus 13.5% on PL-DPO-NLL, the structural share on the aligned model and the cross-configuration asymmetry remain statistically inconclusive at N=5 due to wide bootstrap intervals. A perception noise ablation further confirms that CSN's benefit survives distance errors up to +/-5 m, speed noise up to +/-30%, and 20% actor miss rates. Finally, we report a negative result on combining CSN with runtime supervision: a naive direction-conflict monitor never fires on this benchmark and, when paired with CSN, degrades DS via passive control clamping that truncates CSN-guided evasive maneuvers. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[GPT-6 Astra Is a Native Visuo-Tactile Policy for Contact-Rich Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26846)**  `arXiv:2610.26846`  `cs.RO`  
  _Chen Yang, Linzhe Shi, Hang Zhang, Mu Xu, Jiansheng Fan, Chen Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in multimodal models have enabled GPT-6 Astra to perform robotic manipulation through in-context learning. Yet contact-rich control and tactile perception are often regarded as strengths of specialist policies, leaving open whether Astra can directly understand and use tactile observations. We provide, to our knowledge, the first demonstration that Astra directly interprets vision-based tactile images and uses their contact cues for manipulation. Tactile RGB images enter through its existing visual interface, enabling few-shot closed-loop control on nineteen simulated UniVTAC and OpenViTac tasks with 210 sampled scene seeds per setting. With visuo-tactile input, Astra achieves mean success rates of 93.8% and 81.8%, exceeding the strongest reported specialist baselines in our comparison by 22.9 and 13.1 percentage points, respectively. Vision-only performance also leads these baselines, reaching 76.3% and 80.6%. Trajectory analysis reveals active probing, cautious grasping, contact-guided alignment, and recovery, alongside difficulties with tangential loading and corrective execution. Our findings challenge the exclusivity of specialist policies in contact-rich manipulation and show that fine-grained vision can partially compensate for missing touch. They also identify vision-based tactile sensing as an interface for scaling contact understanding with foundation models and reveal signs of benchmark saturation. These results motivate joint visual-tactile pretraining, harder contact-rich evaluations, and physical-robot validation.
  </details>

- **[HandsOn: A Large-Scale Simulation Benchmark for Generalizable Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26765)**  `arXiv:2610.26765`  `cs.RO`  
  _Tianwei Luo, Yiming He, Hongjin Wu, Ting Pan, Debin Ren, Haorui Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General-purpose manipulation requires reliable behavior across embodiments, objects, and task demands. We introduce HandsOn, a composable simulation platform and benchmark for cross-embodiment dexterous manipulation. It supports five robotic arms, six dexterous hands, and approximately 200 rigid, articulated, and deformable assets in randomized scenes. The benchmark pairs 18,000 curated human demonstrations with 66 human-verified tasks: Core-50 supports demonstration-based learning and execution tests, while Transfer-16 evaluates asset and relation transfer, skill composition, and deformable manipulation without target-task training demonstrations. Qualitative real-robot rollouts illustrate sim-to-real transfer. Across diffusion, vision-language-action, world-action, and prompted VLM-based methods, the best trained multi-task policy achieves 29.1% Core-50 success. Without benchmark finetuning, GPT-6 using move_eef for palm-and-finger control achieves 5.2% on Core-50 and 1.9% on Transfer-16 over 660 trials. Trained-policy stage diagnostics expose failures in contact establishment, action coordination, and maintaining achieved goals, with additional sequencing errors in composed tasks. HandsOn provides a shared basis for investigating manipulation generalization and the limits of reliable physical interaction.
  </details>

- **[RoboValue: A Fine-Grained Sim-and-Real Benchmark for Unified Evaluation of Robotic Value Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26663)**  `arXiv:2610.26663`  `cs.RO`  
  _Shengbang Liu, Zhengye Du, Zhilong Wan, Honghao Su, Chenxiang Xia, Chang Ge, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robotic value models assess task execution from visual observations and instructions, providing feedback for policy learning and execution monitoring. However, existing evaluations cover only selected aspects of execution understanding, leaving the reliability of value feedback insufficiently tested. We introduce RoboValue, a fine-grained sim-and-real benchmark for unified evaluation of robotic value models. Its framework evaluates four complementary capability dimensions through shared interfaces: task-state understanding, temporal progress monitoring, failure and recovery reasoning, and value consistency. Diagnostic trajectories and instruction variations test whether value judgments accurately reflect task requirements and changes throughout execution. RoboValue-Dataset covers 15 simulation and 20 real-world manipulation tasks, providing expert demonstrations for training and annotated trajectories for fine-grained evaluation. We evaluate representative robotic value models in zero-shot and one-shot settings under standard conditions and separate shifts in robot embodiment and environment. Our results reveal errors in fine-grained instruction grounding, history-dependent progress tracking, and recovery assessment, even in models with strong outcome and forward-progress scores. The same semantic subtask can also receive substantially different value gains across valid execution orders. These findings identify concrete targets for value supervision, while the dataset and public leaderboard support continued assessment of feedback reliability. The website is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ReInsVLN: Revisiting Historical Evidence for Training-Free Vision-and-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26545)**  `arXiv:2610.26545`  `cs.RO`  
  _Siwei Cheng, Hao Wu, Zitong Qi, Xinming Zhang, Haoyu Xi, Peiran Yin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-and-language navigation (VLN) relies on retaining and using relevant observations. Yet growing histories introduce redundancy, and retained information may remain underused. We introduce ReInsBench, a diagnostic benchmark that examines history utilization through controlled continuation trials. Its results show that history-review reinstruction can recover navigation failures using the existing task context. Motivated by this finding, we propose ReInsVLN, a training-free framework supporting memory construction and utilization in frozen navigators. Coverage- and Instruction-Aware Compression constructs compact visual context through temporal redundancy reduction, coverage-aware protection, and instruction-guided token selection. Forgetting-Inspired History Reminders guide navigators to revisit retained observations according to a predefined schedule, without additional model calls. On R2R Val-Unseen, ReInsVLN improves success rates by 8.48 and 3.37 percentage points over the native StreamVLN and InternVLA-N1, reaching 64.27% and 64.44%, respectively. Visual compression largely preserves navigation performance while reducing prefill latency and KV cache memory by up to 21.77% and 32.25%, respectively, on matched observation workloads. Real-world trials on humanoid and quadruped robots further support the applicability of ReInsVLN to sequential instruction following and ordinal navigation.
  </details>

- **[AeroIntent: A Synchronized Multimodal Benchmark for UAV Mission Intent Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25869)**  `arXiv:2610.25869`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yutong Chen, Jingxin Mao, Zhiwei Wei, Bing Li, Jianzhong Ni, Rongqing Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable recognition of mission-level UAV intent from multimodal observations is essential for low-altitude airspace surveillance and security. Existing multimodal UAV datasets primarily support detection, tracking, and localization, leaving limited support for evaluating mission-intent recognition across held-out trajectory programs and incomplete radar histories. We introduce AeroIntent, a simulation-based benchmark comprising 990 synchronized multimodal sequences generated by 22 trajectory programs across five mission intents. The benchmark provides radar, RGB, infrared, and depth observations, together with program-disjoint, radar-gap, and observation-prefix evaluation protocols. Building on this benchmark, we propose AeroMIR, a structured Multimodal Intent Reasoner that integrates radar trajectories, derived behavioral features, selected visual evidence, and task knowledge for candidate-based UAV intent recognition. Under a strict program-disjoint protocol evaluating unseen tactical realizations, AeroMIR achieves a mean accuracy of 71.56% and a mean Macro-F1 of 69.84% across three seeds, outperforming the strongest trajectory-only baseline by 7.79 Macro-F1 points and a conventional radar-RGB late-fusion baseline by 19.32 points. AeroMIR also demonstrates strong performance under controlled radar gaps and early intent recognition settings. AeroIntent and AeroMIR provide a reproducible foundation for multimodal UAV intent recognition. The benchmark is available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[ScaleNav: Scalable Internet Video Pretraining for Language-Conditioned Local Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25690)**  `arXiv:2610.25690`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yihui Yang, Yuanhong Yu, Jiawei Wang, Zhongrui Yu, Jia Shi, Kun Zhan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study language-conditioned local navigation, where a robot must navigate to a text-specified target visible in its initial observation and stop at the described location. End-to-end navigation policies require diverse training data to generalize across scenes, yet real-robot data is costly to collect and simulated data leaves a sim-to-real gap. Internet egocentric videos offer a scalable source of real-world navigation experience. Our key insight is to learn goal-directed visual dynamics from Internet egocentric videos, and use these dynamics to support robot action generation. To this end, we propose ScaleNav, a unified world-action framework with three-stage training. We first perform instruction-conditioned video pretraining on these videos to learn how observations evolve as the camera approaches instructed targets across diverse real-world scenes. Action alignment then uses action-labeled simulated data to train the model to predict executable action chunks. Joint fine-tuning subsequently optimizes all trainable components together for navigation. ScaleNav achieves success rates of 86.5% on a single-view adaptation of Short-Horizon OVON and 94.9% on our HabitatGS-LN benchmark, outperforming the evaluated baselines on both benchmarks. We further deploy and evaluate ScaleNav on a real quadruped robot, with results supporting the model's ability to generalize to real-world navigation. Code and videos are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Vector-Quantized World Model: Latent Feedback Motion Planning for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25600)**  `arXiv:2610.25600`  `cs.RO`  
  _Xuezhou Xu, Chung-Pang Wang, Zhenzhen Li, Benjamin Riviere_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual world models have shown strong potential for robot planning, yet long-horizon manipulation remains challenging due to compounding prediction errors, large search complexity, and latent plans that the low-level controller cannot reliably reach. In this work, we introduce Vector-Quantized World Model (VQ-WM), a latent feedback motion planning framework that integrates discrete long-horizon search with continuous visual feedback control: a discrete latent predictor models transitions between vector-quantized latent states, allowing the planner to look far ahead and find a shortest path to the goal through discrete search and a continuous learned controller sequentially reaches the planned states through visual feedback, correcting deviations to mitigate compounding errors. Whereas most vector quantization uses Euclidean distance, we use a novel estimator of the first-passage-time as a quasi-metric, which aligns the planning abstraction with the controller's capabilities. We evaluate on the physical robot manipulation of the Tower of Hanoi puzzle: whereas existing state-of-the-art foundation models (VLAs, WAMs) only succeed when the solution trace in their training dataset, our method achieves 100\% success rate with only short-play data, demonstrate its ability to compose long-horizon solutions beyond the short demonstrated trajectories and execute them reliably. Our solution also outperforms strongest world-models by 13.3--85.3 percentage points on simulation benchmarks spanning manipulation and locomotion, demonstrating consistent progress and new capabilities.
  </details>

- **[AQUAACT: A Joystick-Level Underwater Teleoperation Dataset and Closed-Loop Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25534)**  `arXiv:2610.25534`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zhenqi Wu, Trung Tien Dong, Aditya Penumarti, Mohamed A. Fouad, Menna A. Fouad, Mingyang Mao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Underwater robots support a growing range of work, from inspecting offshore structures and pipelines to surveying reefs and accompanying divers. Most of these tasks, however, are still flown by hand by a pilot, which raises the question of whether they can be carried out autonomously instead. On land, vision-language-action (VLA) models already let robots carry out tasks autonomously from language instructions, which makes them a natural candidate for taking the pilot's place. Underwater, however, there is little data to train such models. We take inspiration from how these tasks are flown today: the pilot watches the camera image and steers with a joystick, whose commands the flight controller turns into motion. We therefore record the pilots and introduce AQUAACT, to our knowledge the first publicly available language-annotated underwater dataset pairing camera images with human joystick commands. It comprises 1.9 hours of real-world and 17.9 hours of simulated demonstrations, both flown by human pilots, together with the vehicle's inertial measurements. Experiments with AQUAACT show that its simulated demonstrations transfer to real data and greatly reduce the real demonstrations a policy needs. A closed-loop benchmark of seven policies in simulation, from imitation-learning baselines to VLA models, each trained with its default recipe, shows that these tasks remain far from solved: no policy completes more than a fifth of the episodes. Pretraining on land data gives large VLA models no advantage over small policies trained from scratch, a gap that land-trained models have yet to close underwater.
  </details>

- **[CusADi2: Latency-Optimized Batch Computation of Robot Kinematics, Jacobians, and Hessians on the GPU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25476)**  `arXiv:2610.25476`  `cs.RO`  
  _John Viljoen, Lasse Peters, Masayoshi Tomizuka, Negar Mehr_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Trajectory optimization methods, from sampling-based zero-order to gradient-based first- and second-order approaches, require many evaluations of dynamical system functions, i.e., functions derived from a robot's dynamical model, such as forward kinematics and their Jacobians and Hessians. These evaluations are largely independent, e.g., across sampled trajectories or time steps, and can thus be evaluated in parallel as a batch. When used to make decisions online, these methods require low latency, i.e., each batch must complete in as little wall-clock time as possible. Existing frameworks utilize modern GPU hardware primarily to parallelize across batch instances, thereby optimizing throughput but not latency. We present CusADi2, a software library that reduces the batch evaluation latency by additionally parallelizing computation within each batch instance. We benchmark forward kinematics, Jacobians, and Hessians for all 67~robots in the MuJoCo Menagerie, including arms, hands, quadrupeds, humanoids, and biomechanical models, against MuJoCo XLA, MuJoCo Warp, frax, CusADi, and CasADi. CusADi2 achieves lower latency than all baselines at batch sizes of 64 to 4096, while matching them at larger batch sizes. We also demonstrate the performance advantage of CusADi2 in a sampling-based control application. All code is open-source at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[T(R,O) Grasp++: Cross-Embodiment Dexterous Grasping in Cluttered Environments at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25410)**  `arXiv:2610.25410`  `cs.RO`  
  _Xin Fei, Zhixuan Xu, Huaicong Fang, Fangjun Chen, Zhaoyu Mu, Rui Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dexterous grasping in cluttered scenes remains a central challenge in robotic manipulation, as it requires high-dimensional hand coordination under complex object-environment constraints. To address this problem, we introduce one of the largest simulation benchmarks to date, comprising around 0.5B grasp annotations across eight dexterous hands and approximately 50K objects. Furthermore, we propose T(R,O) Grasp++, a scalable diffusion-based framework for cross-embodiment dexterous grasp synthesis in cluttered environments. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our model significantly outperforms existing baselines, achieving an average success rate of 88% across eight dexterous embodiments. Meanwhile, our pipeline achieves only 0.3s inference latency while improving inference sample efficiency by over 10 times.
  </details>

- **[The Bitter Lesson Comes to Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25287)**  `arXiv:2610.25287`  `cs.RO`  
  _Christopher Leet, Achyutha Menon, Sravanthi Machcha, Sven Koenig, Tzu Kit Chan, Jay Chooi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General purpose multimodal language models (LMs) are increasingly capable of directly controlling robotic arms (e.g., by commanding end effector poses) to manipulate their environment. Tracking the development of these capabilities is important for understanding their potential benefits, such as improving industrial automation, and risks, such as enabling malicious users to produce dangerous chemical or biological agents. To this end, we investigate the ability of frontier multimodal LMs to directly control physical robotic arms. We evaluate seven models at multiple reasoning-effort settings on six manipulation tasks taken from the RoboDojo benchmark. To our knowledge, this is the first systematic comparison of a broad range of frontier LMs across a common suite of manipulation tasks on physical hardware. We find that current frontier models can directly control real robotic arms to perform a variety of manipulation tasks but are not yet precise or efficient enough to complete them within a practical action budget. However, these capabilities are improving rapidly: performance increases on our benchmark between July and September 2026 (inclusive) correspond to a performance doubling time of 44.5 days, while the best recorded execution rate increased by a factor of 4.4 and the lowest recorded execution cost fell by a factor of 6.2. These trends raise the possibility that the Bitter Lesson is coming to robotics; the capabilities of embodied general purpose LMs may one day be comparable to those of more specialized models.
  </details>

- **[Relational Spatial Alignment for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24705)**  `arXiv:2610.24705`  `cs.RO`  
  _Chaoran Zhu, Hengyi Wang, Chenglin Cui, Yazhe Wan, Taein Kwon, Changjae Oh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial understanding is essential for robots to perceive their surrounding environments and execute actions. Existing approaches typically encode 3D awareness into vision-language-action (VLA) models by directly aligning their representations with pre-trained 3D foundation models. However, we observe that such direct geometric feature alignment essentially exploits 3D foundation priors as a form of low-rank regularization in the feature space, resulting in severe rank collapse of the pre-trained VLA representations. In contrast, we shift the focus from enforcing point-wise equality to aligning pair-wise relationships. We propose a representation-alignment method that enables the injection of 3D spatial awareness without compromising the model's original expressivity. Extensive experiments on public benchmarks, our new spatial reasoning benchmark, and real-world manipulation scenarios demonstrate that our approach effectively incorporates 3D spatial representations into VLA reasoning, achieving robust performance and enhanced spatial awareness across diverse manipulation tasks.
  </details>

- **[Noise-Invariant Spatial Encoding for 3D Manipulation Policies under Depth Corruption](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24674)**  `arXiv:2610.24674`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zhiyuan Yang, Jiaju He, Ruiteng Zhao, Anqi Cheng, Haiyue Zhu, Kezhi Mao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D manipulation policies derive actions from explicit scene geometry rather than color images alone, enabling them to reason over metric distances, object relations, and robot-scene reachability. This geometric interface is also a vulnerability: commodity RGB-D sensors return missing measurements, boundary artifacts, and plausible but wrong surfaces around transparent, reflective, thin, or textureless objects. We study this failure under realistic depth corruption and observe a sharp depth-noise cliff across modern 3D policies, even when they are trained with matched noisy depth. This residual gap motivates a spatial-encoding approach: corrupted depth becomes entangled with the token coordinates and geometry features consumed by the action expert. We propose Noise-Invariant Spatial Encoding (NISE), a 3D policy architecture that learns trustworthy geometry features even facing imperfect depth. NISE uses Geometric Cross-View Self-Supervision to shape shared policy features with calibrated multi-view depth, and Gaussian Marginalized 3D Attention to carry position uncertainty into deployed attention rather than treating every lifted token as exact. Across simulated benchmarks with sensor-realistic corruption and real-robot tasks involving transparent and reflective materials, NISE improves depth-noise robustness while preserving clean-depth performance, supporting 3D policies designed for commodity RGB-D deployment rather than clean-depth benchmarks alone.
  </details>

- **[Refuse the Harm, Complete the Task via Graph-of-Rules Planning for LLM-Enabled Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24603)**  `arXiv:2610.24603`  `cs.RO`  
  _Ali Taheri, Alireza Taban, Qizhou Wang, Wanhua Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used in embodied AI for robotic manipulation and planning, but their deployment in physical systems exposes vulnerabilities such as jailbreak attacks that induce unsafe behavior. Existing inference-time defenses are mainly evaluated on short instructions containing a single hazard, whereas realistic tasks may interleave safe and unsafe sub-tasks, conceal hazardous steps, or combine individually safe actions into unsafe outcomes. Such compound instructions make it harder for a single safety decision to distinguish hazardous sub-tasks from legitimate ones, risking either overlooking the hazard or rejecting the whole instruction. We introduce safe task completion, a task that requires an LLM-enabled robot to refuse the harmful part of an instruction while completing its legitimate part. To address this problem, we propose RuleGraph, a multi-agent Graph-of-Rules planning framework that distributes safety enforcement across multiple reasoning and verification stages. Five cooperating agents are used to ground general safety principles into world-specific rules, assess the instruction's sub-tasks in context, and derive a safe goal from the legitimate ones. Planning toward this goal is then organized as a graph of rule-specific nodes, where higher-level nodes inherit the union of their parents' rules, and candidate plans are progressively verified and aggregated until a single plan satisfies every relevant rule. In this way, RuleGraph reduces reliance on a single centralized safety decision. We evaluate RuleGraph on pure-attack and compound instructions across two benchmarks in two environments using Attack Success Rate (ASR) and Task Success Rate (TSR). RuleGraph reduces ASR to 0% on both benchmarks while achieving the highest TSR among all methods, reaching 98.57% on the RoboGuard benchmark and 84.54% on our long-horizon compound instructions.
  </details>

- **[LIBERO-Pro2.0: A Benchmark for Evaluating the Robustness of Embodied Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24587)**  `arXiv:2610.24587`  `cs.RO`  
  _Xueyang Zhou, Yangming Xu, Qianjiang Li, Guiyao Tie, Junhao Wu, Ziheng Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LIBERO has emerged as a widely adopted benchmark for evaluating robotic manipulation policies. However, strong performance on LIBERO does not necessarily indicate robust embodied capabilities, as models may exploit benchmark-specific regularities rather than generalize to diverse operating conditions. In this work, we introduce LIBERO-Pro2.0, a comprehensive benchmark for systematically evaluating the robustness of embodied foundation models under distribution shifts. LIBERO-Pro2.0 encompasses 22 static perturbations and 20 runtime interventions across six dimensions: Condition, Environment, Observation, Execution, Robot State Configuration, and Language, yielding 1,648 validated shifted task instances. To support robustness-oriented learning, we further collect 39,390 training trajectories through model-generated and human demonstrations. All shifted task instances undergo six rounds of iterative human review and correction to ensure physical plausibility and task validity. We evaluate 17 representative robot policies spanning vision-language-action models, world-action models, and robustness-oriented policies. Our results reveal substantial robustness gaps across all evaluated paradigms: under runtime distribution shifts, the evaluated models exhibit an average performance drop of 26.5 percentage points, with individual drops reaching 32.5 points. These findings highlight the gap between nominal benchmark performance and robustness under distribution shifts, underscoring the need for systematic robustness evaluation of embodied foundation models.
  </details>

- **[LIBERO-Dynamic: Benchmarking Robotic Manipulation in Dynamic Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24579)**  `arXiv:2610.24579`  `cs.RO`  
  _Shaojie Ren, Jingwen Sun, Zezhi Liu, Hongjin Chen, Xin Jin, Wei Zou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most simulation benchmarks for robotic manipulation keep target objects static and treat policy inference as instantaneous. These assumptions simplify evaluation but hide how a moving target evolves during computation and how stale an observation is when an action is executed. We introduce LIBERO-Dynamic, a dynamic extension of all 40 LIBERO tasks. It drives objects through contact friction along three trajectory families and speed tiers, while advancing the physical world according to calibrated, model-specific inference latency. The same mechanism supports training by replaying verified static demonstrations and compensating robot actions online for target displacement, producing 9,000 quality-filtered dynamic trajectories. We evaluate six pretrained policies on 1,800 dynamic conditions per configuration, compare execution strategies and action horizons with fixed checkpoints, and fine-tune three architectures using dynamic and mixed data. Faster motion degrades every policy but not uniformly; execution timing and horizon can change the behavior of the same policy; and asynchronous replanning benefits from frequent feedback only when the action queue avoids underflow. Dynamic demonstrations improve each architecture under at least one recipe, with gains varying by temporal conditioning, data composition, and training budget. A small-scale physical study shows task-dependent execution-strategy differences consistent with simulation. LIBERO-Dynamic makes object motion, inference latency, and action execution explicit and controllable for evaluating and improving robot policies in moving-target manipulation.
  </details>

- **[SocialCoAct: Enabling and Benchmarking Egocentric Social Co-Action for VLM-Based Humanoid Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24559)**  `arXiv:2610.24559`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jiancong Ma, Kun Li, Jin Jiang, Shengcai Liao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Enabling humanoid robots to participate effectively in social interactions is important for their integration into human-centered environments. Existing methods, however, typically map pre-generated speech directly to continuous motion trajectories but have limited awareness of complex social environments and lack explicit high-level semantic action planning. We introduce SocialCoAct, a unified agent-centered framework for VLM-based egocentric social co-action. We formulate egocentric social co-action as an end-to-end agent task and develop SocialCoAct-Agent, which transforms egocentric observations and dialogue history into verbal responses and atomic action sequences, aligns each action with its corresponding utterance span, and executes the resulting behaviors in MuJoCo and on a physical humanoid robot. To evaluate the VLM capabilities required by this task, we construct SocialCoAct-Bench from continuous egocentric recordings of natural multi-party interactions. The benchmark comprises 423 human-verified multiple-choice questions across three subtasks: Long Episode Understanding (LEU), Speaker Intention Understanding (SIU), and Coordinated Action Planning (CAP). Across a diverse set of closed-source and open-weight VLMs, including general and egocentric models, the highest overall accuracy is 77.54%, while accuracy on the hard subset reaches only 46.51%. Extended reasoning improves Qwen3-VL-2B and Qwen3-VL-4B by 19.62 and 17.02 percentage points, respectively, but reduces inference throughput by 53.6$\times$ and 20.2$\times$. Visual-input ablations further show that the benefits of visual context vary across models. SocialCoAct provides a foundation for developing and evaluating VLM-based humanoid agents that coordinate context-aware speech and action in complex social interactions. The codebase and benchmark will be made publicly available upon publication.
  </details>

- **[ContactBench 1.0: A Benchmark for Everyday Contact-Rich Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24512)**  `arXiv:2610.24512`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jingyi Xiang, Yixuan Wang, Jeannette Bohg, Wenzhen Yuan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Contact-rich manipulation is essential to everyday activities, yet evaluating robots' ability to perform these activities remains challenging because of their diverse contact behaviors and the difficulty of reproducing tasks across laboratories. Drawing on observations of everyday human manipulation, we identify eight recurring skill categories that provide a practical framework for studying these capabilities. We introduce ContactBench 1.0, an extensible benchmark that instantiates these skills through 36 reference tasks and more than 40 object assets. The assets capture task-relevant geometry and contact behavior, support reproducible fabrication using consumer-grade 3D printers, and are paired with simulation models with tunable resistance loads. Controlled task variations enable evaluation of policy generalization. We evaluate recent vision-based and vision-tactile policy learning methods on six benchmark tasks. The results reveal that the evaluated vision-only policies struggle to perceive or react to contact events, while the evaluated visual-tactile policies do not meaningfully improve performance. By providing shared tasks and physical and simulated assets, ContactBench 1.0 establishes a foundation for reproducible evaluation of contact-rich manipulation research. The benchmark is open-sourced atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[FLASH-CBF-RRT: Fast Lipschitz-Adaptive Safe Horizon CBF Steering for Certified Motion Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24149)**  `arXiv:2610.24149`  `cs.RO`  
  _Mani Amani, Hesam Mojtahedi, Reza Akhavian, Nikolay Atanasov, Ki Myung Brian Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fast motion planning requires both sample-efficient exploration and computationally efficient local steering. Conventional straight-line steering is inexpensive but wastes samples by rejecting colliding extensions. Quadratic program controllers with control barrier function (CBF) constraints can be used to achieve effective local steering but can increase the planning time due to the expensive sequential evaluations. We develop a method to accelerate CBF steering by computing a time horizon within which CBF-filtered velocity may be applied constantly using closed-form kinematic bounds on collision-clearance. Our method, FLASH-CBF-RRT, significantly reduces the number of evaluations compared to prior CBF-RRT approaches because the planner can take adaptive steering step sizes that are shorter near obstacles and longer in open space. We implement this steering procedure as an extension of RRT-Connect in OMPL. Across 689 MotionBenchMaker problems, FLASH-CBF-RRT matches RRT-Connect's 100% success rate while reducing median planning time by 3.82x, and is 3.88x faster than fixed-step CBF-RRT. Hardware experiments on a 17-DoF mobile manipulator demonstrate 2.2x faster planning and 4.2x fewer evaluations. Videos and results are available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Rollbot 2: Agile Locomotion by a Single-Actuator Spherical Robot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24129)**  `arXiv:2610.24129`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jingxian Wang*, Chen Yu*, Sam Kriegman, Michael Rubenstein_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reducing actuator count simplifies robot hardware but also removes independent inputs for maneuvering. The original Rollbot steered by modulating a quasi-stable helical orbit, but moved slowly and relied on external motion capture. Rollbot 2 preserves the single-actuator design while its redesigned mass distribution enables near-straight rolling and more direct travel toward commanded paths and beacon targets. Onboard inertial and ultra-wideband sensing support learned steering, beacon seeking, and waypoint following through one motor-velocity command. Rollbot 2 autonomously approaches UWB beacons without motion capture and completes a 468~m manually commanded indoor route at 0.87~m/s. On the shared 4.8~m ``N'' waypoint benchmark, all 16 trials complete the path with a mean traversal time of 10.8~s and mean time-averaged deviation of 7~cm. Its fastest traversal takes 8.0~s, over 70 times faster than the original Rollbot's 564~s run despite minor protocol differences. These results show how mechanical redesign, onboard sensing, and learning-based control improve the agility and autonomy of a single-actuator spherical robot.
  </details>

- **[PrismPlan: A Shared GPU Substrate for Motion Planning Across Paradigms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23884)**  `arXiv:2610.23884`  `cs.RO`  
  _Hong Wu, ChaoXuan Liu, ShuJie Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GPU acceleration has made motion planning one to two orders of magnitude faster, but it has not made the three paradigms---sampling, search, and optimization---comparable to one another: every GPU-accelerated planner is built around a single paradigm, so comparing across libraries also compares their collision checkers. All three, however, bottleneck on the same GPU primitive: batched collision detection and the batched forward kinematics beneath it. PrismPlan builds that primitive once, as a shared substrate of per-sphere collision--kinematics device code that every planner consumes. Because they all execute the same device code, the backend is held fixed by construction and the paradigms can be compared head-to-head. We run six planners over 1800 MotionBenchMaker problems on three robots (7--14 DoF, one a dual-arm whole-body composite) at an equal 2s budget. On the 7-DoF Panda, sampling (pRRTC) is the most reliable paradigm at 98%, while parallel search (wPA*SE) follows within four points at a 3-4 times$ lower median latency, and the local optimizers trail well behind when made to solve from scratch. On the 14-DoF dual-arm composite, sampling still solves about 90% of feasible problems, while no search or optimization planner clears a third of them. Our open-source code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Stress-Query and Temporal Reliability of Vision-Language Models as copilots in Autonomous UAV Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23877)**  `arXiv:2610.23877`  `cs.RO`  
  _Hiago Sodre, Sebastian Barcelona, Vincent Sandin, Pablo Moraes, Andre S. Kelbouscas, Ricardo B. Grando_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Integrating Vision-Language Models (VLMs) into autonomous UAV navigation enables high-level semantic reasoning but introduces critical temporal bottlenecks that can destabilize flight control. This paper presents a systematic benchmark that evaluates the operational trade-offs among model scale, hardware constraints, and flight reliability. By subjecting the system to extensive stress tests on both a high-performance workstation and a constrained integrated-graphics laptop, we isolate the kinetic impact of inference latency against reasoning accuracy. Our findings reveal a performance dichotomy: lightweight models struggle with structural hallucinations that break deterministic state machines, while heavyweight models induce trajectory overcorrections due to inference lag. We demonstrate that a mid-weight architecture strikes the optimal balance, successfully rehabilitating navigation in degraded environments without compromising real-time stability across different hardware profiles.
  </details>

- **[Flow structure decides whether a robot searching for odour should follow the wind](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20970)**  `arXiv:2610.20970`  `cs.RO`  
  _Junlong Shen, Shunsuke Shigaki_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robots searching for odour usually surge upwind on contact, steering by the wind sensed now. In simulated reversing wind this rule collapses, favouring controllers that hold an earlier heading. We show that on our benchmark the collapse came from the simulated anemometer. Reading the nominal wind, wind-following controllers reached the source in at most 3.5% of episodes. Reading the odour-carrying flow, the best reached 90%, ahead of a held heading. The winner then depends on the flow. A held heading won in smooth fields that reverse quickly and lost in a stochastic field. A controller-free measure of the sensed wind ranked a moth-inspired follower's success. On a quadruped in a reversing fan arena, wind-following controllers were pushed away from the source, while a heading held from a source-facing start was not. Benchmarks should state which wind their sensor reads and test more than one flow structure.
  </details>

- **[Accelerating VLA Inference with Perturbation Informed Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19656)**  `arXiv:2610.19656`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jehun Lee, Hajun Lee, Jimin Ryu, Youhan Lee, Beomseok Kang, Jonghyun Choi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Action-chunking vision-language-action (VLA) models predict sequences of future robot actions. Under fixed-horizon execution, the robot executes a fixedlength prefix of each predicted chunk before invoking the policy again, leaving the remaining predictions unused. For a given number of executed actions, short execution horizons require more policy calls. Extending the execution horizon amortizes policy inference cost over more actions. However, uniformly applying long horizons across episodes can limit opportunities for precise corrections. To address this limitation, we propose PICES (Perturbation-Informed Chunk and Execution Horizon Selection), a training-free method that jointly selects an action chunk and the number of actions executed before the next policy call. Within a single policy call, the action expert generates a batch of action chunks from multiple initial noise samples and their locally perturbed counterparts, using the same conditioning features across all samples. PICES exploits differences between corresponding actions in each nominal chunk and its counterpart to determine candidate-specific execution horizons and select a nominal chunk for execution. Experiments on simulation benchmarks and real-world manipulation tasks demonstrate that PICES achieves speedup by up to 2.3x in wall-clock policy inference time on Jetson Orin and Thor relative to fixed horizon baselines while improving the success rate.
  </details>

- **[LynxScenes: A Large-Scale Real-World Benchmark for Outdoor Wheel-Legged Robot Perception, Navigation, Imagination and Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18093)**  `arXiv:2610.18093`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jun Tian, Hao Wu, Ruo Chen, Yili Liao, Hongyi Lin, Jincheng Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Wheel-legged robots operating in outdoor spaces shared with humans require accurate perception, socially compliant navigation, future-scene reasoning, and scene reconstruction. However, existing datasets typically support these capabilities in isolation and rarely provide dense spatial and behavior-level supervision on real wheel-legged platforms. We introduce LynxScenes, a large-scale real-world dataset and benchmark collected with a LYNX M20 wheel-legged robot. It contains approximately 100K 30-s navigation clips spanning 833.6 h and 3,294.7 km, with 3.0M annotated frames. The synchronized recordings include four fisheye cameras, dual LiDARs, IMU, odometry, and robot states. Annotations comprise 20.82M oriented 3D boxes across 27 categories, 13.89M road-structure annotations, and 20 navigation instructions organized into three layers and aligned with continuous future trajectories. LynxScenes supports four complementary tasks: instruction-conditioned trajectory prediction, BEV-based perception of 3D objects and road structures, future scene prediction, and multi-view scene reconstruction. For trajectory prediction, we further introduce a log-replay-based pseudo-closed-loop evaluator that measures safety, progress, and instruction compliance beyond trajectory imitation. Extensive evaluations across representative navigation, perception, world-model, and reconstruction methods reveal substantial remaining challenges in close-range outdoor robot navigation. The dataset and evaluation toolkit are planned for public release.
  </details>

- **[Embodied Agents Take Control: Minimal-Interface Zero-Shot Agents Rival Industrial-Scale Policies in Vision-and-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26148)**  `arXiv:2607.26148`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jian Zhou, Xunyi Zhao, Gengze Zhou, Zerui Li, Sihao Lin, Jiajun Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous embodied agents must sustain a long decision-making loop of perceiving, acting, verifying, and self-correcting. In trained end-to-end policies and hand-designed workflows, the interaction structure remains externally specified. We study how much of this loop a general-purpose model can direct through a minimal interface. We term this organization agentic embodied control: the reasoning model directly steers every action, keeping reasoning and control aligned. We evaluate fourteen frontier multimodal models as provided by their vendors, with monocular RGB and primitive actions across six navigation and embodied question-answering benchmarks. On 100-episode subsets, the best agents exceed published scores and approach or surpass our human tester. Who directs the loop matters: SmartWay and O2C-Nav using the same strong models achieve substantially lower success rates on R2R-CE. The lightweight mini-swe-agent harness performs comparably to vendor harnesses. Stronger models benefit from optional rather than imposed waypoints; adding SLAM raises R2R-CE success to 88%. Physical trials expose limitations in body awareness, execution feedback, and persistent spatial understanding.
  </details>

- **[SA-LIVO: Efficient LiDAR-Inertial-Visual Odometry with Subspace-Aware Degeneracy Handling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25699)**  `arXiv:2606.25699`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yinong Cao, Xin He, Yijie Wang, Tianzhen Ma, Pingfeng He, Chenyang Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Light detection and ranging (LiDAR)-inertial-visual odometry fuses geometric depth with visual measurements, but its sensors fail independently: LiDAR in under-constrained geometry, vision under poor illumination or texture. Weak information is harmless, since the filter then relies on the other sensor or the inertial prediction; over-confident information is not. Existing countermeasures act on weak information or switch vision by hand-set thresholds. This article presents SA-LIVO, which fuses vision through explicit, online-estimated error models. A frame-level noise scale, estimated by covariance matching, absorbs whole-image errors, and Schmidt filtering carries the correlation of reused observations. Subspace-aware information fusion models the error shared by a frame's observations as a pose offset capping the visual information per direction; the exact fusion attenuates vision only above that cap and reduces to the information sum when the visual model is consistent. LiDAR and visual residuals share one invariant extended Kalman filter loop. On 24 handheld and aerial sequences, SA-LIVO is the most accurate or tied on 22 and stays bounded where others diverge; on the HILTI 2022 benchmark it needs 13.4 ms per frame on a laptop processor and 2.0-6.2x less memory than FAST-LIVO2, R3LIVE, and SR-LIVO.
  </details>

- **[VQActFlow: Vector-Quantized Action Mode Steering for Multi-Task Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21600)**  `arXiv:2606.21600`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zhigen Zhao, Mark Leggiero, Yipu Chen, Haoran Liu, Yifan Wu, Huishu Xue, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-task robot manipulation policies are challenging to learn from demonstration because traditionally a single network must select among qualitatively different action modes from a multimodal demonstration distribution, conditioned on language and visual context. A wrong mode selection means executing the wrong task or an action infeasible in the scene. Tokenizing continuous actions into a learned discrete codebook separates these modes at the representation level, offering structural advantages for multi-task learning. We propose VQActFlow, a multi-task manipulation policy that tokenizes action chunks and generates code sequences via Variational Flow Matching. VQActFlow maintains an explicit preference over action modes throughout generation. Inference-time guidance acts on this preference to steer mode commitment. We instantiate this with classifier-free guidance over language conditioning, which steers the policy toward the instructed action mode, and a learned codebook critic that supplies a complementary feasibility signal. We evaluate VQActFlow on three platforms: the LIBERO simulation benchmarks, a Unitree G1 humanoid performing whole-body pick-and-place, and an ALOHA-style bimanual platform performing contact-rich tasks. Across these benchmarks, VQActFlow outperforms both continuous and discrete baselines.
  </details>

- **[SADP: Subgoal-Aware Diffusion Policy for Long-Horizon Manipulation Learned from Foundation Model Generated Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16871)**  `arXiv:2605.16871`  `cs.RO`  
  _Site Hu, Takato Horii_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon robot manipulation requires policies to coordinate multiple intermediate subgoals and determine when to advance between them. However, most imitation learning methods are trained solely on task-level demonstrations, without explicitly modeling the active subgoal or its execution progress. This limitation is further exacerbated by the scarcity of subgoal-level supervision in standard robot learning datasets, which makes explicit subgoal-conditioned control and online transition modeling difficult to learn. To address this issue, this paper proposes Subgoal-Aware Diffusion Policy (SADP), a framework that leverages foundation models to autonomously generate subgoal-annotated demonstrations and trains diffusion policies on these datasets. SADP structures policy execution around explicit natural-language subgoals by conditioning action generation on both task-level and subgoal-level descriptions. A lightweight auxiliary head further predicts a continuation score that drives online subgoal switching and supports stage-level progress monitoring. Experiments in RLBench simulations and real-world evaluations on a UR5e robot demonstrate that SADP maintains competitive task performance while exposing temporally aligned subgoal-level execution signals for progress monitoring. These results show that explicit subgoal progression can be incorporated into a diffusion policy without degrading task-level performance.
  </details>

- **[MAGS-SLAM: Monocular Multi-Agent Gaussian Splatting SLAM for Geometrically and Photometrically Consistent Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10760)**  `arXiv:2605.10760`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zhihao Cao, Qi Shao, Shuhao Zhai, Jing Zhang, Anh Nguyen, Hesheng Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Collaborative photorealistic 3D reconstruction from multiple agents enables rapid large-scale scene capture for virtual production and cooperative multi-robot exploration. While recent 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) SLAM algorithms can generate high-fidelity real-time mapping, most of the existing multi-agent Gaussian SLAM methods still rely on RGB-D sensors to obtain metric depth and simplify cross-agent alignment, limiting their deployment on low-cost or power-constrained robotic platforms, especially given the wider availability of RGB cameras. To address this challenge, we propose MAGS-SLAM, the first RGB-only multi-agent 3DGS SLAM framework for collaborative scene reconstruction. Each agent independently builds local monocular Gaussian submaps and transmits compact submap summaries rather than raw observations. To facilitate robust collaboration in the presence of monocular scale ambiguity, our framework integrates compact submap communication, geometry- and appearance-aware loop verification, and occupancy-aware Gaussian fusion, enabling coherent global reconstruction without active depth sensors. We further introduce ReplicaMultiagent Plus, a benchmark containing larger robot teams for evaluating collaborative Gaussian SLAM. Extensive experiments on both synthetic and real-world datasets demonstrate that MAGS-SLAM, using RGB images alone, achieves tracking accuracy, rendering quality, and geometric quality that are competitive with or superior to those of state-of-the-art RGB-D collaborative Gaussian SLAM methods.
  </details>

- **[Not All Features Are Created Equal: A Mechanistic Study of Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.19233)**  `arXiv:2603.19233`  `cs.RO`  
  _Bryce Grant, Xijia Zhao, Peng Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A fine-tuned Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policy can easily place the alphabet soup in the basket on command, just as it saw during fine-tuning. However, if the basket is shifted five centimeters to the left, it drops the soup off the table. We developed an interpretability pipeline to investigate what such a policy is computing. Injecting task A's activations into the action expert while the policy looks at task B's scene makes pi0.5 execute task A's motor trajectory in 99.6\% of episodes ($n{=}1{,}968$). The injected program is bound to absolute workspace coordinates and ignores the visible scene, which provides a mechanistic account of the perturbation brittleness that concurrent benchmarks report. The binding holds on a physical UR10e: directed replay redirects the commanded task in 26/26 rollouts in one direction and 18/26 in the other, with no-injection and random-direction controls at the floor, and once the source object is moved 10--15\,cm the arm grasps and releases at the recorded coordinates in 50/50 trials, picking up the wrong block when one sits there and closing on empty air when none does. Applying the same interventions to six VLAs on LIBERO, MetaWorld, SimplerEnv, and ALOHA over 420{,}000+ rollouts yields three further findings: every architecture encodes the instruction, and four of the five weight it below vision whenever the scene alone identifies the goal; under the standard replacement write-back the SAE pooling that preserves behavior is architecture-specific (pi0.5 per-token, X-VLA mean-pool, SmolVLA indifferent), and goal selection and motor generation separate into VLM and expert pathways wherever the two are distinguishable. We release \textbf{Action Atlas} (this https URL), an interactive platform covering all 520+ trained SAEs, 79 identified concepts, and the scored UR10e rollouts. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Discovery-Probability Graph Planning for Embodied Agents in Tasks with Hidden Cooperation Requirements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26359)**  `arXiv:2610.26359`  `cs.MA`  
  _Zihuang Cai, Changjian Chen, Zhuo Tang, Chang Tang, Kenli Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied agents are increasingly capable of perceiving, planning, and acting autonomously in dynamic environments, demonstrating strong performance on complex cooperative tasks. Recent advances typically assume that cooperative tasks are known in advance. In real-world applications, however, hidden-cooperation tasks are more common: the need for cooperation only becomes apparent after an agent observes and interacts with task-related targets. In this paper, we first present HiddenCoop, a benchmark for effectively evaluating agent cooperation in hidden-cooperation tasks. We further propose Discovery-Probability Graph Planning (DPGP), a method for enabling effective agent cooperation in such tasks. At the core of DPGP is a discovery-probability graph that encodes the probability distribution over the number and locations of task-related targets, facilitating effective and efficient information exchange among agents. A search algorithm is then used to derive action plans that minimize expected costs. Experiments show that DPGP achieves cooperative-task success rates of 99.3% on C-WAH and 58.3% on TDW-MAT, surpassing all compared baselines, while also reducing overall embodied execution time and substantially lowering LLM token usage. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Learning Who Talks to Whom: Role-Aware Communication Topology Scheduling for Efficient LLM Multi-Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25851)**  `arXiv:2610.25851`  `cs.MA`  
  _Haibin Li, Wenya Yu, Xuefeng Jiang, Jia Li, Xiao Han, Shuai Su, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent systems driven by large language models (LLMs) rely on structured communication topologies to facilitate information exchange and collaborative reasoning. However, effectively scheduling these topologies to dynamically determine who talks to whom and when to communicate remains a fundamental challenge in balancing collective reasoning performance against exponential token overhead. Existing topology scheduling approaches either enforce static communication graphs that cannot adapt to dynamic problem-solving states, or overlook agent role complementarity while failing to explicitly incorporate the synthesized graph structure into team-level decision evaluation. To tackle these limitations, we propose ACTS, a role-aware adaptive communication topology scheduling framework for efficient LLM multi-agent collaboration. Orchestrated within a Centralized Training with Decentralized Execution (CTDE) paradigm, ACTS introduces a novel dual-attention mechanism. Specifically, a Role-conditioned Relational Attention module dynamically selects variable-cardinality interaction partners based on agent roles and evolving execution states, while a Topological Attention Mixer explicitly embeds the directed communication topology into team value decomposition for precise credit assignment. Comprehensive evaluations across 9 challenging benchmarks covering code generation, multi-step reasoning, general knowledge, and competitive mathematics demonstrate that ACTS consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, boosting average accuracy by up to 6.19 percentage points while reducing token consumption by over 15%. The project website is available atthis https URL.
  </details>
