- **[ROAR: Benchmarking LMM Agents under Real-Time Constraints in Multiplayer Action-Shooter Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24282)**  `arXiv:2610.24282`  `cs.MA`  
  _Dawei Wang, Di Zhao, Jianran Yu, Chengming Zhou, Marci Chi Ma, Xiaoyang Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large multimodal models (LMMs) are increasingly deployed as agents that perceive, reason, and act in interactive multi-agent environments. Yet most existing benchmarks for LMM-based agents in multi-agent settings remain turn-based: the environment and other agents pause while an agent performs inference. Such protocols obscure a fundamental challenge of real-time constraints, where the world continues to evolve and delayed decisions may become outdated before execution. Motivated by this limitation, we introduce ROAR, a multiplayer action-shooter game environment for evaluating LMM-based agents in multi-agent settings under real-time constraints. ROAR covers cooperative, competitive, mixed cooperative-competitive, and basic single-agent scenarios. We further propose a Multi-Agent Asynchronous Gym interface that decouples environment evolution from agent reasoning by running the environment and each agent in separate threads, enabling the environment to advance at fixed time intervals without waiting for LMM inference and allowing agents to reason independently without blocking one another. ROAR also features convenient deployment, multimodal observations, and customizable levels. ROAR offers a practical testbed for advancing LMM-based agents beyond static, turn-based evaluation toward real-time multi-agent decision-making.
  </details>

- **[Federation over Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24007)**  `arXiv:2610.24007`  `cs.MA`  
  _Dixi Yao, Tahseen Rabbani, Tian Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern LLM-powered agents increasingly improve through experience sharing, where distributed agents distill local reasoning experience into reusable knowledge, such as a shared insight library. Existing approaches, however, still heavily rely on manually designed prompts and workflows that determine both how local agents extract useful reasoning traces and how a central/global agent aggregates them to obtain common patterns and insights. We propose Federation over Harness (FoH), a framework that automatically evolves the harness for both local and global components in this distributed multi-agent reasoning pipeline. A server agent evolves the client harness for task solving and self-reflection while, at the same time, evolving its own harness for aggregating distributed reflections, maintaining shared knowledge, and updating subsequent client harnesses. Importantly, FoH requires no extra validation signals, such as ground-truth labels or validation data, during evolution; it learns solely from self-generated reflections produced by distributed agents. Experiments on multiple benchmarks show that FoH consistently improves over single-agent baselines with a fixed harness and with harness self-evolution by 53.57% and 29.76%.
  </details>

- **[Open-Ended Video Game Glitch Detection with Agentic Reasoning and Temporal Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.07818)**  `arXiv:2604.07818`  `cs.MA`  
  _Muyang Zheng, Tong Zhou, Geyang Wu, Zihao Lin, Haibo Wang, Lifu Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open-ended video game glitch detection aims to identify glitches in gameplay videos, describe them in natural language, and localize when they occur. Unlike conventional game glitch understanding tasks which have largely been framed as image-level recognition or closed-form question answering, this task requires reasoning about game-specific dynamics such as mechanics, physics, rendering, animation, and expected state transitions directly over continuous gameplay videos and distinguishing true glitches from unusual but valid in-game events. To support this task, we introduce VideoGlitchBench, the first benchmark for open-ended video game glitch detection with temporal localization. VideoGlitchBench contains 5,238 gameplay videos from 120 games, each annotated with detailed glitch descriptions and precise temporal spans, enabling unified evaluation of semantic understanding and temporal grounding. We further propose GliDe, an agentic framework with three key components: a game-aware contextual memory for informed reasoning, a debate-based reflector for multi-perspective glitch detection and verification, and an event-level grounding module that recovers complete glitch intervals from fragmented temporal evidence. We also design a task-specific evaluation protocol that jointly measures semantic fidelity and temporal accuracy. Experiments show that this task remains highly challenging for current multimodal models, while GliDe achieves substantially stronger performance than corresponding vanilla model baselines.
  </details>

- **[Latent World Models Should Plan with Distances Along the Latent Manifold](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25013)**  `arXiv:2610.25013`  `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Zunzhi You, Linwei Tao, Siyu Xu, Yunke Wang, Chang Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Latent world models such as LeWM plan by searching for an action sequence whose predicted terminal latent is closest to the goal latent under an ambient norm, such as the Euclidean distance. Planning with such a cost assumes that proximity in the ambient latent space reflects proximity to the goal in the environment. Studying LeWM, we find that this assumption holds only in a small neighborhood around the goal, whereas farther away the L2 cost forms spurious low-cost basins that trap the planner. Yet neighborhood-based embeddings show that the latents retain the underlying physical state. We therefore hypothesize that the latents lie near a curved, low-dimensional manifold, and that the terminal cost should measure the distance along this latent manifold rather than through the ambient space. To estimate this manifold distance, we propose the Neighborhood Graph Cost (NGC), which computes shortest-path distances on a mutual nearest-neighbor graph constructed over training latents. NGC changes only the terminal cost and requires no retraining. This single change improves the planning success rate of LeWM across all four benchmark tasks and nearly solves TwoRoom and Reacher: even at the farthest goal offsets tested, NGC reaches 95% and 100% success, whereas the L2 cost falls to 27% and 81%. These results support the manifold hypothesis and our argument that latent world models should plan with distances along the latent manifold.
  </details>

- **[Fast Construction of Inner Sphere Trees Using Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23940)**  `arXiv:2610.23940`  `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Jianneng Huang, Wenjia Rao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Collision detection for complex non-convex objects is a bottleneck in physics simulation, virtual reality, and robotics. Inner sphere trees (ISTs) answer proximity and penetration queries efficiently, but their greedy leaf-sphere construction is sequential and computationally expensive, which limits both large-scale preprocessing and runtime use. In this work, we present ML-IST, a machine-learning-based method that reformulates leaf-sphere generation as a single-shot set-prediction problem: a network predicts a fixed set of inscribed sphere candidates from multichannel voxel geometry, differentiable coverage feedback corrects the coarse prediction and fills residual gaps at finer scales, and a lightweight geometric projection enforces validity. Averaged over the test set, a single-GPU implementation of ML-IST reduces mean leaf-generation time by a factor of $2.0$--$2.6$ over single-core CPU construction across target capacities of 64--512 spheres, and qualitative results on complex models show multiscale sphere distributions that follow principal volumes and slender features. We further integrate ML-IST into the Jolt Physics and Bullet engines; in an alpha-puzzle benchmark it reaches 55.89~FPS, the highest among the compared representations (conventional IST, GImpact, and convex decomposition). These results support learned leaf generation as a practical component for fast, runtime construction of detailed collision representations.
  </details>

- **[Alliance of Stochastic Routing and Reinforcement Fusion for Multi-Model Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24054)**  `arXiv:2610.24054`  `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Selim Furkan Tekin, Ling Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper studies dynamic model routing under uncertainty in multimodal settings, where a system must select and potentially compose multiple models to answer a query. Standard reinforcement learning approaches for routing often suffer from premature convergence to suboptimal models due to noisy rewards and insufficient exploration. We propose a unified vision-language framework (VL-Scout) for efficient multimodal and multi-step reasoning with turn-based reinforcement fusion. First, VL-Scout combines Bayesian priors, policy gradient optimization, and sequential decision-making to enable structured exploration and adaptive inference. A context-dependent Dirichlet belief model allows for epistemic uncertainty over model quality and is updated online based on observed rewards and context-specific evidence, enabling Thompson-style exploration while serving as a prior for regularization. Second, a routing policy is trained via a KL-regularized REINFORCE objective by maximizing a reward-weighted evidence lower bound, where the policy acts as an amortized posterior, and the Dirichlet belief serves as a prior. Third, we introduce a fusion policy trained with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) or Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR), which operates over intermediate model rationales and selects between two actions: CONTINUE or ANSWER, terminating with a generated output. We evaluate VL-Scout on four representative multi-modal benchmarks (MMMU, MMMU-Pro, OKVQA, and RealWorldQA), compared against 11 representative routing and policy optimization baselines. Our method provides consistent performance improvements while reducing premature policy collapse. These results highlight the importance of integrating uncertainty-aware priors with both amortized policy learning and sequential fusion for robust and efficient model selection in discrete action spaces
  </details>

- **[CAVA: Complementary-Label-Aware Data Valuation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26734)**  `arXiv:2610.26734`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Tan-Ha Mai, Hsuan-Tien Lin, Vu Nguyen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Complementary-label learning (CLL) trains a classifier from labels that name a class an instance does not belong to. Such labels are cheap to collect from crowdworkers or vision-language models (VLMs), making them attractive for large-scale annotation. However, both sources can mistakenly assign the true class as the complementary label. Such annotation errors are particularly harmful in CLL because the model is explicitly trained to suppress the correct answer. Optimal transport (OT) data valuation identifies harmful samples by comparing training data with a clean validation set, but transferring it directly to CLL creates a semantic mismatch: label $c$ means not class $c$ in the training but class $c$ in the validation. We show that this makes the OT label groups noisy mixtures whose behavior depends on the noise rate. The transferred score is informative only at sufficiently high noise, becomes uninformative at the crossover rate $1/M$ for $M$ classes, and reverses below it, where practical CLL annotation typically operates. We propose CAVA, which introduces a complementary label cost and a label margin that isolates the contribution of each complementary label to the transport potential. The margin keeps a consistent direction across noise rates, detects diagonal noise with a parameter-free threshold of closed-form precision, and allows detected samples to be recycled as ordinary-label supervision. On synthetic and human-annotated benchmarks, CAVA separates diagonal noise almost perfectly where directly transferred OT valuation performs worse than random. With only a fraction of the clean validation data, it also improves every CLL objective we evaluate, showing that OT-based valuation becomes reliable once the semantics of complementary supervision are modeled explicitly.
  </details>

- **[SAGE-CP: When Marginal Coverage Hides Route-Conditional Failures in Adaptive VLM Ensembles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23864)**  `arXiv:2610.23864`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Mushir Akhtar, M. Tanveer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Adaptive VLM ensembles save computation by stopping early on easy images and escalating harder ones. Although a single conformal threshold can make the final predictor marginally valid, it can still fail severely along individual computational routes. We characterize this failure precisely: if a route has prevalence pi, marginal 1-alpha coverage allows its conditional coverage to fall to max{0, 1-alpha/pi}. To address this mismatch, we learn the router on development data, freeze all routing choices, and calibrate the returned score separately for early exits and escalated inputs. We call this pipeline SAGE-CP. Its calibration step applies Mondrian split conformal prediction to the operational groups created by the router. Under exchangeability, the resulting prediction sets achieve finite-sample coverage within each route. Across 10 benchmarks, 4 frozen VLMs, and 5 aligned splits, both global calibration and SAGE-CP achieve 0.900 marginal coverage at a 0.90 target, but their route behavior differs substantially. SAGE-CP raises mean worst-route coverage from 0.623 to 0.887 and reduces the mean route-coverage gap from 0.324 to 0.025, using the same routed scores and 1.177 source calls per image. The stronger guarantee increases the mean prediction-set size from 1.893 to 2.212; nevertheless, the sets remain smaller than those of the full ensemble (2.486) while requiring 70.6 percent fewer source calls. The route-level reliability persists across 6 fixed query fractions, while finer exit-depth groups demonstrate the need for additional calibration data on rare routes. Across 5 datasets, SAGE-CP also reduces measured batch-one latency by 58.8 to 63.4 percent and gross GPU energy by 55.7 to 57.5 percent relative to full inference. Thus, routing provides computational savings, while route-stratified calibration attaches coverage guarantees to the operational paths created by the router.
  </details>

- **[Radial-Angular Geometry for Reliable Update Diagnosis in Noisy-Label Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17429)**  `arXiv:2605.17429`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Ningkang Peng, Jingyang Mao, Xiaoqian Peng, Weiguang Qu, Yanhui Gu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Noisy-label methods often estimate sample reliability from forward-space signals such as loss, confidence, or entropy. These signals indicate whether a sample is difficult to predict, but they do not directly test whether its observed label induces a reliable parameter update. This gap matters because hard clean samples and mislabeled samples can have similar loss while inducing different updates. We recast reliability estimation as diagnosis of the observed-label update. The sample-wise empirical Fisher trace gives a backward-space measure of update energy: for the classifier layer, it factorizes into a prediction-residual term and a feature-sensitivity term, so it captures information beyond scalar loss. Trace, however, is still a radial magnitude signal and cannot decide whether a large update is useful or harmful. We therefore propose Relative Geometric Conflict (RGC), which compares the observed-label gradient with a reference gradient induced by an EMA teacher. The conflict term helps distinguish large but aligned hard-clean updates from large conflicting updates caused by corrupted labels. Across synthetic and real-world noisy-label benchmarks, RGC improves hard-clean preservation and accuracy under our evaluation protocol.
  </details>

- **[Robust and Efficient Finetuning of Vision Foundation Models via Implicit Ensembling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.06982)**  `arXiv:2510.06982`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Masih Aminbeidokhti, Heitor Rapela Medeiros, Eric Granger, Marco Pedersoli_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robust finetuning of foundation models seeks to improve out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization during in-distribution (ID) adaptation. We revisit Mixout, a stochastic regularizer that intermittently replaces finetuned parameters with a reference anchor, and study its effectiveness for robust finetuning of vision foundation models. We reinterpret Mixout as a single-run, weight-sharing implicit ensemble and analyze its expected OOD error through a bias-variance-covariance-locality (BVCL) lens. This perspective reveals three key factors that govern the ID-OOD trade-off: the choice of masking anchor, the mask resampling frequency, and mask sparsity. Guided by this analysis, we propose GMixout, which generalizes Mixout in three ways. First, it explicitly controls the masking period through a resampling-frequency hyperparameter. Second, it replaces the fixed pretrained anchor with an exponential moving average snapshot that adapts throughout training. Third, it uses sparse kernels to update only a small subset of parameters at each iteration, introducing no inference-time overhead and enabling large-model finetuning on consumer-grade GPUs. Experiments across five vision benchmarks show that GMixout consistently outperforms Mixout, Model Soups, and strong parameter-efficient finetuning baselines under both covariate shift and class imbalance.
  </details>

- **[HarnessPro: Recursive Harness Self-Improvement with Pareto Optimization for Performance-Cost Trade-offs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26770)**  `arXiv:2610.26770`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Zhuoshi Pan, ZhiYuan Feng, Yukuo Cen, Honglin Lin, Junru Lu, Zhongxia Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Harness development plays a crucial role in building capable AI agents. Recent approaches improve harnesses through recursive self-improvement (RSI), but their performance gains often come with higher execution costs. We introduce HarnessPro, a label-free method for evolving harnesses to improve task performance while reducing execution cost. In this setting, candidate harnesses are evaluated using LLM-generated tests without access to ground-truth solutions or official tests. Specialized agents diagnose execution trajectories, plan updates with different performance-cost priorities, and implement them as candidate harnesses. Rather than returning a single harness, HarnessPro uses normalized weighted squared-distance selection to provide cost-focused (lite), balanced (medium), and performance-focused (heavy) harnesses for different deployment budgets. Selected harnesses enter the next iteration, where execution feedback guides further updates. Our experiments cover Terminal-Bench 2, SWE-Bench Pro, and ProgramBench, using GPT-5.5 with Codex and GLM-5.2 with Claude Code. Across these benchmarks using GPT-5.5 with Codex, the heavy mode of HarnessPro achieves an average pass-rate gain of 2.0 percentage points and an average per-task cost reduction of 26.1% relative to RHO. This advantage also holds when using GLM-5.2 with Claude Code. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Parallel Language Models via Variational Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26693)**  `arXiv:2610.26693`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Felix Draxler, Robin C. Geyer, Daniel Geyfman, Farrin Marouf Sofian, Justus Will, Philipp Hildebrandt, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive language models emit one token per forward pass. This causes high inference latency, in particular as models are scaled up for quality. In this paper, we introduce Parallel Language Models (PLMs), which generate mutually consistent tokens in one forward pass by routing dependencies through latent auxiliary variables. PLMs are trained using a variational bound that reduces to an autoregressive loss plus a parallel decoding loss. Empirically, PLMs are on a faster and better Pareto frontier than autoregressive and discrete diffusion baselines. Pretrained PLMs achieve near-autoregressive pretraining loss on model scales of 80M to 3B parameters, and speedup follows a power law in model size. When post-trained, our 1.3B model matches or exceeds an identically trained autoregressive model on math, coding, and instruction-following benchmarks, speeding up inference $2.3\text{-}5.7\times$. Together, we show that parallel generation can be trained from a single training objective at autoregressive quality. We will make our code available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Curriculum Learning Teaches LLMs Long-Horizon Implicit Reasoning, Up To A Limit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25612)**  `arXiv:2610.25612`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Jimson Huang, Maxwell Nickell, Ruowang Zhang, Abulhair Saparov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLMs "reason" by externalizing intermediate steps as tokens through chain-of-thought (CoT), yet even frontier reasoning models hit abrupt performance cliffs on long-horizon tasks. Implicit reasoning (reasoning within a single forward pass) is useful during CoT generation, as the model can "search ahead" to decide the correct next move without explicit search. Models with weak implicit reasoning are more likely to commit to wrong paths and must attempt to recover via backtracking, while models with strong implicit reasoning identify the correct proof strategy directly, shortening the CoT. However, prior work shows LLMs struggle to reason implicitly beyond shallow depths, with no relief from scale. We investigate whether curriculum learning can extend this depth by fine-tuning on examples with progressively increasing vocabulary size and lookahead (the number of steps a model must search ahead to identify the correct next move). At matched compute, curriculum-trained models achieve lookaheads where no-curriculum training fails. Qwen3-0.6B reaches 98% accuracy on problems with lookahead up to 256 with curriculum, compared to 45% without. However, progress slows as fine-tuning continues, following a saturating exponential that suggests a maximum intrinsic lookahead to which models can learn to reason implicitly. Larger models reach higher fitted ceilings, fine-tuning pretrained models far outpaces training from scratch, and curriculum step sizes that are too large stall progression. Finally, the capability acquired on synthetic data transfers to natural language reasoning, with curriculum-trained models outperforming models trained without curriculum at matched compute on 11 natural language reasoning benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Text Dominance in Paralinguistic Conflict: Tracing Suppression Through Audio LLM Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25599)**  `arXiv:2610.25599`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Ankur Banerjee, Vishnu Guntakala, Danity Pike, Advay Roongta, Kevin Yang, Ruizhe Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Audio LLMs deployed in settings such as crisis hotlines and customer support are only useful beyond speech-to-text pipelines if they use acoustic cues rather than the transcript alone. We test whether they prioritise transcript content over paralinguistic cues (emotion, speaker identity, sarcasm) and trace the mechanism when they do. Across six audio LLMs under LISTEN-style conflict conditions, the mean text-dominance rate on mismatched text+audio items is 0.78 (0.86 excluding sarcasm). In the most text-dominant screened model, probing shows speaker identity remains linearly decodable at ceiling at the language model's input, so the failure is not perceptual. Back-patching, reported to repair lexical text dominance, has no measurable effect here; but it also fails on its own source task in our replication (n=300), so we treat its null as uninformative rather than as evidence of non-transfer. Attention knockout partially restores audio-grounded output by blocking the claim from propagating through intermediate tokens during open-ended generation, whereas blocking the answer position's direct connection to the conflicting claim has no effect. The claim's influence therefore travels through an intermediate relay rather than a single severable edge. The effect appears only in free-form generation and not in closed-set multiple choice, suggesting that benchmarks relying on that format may be systematically blind to interventions of this kind. Code and data are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CoEvo-RSI: When Harnesses Evolve, Which Models Should Follow?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25078)**  `arXiv:2610.25078`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Jiaqi Su, Guibin Zhang, Jie-Jing Shao, Cong Pang, Tiankuo Yao, Ivor Tsang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Model--harness co-evolution has emerged as a prominent paradigm for recursive self-improving (RSI) agents, jointly evolving their execution scaffolds and intrinsic capabilities. We study the persistent yet often overlooked brain--body mismatch problem in this paradigm, where an evolving harness (body) demands behaviors its model (brain) has not learned or restricts capabilities it already possesses. We introduce CoEvo-RSI, a RSI framework that advances model--harness co-evolution through agent-driven, bidirectional historical inheritance. Drawing on a shared evolutionary archive and execution feedback, a meta-agent coordinates harness development from historical designs with model checkpoint selection or merging and subsequent adaptation under the selected harness, aligning inherited capabilities with evolving execution requirements to reduce brain--body mismatch. Using only supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on its own rejection-sampled trajectories, CoEvo-RSI makes Qwen3.5-9B competitive with larger models across diverse vertical applications, including multi-agent failure attribution, role-aware text-to-SQL, and smart-home instruction following. It surpasses DeepSeek-V4-Pro on TraceElephant (+14.16 agent accuracy) and GLM-5.3 on HomeBench VM (+4.90 success rate), underscoring the need for brain--body alignment and the potential of bidirectional historical inheritance to advance agent co-evolution.
  </details>

- **[Beyond the Teacher: Understanding and Preserving Distillation Overshoot in Agentic On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25034)**  `arXiv:2610.25034`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Jike Zhong, Ming Li, Yuxiang Lai, Xi Xiao, Sheng Zhang, Ritwick Chaudhry, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) trains agents on their own trajectories using supervision from a stronger teacher. We uncover a counterintuitive phenomenon in multi-turn agentic tasks: a smaller, initially weaker student can substantially outperform its teacher during training, only to regress as distillation continues. We term this phenomenon distillation overshoot. Through decision- and task-level analyses, we trace overshoot to two competing effects of OPD: repairing weak student decisions and eroding successful student choices that the teacher disfavors. Early in training, repair outpaces erosion, allowing the student to combine the teacher's corrections with its own strengths and surpass the teacher. As training continues, erosion overtakes repair, causing these gains to vanish. Motivated by this finding, we propose Success-Preserving On-Policy Distillation (SPOD), which selectively masks conflicting teacher supervision on successful student choices and introduces a soft probability floor to counteract their erosion, while retaining teacher guidance elsewhere. SPOD requires only binary episode success and model probabilities already available during OPD. Across two model families and three agentic benchmarks, SPOD consistently outperforms ordinary OPD and evaluated variants, improving final success by up to 15.2 percentage points and surpassing the teacher by up to 20.0 points. Moreover, SPOD's final checkpoints outperform even ordinary OPD's best intermediate checkpoints in all six settings, demonstrating that beyond-teacher gains can be not only preserved but further extended. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[ProbePO: Sparse Probes, Redistributed Credit for Critic-Free LLM Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24515)**  `arXiv:2610.24515`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Diyi Hu, Ruizhong Qiu, Lin Qiu, Hanghang Tong, Hanqing Zeng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards assigns one terminal reward to an entire reasoning trajectory, obscuring how individual reasoning steps contributed to the outcome. GRPO broadcasts a group-normalized advantage to every response token, while finer-grained approaches often rely on dense auxiliary rollouts or branching schemes that can also alter the data used for policy updates. We cast the missing middle as budgeted measurement: with a limited rollout budget, which partial solutions should be evaluated, and how should their value estimates refine credit? ProbePO selects partial-solution states with a reward-free uncertainty score and estimates their expected verifier rewards under a capped continuation budget. ProbePO redistributes, rather than replaces, GRPO's trajectory-level advantage across tokens while preserving each trajectory's mean token-level advantage; probe continuations serve only as measurements. Across three DeepSeekMath and Qwen-family models spanning 1.5B-7B parameters, ProbePO consistently outperforms GRPO and achieves the highest accuracy among the four evaluated methods in 16 of 18 model-benchmark comparisons. Against dense-probing VinePPO on DeepSeekMath-7B, ProbePO achieves higher accuracy on all six benchmarks with 76% less end-to-end training time.
  </details>

- **[ProtSent: Protein Sentence Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06830)**  `arXiv:2605.06830`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Dan Ofer, Oriel Perets, Michal Linial, Nadav Rappoport_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Protein language models produce rich per-residue representations, but the sequence embeddings obtained by mean-pooling them are never trained to place related proteins near one another, so the geometry that retrieval, clustering and nearest-neighbor transfer depend on is never optimized. We present ProtSent, a contrastive post-training framework that supervises a protein language model with three types of biological relation at once, Pfam family co-membership, AlphaFold DB structural clusters and STRING interactions, and with deep mutational scanning fitness ordering as an optional fourth. It needs no structure at training or inference, trains on corpora filtered at 40% sequence identity against the benchmark test sets they most resemble, and is applied to ESM-2 (35M and 150M parameters) and ESM-C (300M). Post-training makes structural family membership recoverable from the frozen embedding without a trained readout: on SCOPe-40, the adjusted Rand index of the 35M model rises from 0.054 (near chance) to 0.507, and family retrieval gains +0.185, +0.190 and +0.424 Recall@1 over the three frozen backbones, with paired bootstrap intervals excluding zero. On the CATH midnight-zone benchmark, homologous-superfamily accuracy rises from 40.7% to 56.7% at 35M and from 43.3% to 62.7% at 150M, more than the 12 points a CATH-supervised model gains over its own base. Against a maximally sensitive HMMER search, alignment keeps the best single hit at both ESM-2 scales, but the embedding leads at ranking depth (MAP 0.705 against 0.607 at 150M) and returns a ranking for every query, including those where alignment finds none. Under a trained linear probe, however, the same embeddings fall below the untuned backbone, as do structure-distilled embeddings against theirs, so the gain in neighborhood structure comes at a cost in linear-probe accuracy. Code and models:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Bayesian Preference Learning for Test-Time Steerable Reward Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08819)**  `arXiv:2602.08819`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Jiwoo Hong, Shao Tang, Zhipeng Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reward models are central to aligning language models with human preferences via reinforcement learning (RL). As RL is increasingly applied to settings such as verifiable rewards and multi-objective alignment, RMs are expected to encode more complex and multifaceted preference distributions. However, classifier RMs remain static once trained, limiting their adaptability at test time. We propose Variational In-Context Reward Modeling (ICRM), a novel Bayesian reward modeling objective that enables test-time steerability via in-context preference demonstrations. ICRM casts reward modeling as amortized variational inference over a latent preference probability under the Bradley-Terry model using a conjugate Beta prior. We show that ICRM adapts to unseen preference distributions at test time for both single and multi-objective settings. With more demonstrations, ICRM improves RM-Bench accuracy from 60.5 to 70.8, achieves lower calibration error than a generative judge on moral dilemma preferences, and expands the attainable Pareto frontier under conflicting preferences. We further study the practical applicability of ICRM for RL training, showing that it can effectively encode verifiable rewards by outperforming a conventional RM in math reasoning. Finally, we provide theoretical guarantees that the variational objective admits a global interior optimum with finite confidence, followed by an ablation study on its role for the test-time steerability, and we analyze how KL regularization mitigates reward over-optimization.
  </details>

- **[KernelClaw: A Multi-Agent Framework for GPU Kernel Optimization with Dual-Memory Mechanisms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.10085)**  `arXiv:2603.10085`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Qitong Sun, Jun Han, Tianlin Li, Zhe Tang, Sheng Chen, Fei Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Improving GPU kernel efficiency is crucial for efficient AI systems. Recent work has explored leveraging large language models (LLMs) for GPU kernel generation and optimization. However, existing LLM-based kernel optimization pipelines typically rely on opaque, implicitly learned heuristics within the LLMs to determine optimization strategies. This leads to inefficient trial-and-error and weakly interpretable optimizations. Our key insight is to replace implicit heuristics with expert optimization knowledge that is aware of task trajectories. Specifically, we present KernelClaw, a multi-agent framework with a dual-level memory architecture. KernelClaw operates by coordinating agents with long-term memory of reusable expert knowledge and short-term memory to prevent repetitive backtracking. On KernelBench Levels 1-3, KernelClaw achieves a 100% success rate and average speedups of 6.98x, 7.89x, and 1.94x over Torch Eager on Levels 1, 2, and 3, respectively, outperforming prior baselines. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[WeAct: Dynamic Weight Switching as Agent Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26847)**  `arXiv:2610.26847`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Rui Shi, Xunjian Yin, Jiacheng Sang, Zichuan Liu, Shuyan Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon agents must learn heterogeneous capabilities from interleaved trajectories. Updating shared parameters to learn new capabilities can degrade previously acquired ones, resulting in catastrophic forgetting. As a trajectory unfolds, different stages may require different capabilities, yet conventional joint training and static adapter merging maintain a fixed parameter configuration throughout execution. We introduce WeAct (Weight-level Internal Actions), which trains independent capability-specific LoRA adapters over a frozen backbone and adds adapter selection to the agent's action space. These internal actions use the model's native tool-calling interface for on-demand capability composition in a shared context. Across eight deep-research benchmarks, Qwen3-32B with WeAct improves average accuracy by 6.2% relative to full fine-tuning counterparts despite using less compute, and by 27.5% relative to the strongest static LoRA-merging method. WeAct also supports modular continual adaptation, by adding a new code computation adapter to expand the agent's capabilities and updating the existing writing adapter to improve report quality. Both updates leave the backbone and other adapters unchanged, supporting modular continual adaptation in agents.
  </details>

- **[Enhanced Masked Diffusion Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25447)**  `arXiv:2610.25447`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Tomer Garber, Tom Tirer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Masked diffusion language models (MDLMs) generate text by iteratively unmasking tokens with bidirectional context. As these models reach real-world deployment, machine unlearning becomes essential. This process aims to remove the influence of a designated "forget set" without retraining from scratch, so that sensitive, copyrighted, or restricted content can no longer be elicited while preserving retained capabilities. Masked Diffusion Unlearning (MDU) is the first publicly available unlearning method customized for these models: it tunes the model $p_\theta$ to match a frozen prompt-masked anchor $p_{\mathrm{anchor}}$ on the forget set by minimizing the anchor-last divergence $\mathrm{KL}(p_\theta \Vert p_{\mathrm{anchor}})$. We identify two failure modes of MDU. First, random answer masking can leave key forget tokens visible to the anchor even when the question is fully masked. Second, at a memorized (near-one-hot) prediction, the anchor-last logit gradient vanishes while the anchor-first divergence $\mathrm{KL}(p_{\mathrm{anchor}} \Vert p_\theta)$ logit gradient does not. As a result, MDU training stalls on the tokens we most need to forget. Backed by theoretical and empirical analyses, we propose Enhanced Masked Diffusion Unlearning (EMDU): anchor-first KL divergence toward a frozen anchor whose entire answer span is masked. On TOFU and RWKU benchmarks using LLaDA-8B and Dream-7B, our EMDU matches or exceeds the forgetting of MDU and adapted AR baselines, while better preserving retain utility.
  </details>

- **[CasePlay: Self-Play Reinforcement Learning from Case Reports for Medical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24952)**  `arXiv:2610.24952`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Hao Wang, Zihan Wang, Kai Wu, Jian Yao, Yiwen Ye, Yongcan Luo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) is a promising way to improve the clinical reasoning abilities of large language models (LLMs), but current pipelines often rely on expert-labeled questions or costly synthetic-data generation. Meanwhile, the medical literature offers a wealth of high-quality unlabeled documents, including clinical case reports that trace the full clinical course from initial presentation to final treatment decisions. We study whether document-grounded self-play can convert these reports into effective RL training signals. We identify two challenges that limit existing self-play methods in this setting: knowledge-point fixation}, where the proposer repeatedly asks about a narrow set of salient clues in a source report, and difficulty-quality mismatch, where appropriately difficult questions may still be poorly formed or shallow, limiting their value for reasoner training. To address these challenges, we propose CasePlay, a self-play framework with two key components: (a) a Knowledge-Conditioned Proposer, which anchors question generation to case-specific knowledge points so that questions draw on a broader range of evidence from the source report; and (b) a Rubric-Judge Reasoner, which augments answer-accuracy feedback with rubric-based quality feedback on clue integration, reasoning depth, and option quality, guiding the proposer toward reasoning-intensive questions that better support reasoner training. Extensive experiments across seven medically related benchmarks demonstrate that CasePlay outperforms existing self-play baselines and improves the SFT warm-start model by 3.1 points on average, highlighted by a 6.3-point gain on LiveClin. This approach offers an effective path toward continually improving medical reasoning ability through self-play RL.
  </details>

- **[Arctic-SWE: Revisiting the Long Horizon Agent Training by Modeling-System Co-Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24914)**  `arXiv:2610.24914`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Boyi Liu, Yite Wang, Xinyu Lian, Fan Shu, Sida Li, Feng Yan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon agent training, including software engineering, is advancing rapidly, but the recipe and infrastructure needed for a strong reinforcement-learning baseline remain difficult to reproduce. This makes gains from new algorithms or data hard to distinguish from improvements to an under-tuned baseline. We present Arctic-SWE, a fully asynchronous RL framework that establishes such a baseline through model-system co-design, using overfitting on a small task pool as the testbed. This testbed exposes failures that early reward gains conceal and guides the integration of established components: gated reward, importance-weight control, aligned training and inference engines, and consistent state across policy updates. No single component is new, yet together they form the missing foundation. On raw R2E-Gym data, without new algorithms or data work, Arctic-SWE trains Qwen3.5-4B to 62.0% pass@1 on SWE-bench Verified. This is comparable to concurrent FrogNano (61.5%), which relies on online task synthesis, after about one-sixth as many training rollouts. Building on this foundation, we further filter and refine R2E-Gym, SWE-rebench V2, and Scale-SWE. With this curated subset, the 4B model improves from 47.4%/25.0%/37.9% to 65.0%/38.3%/59.7% on SWE-bench Verified/Pro-v1/Pro-v2, and the recipe also improves Qwen3.5-9B from 56.2%/31.9%/48.8% to 65.7%/39.6%/70.2% on Verified/Pro-v1/Pro-v2. Controlled comparisons show that both curation and test strengthening improve held-out performance, although stronger tests reduce apparent training-task mastery. Arctic-SWE provides a documented starting point for studying better algorithms, data and environments for asynchronous agent RL.
  </details>

- **[Toollery: Scaling LLM Agents to Thousands of Skills and Tools](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22218)**  `arXiv:2609.22218`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Xiangxi Tian, Ran Guan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As LLM agents are exposed to hundreds to tens of thousands of skills, tools, and API functions, full-library prompting becomes costly, slow, and less reliable: each added candidate increases prompt tokens and latency, while longer candidate lists introduce more distractors for LLM selection. We present Toollery, a training-free candidate-compression framework for scalable LLM skill/tool selection. Following established document-side query expansion, Toollery generates user-intent queries from each skill/tool specification and builds a retrieval index that maps real user requests to compact candidate sets before final LLM decision-making. By treating high-level skills and atomic tools as selectable capabilities, Toollery can be applied to both skill libraries and tool registries. We evaluate Toollery on the roughly 79K-capability SkillRouter benchmark, BFCL-V4 with over 440 atomic tools, and 3,396 proprietary smart-cockpit requests over 220 tools. Across these settings, Toollery keeps online selection bounded to a compact top-K candidate set and improves recall over ordinary specification retrieval. At a fixed top-10 budget, Toollery improves end-to-end selection on the cockpit dataset, and maintains comparable AST Accuracy on BFCL-V4. These results support Toollery as a practical candidate-compression framework for large and evolving agent capability libraries, while showing that quality and cost gains depend on workload coverage and provider caching.
  </details>

- **[Selection, Recombination, or a Fresh Solve? A Candidate-Free Control for Single-Pass Test-Time Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18379)**  `arXiv:2608.18379`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Guiv Farmanfarmaian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An aggregation pass gives a language model two things: candidate solutions and another chance to answer. We ask whether the candidates improve that attempt, and whether aggregation improves on selecting an existing answer. Our candidate-free control asks the same model to solve the problem again from the problem alone, using the same decoding settings and maximum output-token allowance. On AIME-2025 and HMMT-2025, Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 reaches 15.5% accuracy when all four candidates are wrong, compared with 27.7% for a fresh solve. When at least two candidates are correct, the ordering reverses: 88.7% for aggregation versus 59.7% for a fresh solve. With exactly one correct candidate, the benefit remains unresolved under the specified test. Yet plurality voting reaches 96.0% in the multiple-correct regime without another model generation; its accuracy difference from aggregation is inconclusive (raw $p = 0.180$). The all-wrong accuracy loss and multiple-correct gain survive a statistical correction for adding the second benchmark, developed after data collection, and persist when fresh responses are scored by their final answers. These findings concern one 4B model, two mathematics benchmarks, and a single aggregation pass over candidates truncated at their first answer. All-wrong recovery can arise from solving the problem afresh. The fresh-solve control tests whether candidates improve accuracy. Comparing with selection asks whether the additional generation improves on an existing answer.
  </details>

- **[Rubric Dropout: A Simple Way to Mitigate Reward Hacking in Rubric-Based RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11669)**  `arXiv:2608.11669`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Minglai Yang, Xinyu Guo, Zhengliang Shi, Utkarsh Tyagi, Mian Zhang, Razvan Dumitru, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rubric-based reinforcement learning, in which an LLM judge scores responses against prompt-specific criteria, has become a standard way to post-train language models on tasks without verifiable answers. The rubric, however, is only a proxy for response quality, and a policy trained against it long enough learns to exploit the difference. We train models with GRPO on medical and science rubrics and grade their responses on out-of-distribution (OOD) benchmarks throughout training with two judges: the training judge and a stronger OOD judge. The two scores first rise together and then diverge. The policy is learning to satisfy the training judge without writing better responses, which is a form of reward hacking. To mitigate it, we propose Rubric Dropout, which applies dropout to rubric criteria: at each training step, a random subset of each rubric's criteria is removed from the reward, so the rewarded subset of criteria is resampled at each step. All responses sampled for the same prompt are scored on the same subset, so their rewards remain comparable, and evaluation always uses the full rubric. We also introduce Block Dropout, which drops semantically similar criteria together so that a near duplicate of a dropped criterion cannot keep rewarding the same behavior. Across both benchmarks and models from 1.7B to 8B, dropout improves the gold judge's score on OOD benchmarks by up to 3 percent on HealthBench-Hard and 7 percent on ResearchQA, and it reduces both the gap between the two judges and over-crediting. It keeps in-domain reward close to the baseline, and dropout rates of 30-50 percent work best. Block Dropout improves the gold judge's OOD score further at the same dropout rate. Changing which criteria are rewarded at each step is a simple, inexpensive way to make rubric rewards harder to exploit.
  </details>

- **[Multilingual Safety Alignment via Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.02971)**  `arXiv:2605.02971`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Ruiyang Qin, Qingzhuo Wang, Dongrui Liu, Qiang Li, Zhihua Wei, Wen Shen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) exhibit severe multilingual safety misalignment: they possess strong safeguards in high-resource languages but remain highly vulnerable to jailbreak attacks in low-resource languages. Current safety alignment methods generally rely on high-quality response data for each target language, which is expensive and difficult to generate. In this paper, we propose a cross-lingual safeguard transfer framework named Multilingual Self-Distillation (MSD). This framework transfers an LLM's inherent safety capabilities from high-resource (e.g., English) to low-resource (e.g., Javanese) languages, overcoming the need for response data in any language. Our framework is flexible and can be integrated with different self-distillation strategies. Specifically, we implement two concrete methods -- on-policy MSD and off-policy MSD -- both of which enable effective cross-lingual safety transfer using only multilingual queries. Furthermore, we propose Dual-Perspective Safety Weighting (DPSW), a divergence measure to optimize the distillation objective. By jointly considering the perspectives of both the teacher and the student, DPSW adaptively increases the penalty weights on safety-critical tokens while reducing the weights on non-critical tokens. Extensive experiments on representative LLMs across diverse multilingual jailbreak and utility benchmarks demonstrate that our method consistently achieves superior multilingual safety performance. Notably, it generalizes effectively to more challenging datasets and unseen languages while preserving the model's general capabilities.
  </details>

- **[TTCS: Test-Time Curriculum Synthesis for Self-Evolving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.22628)**  `arXiv:2601.22628`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Chengyi Yang, Zhishang Xiang, Yunbo Tang, Zongpei Teng, Chengsong Huang, Fei Long, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-Time Training offers a promising way to improve the reasoning ability of large language models (LLMs) by adapting the model using only the test questions. However, existing methods struggle with difficult reasoning problems for two reasons: raw test questions are often too difficult to yield high-quality pseudo-labels, and the limited size of test sets makes continuous online updates prone to instability. To address these limitations, we propose TTCS, a co-evolving test-time training framework. Specifically, TTCS initializes two policies from the same pretrained model: a question synthesizer and a reasoning solver. These policies evolve through iterative optimization: the synthesizer generates progressively challenging question variants conditioned on the test questions, creating a structured curriculum tailored to the solver's current capability, while the solver updates itself using self-consistency rewards computed from multiple sampled responses on both original test and synthetic questions. Crucially, the solver's feedback guides the synthesizer to generate questions aligned with the model's current capability, and the generated question variants in turn stabilize the solver's test-time training. Experiments show that TTCS consistently strengthens the reasoning ability on challenging mathematical benchmarks and transfers to general-domain tasks across different LLM backbones, highlighting a scalable path towards dynamically constructing test-time curricula for self-evolving. Our code and implementation details are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Kronecker Delta Rule for Long Context Linear Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26834)**  `arXiv:2610.26834`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Anamaria-Roberta Hartl, Levente Zólyomi, Michael List, Günter Klambauer, Sebastian Böck, Sepp Hochreiter_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose KronLSTM, a linear attention architecture based on the Kronecker Delta Rule, a novel update rule for associative matrix memories. KronLSTM extends the xLSTM family with a memory-revision mechanism that corrects targeted associations while modifying the memory only along a single direction in matrix space. This minimally invasive memory revision reduces interference with previously stored information compared with delta-rule updates, making it particularly well suited for long-context language modeling. The resulting memory dynamics can be mathematically described as a Markov chain that converges to a steady-state distribution, while the expected norm of the memory matrix remains uniformly bounded throughout the entire input sequence. Hence, KronLSTM ensures stable memory dynamics without requiring additional normalization and stabilization mechanisms for the memory state. Experiments show that KronLSTM is competitive with, and often outperforms, existing linear attention architectures, including models of the (Gated) DeltaNet family, with particularly strong performance on long context benchmarks. These results establish the Kronecker Delta Rule as an effective and analytically tractable foundation for stable associative memory in recurrent linear attention architectures. Our code (this https URL) and models (this https URL) are open-source.
  </details>

- **[Concerns in Benchmarking Jailbreaks: An Empirical Study of Attack Success Rates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26595)**  `arXiv:2610.26595`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Leon Tu Nguyen, Rohan Menon, Rafael Tappe Maestro, Eric Aubinais, Chih-Hong Cheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Jailbreak methods are commonly compared using Attack Success Rate (ASR), but these comparisons depend on how responses are generated and judged. In this paper, we examine eight attacks against three open-weight language models using three evaluation procedures. Low-resource language attacks achieve a mean ASR of 96.0% under keyword matching but 2.7% under a Qwen3 evaluator following StrongREJECT scoring instructions. Changing the number and arrangement of attempts reverses the ranking of DeepInception and rewriting. In a separate experiment, sampling 100 responses for each unchanged request at Ollama's default temperature of 0.8 yields 100% ASR under keyword matching but 50% under the Qwen3 evaluator. Beyond evaluator sensitivity, these findings raise a safety concern: repeated sampling can expose residual unsafe behavior despite an initial refusal, suggesting probabilistic suppression rather than robust exclusion. We recommend explicit success definitions, matched response budgets, validated evaluators, and reporting cumulative failure under repeated interaction rather than relying on single-generation tests.
  </details>

- **[CIS-Transolver: A Cross-Layer Incremental-State Neural Operator for Fine-Scale Automotive Aerodynamic Flow Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26337)**  `arXiv:2610.26337`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jianglong Liu, Zhicheng Zhu, Jia Hao, Liangyue Jia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural operators offer an efficient data-driven approach to rapid prediction of complex automotive aerodynamic flows. However, spectral bias and other factors limit accurate reconstruction of fine-scale flow variations near complex geometries. Existing studies improve the representation and fitting of fine-scale information mainly through information extraction and training objectives. Further study is needed to determine how extracted local details can be used continually in subsequent operator computations and contribute effectively to the overall flow-field representation. We propose CIS-Transolver, a cross-layer incremental-state neural operator that extends fine-scale modeling from local information extraction to its use across layers. The method extracts fine-scale information through local multiscale analysis and encodes it as geometric moment states that are propagated and updated across layers. By combining information from previous layers with feedback to the value representations in Physics-Attention, the method allows local details to contribute continually to subsequent modeling of global physical correlations. We systematically evaluate CIS-Transolver on three public automotive aerodynamics benchmarks, ShapeNet-Car, AhmedML, and DrivAerNet++, covering surface pressure fields and volumetric pressure and velocity fields. CIS-Transolver achieves the lowest mean relative L2 error among the evaluated methods for all five prediction metrics across the three benchmarks. The relative error reductions average 8.72% across the five metrics compared with the best baseline for each metric, reaching 16.38% for ShapeNet-Car pressure prediction and 15.60% for AhmedML velocity prediction. These results provide a new approach to fine-scale flow prediction for complex geometries.
  </details>

- **[UtilityBench: Measuring Language Model Utility When Errors Have Different Costs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25924)**  `arXiv:2610.25924`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Qifeng Lei, Zhiyong Yang, Zitai Wang, Shilong Bao, Linchao Zhang, Qianqian Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As language models are increasingly deployed in real-world scenarios with important outcomes, the impact of their mistakes often extends far beyond what correctness can capture. In domains like medicine, a single error can lead to serious consequences and put patient safety at risk. However, mainstream evaluation protocols still treat all errors as equal, overlooking the spectrum of real-world costs. To address this, we propose a utility-based evaluation paradigm that explicitly incorporates the varying consequences of errors. Guided by this principle, we develop UtilityBench, a large-scale benchmark of 15,000 instances with verifiable answers, quantifying error severity using Best-Worst Scaling to elicit meaningful, data-driven cost estimates. These quantified utilities shape when a model should answer or abstain, guiding it to make decisions that align with the true importance of each scenario. As another merit, we introduce UtilityAUC, a metric that measures the area under the utility curve determined by a model's response ordering, thereby enabling utility preference alignment beyond error frequency. Our theoretical analysis demonstrates that UtilityAUC can distinguish between models where standard accuracy and AURC fall short. Empirically, evaluating 20 state-of-the-art models, we find that greater accuracy or more advanced architectures do not reliably translate to higher UtilityAUC, highlighting a gap between problem-solving ability and responsible decision-making in language model evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Cognitively Decoupled Meta-Induction for Out-of-Distribution Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25749)**  `arXiv:2610.25749`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuqian Wang, Yuanzheng Wang, Chaojun Xiao, Ke Xu, Bingxiang He, Jinghan Tan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In-context learning (ICL) tackles downstream tasks conditioned on demonstrations. This paradigm relies heavily on neighbor-based matching between queries and demonstrations, limiting out-of-distribution (OOD) performance. For better generalization, rule-based inductive reasoning derives task-level regularities, which we term task-level inductive representations (TIR), before directly answering. However, existing methods lack generalizable inductive ability across diverse tasks. Inspired by meta-learning, we propose Cognitive-Decoupling meta-Induction (CoDI) for TIR-based inductive reasoning on general tasks. CoDI comprises a Cognitive-Decoupling Framework for query-blind TIR induction, and a Dual-Loop Meta-Inductive Learning method for cross-task inducer optimization. Empirically, CoDI achieves substantial performance gains on five ID and OOD benchmarks, and preserves OOD gains without explicit induction at inference. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Safety Offset Learning: When Benign Fine-Tuning Misaligns LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25611)**  `arXiv:2610.25611`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jesson Wang, Zhanhao Hu, Minghao Lin, Yuchen Wu, Aylin Caliskan, Mengyuan Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safety risks from fine-tuning are commonly characterized by the harmfulness of the training data:harmful fine-tuning is considered dangerous, while benign fine-tuning is often assumed to preservealignment. We argue that this view misses a more fundamental factor-the behavioral transition inducedby supervision. Harmful fine-tuning and safety-degrading benign fine-tuning can both be understoodas learning the same refusal-to-compliance transition, but on different data distributions. We studywhether such a transition learned on benign inputs can generalize to harmful requests. We constructbenign examples that an aligned model initially tends to refuse and pair them with their originalharmless responses, creating a discrepancy between the model's initial refusal and the supervisedanswer. Fine-tuning on these examples increases harmful compliance, but only when two conditionshold together: the discrepancy is large enough, and it cannot be resolved through a narrow surface-levelshortcut. With the induced discrepancy matched, varying only whether it is localized changes harmfulcompliance by an order of magnitude; raising the discrepancy does not help while a shortcut remainsavailable, and removing the shortcut does not help while the discrepancy is small. Building on thisobservation, we introduce SAFEOFF, a dataset of benign instructions rewritten to raise the alignedmodel's refusal likelihood and paired with their original answers. Across three aligned LLMs and threesafety benchmarks, SAFEOFF substantially weakens safety alignment despite containing no explicitharmful supervision, suggesting that fine-tuning safety depends not only on what data a model sees, butalso on which behavioral boundary the supervision moves.
  </details>

- **[MOSAIC: Multi-Objective Search for Allocating Inference Cache Across LLM Decoder Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25431)**  `arXiv:2610.25431`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Sravanth Kodavanti, Manjunath Arveti_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The Key-Value (KV) cache is the main memory bottleneck of long-context LLM inference. Token eviction methods such as SnapKV, H2O, AdaKV and L2Norm shrink it, but they give every transformer layer the same budget, although layers differ in which tokens they need. We present MOSAIC, a multi-objective neural architecture search that learns per-layer KV-cache budgets for any eviction method. One search maps the trade-off between average budget and task score; its best allocation transfers to every target budget without further search, and an optional budget-constrained search refines it. Across four eviction methods on Llama-3-8B-Instruct and Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.2, MOSAIC beats uniform allocation in 146 of 154 (task, method, budget) settings, by up to +48 points on RULER and +11.9 on LongBench (LCC). On both models, it reaches the score of uniform allocation with up to 4x less KV cache (75% smaller). Against EvolKV, MOSAIC explores a ~10^40 x larger search space with 4x fewer evaluations and 18% fewer GPU-hours per budget, and scores higher in 5 of 6 settings. The learned allocations consistently favour a small set of middle layers.
  </details>

- **[CD-OPD: Contextual Digest for On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25359)**  `arXiv:2610.25359`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Mingyuan Li, Yucheng Li, Xiaxia Wang, Gong Cheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Teacher takeover in on-policy distillation (OPD) can produce incorrect responses even when the student and teacher independently answer the same problem correctly, a failure we quantify using Collaboration Failure Rate (CFR). To address it, we propose Contextual Digest for On-Policy Distillation (CD-OPD). CD-OPD structures the earlier context of teacher guidance through a teacher-generated dependency graph, curating the required problem conditions and preceding reasoning as node summaries alongside the unchanged guidance in a Contextual Digest before student continuation. We further develop online meta-reweighting, which compares Digest segment gradients with a task gradient from independent student responses to improve student-only performance, favoring updates predicted to reduce task loss. Across eight benchmarks, CD-OPD improves average accuracy by 1.32--7.05 percentage points over representative existing OPD methods. It also reduces CFR by 27.4\%--56.4\% relative to existing takeover-based OPD methods.
  </details>

- **[EG-GRPO: Evidence-Guided Step-level Credit Assignment for Deep Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25269)**  `arXiv:2610.25269`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Sijia Luo, Mengjie Gao, Yuxiao He, Mengshu Sun, Cuiping Li, Hong Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based search agents demonstrate strong capabilities in long-horizon information seeking and reasoning, typically trained via reinforcement learning. However, existing approaches predominantly rely on trajectory-level outcome rewards, providing limited supervision for complex search and reasoning processes. Although step-level credit assignment offers a promising solution, obtaining reliable and task-grounded supervision for open-ended search remains challenging. In this paper, we propose Evidence-Guided Group Relative Policy Optimization (EG-GRPO), a framework that derives step-level supervision from the evidence annotations available in synthetic deep search data. EG-GRPO identifies the first supporting citation for each required evidence claim and uses the newly acquired evidence to construct step-level process rewards. By representing the accumulated evidence as an evolving knowledge state, EG-GRPO further performs state-conditioned relative advantage estimation and combines the resulting step-level signal with the standard trajectory-level outcome advantage. Experiments on five challenging deep search benchmarks show that EG-GRPO generally outperforms standard GRPO and existing reward-enhanced and step-level RL baselines across different model scales. These results demonstrate that task-grounded evidence annotations can provide effective process supervision for credit assignment in deep search RL.
  </details>

- **[BatchParScale: Sparse Batch-Mixing for Sequence Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25254)**  `arXiv:2610.25254`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Hans Peter Lyngsøe Raaschou-jensen, M. Onurcan Kaya, Desmond Elliott_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent test-time scaling methods improve model performance by increasing inference-time compute. One practical form of this is parallel scaling, where a model samples multiple independent reasoning streams and aggregates their answers using methods like voting, reranking, or verification. While effective, this approach leaves the streams uncoordinated, and a discovery made in one rollout cannot influence any other. Multi-Sequence Verifiers jointly score candidate answers after generation, but the reasoning streams remain independent during inference. ParScale takes a step toward model-internal parallel scaling by learning to aggregate multiple forward passes, but its streams still do not exchange information while processing a sequence. We introduce BatchParScale, an attention mechanism that allows parallel streams to exchange information through a small set of communication positions. We evaluate BatchParScale on a new Parallel Needle-in-a-Haystack retrieval benchmark and in pretraining, fine-tuning, and cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning. Across these settings, BatchParScale outperforms ParScale on language-model loss and remains competitive on coordination-sensitive MARL tasks. Masked pretraining runs beat an unmasked single-sequence baseline despite local sequence corruption, and LoRA fine-tuning shows the mechanism can be added to a pretrained Transformer. At the same time, fact retrieval performance for pretrained and finetuned BatchParScale models remains unclear.
  </details>

- **[ViCoR: Estimating Visual Necessity via Counterfactual Residuals for Multimodal Medical Data Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25202)**  `arXiv:2610.25202`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Haotian Gan, Yudong Li, Xintian Li, Jiarui Han, Weidong Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical vision-language models often exhibit hidden stratification, where high overall performance masks failures on subtle visual findings and overlapping clinical conditions. We trace this failure mode to modality imbalance during fine-tuning, where strong linguistic priors can suppress visually grounded learning and induce plausible but weakly grounded rationales. Existing data selection methods usually score image-text pairs as holistic units, without separating visually necessary samples from shortcut-solvable ones. To address this problem, we propose ViCoR, a counterfactual-residual-based data selection method for Med-VLM fine-tuning. ViCoR estimates visual necessity through image-dependent optimization residuals, calibrates the resulting scores with medical supervision compatibility, and constructs a diversity-aware fine-tuning subset. Across two backbones, four external generalization benchmarks, and one multi-disease robustness benchmark, ViCoR-selected 20\% subsets achieve the strongest average performance compared with full-data fine-tuning and selection baselines. Gradient-level analysis and perturbation audits further show that ViCoR enriches image-dependent training signals and strengthens image-level and evidence-region dependence. The code will be available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Auto-MD-bench: Evaluating Autoresearch Agents for Real-World Medical Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25069)**  `arXiv:2610.25069`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Shenqi Zhou, Qiran Zou, Wenhao Zhao, Zhenyu Liao, Dianbo Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous agents are increasingly capable of developing machine learning (ML) models end to end. In medical practice, however, strong performance on the target metric does not fully characterize how good the models are in real-world use cases. For example, medical ML models should also provide well-calibrated risk estimates and remain robust across patient populations. Yet autonomous model development is typically guided by task objectives that emphasize simple predictive discrimination (e.g. AUROC). This raises a key question: are these objectives sufficient to guide agents toward models that meet the broader requirements of real-world medical use? In this study, we introduce Auto-MD-bench, a benchmark of 15 real-world medical tasks from 10 clinical datasets, spanning survival analysis, classification and sequential prediction, with external validation across cohorts, hospitals and time periods. We evaluate four agents: AIDE, AI Scientist v2, Codex, and Autoresearch. The strongest agents achieve competitive primary scores, yet their predicted risks are less calibrated than those of the simple baseline models, which could lead to severe medical accidents. Moreover, models with nearly identical primary scores can exhibit markedly different calibration, while performance varies substantially across population shifts. These findings show that primary task performance alone is insufficient to assess autonomous clinical modeling and multiple different metrics widely used in healthcare practice need to be taken into consideration.
  </details>

- **[Synergy-Aware Residual Flow Matching for Single-Cell Perturbation Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25021)**  `arXiv:2610.25021`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jingrui Wu, Wangyun Dan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predicting single-cell responses to genetic and chemical perturbations is important for understanding regulatory mechanisms and prioritizing therapeutic interventions. Existing methods have made progress by modeling perturbation-conditioned expression shifts with neural networks, graph priors, foundation-model representations, or distributional generative models. However, combinatorial perturbation prediction remains challenging because much of the response can be explained by additive single-perturbation effects, while the biologically informative signal often lies in a smaller non-additive residual induced by perturbation interactions. Directly generating the full post-perturbation expression profile may therefore allocate substantial capacity to the dominant additive component and underemphasize synergy-specific structure. To address this issue, we propose \textbf{SynGeneFlow}, a synergy-aware flow matching framework that anchors the source distribution at an additive prior and learns a residual flow toward the true perturbed state. SynGeneFlow further incorporates perturbation-conditioned gene interaction modeling, where a static gene-gene mask provides biological structure and a perturbation-dependent deformation bias dynamically modulates gene-gene coupling. To preserve population-level response structure, we align both endpoint distributions and biological residual distributions with MMD-based regularization. We evaluate SynGeneFlow on genetic and drug perturbation benchmarks under observed-single, held-out, and cross-modality settings. The results demonstrate that residualizing flow matching around additive perturbation effects provides a biologically grounded and robust framework for single-cell perturbation response prediction.
  </details>

- **[UpLoop: Upcycling Large Language Models with Gated Loop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25004)**  `arXiv:2610.25004`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Tieyuan Chen, Huabin Liu, Yingtao Zhang, Shijie Li, Tengyu Pan, Weiyao Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped large language models trade parameter storage for recurrent computation, but most are trained from scratch. In practice, pretraining with looped depth is expensive, making post-training upcycling of a pretrained non-looped LLM an attractive alternative. We introduce \textbf{UpLoop}, a post-training recipe based on \emph{layer-wise} recurrence. Instead of looping a whole model or a middle block, UpLoop loops each selected layer locally, preserving the pretrained depth hierarchy and avoiding feature-space mismatch. To make loop execution useful, we add hidden-wise gates on the attention and MLP residual branches, so later loops act as progressive refinements rather than redundant rewrites. Empirically, UpLoop breaks the commonly observed saturation at two loops ($R=2$) in prior looped models. Starting from Qwen3-4B Base and applying SFT, UpLoop improves the average accuracy across 20 benchmarks by 6.17\% at $R=3$. We use $R=3$ as the main setting for the best accuracy--cost trade-off, while both native and extrapolated $R=4$ yield further gains.
  </details>

- **[ControlJEPA: Principled Trajectory Regularization via Lyapunov Tube Loss](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24951)**  `arXiv:2610.24951`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Thai-Khanh Nguyen, Nhi Y. Pham, Uyen N.B. Vo, Thieu N. Vo, Cuong Pham, Tan M. Nguyen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Next-token prediction leaves hidden-state trajectories largely unconstrained, while existing geometric regularizers primarily control the direction rather than the magnitude of trajectory deviations. We introduce \emph{ControlJEPA}, a trajectory regularization framework based on a discrete-time Lyapunov contraction condition from practical Input-to-State Stability theory. Its Lyapunov Tube Loss penalizes violations of this condition on a normalized, scale-invariant measure of transversal deviation. When the contraction condition holds at every step, the hidden-state trajectory remains within an explicit tube around the reference secant. In the partial-ISS regime, we bound mean transversal energy, temporal variance, and average tube width using the Lyapunov loss and contraction parameters, with horizon-independent variance bounds under uniformly bounded violations. ControlJEPA requires no architectural modifications. Across six benchmarks and six model families, it consistently improves accuracy and data efficiency over standard fine-tuning and prior trajectory regularizers. The resulting smoother transversal profile also persists empirically during inference, when the regularizer is inactive. The code is publicly available at: \url{this https URL}
  </details>

- **[Pair-Aware Optimization: Coordinating Exploration and Refinement for Jailbreaking LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24823)**  `arXiv:2610.24823`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Juanyang Xu, Zheng Wang, Xingyu Zhao, Siddartha Khastgir, Andi Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM) agents are vulnerable to adversarial inputs that induce attacker-specified malicious behaviors, commonly referred to as jailbreaking. Existing optimization-based jailbreak attacks primarily employ first-order (FO) methods that leverage gradient information or zeroth-order (ZO) methods that rely on function-value feedback. However, their complementarity and optimization ordering remain underexplored. We systematically investigate four strategies: pure ZO, pure FO, ZO->FO, and FO->ZO, and develop a ZO-FO framework that explores adversarial prefixes through ZO optimization and refines their corresponding suffixes through FO optimization. To coordinate the two processes, we introduce Pair-aware optimization, which retains multiple prefix candidates, conditionally refines their suffixes and selects among the resulting prefix-suffix pairs. We further investigate agent jailbreaking from a more general stochastic-generation perspective, where the same adversarial input can induce diverse reasoning trajectories and actions that a single greedy output cannot capture. Accordingly, we adopt consistent stochastic decoding during both optimization and evaluation. Experiments across malicious-instruction and malicious-environment benchmarks demonstrate that hybrid optimization improves attack effectiveness over single-optimizer methods, while optimization order substantially influences performance.
  </details>

- **[First-Order LoRA Training with Phone NPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24816)**  `arXiv:2610.24816`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xu Zheng, Wei Cheng, Zhuomin Chen, Dongkuan Xu, Mo Sha, Jingchao Ni, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-device fine-tuning lets a large language model (LLM) learn from its user's private data without uploading it. Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) reduces the trainable parameters, but each first-order training step still multiplies by the frozen base-model weights during the forward pass, activation recomputation, and input-gradient computation. A phone's neural processing unit (NPU) already holds these weights for inference; using it for training also requires computing input gradients, which its inference interface does not support. We present a system that runs all three frozen-weight products on the NPU while keeping the training graph, adapters, and optimizer on the CPU.
