Its input-gradient kernels reuse the resident weight tiles and the forward dequantizer, avoiding a transposed weight copy and ensuring forward and backward use the same effective weights. To preserve usable gradients, the system selects precision by gradient sensitivity and applies a per-row range guard for half-precision (FP16) outputs. Its runtime fuses the remaining CPU operators and overlaps NPU execution with adapter computation, while resumable execution and thermal pacing let training proceed as a background job. We evaluate it on two Qualcomm Snapdragon platforms using four LLMs from the Qwen and Llama families. Our workloads cover nightly personalization with labels supplied on the phone and the public LaMP-2 benchmark; comparisons include an optimized CPU trainer, a phone-GPU trainer, forward-only training, and GPU-trained references.
  </details>

- **[Contrastive Adversarial Training for Robust Graph Neural Networks under Label Poisoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24685)**  `arXiv:2610.24685`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Manshika Charvi Bissessur, Melis Ilayda Bal, Michael Muehlebach_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) are effective for modeling relational data but are vulnerable to label poisoning, where a small number of corrupted training labels can propagate errors across the graph via message passing. Despite this risk, defenses against label poisoning remain underexplored: existing methods are primarily designed for label noise and often rely on robust losses or heuristic data cleaning that fail to distinguish adversarial poisoning from naturally hard examples. In this paper, we propose CoLAT (Contrastive Label-flipping for Adversarial Training), a novel contrastive adversarial training framework tailored for robust node classification on graphs. The core of our approach is a structure-aware detection model that uses contrastive learning to identify label-structure inconsistencies. Unlike prior contrastive methods that focus on representation learning, CoLAT leverages contrastive embeddings to select high-risk nodes that guide adversarial label-flipping during training. This alternating optimization not only performs structure-aware adversarial training but also implicitly sanitizes some corrupted labels, improving robustness against diverse attacks from the literature. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks show that CoLAT consistently outperforms existing defenses under various poisoning intensities while scaling efficiently to large graphs.
  </details>

- **[On the Selectivity of Generative Models in Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24124)**  `arXiv:2610.24124`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ella Miray Rajaonson, Jungyoon Lee, William Chau, Alan Aspuru-Guzik, Benjamin Sanchez-Lengeling, Dominique Beaini, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Designing small-molecule drugs that selectively bind to a target protein while avoiding unintended interactions with other proteins is essential for reducing side effects that contribute substantially to clinical attrition. Although structure-based drug design (SBDD) has advanced rapidly with deep learning, most generative models still optimize binding to a single target and disregard selectivity due to limited training data and unknown off-target interactions. Motivated by this gap, we introduce SELECTBENCH, a comprehensive benchmark assessing the selectivity of generative SBDD models. SELECTBENCH provides three curated evaluation tasks reflecting different real-world drug discovery scenarios: literature-based case studies, safety screening panels used in pharmaceutical development, and large-scale structure-based evaluation sets. We further introduce a suite of metrics to quantify selectivity and conduct a systematic evaluation of nine representative SBDD models, including methods explicitly trained for selectivity and widely-used inference-time guidance techniques. Our results show that even selectivity-trained models achieve only modest off-target discrimination and inference-time guidance provides limited gains, underscoring the need for method improvement. SELECTBENCH provides both a characterization of where current generative SBDD stands on selectivity and an open, extensible platform to accelerate progress. The benchmark is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[TWIN-MoE: Two-stage Intra-Expert Imputation Network for Incomplete Multimodal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24014)**  `arXiv:2610.24014`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Liang Nathan Zheng, Jiyuan Liu, Wenhao Liang, Wei Emma Zhang, Weitong Chen, Lin Yue_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) approaches address missing modalities either by routing each missingness pattern to a dedicated expert, which scales poorly as the number of modalities grows, or by imputing the missing modality from representations contributed by multiple experts, which requires a costly retrieval mechanism. We show that routing induces expert-conditioned representation neighborhoods and derive an imputation-error bound controlled by their local feature dispersion. When these expert-conditioned neighborhoods are more compact than the global representation space, intra-expert aggregation admits a tighter worst-case reconstruction bound than unconstrained global aggregation. Guided by this analysis, we propose TWIN, a two-stage intra-expert imputation framework. Pre-imputation generates a context-conditioned estimate in the encoder embedding space and radially soft-clips it using modality-specific statistics maintained in an observed FIFO queue, this ensures that the pre-imputed modality stays in empirically calibrated region of the target modality space. Post-imputation refines the missing representation after MoE by aggregating expert-conditioned observed representations within the same sample. Across four missing-modality benchmarks under natural, random, and asymmetric dropout protocols, TWIN achieves state-of-the-art performance while using substantially fewer parameters than competing MoE-based imputation methods.
  </details>

- **[ReTTT: Reflection-Driven Test-Time Training via Experience-Based Parametric Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23942)**  `arXiv:2610.23942`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Kangcong Li, Hangfan Zhang, Shuyue Hu, Shengji Tang, Tao Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents accumulate valuable task-specific information through their actions, observations, and environmental feedback during multi-turn task execution. When effectively used, this information can help agents refine subsequent decisions and improve task performance. However, the learning value of an interaction depends on its context and subsequent feedback, making it challenging to extract reliable, actionable lessons and revise them as the task unfolds. In this paper, we introduce ReTTT, which enables experience-driven self-adaptation through test-time training on lessons constructed and continually revised within an episode. ReTTT lets the agent reflect on interaction feedback, maintain the resulting lessons in an explicit memory, and learn from them by updating an episode-local LoRA adapter while keeping the backbone frozen. The adapted model then performs both subsequent actions and reflection, coupling policy adaptation with the construction of future learning experience. We evaluate ReTTT on ALFWorld, ScienceWorld, and SWE-bench Lite using two model families. ReTTT outperforms trajectory-based test-time training across all six model--benchmark settings, improving task success by up to 12.14 percentage points. A frozen-reflection comparison further shows a 7.14-point benefit from adapting the reflection policy, while trajectory analysis illustrates how adapted reflection derives more task-specific guidance from failed attempts. These findings suggest that learning to reflect on its own behavior can boost agents' performance on long-horizon tasks.
  </details>

- **[PeekKV: The First Content Token Already Knows Which Heads Matter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23939)**  `arXiv:2610.23939`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Minyoung Go, Yongjun Park_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The size of the KV cache grows linearly with context length, making it a major memory bottleneck in LLM serving. Motivated by recent progress in identifying the functional roles of attention heads, a growing body of work compresses the KV cache non-uniformly across heads. Existing methods either fix the important heads via offline profiling or select them using attention signals from the prefill stage. In both cases, however, accuracy fluctuates substantially across workloads. While investigating the cause of this instability, we find that the active heads shift between the prefill and decode stages even within a single input. Along the decode path, by contrast, the activehead composition remains stable across content tokens once format tokens are filtered out. Based on this observation, we propose PeekKV. PeekKV observes a single content token immediately after prefill - a one-token peek - to jointly obtain head-group scores and token-importance scores. Guided by these scores, PeekKV allocates KV cache budgets to head groups in proportion to their group scores and retains only the top-scoring tokens within each budget. PeekKV requires no offline profiling or training and thus applies directly to unseen workloads and new models. Across four public benchmarks, PeekKV achieves the highest accuracy in more than half of the per-model comparisons against state-of-the-art methods, and stays closest to the full-cache accuracy on RULER and LongBench. On Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, PeekKV retains 95.7% of the full-cache accuracy at a 10% memory budget on RULER with 16K contexts and 95.9% at a 1.5% budget on LongBench.
  </details>

- **[Factorization-Guided Training and Inference for Diffusion-Drafted Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23935)**  `arXiv:2610.23935`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiakun Fan, Min Xiao, Qiang Wang, Neiwen Ling, Zhichao Liu, Zhihao Jia, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates large language model inference through parallel verification of tokens proposed by a drafter. Diffusion drafters further parallelize proposal generation, but greater proposal capacity or higher mean accepted tokens (MAT) need not yield higher throughput. Because verification accepts only a consecutive prefix, early rejections can render later correct predictions ineffective, limiting MAT gains, while even higher MAT may not improve throughput if per-round latency increases. We introduce DScout, which uses two complementary factorizations to guide diffusion drafter training and inference for higher throughput. For training, DScout factorizes position-wise loss into inclusion, weighting, and prediction loss. This guides the design and combination of mixed-horizon training, intra-block unmasking, and acceptance-weighted loss to align supervision with prefix acceptance and improve MAT. For inference, DScout factorizes throughput as the ratio of MAT to per-round latency, guiding target-conditioning depth, drafter depth, and inference block size selection to identify a high-throughput configuration for each workload. Across six benchmarks, DScout achieves $3.84\times$ and $4.14\times$ mean speedups over autoregressive decoding on Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B, respectively, improving mean speedup over the state-of-the-art baseline by 9.1% and 7.1%. Across models and concurrency settings, our inference configuration policy provides up to 9.75% additional mean speedup over DScout's fixed configuration.
  </details>

- **[Improving Fake Profile Detection Through Multimodal Learning and Research-Guided Feature Integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23926)**  `arXiv:2610.23926`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jean Claude Hirwa, Jamiu Dahunsi, Candide Giramata, Lowami Uwimana, Thierry Henry Ishimwe_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fake accounts continue to undermine online platforms, yet it remains unclear whether multimodal learning offers meaningful improvements over strong metadata baselines or graph-based approaches. In this work, we study bot detection by integrating textual, visual, and account metadata signals on the TwiBot-22 benchmark using a subsample of 30,000 users. We propose a multimodal framework combining pretrained textual (XLM-RoBERTa) and visual (CLIP) embeddings with structured account-level features such as follower counts, posting frequency, and account age, and evaluate multiple modality combinations using CatBoost alongside a Relational Graph Convolutional Network (R-GCN). Results show that metadata alone is highly predictive, while multimodal fusion yields only marginal and largely statistically insignificant gains. The graph-based model underperforms classical variants under the subsampled graph conditions of this study. Overall, our findings suggest that under practical constraints, feature quality and metadata richness matter more than fusion complexity.
  </details>

- **[Web Agent Agentic Reinforcement Learning Decision Model Under Multi-Cost and Failure Risk](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23857)**  `arXiv:2610.23857`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Qianli Ma, Limengxi Yue, Shuyang Xu, Yanpei Shi, Hongrui Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Intelligent agent interactions in real-world web environments are commonly constrained by request budgets, time delays, anti-crawling restrictions, and operational failure risks. Strategies solely optimizing task success rates often exhibit unusable phenomena such as "high success but high cost" or "low risk but conservative failure." This paper proposes a constrained agentic reinforcement learning model for web agents, unifying page access, search requests, and external API calls into a unified long-term decision-making framework with associated costs. It simultaneously incorporates cost budget constraints and tail risk control into the optimization objective: constructing a multidimensional cost vector comprising cumulative request count, total latency, and failure penalties to achieve budget compliance via Lagrange dual updates, while employing a CVaR risk term to suppress excessive exploration of high-failure-probability paths, thereby achieving an adaptive balance among "completion rate, cost, and risk."Experiments were conducted across 30-70 site/page templates and 800-1,500 end-to-end web tasks, including information extraction, price comparison, form submission, and cross-page navigation. Interaction sequences spanned 20-120 steps with tool scales of 30-200. Performance was benchmarked against unconstrained RL, budget-constrained RL, and rule-based/scripted web agents, quantifying task completion rates, cost-per-success, failure rates, and policy stability.Results demonstrate that at equivalent completion rates, our method reduces C-PS by 22%-31% and lowers failure rates by 18%-26% under high failure penalties. Under fixed budgets, task completion rates increase by 10%-16%, highlighting the necessity and effectiveness of constraint modeling for practical web agent deployment.
  </details>

- **[OrionMoE-v1.0: A Mixture-of-Experts Foundation Model for Tabular In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20418)**  `arXiv:2610.20418`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Mohamed Bouadi, Nassim Bouarour, Aditya Tanna, Vinay Kumar Sankarapu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tabular foundation models enable in-context learning by predicting unseen observations from a labeled support table without task-specific parameter updates. Despite recent advances, most existing approaches rely on dense architectures that apply the same transformations across features and datasets. We introduce OrionMoE-Small-v1.0, a compact tabular foundation model that combines distribution-aware representations with dataset-conditioned sparse mixture-of-experts computation. Built on a dual-axis transformer architecture, OrionMoE-Small selectively activates experts with routing decisions informed by both individual cell representations and the broader dataset context. This design enables adaptive allocation of computation across heterogeneous tabular problems while retaining a shared computational pathway across tasks. The model is pretrained entirely on synthetic classification tasks generated from a structured causal prior and performs downstream prediction without gradient-based adaptation. Experiments across multiple tabular benchmarks demonstrate competitive performance against substantially larger foundation models, with particularly strong results on small-context and high-dimensional datasets. Further analysis reveals favorable relative performance under class imbalance and highlights the importance of feature composition.
  </details>

- **[MMTA: Benchmarking Multimodal Temporal Analysis with Time Series, Text, and Vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17856)**  `arXiv:2610.17856`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ziyang Zhang, Shenyi Li, Yilin Wang, Ziyun Cui, Bowen Zhou, Wen Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time-series analysis is increasingly studied in multimodal settings, yet most benchmarks pair temporal signals with text or with visualizations derived from the same signal. This leaves open whether multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can integrate time series with real-world visual evidence. We introduce MMTA (multimodal temporal analysis), a benchmark of tri-modal tasks with diverse cross-modal complementarity patterns among time series, text, and vision. MMTA spans nine domains -- robotics, autonomous driving, human action, manufacturing, AR assistance, healthcare, recommendation, finance, and climate -- under video+TS and image+TS layouts, with a 3,524-sample test split covering classification and forecasting. The benchmark standardizes heterogeneous real-world sources into a shared schema with domain-specific prompts, provenance documentation, and success-weighted metrics that penalize both wrong answers and failed generations. Evaluating eight open-source and proprietary MLLMs reveals a shared bottleneck: representing time series as text creates very long token sequences and weakens fine-grained alignment with visual evidence, especially in long-video settings. We then introduce TimeOmni-v, a multimodal LLM extension with a dedicated time-series encoder, temporal positional alignment, modality-aware token layout, and a forecasting head. TimeOmni-v improves long-video classification and time-series inference efficiency, showing that MMTA not only exposes representation and grounding failures but also motivates targeted model design.
  </details>

- **[SLDBench v2: Active Scaling Law Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16297)**  `arXiv:2610.16297`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Lingbei Meng, Rui Huang, Zhirong Shen, Jinxin Liu, Lingxiao Jing, Haorui Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling laws predict large-model performance from small experiments, but finding a reliable law itself requires costly experiments. SLDBench tests whether language-model agents can discover scaling laws when all experimental results are given upfront. We introduce SLDBench v2 with two extensions. First, we expand the corpus from 8 to 127 scenarios spanning language-model pretraining, downstream performance, and vision-language transfer, and select 30 challenging scenarios on which a reference power-law fit reaches $R^2 \le 0.8$; they represent at least $1.09 \times 10^{23}$ FLOPs of source-reported training compute. Second, we introduce active scaling law discovery: an agent chooses which experiments to run, pays each one's recorded cost (e.g., training FLOPs), and revises its law as outcomes arrive, and is scored on the same hidden extrapolation targets as in the passive setting. Across 8 frontier agent systems, median $R^2$ drops from 0.72-0.75 (passive) to 0.24-0.57 (active), and prediction error rises 1.5-4.7 times, depending on the system. Agents favor cheap experiments, spending 8-44% of the full-pool cost on average; low-cost acquisition can yield strong predictions, but cheap purchases also leave experimental conditions needed for extrapolation unobserved. Experimental planning thus remains a key open challenge for agents that assist model development in the prevailing AI-for-AI study. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Channel-Dependent State Space Model for Multivariate Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36453)**  `arXiv:2609.36453`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yu-Cheng Wu, Fan-Keng Sun, Li-Chun Lu, Duane S. Boning_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multivariate time series forecasting (MTSF) is critical across many real-world domains. Existing deep learning approaches fall into two paradigms with distinct limitations: channel-independent (CI) methods unconditionally ignore cross-variable dependencies and model only temporal dynamics, while channel-dependent (CD) methods consider both but typically rely on architectural compromises to mitigate overfitting and computational overhead. We therefore propose Chameleon, a specialized CD state space model (SSM) that enables data-dependent, fine-grained interactions across variables while scaling linearly with their number. By connecting selective SSMs with the Kalman filter, we leverage the missing measurement update in the former for cross-variable modeling while preserving the SSM backbone for robust temporal modeling. We further identify favorable inductive biases of GatedDeltaNet for time series, adapt it as our backbone, and improve generalization through additional techniques, including a previously unexplored stochastic perturbation of reversible instance normalization. On strongly dependent ODE and PEMS datasets, Chameleon achieves the best MSE and MAE across all settings, while its CI ablation and prior CD methods incur 61-178% higher MSE on average. Across 28 standard benchmark settings, Chameleon also achieves better MSE and MAE than each baseline in at least 27 and 22 cases, respectively. Training-time and peak-memory analyses on Traffic and ETT further demonstrate competitive efficiency and favorable memory scalability across different variable counts.
  </details>

- **[STAR: Adaptive Spatial-Temporal Normalization for Unified Microservice Incident Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31645)**  `arXiv:2609.31645`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xinhua Miao, Linyu Zhu, Bowei Yang, Zhengong Cai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated incident management in large-scale microservice systems relies on learning robust representations from multimodal observability data, including metrics, logs, and traces. Although recent self-supervised frameworks enable unified modeling for anomaly detection (AD), failure triage (FT), and root cause localization (RCL), they often struggle with non-stationary temporal dynamics and heterogeneous service dependency structures. In this paper, we propose STAR, a Spatial-Temporal Adaptive Representation learning framework that explicitly addresses these challenges through adaptive normalizations. STAR introduces two tightly coupled mechanisms: Temporal Adaptive Normalization (TAN), which dynamically normalizes multivariate time series using multi-scale temporal context, and Spatial Adaptive Normalization (SAN), which performs structure-aware normalization over service dependency graphs. Unlike prior methods that treat normalization as static or task-agnostic, STAR formulates it as a learnable, context-conditioned transformation aligned with the intrinsic properties of microservice systems. The resulting adaptive representations are integrated into a unified self-supervised framework, enabling end-to-end unsupervised support for AD, FT, and RCL tasks. Extensive experiments on two real-world microservice benchmarks demonstrate that STAR consistently outperforms all state-of-the-art baselines, yielding significant and stable improvements across all three tasks. Our results highlight adaptive normalization as a principled and effective mechanism for robust multimodal representation learning in complex software systems.
  </details>

- **[Good Memory Has ECC: Evaluating the Memory of Vision-Language Models Beyond Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00103)**  `arXiv:2609.00103`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Shmuel Berman, Jia Deng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory is widely viewed as an important unsolved problem for LLMs and VLMs, and current benchmarks typically evaluate it by testing accuracy over long text or video. However, accuracy alone misses properties that matter for real long-horizon tasks. We introduce ECCBench, a benchmark and evaluation protocol that measures memory beyond a system's capacity--its raw accuracy at a specific budget--via three axes we call ECC: efficiency--the computation, in FLOPs, needed to answer from memory; compression--whether compressible inputs are remembered more accurately or efficiently; and calibration--whether the system abstains in response to its own uncertainty and the cost of an error. We find that pretrained VLMs compress their memory over text but not video and are poorly calibrated on both. Among a broader set of memory backbones, several non-Transformer architectures achieve better compression-calibration tradeoffs than RoPE Transformers, suggesting they may be useful components for agents operating over long horizons.
  </details>

- **[Circuit Claims Depend on What Is Extracted and How It Is Compared](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18921)**  `arXiv:2607.18921`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yang Sheng, Jie Fu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Circuit extraction identifies model components whose presence preserves a target behavior under ablation, but preserving behavior does not single out one circuit. We study this in a synthetic Lean tactic-prediction benchmark with fixed proof rules and randomized surface form. Across dense and weight-sparse checkpoints of the same transformer, evaluated on atomic and compositional proofs, we vary which extracted object is reported: a compact prediction-preserving circuit, a broader graph retaining surrounding read, write, and routing structure, or a subgraph extracted with a post-ablation loss budget. First, overlap between groups of component-to-component connections is low, sometimes close to a random baseline. Overlap is much higher for selected attention-head sets under deterministic selection, but falls sharply when selection among equally scored heads is randomized. Second, the normalized graph-size ranking of conditions that differ in which supervised checkpoint initializes reinforcement learning (RL) agrees between repeated extractions of the compact circuit and between joint and separate query/key representations of each of the other two graphs. For the loss-budgeted graph, query/key ordering agreement persists through the budget sweep. Finally, across those same conditions, the largest exact-match accuracy gains from RL on compositional proofs come with the largest proportion of components in the compositional circuit that lie outside the corresponding atomic circuits. Ablating these additional components in the sparse RL model reduces compositional accuracy while largely preserving accuracy on the corresponding atomic tasks. A circuit-level claim therefore requires specifying which circuit is reported and how it is extracted and compared; behavior alone does not determine its interpretation. We distill these requirements into a reporting practice for circuit-extraction studies.
  </details>

- **[SOS-LoRA: Static Orthogonal-Subspace Low-Rank Adaptation with Fixed Multi-Scale Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16252)**  `arXiv:2607.16252`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yupeng Chang, Yuan Wu, Yi Chang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is a widely used parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) method for large language models. Under a fixed rank budget, LoRA parameterizes each adapted weight through a single low-dimensional input-side pathway, which may couple heterogeneous behaviors through shared input directions and induce interference during optimization. We propose Static Orthogonal Subspace LoRA (SOS-LoRA), a drop-in extension that reparameterizes a rank-rtot update as a sum of K static (always-on, non-routed) low-rank experts. SOS-LoRA (i) decomposes the total rank across experts, (ii) applies a fixed multi-scale scaling scheme to encourage scale-separated optimization dynamics, and (iii) promotes diverse input-side directions via cross-expert orthogonal initialization and a lightweight regularizer. SOS-LoRA remains fully mergeable, adding no inference-time parameters or latency after merging. Experiments on reasoning and knowledge-intensive benchmarks (Llama 2/3), encoder-based NLU (GLUE), and math reasoning (GSM8K/MATH) show consistent gains over matched-budget LoRA baselines and recent variants. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Derived Fields Increase the Recoverable Information in Budgeted Neural PDE Surrogate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.29224)**  `arXiv:2603.29224`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Wenshuo Wang, Fan Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  For neural surrogates of time-dependent partial differential equations (PDEs), storage and memory budgets require training data to be obtained by coarsening and quantizing very-high-resolution numerical simulations. Fine-scale information can thus be lost before it reaches the model. We analyze this effect in periodic incompressible Navier-Stokes: quantization spreads error evenly over the retained modes, so the finest retained band, which holds the least energy, is the most corrupted, and velocity and vorticity are distorted differently under the same operator. Building on this analysis, we propose Derived-Field Optimization (DerivOpt), which chooses which primitive and derived fields to carry from a supplied library, and how many bits each receives, by exactly minimizing a closed-form posterior risk under a calibrated channel model. This optimization problem always has an optimum and requires no knowledge of the governing equation. On PDEBench, DerivOpt not only lowers the pooled rollout nRMSE but also substantially reduces the fine-band error. These gains are already present in the predictor-free input state and add to those of a multiscale architecture. Which fields are carried, and at what precision, is thus an optimizable design variable upstream of the predictor that determines how much information a fixed budget leaves recoverable.
  </details>

- **[Brittlebench: Quantifying LLM robustness via prompt sensitivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.13285)**  `arXiv:2603.13285`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Angelika Romanou, Mark Ibrahim, Candace Ross, Chantal Shaib, Kerem Oktar, Samuel J. Bell, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing evaluation methods largely rely on clean, static benchmarks, which can overestimate true model performance by failing to capture the noise and variability inherent in real-world user inputs. This is especially true for language models, which can face human-generated text queries containing mistakes, typos, or alternative ways of phrasing the same question. In this work, we introduce a theoretical framework for quantifying model sensitivity to prompt variants, or brittleness, that can enable us to disentangle data-induced difficulty from prompt-related variability. Using this framework, we design a novel evaluation pipeline, Brittlebench, to holistically evaluate the sensitivity of frontier models. We apply semantics-preserving perturbations to a suite of popular benchmarks, and observe model performance to degrade as much as 12%. However, these perturbations do not affect all models equally: even a single perturbation alters the relative ranking of models in 63% of cases, impacting conclusions about comparative model performance. Decomposing the total variance of both state-of-the-art open-weight and commercial models, we find that semantics-preserving input perturbations can account for up to half of the performance variance for a given model. Brittlebench highlights the need for more robust evaluations and models, and allows us to systematically understand model brittleness.
  </details>

- **[IRPM: Intergroup Relative Preference Modeling for Pointwise Generative Reward Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.00677)**  `arXiv:2601.00677`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Haonan Song, Qingchen Xie, Huan Zhu, Feng Xiao, Luxi Xing, Liu Kang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative Reward Models (GRMs) have demonstrated strong performance in reward modeling, due to their interpretability and potential for refinement through reinforcement learning (RL). However, widely used pairwise GRMs create a computational bottleneck in reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF), when calibrating or aggregating preference signals over n candidates, often incurring O(n^2) pairwise judgments. To address this issue, we propose Intergroup Relative Preference Modeling (IRPM), an RL-based method that extends the Bradley-Terry preference-learning paradigm via intergroup comparisons to train pointwise GRMs from pairwise preference data. IRPM derives a pointwise reward for each response by contrasting groups of chosen vs. rejected samples, enabling pointwise scores comparable across candidate sets and O(n) reward evaluation for a variable number of candidates during RL training, while preserving interpretability and scalability. Experiments show that IRPM achieves state-of-the-art performance among pointwise GRMs on JudgeBench and RewardBench, and approaches the performance of leading pairwise GRMs. In addition, IRPM achieves substantial gains in post-training evaluations, demonstrating its effectiveness. Our models and code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[UNO: Unlearning via Orthogonalization in Generative models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04712)**  `arXiv:2506.04712`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Pinak Mandal, Georg A. Gottwald_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As generative models become increasingly powerful and pervasive, the ability to unlearn specific data due to privacy concerns, legal requirements, or harmful content has become increasingly important. Unlearning aims to selectively remove the influence of particular data without costly retraining from scratch. Effective unlearning requires (i) forgetting undesired data, (ii) preserving generation quality, (iii) retaining the influence of desired training data on model parameters, and (iv) computational efficiency. We propose fast unlearning algorithms based on loss gradient orthogonalization for unconditional and conditional generative models. We consider the loss function of the original model restricted to the retain data and augment it by a term promoting the orthogonality between the two gradients of the loss function evaluated at the retain dataset and the forget dataset. We coin this method unlearning via orthogonalization (UNO) and show that the additional weak orthogonality constraint accelerates unlearning. The inclusion of the weak orthogonality constraint implies that each gradient descent step utilizes local information about the geometry via the Hessian of the loss function. Our geometry aware gradient descent step significantly accelerates the learning of the retain data loss function compared to a gradient descent which uses only gradient information. By alternating between an UNO and a gradient surgery step, we obtain UNO-S, which further reduces execution time. We demonstrate that our algorithms effectively forget data while preserving the fidelity of the original model. On standard image benchmarks, they achieve orders-of-magnitude faster unlearning than existing methods such as gradient surgery. We validate our approach on datasets of increasing complexity (MNIST, CelebA, and ImageNet-1K) and generative architectures ranging from VAEs to diffusion transformers.
  </details>

- **[Gradient Boosting Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.08250)**  `arXiv:2407.08250`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Benjamin Fuhrer, Chen Tessler, Gal Dalal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Gradient Boosting Reinforcement Learning (GBRL), a framework that adapts the strengths of gradient boosting trees (GBT) to reinforcement learning (RL) tasks. While neural networks (NNs) have become the de facto choice for RL, they face significant challenges with structured and categorical features and tend to generalize poorly to out-of-distribution samples. These are challenges for which GBTs have traditionally excelled in supervised learning. However, GBT's application in RL has been limited. The design of traditional GBT libraries is optimized for static datasets with fixed labels, making them incompatible with RL's dynamic nature, where both state distributions and reward signals evolve during training. GBRL overcomes this limitation by continuously interleaving tree construction with environment interaction. Through extensive experiments, we demonstrate that GBRL outperforms NNs in domains with structured observations and categorical features while maintaining competitive performance on standard continuous control benchmarks. Like its supervised learning counterpart, GBRL demonstrates superior robustness to out-of-distribution samples and better handles irregular state-action relationships.
  </details>

- **[CTLite: Lightweight Irregular Multivariate Time Series Forecasting with Reusable Continuous-Time Summaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26379)**  `arXiv:2610.26379`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shusen Ma, Xudong Zuo, Yangchun Xie, Yun-Bo Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Irregular multivariate time series forecasting (IMTSF) requires capturing temporal dynamics and cross-variable dependencies from sparse, asynchronous observations. Existing methods often rely on learned temporal encoders, coupling historical representation construction with model optimisation and repeatedly processing the same observations. We propose CTLite, a lightweight forecasting framework that decouples historical summarisation from learned prediction. Specifically, a parameter-free continuous-time moment encoder summarises local levels and trends at multiple temporal scales while suppressing poorly supported slope estimates. A compact readout combines damped local extrapolation with a low-rank cross-variable correction that accounts for observation freshness, target availability, and forecast horizon. Since the historical summaries are independent of trainable parameters and forecast queries, they can be reused across optimisation steps and repeated predictions for an unchanged history without altering predictions or parameter gradients. Experiments on four real-world benchmarks show that CTLite achieves the best or second-best mean MSE and MAE among the evaluated methods with only 88-2,537 trainable parameters. Under matched GPU workloads at batch size 32, CTLite reduces inference latency by 18.6-87.3% compared with the latest models, including the cost of historical encoding. Reusing summaries further reduces total runtime by approximately 52% over 16 forecast calls on the same history compared with recomputing the encoder for every call, including the initial encoding cost. The code is at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[FinHopper: A Progressive Multi-Hop Benchmark for Complex Financial Agents with Step-wise Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26036)**  `arXiv:2610.26036`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuxuan Wu, Miaopei Lin, Qiufang Ying, Lin Dan, Hao Zhao, Qirui Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Financial agents are expected to answer factual investment queries through open search, but financial multi-hop retrieval differs from general multi-hop QA in a crucial way: each intermediate answer is an exact typed constraint, such as an entity, date, event, or numerical indicator, that determines the next query. This strict answer-conditioned dependency makes small intermediate errors cascade into plausible but wrong search trajectories. We introduce FinHopper, a benchmark for complex financial agents with controlled reasoning depth from 1 to 6 hops, covering stocks, funds, and macroeconomic facts, with gold intermediate answers for process-level evaluation. To construct FinHopper at scale, we design a state-transition-based synthesis framework that composes verified atomic financial QA patterns into dependency-preserving chains, followed by targeted in-synthesis verification, model-ensemble checking, and public-search validation by financial experts. Experiments with 20 LLM-agent configurations show that public search tools are necessary but insufficient: performance drops sharply as hop depth increases, the best model remains far below human experts, and process-aware scoring reveals partial reasoning progress hidden by final-answer-only evaluation. The benchmark is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ProtGlycanDock: Towards Accurate Protein-Glycan Docking with Tailored Dataset, Benchmark and Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26009)**  `arXiv:2610.26009`  `cs.LG`  
  _Minghao Xu, Jiaze Song, Jinyu Wang, Wentao Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Performing accurate protein-glycan docking is critical to understanding protein-glycan interactions which govern many biological processes. However, this important task is hampered by the lack of tailored datasets, benchmarks and models for protein-glycan docking. To fill this blank, we first curate a standard benchmark ProtGlycanDock for training and evaluating protein-glycan docking models under both protein and glycan generalization settings. On this benchmark, we evaluate ten models in three categories, physics-based docking methods, machine-learning-based docking models and molecular structure prediction models. To obtain more precise binding poses, we further propose two finetuning techniques to inject the knowledge of glycan conformations into a general-purpose molecular structure prediction model. These two techniques respectively augment model inputs with glycan topological features and refine glycan binding pose with a glycan energy loss. Benchmark results show the superiority of AlphaFold 3 among existing methods, and the two proposed finetuning techniques can be well combined to achieve a state-of-the-art model for protein-glycan docking. Also, we perform stratified benchmark analysis and case studies to better understand the strengths and weaknesses of current models, providing insights for future model improvements. We maintain datasets atthis https URLand source codes atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Maestro: Agentic Formal Proof Development by Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25920)**  `arXiv:2610.25920`  `cs.LG`  
  _Haoyu Li, Zeren Shui, Yuhao Zhang, Ferhat Erata, Benjamin Mikek, Murali Krishna Ramanathan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Lean is increasingly used to verify mathematical proofs and software produced by LLM agents, but writing formal proofs remains hard. Existing prover systems take a divide-and-conquer approach that recursively decomposes hard proof obligations into easier lemmas and splits again any lemma that resists proof. However, a lemma can resist proof for different reasons, including insufficient guidance, a false statement, or a flaw in the decomposition that produced it. Existing systems cannot tell these apart as a failed attempt reports little beyond its failure, and responses are confined to the failing node without considering the rest of the proof or revising the plan above it. The system may thus keep decomposing obligations along a flawed proof direction until the budget runs out, causing the whole proof attempt to fail. We introduce Maestro, a Lean prover harness that handles failures through global coordination. Provers report why they failed, not just that they failed, and a coordinator that sees the whole proof chooses an appropriate response, such as guiding a stuck prover, correcting a false lemma, or retracting a flawed subtree and replanning it. The same harness extends to verified code synthesis, where a checked counterexample sends the code back for repair. We evaluate Maestro with four frontier LLMs on seven mathematics and three software verification benchmarks. It proves more targets than each model's native coding agent (Claude Code or Codex) on every benchmark the agent has not already saturated, with the largest gains on research-level mathematics. With Claude Opus 5, it discovers its own proofs of 34 of the 48 theorems in the June 2026 release of arXivLean, against 19 for the native agent. It also outperforms all published prover systems on every benchmark they share, solving 669/672 PutnamBench, 59/60 Lean-IMO-Bench, and 6/6 IMO 2026.
  </details>

- **[An Analysis of Semantic and Spatio-Temporal Co-evolution for Edge Classification on Dynamic Text-Attributed Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25906)**  `arXiv:2610.25906`  `cs.LG`  
  _Tyler Bonnet, Marek Rei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dynamic edge classification on text-attributed Web graphs, such as review networks, community forums, and news-derived knowledge graphs, requires high quality, adaptive semantic representations in the context of network evolution. Existing dynamic graph architectures typically initialize text feature vectors with pretrained language models and hold them static during training to preserve their semantic structure, forgoing the potential benefits of allowing them to co-evolve with network topology. In this work, we investigate the effects of enabling such co-evolution with a unified text-graph alignment framework that integrates a lightweight Transformer adapter to update token-level text representations via spatio-temporal gradients from a contrastive pretraining objective. To prevent detrimental semantic distortion, a language model maintenance objective anchors the evolving text embeddings to their original semantic structure. Evaluations on the Dynamic Text-Attributed Graph Benchmark demonstrate that this anchored text-graph co-evolution consistently improves upon the edge classification performance of frozen-text baselines across diverse Web-related domains. Representational analyses reveal that the framework reduces anisotropy, improving the linear separability of text embeddings, and effectively encodes structural and temporal information into the semantic space. A computational efficiency analysis confirms that this beneficial semantic and spatio-temporal co-evolution is achieved without incurring the prohibitive computational overhead typical of end-to-end joint training.
  </details>

- **[Asynchronous On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25880)**  `arXiv:2610.25880`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xidong Wu, Qi Qi, Yuqiong Ji, Shangqian Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unlike on-policy distillation (OPD), which depends on a separate teacher model, on-policy self-distillation (OPSD) leverages a single language model acting simultaneously as the student and the teacher, with the teacher conditioned on privileged information. Despite this architectural simplicity, synchronous rollout generation dominates overall training time, creating a severe systems bottleneck when scaling OPSD. To address this, we introduce Asynchronous On-Policy Self-Distillation (AsyncOPSD), a framework that decouples rollout generation from learner updates to improve training throughput. Since the teacher and student share parameters, AsyncOPSD eliminates the multi-model coordination overhead inherent to OPD architectures in asynchronous training. As the student policy evolves, buffered trajectories generated by older policies become stale, potentially degrading performance. To mitigate this, we introduce a distillation objective based on staleness-aware policy control and token-level entropy weighting. Across three model sizes and mathematical reasoning benchmarks, AsyncOPSD improves the training throughput by $1.96$--$2.25\times$ over synchronous OPSD while increasing average benchmark accuracy.
  </details>

- **[ACE-Trader: Adversarial Co-Evolution for Generator-Critic Trading Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25847)**  `arXiv:2610.25847`  `cs.LG`  
  _Chang Zhou, Jiarun Zhou, Xingtong Yu, Hong Cheng, Hui Zhang, Xinming Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generator--critic trading systems pair an LLM-based trader that makes trading decisions with a red team agent that evaluates these decisions and provides feedback for revision. However, improving individual trading decisions does not ensure that both agents learn from experience and improve together over time. This raises an important question: how can the trader and red-Team select useful experience and learn from each other's evolving behavior? On one hand, experience that benefits decisions under one market condition may become ineffective or harmful under another. On the other hand, trading outcomes reflect both agents' actions, making it difficult to derive appropriate experience for each role. In this work, we propose ACE-Trader, an adversarial co-evolution trading framework. Specifically, we first develop separate context-dependent memory selectors that learn to identify useful experience for the current trading decision. Both selectors receive shared feedback from net-return differences between complete memory-assisted and memory-free trading pipelines, with online updates emphasizing recent outcomes. We further design a co-evolution mechanism that links role-specific memories through shared trading records. We use these records to evaluate the Red-Team's risk assessments and the effects of the Trader's revisions against realized market outcomes, then derive experience for their respective memories. By linking this experience to the corresponding records, we enable the Trader to improve its proposals based on prior critiques and the Red-Team to adjust its review focus as the Trader's behavior changes. Experiments on U.S. stock trading show that ACE-Trader outperforms all 22 active baselines in cumulative return and achieves the highest Sharpe ratio and lowest maximum drawdown across both active methods and passive benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Bayesian Model Discovery with Language Models: Closing the Loop between Hypotheses and Experiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25747)**  `arXiv:2610.25747`  `cs.LG`  
  _JooKyung Yuh, Byungmin Park, Eunseo Choi, Jinheon Baek, Junmo Cho, Kyu Hyun Choi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific model discovery requires proposing candidate models of an unknown system and designing experiments that discriminate among them. However, LLM-based approaches typically rely on in-context learning to predict outcomes and select experiments, lacking explicit model evaluation to systematically guide hypothesis search, while Bayesian approaches enable principled model comparison but are confined to a predefined candidate set which results in limited search spaces. To this end, we introduce BMD, which closes the loop between LLM-driven hypothesis generation and Bayesian experimental design. Specifically, an LLM proposes model families, while a numerical engine fits their parameters, weighs them by model evidence, and averages their predictions. Then, BMD chooses the next experimental conditions by maximizing the joint expected information gain over families and their parameters, while feeding the updated beliefs and residual diagnostics back to the LLM to guide its next proposals. Across four scientific discovery benchmarks, BMD achieves lowest final prediction error than in-context learning and LLM-based scientific agents while recovering exact functional forms more often, with ablations supporting numerical feedback and the joint objective.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Couple: Context-Adaptive Joint Denoising for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25723)**  `arXiv:2610.25723`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zilei Shao, Ian Li, Heng Zhao, Kewei Lian, Guy Van den Broeck, Zhe Zeng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models accelerate generation by predicting multiple tokens in parallel, but factorized denoisers treat simultaneous predictions as conditionally independent. Decoding heuristics reduce the resulting errors by controlling which tokens are committed, but leave the underlying distribution factorized. Joint modeling addresses the source of the problem, yet existing methods attach separately learned layers to frozen denoisers, leaving the backbone unadapted to the joint objective and the coupling parameters fixed across contexts. We propose Codpula, a context-parameterized denoiser combining a Transformer with a tractable probabilistic inference layer. Given a partially masked input, the Transformer produces token potentials that shape per-position predictions and compact gates that modulate the layer's coupling parameters, yielding a context-dependent joint distribution while retaining exact conditional inference. We jointly optimize both components under a conditional denoising objective using a dual-flow multiplicative update that preserves valid probabilistic parameters. At inference, Codpula supports standard heuristics and uses conditional queries to adapt commitments to dependencies among proposed tokens without extra Transformer evaluations within a step. Codpula sets new state-of-the-art results across a broad suite of reasoning benchmarks while advancing the accuracy-latency Pareto frontier in mathematical reasoning and code generation.
  </details>

- **[Evidence Sufficiency and Model Comparisons in MexRegQA: An Item-Level Audit of Mexican-Spanish Regulatory QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25710)**  `arXiv:2610.25710`  `cs.LG`  
  _Fred Hu, Yeqi Wang, Xing Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Similarity-gated coverage does not establish that retrieved evidence contains the facts needed to answer a question. We audit Mexican-Spanish financial-regulatory QA and find that 35 of 135 covered items lack some or all load-bearing facts. On the post-hoc full-evidence slice (n=98), eleven prompt-matched models score 95-98/98 and none differs significantly from the anchor; this does not establish equivalence. Removing evidence-defective items changes the nominal significance status of 14 of 55 pairwise comparisons. On 56 zero-evidence items, accuracy spans 33.9%-89.3%. Removing evidence from the audited items under an abstention-permitting prompt reduces accuracy by 63-81 percentage points in all ten tested legs, with no recovered items. Building on sufficient-context research, we contribute MexRegQA v1.0: 192 items with provenance and evidence-status labels, frozen retrieval evidence for 191 evaluated items, predictions, judge outputs and offline checks. The audit was outcome-aware, the later model/panel sweep reused the same questions, and all reported scores and labels rely on machine evaluation without independent human validation. We propose releasing evidence-status labels alongside grounded QA benchmarks so that model comparisons can be examined separately from evidence availability.
  </details>

- **[Predictive Bandit Curriculum Reinforcement Learning via Gradient-Based Sample Group Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25704)**  `arXiv:2610.25704`  `cs.LG`  
  _Liang Xu, Shufan Shen, Zhongni Hou, Chao Bi, Yufei Zhang, Wei Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Curriculum learning, which organizes training samples according to their learning priority, has been widely adopted in reinforcement learning to improve training efficiency. However, existing methods commonly construct curriculum units using predefined data properties and estimate their utility by simply aggregating a single proxy signal from recent trainingthis http URLdesigns may group samples with different optimization effects and fail to anticipate how their utility evolves in relation to the policy. We propose Predictive Gradient-based Bandit (PGB) Curriculum, which first discovers optimization-aware learning units by clustering projected sample-level gradients. PGB then employs a lightweight temporal predictor to forecast the future utility of each unit and converts the predictions into a Boltzmann sampling distribution for adaptive data allocation. Experiments across multiple model scales and reasoning benchmarks show that PGB consistently outperforms standard GRPO, difficulty-based curricula, and history-based scheduling strategies, demonstrating improved data utilization and training efficiency.
  </details>

- **[LOOP: Recovery-Aligned Iterative Layer Pruning via On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25679)**  `arXiv:2610.25679`  `cs.LG`  
  _Liang Xu, Shufan Shen, Zhongni Hou, Yufei Zhang, Wei Lin, Guojun Yin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Layer-level pruning is attractive for large language model (LLM) deployment as it reduces the model size while preserving a dense architecture that can be efficiently executed on standard hardware. Current methods typically follow a one-shot paradigm, which first removes selected layers to reach a target model size and then performs additional training to recover model capabilities. However, pruning directly to the target size can substantially degrade reasoning capabilities, making subsequent recovery more difficult under a limited training budget. Moreover, this paradigm separates layer selection from recovery, limiting the use of recovery information to guide selection and potentially hindering the recovery of reasoning capabilities. To address these limitations, we propose LOOP, a Layer-level Recovery-Oriented On-Policy Pruning framework that interleaves pruning and recovery across multiple shots. In each shot, LOOP removes a single layer and performs short on-policy distillation, mitigating the abrupt degradation in reasoning capabilities associated with one-shot pruning. To guide layer selection in subsequent shots, LOOP reuses on-policy trajectories and optimization signals from recovery to jointly assess each remaining layer's reasoning contribution and recovery sensitivity, thereby making layer selection recovery-aware. Evaluations on several benchmarks show that LOOP consistently outperforms one-shot baselines in average accuracy across different model scales and pruning ratios.
  </details>

- **[Adversarial Post-Training of Foundation Models for High-Frequency Trading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25604)**  `arXiv:2610.25604`  `cs.LG`  
  _Alexandre Bismuth, Kang Li, Aramis Marti-Shahandeh, Valentin Mohl, Jakob Foerster, Anisoara Calinescu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While autoregressive foundation models trained on limit order book (LOB) data show state-of-the-art performance for market simulation, pre-training with cross-entropy neither penalizes the compounding of sampling errors over free-running rollouts nor targets desired market properties. We introduce a post-training method for LOB foundation models using a Wasserstein generative adversarial network (GAN) objective. A critic, trained online on rollouts through the true, non-differentiable matching engine, guides updates from evolution strategies over rank-4 LoRA perturbations of the pre-trained generator, under a KL trust region. For efficiency, we use EGGROLL (Evolution Guided GeneRal Optimisation via Low-rank Learning), a memory-efficient variant of evolution strategies. On a held-out panel, adversarial post-training improves LOB-Bench realism by 17.6% (WS-21) and 17.0% (Mean-$L_1$), while selection-matched random-perturbation and compute-matched continued-pre-training controls yield no improvement. Post-training is thus a necessary stage that more pre-training does not replace. Per-position divergence measurements show that it slows error accumulation over 500-message rollouts from $3.1\times$ to $2.3\times$. Adversarial post-training is also inexpensive, requiring under 50M training tokens and 12 GPU-hours per seed, three orders of magnitude below the 48B-token pre-training budget. Ablations over critic inputs, optimizers, and the population-rollout trade-off, together with an analysis of reward hacking, yield practical design guidelines for adversarial post-training.
  </details>

- **[CVTA: Cross-Variable Temporal Attention for Multivariate Irregular Time Series Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25584)**  `arXiv:2610.25584`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ankitkumar Joshi, Milos Hauskrecht_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Irregularly sampled multivariate time series arise in many real-world domains such as healthcare, climate science, and large-scale monitoring systems. Characterized by asynchronous observations and heterogeneity across variables, such time series pose significant challenges for forecasting. In this work, we introduce Cross-Variable Temporal Attention (CVTA), a short-term, continuous-time forecasting framework that learns prediction functions from its compact, interpretable state summarizing recent temporal dynamics across variables. CVTA uses Variable-Conditioned Embeddings to project heterogeneous variables into variable-specific latent spaces, and applies temporal attention to capture cross-variable dependencies from this state. Furthermore, we demonstrate that CVTA can be integrated as an auxiliary module into existing state-of-the-art prediction models through a dynamic meta-decision model. Extensive experiments on multiple real-world benchmarks show that CVTA achieves strong short-term predictive accuracy, supports efficient online inference, improves existing prediction models when used as an auxiliary module, and learns attention patterns that align well with domain knowledge.
  </details>

- **[FTFM: Factorized Tabular Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25558)**  `arXiv:2610.25558`  `cs.LG`  
  _Josif Grabocka_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tabular foundation models (TFMs) are becoming a powerful alternative to conventional tabular learning, adapting to new datasets in context without task-specific training. Current architectures, however, face a trade-off: models that preserve cell-level representations are expensive because they repeatedly attend across rows and columns, while faster models collapse each row into a fixed embedding and lose this fine-grained state. We introduce Factorized Tabular Foundation Models (FTFM), which preserve cell representations while factorizing their computation through one contextual latent per row and one per column. Cells no longer attend to one another directly; instead, each cell is updated through the interaction of its row and column latents, reducing repeated attention while retaining feature-specific information. FTFM achieves competitive predictive performance in tabular classification on major benchmarks such as TabArena and BeyondArena.
  </details>

- **[KPI-Aware Multimodal Anomaly Detection for 5G and Open RAN Telemetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25510)**  `arXiv:2610.25510`  `cs.LG`  
  _Chiman Salavati, Liang Wu, Kelly Wan, Mayank Darbari, Liangjie Hong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable anomaly detection is a deployment bottleneck for 5G/Open RAN observability and emerging self-optimizing 6G systems, where hundreds of heterogeneous KPIs, severe class imbalance, and strict alert budgets make purely numerical scores brittle. We present KAC, a deployment-oriented KPI-aware multimodal anomaly-detection framework that uses KPI-level residual-text alignment to turn compact operator-style summaries into a learnable feedback signal over full-KPI temporal evidence. This gives the detector broad numerical coverage like modern time-series models, while retaining the sparse KPI-specific reasoning that makes operator feedback sustainable in a future operator-facing workflow. Across public telecom telemetry, enterprise-scale Open RAN observability data, and a Nokia benchmark built from real 5G RAN production packet traces with realism-hardened injected anomalies, KAC achieves strong threshold-based detection performance while remaining competitive on threshold-free ranking metrics. On TelecomTS, at a fixed 2.5 percent false-positive-rate operating point, KAC achieves the highest recall among the evaluated residual-based methods while maintaining comparable precision; across the evaluated benchmarks, it remains robust under substantial class imbalance. KAC is integrated in pre-production shadow mode with Nokia's GKE-based KPI construction pipeline, where it continuously scores telemetry windows alongside an internal packet-level anomaly detector for operational evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Consistent LLM Call Handling Across Java Frameworks: A Reference Implementation and Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25480)**  `arXiv:2610.25480`  `cs.LG`  
  _Oleksandr Klymenko_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Java applications reach large language models (LLMs) through an integration framework, and rely on it to handle a failing provider: to retry what can be retried and nothing else, to wait as long as the provider asks, never to present a truncated answer as complete, and to record each failure in the application's metrics. We state these expectations as a six-clause reliability contract and measure Spring AI 2.0.1 and LangChain4j 1.21.0 against it with a scripted OpenAI-compatible provider: fifteen failure scenarios, synchronous and streaming, three repetitions. LangChain4j meets it in 47 of 77 applicable cases with its defaults and in 51 of 83 when configured explicitly, Spring AI in 72 of 77 and 76 of 83; both deliver a stream that ended without its terminator as a complete answer. We then place one implementation of the contract beneath both frameworks, at the HTTP boundary - an OkHttp interceptor for Spring AI, an HttpClient for LangChain4j, and athis http URLtransport for code without a framework - sharing a 725-line core that depends only on the JDK and the Micrometer Observation API. With it, all three clients behave identically and meet the contract in 90 of 91 cases; the remaining case is a policy trade-off between retrying a hung provider and waiting for a slow first token. Against a local model server the layer rejected none of 700 calls, and it adds a median of 32-217 microseconds to a call.
  </details>

- **[Mining the Self-Rewriting System: A Vision for Data Mining on AI-Native Infrastructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25374)**  `arXiv:2610.25374`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mayank Darbari, Chiman Salavati, Liang Wu, Dawei Wang, Jiong Zhang, Kelly Wan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Classical data mining treats data as generated outside the learner. Emerging AI-native infrastructure invalidates this assumption: as agents observe system state, act, update retrieval stores and models, and promote new artifacts into the serving path, their own decisions reshape the future logs, traces, corpora, and evaluation records used for later training and release. We call this new research object a self-rewriting system and the resulting system-induced distribution shift endogenous drift. Our central, falsifiable claim is that cross-surface causes are not identifiable in general from marginal per-surface monitoring: different coupled failures can produce the same individual signals while differing in their joint behavior, requiring joint analysis for reliable attribution. This agenda comprises four foundational research problems: fusing heterogeneous drift signals into calibrated gating and attribution (C1); mining agent traces whose activity alphabet evolves (C2); maintaining knowledge fabrics through online reindexing, faithfulness, and compression decisions (C3); and safely promoting concurrent learning streams through one shared evaluation gate (C4). These remain fragmented across concept drift, performative prediction, process mining, streaming retrieval-augmented generation, and MLOps, without a unified data-mining agenda. We outline public benchmarks and evaluation criteria, using AI-native telecom only as a motivating exemplar with analogues in finance, software CI/CD, and clinical decision support.
  </details>

- **[Operator-Mismatch Contrastive Learning for World-Relative Trajectory Coherence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25360)**  `arXiv:2610.25360`  `cs.LG`  
  _One Octadion, Firmansyah Sundana, Novanto Yudistira_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  World models are increasingly judged by how well they rank plausible futures, yet plausibility alone is the wrong target. The same scene can evolve under many latent rules, and a future that is smooth and physically valid under one rule can be incoherent under the rule actually in force. We formalize the resulting failure, operator-blindness, and show that it is systematically hidden by standard evaluation: a scorer can attain near-perfect generic ranking while separating current-world from other-world futures no better than chance. We introduce Operator-Mismatch Contrastive (OMC) learning, whose semantic hard negatives are valid-but-wrong-operator trajectories, and a matching protocol that measures world-relative coherence directly. On a controlled synthetic benchmark with in- and out-of-distribution operator parameters, across two trajectory-scoring backbones, and on feature-level external validation with CoPhy ballsCF, OMC raises operator-sensitive scoring far above a chance baseline that nonetheless achieves 0.99 generic top-1. We prove that the wrong-operator negative is necessary both for the protocol to detect the failure and for the objective to fix it, and position OMC as an auxiliary coherence signal (a trajectory-coherence scorer, not a generative model, planner, or control agent) that can be attached to existing world-model representations.
  </details>

- **[Memory as Belief: Bayesian Evolution of Memory for Self-Evolving LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25119)**  `arXiv:2610.25119`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yiming Xu, Qian Yang, Zihan Chen, Yuefei Wu, Wenhan Zeng, Bin Shi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on external memory to accumulate past experiences for continual adaptation. However, effectively reusing memory requires accurately assessing its value, as retrieved experiences may become unreliable when transferred across task contexts. Despite its importance, memory valuation remains largely underexplored, with existing methods relying on historical retrieval success rates that overlook the context dependency of memory usefulness and the varying reliability of estimates under different amounts of evidence. Inspired by the Bayesian brain hypothesis, we propose BEAM (Bayesian-style Evolutionary Agentic Memory), a framework that reformulates memory value evaluation as a parameterized cognitive inference process, where memory value is represented as a context-conditioned dynamic belief rather than a passive historical statistic. Specifically, BEAM infers memory value beliefs from current task contexts and continuously calibrates them using execution feedback as empirical evidence, transforming memory from passive storage into an adaptive cognitive mechanism for continual self-evolution. Extensive experiments across reasoning and code generation benchmarks demonstrate that BEAM consistently improves memory utilization and enables continual self-evolution of LLM agents.
  </details>

- **[Conformal Prediction-Enabled Sequence-level Policy Optimization for Reasoning Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25092)**  `arXiv:2610.25092`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hui Yang, Wan Tian, Tao Ren, Zhongyi Li, Yijie Peng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning improves the reasoning capability of large language models through verifiable rewards for mathematical and coding tasks. In token-level methods such as GRPO, clipping acts on individual token ratios while the reward and advantage apply to a complete response. Recent sequence-level methods align these units but still use fixed clipping bands, whose tail probabilities change as the score distribution evolves. We propose Conformal Prediction-Enabled Sequence-level Policy Optimization (CSPO), which adaptively calibrates sequence-level clipping bounds using recent training statistics. The resulting band targets a specified marginal tail budget and adapts to policy drift; calibration conditional on response length requires additional assumptions. We bound the probability that a fresh score falls outside the band by the target miscoverage level plus explicit CDF discrepancy and drift terms. Under additional moment conditions, this bound also controls the objective and gradient error relative to the unclipped GSPO power surrogate. Experiments with the Qwen3-1.7B series show improvements over GSPO on difficult mathematical reasoning benchmarks and ALFWorld, with clipping endpoints that adapt during training.
  </details>

- **[MLFont: Machine Learning-Driven Font Subsetting and Range-Compact Unicode-Range Slicing for Low-Latency Web Typography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24937)**  `arXiv:2610.24937`  `cs.LG`  
  _Quan Wang, Yiran Mao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  CSS3 @font-face rules with unicode-range descriptors partition web fonts into downloadable slices. While Latin fonts need ~20 KB in WOFF2, Pan-European (LGC) and East Asian (CJK) fonts contain 2,297-30,759 codepoints (158 KB-5.9 MB in WOFF2), where heuristic partitioning causes unused-glyph downloads, high HTTP RTT, CSS bloat, or missing-glyph fallbacks. We present MLFont, a machine learning and optimization framework that learns font slicing strategies from corpora via three stages: (1) a Zipfian High-Frequency Tier (most) isolating top-frequency codepoints; (2) a Latent Co-Occurrence Topic Tier using Online Variational Bayes LDA (Online LDA) or a Deep Neural Network Topic Model (DNNTM) with Shannon Entropy Difference Loss and Sinkhorn-Knopp normalization; and (3) a Memetic Range-Compact Genetic Algorithm (GA) with Potts-Viterbi dynamic programming and island bridging that minimizes topic discrepancy and CSS fragmentation. Across five Google Noto fonts (LGC, KR, JP, TC, SC), MLFont cuts 3G per-glyph latency by 56.7%-75.3% over single-file delivery and 46.1%-66.3% over equal-bin baselines on CJK with 100% coverage, reduces CSS intervals by 17.9%-41.7%, and achieves 1.56x-6.09x cold-cache speedups (65.4%-84.9% CJK and 44.7% LGC bandwidth savings) across 20 websites in simulator and Headless Chrome benchmarks (45/45 exact slice-set parity).Code:this https URLModels:this https URL
  </details>

- **[fxBench: Evaluating and Understanding Formula Suggestions in Spreadsheets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24729)**  `arXiv:2610.24729`  `cs.LG`  
  _Sanket Mhatre, Sumit Gulwani, Vu Le, Yasharth Bajpai, Gust Verbruggen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spreadsheets are among the most widely used tools for computations, and formulas are their core building blocks. Besides agentic spreadsheet tools, proactive formula suggestions enable significant productivity gains and are now available in popular spreadsheet software (Microsoft Excel and Google Sheets). Such suggestions pose interesting challenges: the right context must be collected and represented for a machine learning model, which must then be able to write a correct formula, in a way that is fast and cheap enough to trigger on every = (or subsequent keystrokes for auto-completion) without taking users out of their editing flow. Surprisingly, there is no public benchmark for formula suggestions on real spreadsheets. In this paper, we therefore (1) introduce fxBench as a manually curated benchmark of 503 formula suggestion tasks based on Sheetpedia, (2) break down the formula suggestion problem into four capabilities (gather context, understand context, understand intent, write formula) that a formula suggestion system requires, and (3) evaluate existing and new approaches to understand bottlenecks in these capabilities that pose interesting research directions. Specifically, we annotate each benchmark with the cells that describe intent to determine the theoretical performance limit of a given context selection strategy, we evaluate performance of small and large models on different representations of context, including a dense format with high theoretical coverage at low token counts, and we fine-tune smaller language models to show that we can learn to understand such dense formats. Additionally, we perform quantitative and qualitative analysis on failure modes to understand bottlenecks in the formula writing capability; the best configuration only achieved 58.8% correct suggestions.
  </details>

- **[EdgeKernelBench: Unified LLM-Driven Kernel Generation for Edge Backends](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24648)**  `arXiv:2610.24648`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuhua Zheng, Xueyi Chen, Xinze Zou, Xin Li, Haoji Hu, Huan Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent works show that LLMs can generate kernels with strong performance on server GPUs. Edge deployment presents distinct challenges because the hardware is heterogeneous, the software stacks are fragmented, and the resource budgets are tight. LLM kernel generation across diverse edge backends has yet to be studied systematically. We introduce EdgeKernelBench, which combines a unified IR deployment framework with a hierarchical benchmark for edge workloads. The unified IR captures reusable computation, scheduling, and memory decisions and connects to each target backend through a compiler interface. The benchmark spans progressively larger program scopes, from individual operators to compact model graphs, across representative edge application domains. We instantiate the design with TVM as a reference compiler and validate it across heterogeneous edge backends. With a single generation attempt, the best model achieves 64.5% Correct@1 but only 25.5% Fast@1.0. This gap shows that functional correctness does not consistently translate into performance gains. We therefore introduce the Layered Edge Kernel Agent (LEKA), which organizes optimization through a layered IR. On the agent evaluation subset, LEKA achieves 43.5% Fast@1.0, compared with 13.0% for direct generation and 26.1% for Claude Code. LEKA and Claude Code use the same base model and optimization budget. Together, EdgeKernelBench and LEKA support unified evaluation and layered optimization of kernels generated by LLMs across edge backends.
  </details>

- **[TopoCompile: Molecular Generation with Exact Global Count Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24511)**  `arXiv:2610.24511`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xiang Wu, Rong-Hua Li, Quanqing Xu, Kangfei Zhao, Guoren Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Molecular generation designs novel molecules with desired structures and properties. Global count constraints specify molecular size, elemental composition, and ring content without prescribing detailed structure. Property-conditioned generators steer generation toward such targets but do not guarantee exact satisfaction. We introduce TopoCompile, which combines an autoregressive component-tree generator with a dynamic-programming compiler for supported component-additive integer counts. TopoCompile represents molecules as trees of intact ring systems and individual atoms, making each supported count additive over components.
