  Streaming recurrent models enable efficient 3D reconstruction by maintaining persistent state representations. However, they suffer from catastrophic forgetting over long sequences due to balancing historical information with new observations. Recent methods alleviate this by deriving adaptive signals from the attention perspective, but they operate on single dimensions without considering temporal and spatial consistency. To this end, we propose a training-free framework termed TTSA3R that leverages both temporal state evolution and spatial observation quality for adaptive state updates in 3D reconstruction. In particular, we devise a Temporal Adaptive Update Module that regulates update magnitude by analyzing temporal state evolution patterns. Then, a Spatial Context Update Module is introduced to localize spatial regions that require updates through observation-state alignment and observation changes. These complementary signals are finally fused to determine the state updating strategies. Extensive experiments show that TTSA3R achieves competitive performance on standard short-sequence benchmarks and provides substantially stronger robustness on extended sequences. On NRGBD, as sequences extend from 50 to 250 frames, TTSA3R exhibits only a 1.33$\times$ error increase, compared with 3.8$\times$ degradation for CUT3R. This highlights the practical value of temporal-spatial adaptive updates for long-term reconstruction stability. Our code is available at \url{this https URL}.
  </details>

- **[Semantic-Guided Dynamic Sparsification for Pre-Trained Model-based Class-Incremental Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.21345)**  `arXiv:2601.21345`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ruiqi Liu, Boyu Diao, Zijia An, Runjie Shao, Feixiang Ge, Yongjun Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Class-Incremental Learning (CIL) requires a model to continually learn new classes without forgetting old ones. A common and efficient solution freezes a pre-trained model and employs lightweight adapters, whose parameters are often forced to be orthogonal to prevent inter-task interference. However, we argue that this parameter-constraining method is detrimental to plasticity. To this end, we propose Semantic-Guided Dynamic Sparsification (SGDS), a novel method that proactively guides the activation space by governing the orientation and rank of its subspaces through targeted sparsification. Specifically, SGDS promotes knowledge transfer by encouraging similar classes to share a compact activation subspace, while simultaneously preventing interference by assigning non-overlapping activation subspaces to dissimilar classes. By sculpting class-specific sparse subspaces in the activation space, SGDS effectively mitigates interference without imposing rigid constraints on the parameter space. Extensive experiments on various benchmark datasets demonstrate the state-of-the-art performance of SGDS.
  </details>

- **[OmniEgoCap: Device-Agnostic Sequence-Level Egocentric Motion Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.19283)**  `arXiv:2512.19283`  `cs.CV`  
  _Kyungwon Cho, Jeonghyeon Na, Hanbyul Joo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Commercial egocentric devices capture human behavior in everyday settings, yet full-body motion reconstruction must generalize across diverse cameras and mountings. This is challenging because head trajectories under-determine body motion, while hand observations are intermittent and camera-dependent: a hand may disappear simply because it has left the camera's field of view. Existing methods treat such absences as missing data, relying on fixed visibility regimes or post-hoc optimization, and fail to generalize across devices. We present OmniEgoCap, a sequence-level diffusion framework for device-agnostic egocentric full-body motion reconstruction. Our key insight is that egocentric videos contain sequence-level evidence about both the wearer and the camera-induced visibility structure, enabling OmniEgoCap to infer consistent body shape and coherent motion from sparse head and hand cues. To prevent overfitting to a single camera setup, we further introduce geometry-aware visibility augmentation, which synthesizes visibility patterns from realistic variations in camera geometry. We also present OmniEgoDB, the first mocap benchmark that pairs ground-truth motion with recordings from multiple consumer egocentric devices. Experiments on synthetic, real-device, and in-the-wild settings demonstrate superior motion reconstruction, robust cross-device generalization, and coherent in-the-wild results.
  </details>

- **[Can MLLMs Find Their Way in a City? Exploring Emergent Navigation from Web-Scale Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.15933)**  `arXiv:2512.15933`  `cs.CV`  
  _Dwip Dalal, Utkarsh Mishra, Narendra Ahuja, Nebojsa Jojic_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Leveraging multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to develop embodied agents offers significant promise for addressing complex real-world tasks. However, current evaluation benchmarks remain predominantly language-centric or heavily reliant on simulated environments, rarely probing the nuanced, knowledge-intensive reasoning essential for practical, real-world scenarios. To bridge this critical gap, we introduce the task of Sparsely Grounded Visual Navigation, explicitly designed to evaluate the sequential decision-making abilities of MLLMs in challenging, knowledge-intensive real-world environment. We operationalize this task with CityNav, a comprehensive benchmark encompassing four diverse global cities, specifically constructed to assess raw MLLM-driven agents in city navigation. Agents are required to rely solely on visual inputs and internal multimodal reasoning to sequentially navigate 50+ decision points without additional environmental annotations or specialized architectural modifications. Crucially, agents must autonomously achieve localization through interpreting city-specific cues and recognizing landmarks, perform spatial reasoning, and strategically plan and execute routes to their destinations. Through extensive evaluations, we demonstrate that current state-of-the-art MLLMs, reasoning techniques (e.g., GEPA, chain-of-thought, reflection) and competitive baseline PReP significantly underperform in this challenging setting. To address this, we propose Verbalization of Path(VoP), which explicitly grounds the agent's internal reasoning by probing city-scale cognitive maps (key landmarks and directions toward the destination) from the MLLM, substantially enhancing navigation success. Project Webpage:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Text2Loc++: Generalizing 3D Point Cloud Localization from Natural Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.15308)**  `arXiv:2511.15308`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yan Xia, Letian Shi, Yilin Di, Joao F. Henriques, Daniel Cremers_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We tackle the problem of localizing 3D point cloud submaps using complex and diverse natural language descriptions, and present Text2Loc++, a novel neural network designed for effective cross-modal alignment between language and point clouds in a coarse-to-fine localization pipeline. To support benchmarking, we introduce a new city-scale dataset covering both color and non-color point clouds from diverse urban scenes, and organize location descriptions into three levels of linguistic complexity. In the global place recognition stage, Text2Loc++ combines a pretrained language model with a Hierarchical Transformer with Max Pooling (HTM) for sentence-level semantics, and employs an attention-based point cloud encoder for spatial understanding. We further propose Masked Instance Training (MIT) to filter out non-aligned objects and improve multimodal robustness. To enhance the embedding space, we introduce Modality-aware Contrastive Learning (MCL), incorporating cross-modal, submap-, text-, and instance-level losses. In the fine localization stage, we completely remove explicit text-instance matching and design a lightweight yet powerful framework based on Prototype-based Map Cloning (PMC) and a Cascaded Cross-Attention Transformer (CCAT). Extensive experiments on the KITTI360Pose dataset show that Text2Loc++ outperforms existing methods by up to 15%. In addition, the proposed model exhibits robust generalization when evaluated on the new dataset, effectively handling complex linguistic expressions and a wide variety of urban environments.
  </details>

- **[Joint Multi-Condition Representation Modelling via Matrix Factorisation for Visual Place Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.17739)**  `arXiv:2510.17739`  `cs.CV`  
  _Timur Ismagilov, Shakaiba Majeed, Michael Milford, Tan Viet Tuyen Nguyen, Sarvapali D. Ramchurn, Shoaib Ehsan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We address multi-reference visual place recognition (VPR), where reference sets captured under varying conditions are used to improve localization performance. While deep learning with large-scale training improves robustness, increasing data diversity and model complexity incur extensive computational cost during training and deployment. Descriptor-level fusion via voting or aggregation avoids training, but often targets multi-sensor setups or relies on heuristics with limited gains under appearance and viewpoint change. We propose a training-free, descriptor-agnostic approach that jointly models places using multiple reference descriptors via matrix decomposition into basis representations, enabling projection-based residual matching. We also introduce SotonMV, a benchmark for multi-viewpoint VPR captured over time. Our method improves Recall@1 by up to $\approx$18% over conventional single-reference set matching on appearance-change datasets, and matches or outperforms existing multi-reference baselines across most appearance-change settings. For viewpoint-change datasets, our method supports matching when viewpoint is distributed among the references, with the benefit depending on reference set coverage of the query conditions. On unconstrained reference sets, we additionally observe gains of $\approx$5%, demonstrating strong generalization while remaining computationally lightweight.
  </details>

- **[Shape Distribution Matters: Shape-Specific Mixture-of-Experts for Amodal Segmentation Under Diverse Occlusions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.01664)**  `arXiv:2508.01664`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhixuan Li, Yujia Liu, Chen Hui, Jeonghaeng Lee, Sanghoon Lee, Weisi Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Amodal segmentation aims to predict complete object masks covering both visible and occluded regions, serving as a critical foundation for robust multimedia scene understanding and interactive media editing. This task poses significant challenges in complex visual media due to severe occlusions and extreme shape variation, ranging from rigid furniture to highly deformable clothing. Existing one-size-fits-all approaches struggle to reason about diverse amodal shapes in multimedia content due to limited representation capacity. While a mixture-of-experts framework is a natural solution, naively applying it without considering the underlying shape distribution leads to mismatched routing and redundant experts. To address these issues, we introduce ShapeMoE, a shape-specific sparse mixture-of-experts framework designed for complex visual scene parsing. The key idea is to learn a latent shape distribution space and dynamically route each object to a lightweight expert tailored to its geometric characteristics. Specifically, ShapeMoE encodes each object into a compact Gaussian embedding that captures key shape features. A Shape-Aware Sparse Router then maps the object to the most suitable expert, enabling precise shape-aware routing. Each expert is lightweight and specializes in recovering occluded regions for specific shape patterns. ShapeMoE is designed for shape-specific expert specialization while maintaining computational efficiency. Extensive experiments on three challenging benchmarks demonstrate that ShapeMoE consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods. Beyond advancing fundamental visual reasoning, ShapeMoE provides a robust and efficient visual prior for downstream multimedia applications, particularly in occlusion-heavy immersive environments.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Reason Efficiently with Interventional Step Credit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25876)**  `arXiv:2610.25876`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Guanda Li, Xuefeng Bai, Yulong Chen, Zhiyong Wang, Kehai Chen, Min Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large reasoning models often produce lengthy chain-of-thought (CoT) traces mixing useful intermediate steps with unnecessary ones. Recent methods explore step-level signals such as attention mass and step entropy to identify and suppress unnecessary steps. Despite their effectiveness, such signals reflect the model's behavior at a step rather than that step's contribution to the answer. To address this, we propose the interventional step credit (ISC), which takes a causal-intervention view and assesses step importance to the reasoning through targeted parameter-level interventions. Building on ISC, we further propose IGPO, which uses ISC to guide fine-grained advantage rescaling in reinforcement learning, reducing redundant steps while preserving the important ones. Experiments across two model scales and nine benchmarks show that IGPO achieves up to 62% average token reduction and better overall performance among compared methods.
  </details>

- **[RARE: Reinforcement Learning with Adaptive Rubric Evolution for Open-Ended Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25434)**  `arXiv:2610.25434`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Xueyun Tian, Bingbing Xu, Chuyue Li, Jiaming Liu, Chao Zhang, Weijia Feng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Verifiable rewards have advanced reinforcement learning in mathematics and code. However, many valuable open-ended tasks share three properties: no automatic verifier of overall quality, multiple requirements to satisfy jointly, and multiple valid responses to each query. Training therefore needs feedback on requirement satisfaction across different valid responses. Rubric-based reinforcement learning provides this feedback by aggregating scores from explicit criteria. As learning progresses unevenly, some criteria become fully satisfied and lose discrimination while others remain informative. Dynamic methods revise shared rubrics or select evolving criteria to restore discrimination, but may also change useful criteria. To address this, we propose Reinforcement Learning with Adaptive Rubric Evolution (RARE). The core idea is to use current policy feedback to guide both which criteria change and what their replacements assess. Sample-level updates replace criteria fully satisfied across current outputs with checks of remaining quality differences, retaining all other criteria and scores. Within this rule, we compare rewarding desirable qualities with penalizing observed flaws. Video script generation exemplifies these properties and supports content production. Its demands for coherent, executable narratives under constraints make it a challenging testbed. Targeting flaws performs best across three video types with a mean gain of 3.82 points over matched static rubrics. Model and human preferences support these gains. Improvements also extend to writing and general generation benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Measuring Data Diversity with Embeddings: A Taxonomy and Benchmark for Diversity Measures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24888)**  `arXiv:2610.24888`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Cantao Su, Anna Wegmann, Esther Ploeger, Dong Nguyen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diversity is a critical property of text corpora, with direct implications for training data curation, test set design, and model generalization. However, its measurement remains fragmented and poorly understood. Existing embedding-based diversity measures vary widely in how they are computed and can yield substantially different diversity scores on the same data, yet no systematic comparison of their empirical behaviour exists. We make two contributions toward addressing this gap. First, we present a taxonomy of 21 embedding-based diversity measures, organizing them into four families based on their underlying computations. Second, we introduce EmbDivBench, a benchmark that evaluates these measures along three conceptually distinct components (variety, balance and disparity) on both simulated and natural text data. Although Mean Pairwise Distance is the most commonly used measure, our experiments show that it is outperformed by several alternatives on the three diversity components, with Graph Entropy being the best choice overall. Lastly, we distil these findings into practical recommendations for diversity measure selection.
  </details>

- **[BanglaShield: A Script-Robust Safety Classifier and Benchmark for Bangla Native, Romanized, and Code-Mixed Prompts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24288)**  `arXiv:2610.24288`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Mohsinul Kabir, Md. Tariquzzaman, Mohammad Rashidujjaman Rifat, Tharindu Ranasinghe, Nishat Raihan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safety classifiers for large language models (LLMs) are typically built and evaluated on standard-script text, yet Bangla, like many lower-resource languages, is routinely written in native script, in Latin-script romanization, and code-mixed with English, Hindi, or Urdu. We introduce BanglaShield, a resource and model for graded prompt-safety classification across these written forms. We consolidate 10,245 Bangla prompts from six sources under a four-level taxonomy (Safe, Offensive, Harmful, Severe) and render each prompt in three aligned forms that share labels and a group-aware split, enabling strictly paired comparisons. Benchmarking six contemporary LLMs, including a Bangla-specialized one, on 36,882 classification queries and 24,318 judged responses, we find that LLM classifiers label 47.0% of unsafe native-script prompts as Safe, rising to 63.4% under romanization, while response-level safety accuracy drops by 10.3 points. Three multilingual guard models also pass 30.3-57.3% of unsafe native-script prompts. Using a model's own classification as an input filter reduces harmful compliance by only about five points. In contrast, BanglaShield, a compact fine-tuned encoder, reaches 87.8 macro-F1 on native-script and 84.4 on romanized prompts, outperforming the best LLM on each form by 42.8-46.1 points with ~130x fewer parameters than Gemma 4 31B. We release the corpus, splits, judgments, and model.
  </details>

- **[Preserving Reasoning Diversity in Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards via Adaptive Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24236)**  `arXiv:2610.24236`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Wen-Zhi Li, Sainyam Galhotra, Claire Cardie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) improves the reasoning capabilities of large language models, but often reduces the diversity of their reasoning behaviors. We study this phenomenon from the perspective of finite-sample learning dynamics and show that limited on-policy rollouts can progressively suppress semantically distinct but equally rewarded reasoning modes. Once a mode becomes sufficiently under-sampled, standard on-policy RLVR rarely recovers it. Motivated by this observation, we propose TAR, a Targeted Adaptive Replay framework that preserves diverse correct reasoning behaviors already present in the base policy. TAR constructs a compact memory bank of diverse base-model solutions and selectively replays them through an adaptive mechanism that activates only when stored behaviors are being suppressed during RLVR. Across six mathematical reasoning benchmarks and two Qwen2.5 model scales, TAR consistently achieves a better balance between reasoning performance and diversity preservation than existing diversity-preserving post-training methods, while substantially reducing the forgetting of correct base-model solutions. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Do LMs Know What Variables Do? Interpreting Code Representations via Variable Roles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24118)**  `arXiv:2610.24118`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Célian Vasson, Daniil Ognev, Kentaro Inui, Benjamin Heinzerling_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Variable roles describe recurring behaviors such as accumulation, traversal, and retaining the best candidate encountered. We use Sajaniemi's independently defined taxonomy to study which role distinctions can be recovered from large language models, how sparse autoencoders (SAEs) retain those distinctions, and whether associated directions support control of generation. We introduce static annotation rules for all eleven roles and a corpus of 126,926 natural-code files across five languages. Across five model-SAE systems, residual probes outperform individual SAE features and tested lexical baselines, with modest losses after neutral target renaming. Combining SAE features closes much of the single-feature gap, but probes on SAE reconstructions are less sensitive to role-changing edits reviewed by LLMs. High paired ranking accuracy coexists with frequent false positives at fixed thresholds. During generation, interventions often change role annotations at the cost of correctness; changes accompanied by passing tests show no clear aggregate advantage over random controls. The benchmark separates role-related recoverability, SAE fidelity, and correctness-preserving control through matched prediction, source edits, and generation interventions.
  </details>

- **[Dissecting Training-Free Uncertainty Estimation in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22206)**  `arXiv:2609.22206`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Soroush Seifi, Vaggelis Dorovatas, Lin Li, Yarin Gal, Rahaf Aljundi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are increasingly deployed in safety-critical settings, yet estimating when their answers can be trusted remains underexplored. We systematically compare training-free verbalized, token-level and semantic uncertainty estimation methods on twelve MLLMs and five VQA benchmarks. We find that answer length decides which family wins: token-level entropy on short answers, verbalized methods on sentence-length responses, and semantic methods on long-form generation. Token-level entropy becomes far more informative when estimated at a higher temperature than the low ones used to generate answers, and asking a model to judge its own answer becomes more reliable with model size. Building on this, a length-aware recipe that additionally flags responses on which the model abstains outperforms every individual method on average at about a quarter of the cost of semantic methods, although no method attains the coverage required for safe deployment. These results provide practitioners with principled guidance for selecting estimators by answer length and model scale.
  </details>

- **[Machine Learning for Coding Retail Product Names to Consumer-Price Categories: A Rule-plus-Bag-of-Words Pipeline with Reliability-Weighted Human-in-the-Loop Labeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02004)**  `arXiv:2606.02004`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Vladimir Beskorovainyi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Price statistics increasingly draw on scanner, web-scraped and receipt data whose product names are short, noisy and carry no standard code, so they must be coded to consumption categories. We release a synthetic benchmark of six categories with character-level noise, which favours character features, and hard negatives, and compare seven models trained from scratch (four logistic regressions on word or character n-grams, an MLP, a CNN and an LSTM) and two pretrained encoders under one matched protocol of 30 category-seed cells, reporting wins, losses and ties rather than mean differences alone. A character n-gram logistic regression is the most accurate model in every category and wins every paired comparison against the other models trained from scratch; the CNN and LSTM, once trained to the same stopping rule, are not reliably worse than the word-level models but cost 212 and 258 times as much to train as unigram bag-of-words. A fine-tuned DistilBERT comes within half a point of the character model without passing it, at about 460 times its single-thread CPU inference cost. Character features reduce inference throughput 4.6-fold relative to unigram bag-of-words. A rule-based prefix-tree stage admits 75-86% of positive items, so in a cascade it bounds the recall of the whole pipeline. A Monte Carlo study of label aggregation with simulated annotators shows that an additive reliability weight barely improves on majority vote while Dawid-Skene aggregation recovers labels markedly better, and that this difference carries through to the character n-gram classifier. Sensitivity checks cover a planted shortcut, generator noise, training budget and annotator population. The benchmark is synthetic and English-language because the production data are under non-disclosure. Code, data and scripts are released; accuracies of models trained from scratch reproduce exactly, timings as ratios.
  </details>

- **[TempoMoE: Identifying and Steering Fast-Slow Thinking Experts for Efficient Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25999)**  `arXiv:2610.25999`  `cs.CL` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Xiyue Zhang, Keru Zhou, Ziyi Wang, Bing Hu, Li Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) reasoning models rely on long chain-of-thought (CoT) for complex tasks, yet often overthink easy inputs, increasing token cost and latency, and sometimes degrading accuracy. Existing methods optimize reasoning length during training or impose external control at inference, but leave the underlying reasoning mechanism a black box. We find that fast and slow thinking exhibit distinct expert-routing patterns: holding the input fixed and varying only the prompted reasoning tempo produces stable routing shifts. Suppressing slow-thinking experts shortens the reasoning chain, whereas suppressing fast-thinking experts lengthens it, providing causal evidence that these experts influence reasoning depth. Based on this finding, we propose TempoMoE, a training-free, two-stage framework. It identifies fast- and slow-thinking experts via tempo-contrastive routing analysis and redistributes routing weights within the original top-k set according to sample difficulty. It amplifies fast-thinking experts and suppresses slow-thinking experts on easy inputs and reverses this adjustment on hard ones, enabling difficulty-adaptive control over reasoning depth. We evaluate TempoMoE on Kimi-VL, Qwen3-VL, and ERNIE-4.5-VL across six benchmarks. Compared with Vanilla, TempoMoE consistently improves accuracy while reducing output tokens, with accuracy gains of up to 2.6 points and token reductions of up to 35.4%.
  </details>

- **[PlotGround: Grounding Plot Digitization in Real Scientific Figures and Their Source Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06825)**  `arXiv:2610.06825`  `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Yaohui Zhang, Binxu Li, Haoyi Duan, Jiacheng Miao, Yixin Wang, Xinran Du, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific figures often encode quantitative results that are not readily available in machine-readable form, making accurate plot digitization important for verifying and reusing published findings. Yet it remains unclear how accurately current models recover plotted values from real scientific figures, as existing benchmarks rely largely on synthetic charts or cover only a limited range of chart types. We introduce PlotGround, an automated pipeline for building plot digitization benchmarks from real scientific figures and their author-released source data. PlotGround maps figures to source tables, identifies reconstructable panels, and generates quantitative questions with source-grounded reference values. We use PlotGround to construct PlotGround-1k, a human-verified benchmark of 1,119 questions from 1,066 bioRxiv preprints. Across sixteen multimodal models, the best reaches 87.5% accuracy at a $\pm 5\%$ relative-error tolerance. Tightening the tolerance to $\pm 2\%$ lowers every model's accuracy by 11-24 percentage points, revealing a gap between approximate visual reading and precise quantitative recovery. PlotGround's paired figure-source structure lets us compare how accurately the same values are recovered from figures and from source tables. Providing source tables instead of figures raises a coding agent's accuracy from 90.0% to 97.4% while cutting cost by 72%.
  </details>

- **[A Jury, Not an Electorate: Label-Free Reliability Estimation for Ensembles of Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21085)**  `arXiv:2610.21085`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Alessandro Rozza, Davide Mambelli, Steven Motta, Juri Taresch_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An ensemble of language models is a jury, not an electorate. The answers are testimony from witnesses of unknown reliability, and the question is not what the majority said but who is credible. Most ensemble methods assign each expert a fixed reputation, often learned from labels. We ask whether it can instead be inferred from the pattern of agreement and disagreement among the experts. We model the experts as a jury: each question has a latent true answer, each expert reports it with unknown reliability, and those reliabilities are estimated by Expectation Maximisation (EM) from unlabelled answer configurations alone. We show that its decision rule reduces to a log-odds weighted linear score over the experts' answer distributions. What changes is not how the jury votes, but how it learns whom to trust. On a 1560-question benchmark, the jury, with nine experts of up to 3B parameters, performs comparably to the same fusion rule with label-fitted weights, showing no statistically significant advantage from supervision. Instead, it outperforms the best single model with matched self-consistency and the strongest label-free alternative on the full benchmark.
  </details>

- **[TIRE: Top-Down Formula Instantiation with Reliable Execution for Physics Problem Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25134)**  `arXiv:2610.25134`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Xiaoyu Wu, Zhaoxin Yu, Xinglin Xiao, Nan Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Advancing physics reasoning in large language models requires scalable training data that connect physical laws, concrete problem scenarios, and verifiable numerical answers. Expert-designed problems offer top-down control over physical laws, conditions, and scenarios, but require costly human effort. LLM-based synthesis from texts scales efficiently, but model-generated answers and assessments leave supervision vulnerable to hallucinations. We introduce TIRE, a framework for Top-Down Formula Instantiation with Reliable Execution that brings knowledge-driven problem construction to large-scale data generation. Top-down instantiation uses reusable physical formulas and their applicability conditions to control the physical relations and numerical configurations of generated problems. Reliable execution establishes reference answers before problem generation, providing a common numerical foundation for problem construction and teacher distillation. Together, these mechanisms combine control over problem content with execution-grounded supervision for both supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning. Using TIRE, we construct TIRE-19k, spanning nine subfields of physics. Across 3 backbone LLMs and 4 benchmarks, TIRE-19k outperforms 5 representative datasets with equal amounts of SFT data, exceeding the strongest baseline by 2.86 percentage points on average. Further GRPO raises Qwen3-8B's average accuracy by 1.52 percentage points over its TIRE-19k SFT checkpoint. These results demonstrate the value of generating physics training data from explicit physical knowledge with reliable execution.
  </details>

- **[Write Once, Check Many: Reusable Ledgers for Post-Hoc Policy Compliance Auditing of Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24483)**  `arXiv:2610.24483`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ashwin Nagarajan, Gursimran Panesar, Alicia Yang, Mona T. Diab, Virginia Smith, Aashiq Muhamed_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Auditing LLM agents against policies is critical to ensure safety but requires rereading long trajectories once per policy. Can we instead record each trajectory once and reuse that record for policies not known in advance? We propose write once, check many, a framework in which a writer model converts each agent trajectory into a policy-independent ledger of the agent's actions and outcomes. A reader then checks each new policy against the ledger alone, without access to the original trajectory. On 900 trajectories from SHADE-Arena, $\tau$-bench, and $\tau^2$-bench, we show that a 12B reader of narrative ledgers can either match or in fact achieve higher AUC than the same 12B model reading full trajectories. On SHADE-Arena policies that combine several facts, our approach outperforms a 31B model reading the trajectory (.777 vs. .713 AUC). When checking eight SHADE-Arena policies separately, ledgers use about $5\times$ fewer tokens, including the cost of writing them. A deeper analysis shows that rule-based extraction performs better on some single-action policies, and ledgers can miss evidence in long tool outputs and message contents. Our results suggest that reusable, policy-independent records can make retrospective agent auditing more efficient and effective, and highlight important considerations in ledger design.
  </details>

- **[Composable Parametric Demographic Injection for Values Survey Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23867)**  `arXiv:2610.23867`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Xinyuan Cao, Qingyi Pan, Bangde Du, Yujia Zhou, Hexi Wang, Weihang Su, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as simulated respondents in computational social science, yet accurately reproducing population-specific heterogeneous response distributions remains challenging. Existing methods such as demographic prompting are sensitive to prompt design and increase inference overhead, while parameter-based methods struggle to reuse demographic information efficiently, limiting generalization to unseen combinations. In this regard, we propose PARADE, a composable PARAmetric DEmographic injection framework that represents each demographic attribute value with a reusable and composable LoRA module. Specifically, PARADE separates task-common survey capability from demographic-specific value shifts via a shared Task LoRA and attribute-level Demographic LoRAs, with composition calibration to reduce parameter interference. At inference time, target population models can be constructed on demand by recombining the relevant modules, including demographic combinations not observed during training. Extensive experiments on the World Values Survey and SocioBench across three backbone models show that PARADE achieves strong population-level distribution alignment while maintaining competitive predictive performance, reducing KL divergence by up to 52.8% relative to the strongest baseline.
  </details>

- **[DialFork: Going Beyond Factuality to Evaluate Procedural Hallucination in Conversational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21561)**  `arXiv:2610.21561`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Sergio Burdisso, Esaú Villatoro-Tello, Dairazalia Sánchez-Cortés, Yanis Labrak, Jordi Luque, Milos Cernak, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) deployed as conversational agents are increasingly expected to follow procedural logic. While factual hallucination has received extensive attention, a different failure mode has been overlooked: procedural hallucination, in which an agent produces individually fluent and factually plausible utterances while deviating from the intended conversational process. We introduce DialFork, the first benchmark to measure this failure mode on raw dialogue text. DialFork is built by forking conformant dialogues at controlled turn positions and injecting one of four hallucination types, yielding 36,278 dialogues across 24 tasks in 12 domains; 30 human annotators validate the construction. We define three evaluation tasks that characterize procedural deviation at three granularities: how far a set of dialogues deviates from conformant ones (monitoring deployed agents), how far a single dialogue does (procedural retrieval), and at which turn a dialogue first goes off-track (real-time guardrails). For the first two, we propose dialog-flow-aware metrics, FlowKL and FlowScore, that compare dialogues by how they unfold rather than their content. At the dialogue level, FlowScore significantly outperforms all baselines across seven encoders, showing that semantic similarity is no substitute for procedural similarity. We release DialFork under open licenses atthis https URL, hoping the community will measure conversational process alongside content.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating the Robustness of Proof Autoformalization in Lean 4](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14867)**  `arXiv:2606.14867`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhengtao Gui, Sheng Yang, Zhouxing Shi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Proof autoformalization aims to translate a mathematical informal proof in natural language into a formal proof in a formal language such as Lean 4. Existing LLM-based models for proof autoformalization are typically evaluated only on well-formed informal proofs from curated datasets. We argue that a robust proof autoformalizer must remain faithful even for informal proofs that diverge from these idealized ones, and we present the first study on the robustness of proof autoformalization models. We formulate two categories of perturbations: a global perturbation paraphrases the informal theorem-proof pair in a different style, under which the formalization should remain consistent; a local perturbation alters a value, symbol, or proof step, possibly in a counterfactual way, and a robust formalization should faithfully reflect the perturbation rather than reverting to the original or inferring a different one on its own. We build a benchmark with both perturbations on miniF2F and MATH-500, and automatically measure how stable a proof autoformalization's correctness is under global perturbations and how faithfully its output reflects local perturbations. We evaluate seven recent models: their correctness varies under global perturbations, and they mostly fail to remain faithful under local perturbations.
  </details>

- **[Safe, or Simply Incapable? Rethinking Safety Evaluation for Phone-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07630)**  `arXiv:2605.07630`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhengyang Tang, Yi Zhang, Chenxin Li, Xin Lai, Pengyuan Lyu, Yiduo Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a phone-use agent avoids harm, does that show safety, or simply inability to act? Existing evaluations often cannot tell. A harmful outcome may be avoided because the agent recognized the risk and chose the safe action, or because it failed to understand the screen or execute any relevant action at all. These cases have different causes and call for different fixes, yet current benchmarks often merge them under task success, refusal, or final harmful outcome. We address this problem with PhoneSafety, a benchmark of 700 safety-critical moments drawn from real phone interactions across more than 130 apps. Each instance isolates the next decision at a risky moment and asks a simple question: does the model take the safe action, take the unsafe action, or fail to do anything useful? We evaluate eight representative phone-use agents under this framework. Our results reveal two main patterns. First, stronger general phone-use ability does not reliably imply safer choices at risky moments. Models that perform better on ordinary app tasks are not always the ones that behave more safely when the next action matters. Second, failures to do anything useful behave like a capability signal rather than a safety signal: they track broader phone-use performance and increase when visual input is removed. Across models, failures split into two recurring patterns: unsafe choices in settings where the model can act but chooses wrongly, and inability to act in more visually and operationally demanding screens. Overall, a harmless outcome is not enough to count as evidence of safety. Evaluating phone-use agents requires separating unsafe judgment from inability to act. Code and public data: \url{this https URL}.
  </details>

- **[EngTrace: A Symbolic Benchmark for Verifiable Process Evaluation of Engineering Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.01650)**  `arXiv:2511.01650`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ayesha Gull, Muhammad Usman Safder, Rania Elbadry, Fan Zhang, Veselin Stoyanov, Preslav Nakov, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most engineering problems involve complex multi-step derivations through checkable intermediate quantities. Grading only the final answer misjudges LLMs, crediting correct answers reached through flawed steps and penalizing wrong answers built on mostly sound ones. Yet, existing benchmarks such as SuperGPQA and EEE-Bench score only final answers, and step-level ones such as TPS-CalcBench rely on LLM judges or human graders, or cover a single domain. We therefore introduce EngTrace, a symbolic benchmark for verifiable process evaluation of engineering reasoning. Its 150 expert-certified templates, spanning five branches, 15 domains, and 42 areas, generate each problem with a gold reasoning trace, so that every intermediate quantity is known exactly. We design and validate an evaluator that checks final answers, coverage of the gold trace's intermediate quantities (milestones), and displayed arithmetic, mostly without an LLM judge. Across 11 LLMs, final answers barely separate the strongest models, while their derivations differ. Wrong answers still state about half of the gold milestones, and domain experts trace most wrong answers to calculation slips, with conceptual errors rarer on easy problems than on harder ones. Overall, as LLMs near the final-answer ceiling, checking derivations, as EngTrace does, reveals differences that final answers miss.
  </details>

- **[RefCraft: Benchmarking Visual-Reference-Conditioned Agentic Generation through Web Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26826)**  `arXiv:2610.26826`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Yuchi Wang, Rongyao Fang, Weihao Yu, Xilin Wei, Tiancheng Gu, Dingkang Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Beyond basic tool use and coding skills, effectively incorporating visual references into agentic generation is equally essential for applying AI agents to real-world productivity tasks. We study this crucial capability through webpage development, a practical and readily evaluable setting in which existing benchmarks remain largely idealized: they typically focus on reconstructing visual targets or generating webpages from textual instructions alone, overlooking the more common workflow of developing from visual examples. Specifically, we introduce RefCraft, a benchmark comprising 300 realistic multimodal webpage-development tasks that combine natural-language instructions with screenshots from one to five reference webpages. Agents must selectively interpret and utilize these references while rendering, visually inspecting, and iteratively refining their outputs. We evaluate the resulting open-ended artifacts using independently verifiable keypoints and complementary programmatic and VLM judges. Across nine frontier (M)LLMs, the strongest system achieves an overall score of only 52.12 out of 100, revealing that visual-reference-conditioned agentic generation remains a substantial challenge for current models in practical webpage-development settings.
  </details>

- **[Mitigating Reward Hacking in Preference Reward Models with Evolving Penalties](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26496)**  `arXiv:2610.26496`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Afonso Baldo, António Farinhas, Marcos Treviso, Ricardo Rei, Nuno M. Guerreiro_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning for language models has moved from preference reward models to verifiable rewards, which sustain much longer optimization. Preference models are prone to reward hacking: the policy raises their score without improving response quality, or even while degrading it. Yet for open-ended tasks, where quality can neither be verified nor fully written down as rubrics, they remain valuable. We introduce Dynalize, which mitigates reward hacking online, during policy training. Periodically and asynchronously, a language model analyzes training rollouts and writes penalties for undesirable behaviors that the reward model appears to reward; a judge applies them to every rollout. In clinical counseling and creative writing, training on the reward model alone raises its score continuously but ultimately leads to a decline in external benchmarks. Dynalize sustains longer training and results in improved downstream performance. For example, we find that our method suppresses patterns incentivized by preference models that, without penalties, spread to most responses, and even to the model's reasoning, which the reward model does not explicitly score. Furthermore, we show that its gains hold in multi-turn training, and even after we retrain the reward model on the discovered behaviors.
  </details>

- **[Honeyed Words, Hidden Daggers: Benchmarking and Training LLMs to See Through Mock Politeness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26059)**  `arXiv:2610.26059`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Caixin Kang, Sitong Gong, Tianyu Yan, Kaipeng Zhang, Yoichi Sato, Yifei Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The Chinese idiom "kou mi fu jian" ("honeyed words, hidden daggers") names speech that is polite in form but hostile in function. We present ZhenhuanBench, a 1,592-item benchmark built from expert commentary on Empresses in the Palace, covering speaker intent, emotion-language mismatch, and per-listener uptake. Text-only evaluation covers 34 LLMs. GPT-6 Astra leads the leaderboard at 73.48% overall, while its listener-reaction score is 58.37%. The annotations also supply supervised traces and string-verifiable training rewards. With 338 gold traces, an 8B model outperforms teacher distillation on identical items. Subsequent RL saturates, and chance correction reduces the estimated recoverable performance gap by a factor of 7.5. Our CPR-GRPO loop (Compress-Probe-Replenish) uses this estimate to weight gold supervision, reaching 67.0 on public multiple-choice tasks and 74.5 under the training-matched hidden protocol. Without target-task training, CPR outperforms Base, SFT, and GRPO across six Chinese and English pragmatic and sarcasm evaluations. On three external datasets, training with CPR yields higher mean Macro-F1 than SFT and GRPO.
  </details>

- **[AdaExploreBench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Proactive Exploration and Test-time Adaptation in Evolving Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25950)**  `arXiv:2610.25950`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Rui Wang, Zishen Lai, Jiayu Liu, Cheng Qian, Jeonghwan Kim, Heng Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM) agents are increasingly deployed in unfamiliar and evolving environments, requiring them to proactively explore, understand, and adapt to environmental dynamics. However, existing benchmarks rarely test whether agents can proactively discover how an environment works and adapt as it evolves. To address this gap, we introduce AdaExploreBench, a benchmark for proactive exploration and test-time adaptation in evolving environments. By instantiating environmental dynamics as hidden rules, AdaExploreBench requires agents to actively acquire evidence and infer the rules. Under implicit rule drift, the latent rule changes without announcement, requiring agents to detect from degraded performance that prior knowledge is stale and re-explore. Experiments on eight leading LLMs show that proactive and adaptive exploration remain challenging: GPT-6 Sol achieves only 61.9% calibrated accuracy, while providing sufficient evidence or removing rule drift improves average performance significantly by close to 10%. These results reveal a critical bottleneck in current LLM agents: they can often reason effectively from informative evidence once it is available, but struggle to actively acquire the right evidence and recognize when previously acquired evidence is no longer valid.
  </details>

- **[MapSteal: Map-based Autonomous Knowledge Stealing Attack Against Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25626)**  `arXiv:2610.25626`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhengao Li, Chuxu Zhang, Yushun Dong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge acquisition from black-box large language models (LLMs) offers a practical approach to adapting smaller student models. However, many existing methods rely on predefined queries or student feedback to guide acquisition, leaving autonomous expansion of knowledge targets an important challenge. We propose \textbf{MapSteal}, a student-independent framework for structured recursive knowledge acquisition. Starting from a small set of initial roots, MapSteal represents acquisition targets using structured states and recursively expands them through teacher-proposed directions. Question realization, proposal admission, and deduplication constrain the acquisition process, while root-aware scheduling organizes exploration under a fixed QA budget. The resulting question--answer pairs are used to adapt student models without requiring student feedback during acquisition. Experiments with Qwen3.5 students at 2B, 4B, and 9B scales show competitive downstream performance across six medical and cybersecurity benchmarks. On the 4B medical evaluation, Full MapSteal improves average accuracy by 1.17 percentage points over a simplified recursive acquisition variant. Cross-domain evaluations show that gains depend on the domain and evaluation panel. These results demonstrate the feasibility of structured, student-independent recursive acquisition while highlighting the distinction between generating acquisition targets and obtaining useful training supervision. Our code is available at \href{this https URL} {this https URL}.
  </details>

- **[DiaLong: Benchmarking Reminiscence in Long-Context Dialogues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25463)**  `arXiv:2610.25463`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Owen Bianchi, Samer Aslan, Ryunosuke Saito_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  How well can transformer-based Large Language Models (LLMs) retain and recall memories in long contexts of dialogue? LLMs are known to forget long-range factual information in their context, yet the field lacks a comprehensive benchmark to quantitatively assess these shortcomings. We present DiaLong, a long-context dialogue dataset and accompanying memory task that standardizes evaluation in this space. It consists of 100 unique conversations created by humans and then augmented with LLMs, extending beyond lengths found in existing datasets. Our selected memory task is fact recall: classify 40 candidate facts about a dialogue as true or false. This task directly probes the model's ability to actively retain and retrieve information, serving as a critical measure of memory within extended conversational contexts. In testing both the latest LLMs and proposed memory enhancements on DiaLong, we demonstrate that more work needs to be done to enable LLMs with better memory performance. By enhancing memory capabilities, LLMs can improve in maintaining coherence, relevance, and human-like interaction over extended conversation. Improving these capabilities will help LLMs fulfill diverse user needs and expectations in various conversational scenarios.
  </details>

- **[Aligning Cultural Behaviors in Arabic Language Models via Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25362)**  `arXiv:2610.25362`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Junhong Liang, Daniil Orel, Saeed Almheiri, Bilal Elbouardi, Younes Samih, Abed Alhakim Freihat, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Arabic LLMs have improved rapidly, but aligning them with country-specific cultural behavioral preferences remains underexplored. Previous work has focused primarily on instruction-tuning for factual knowledge, overlooking nuanced cultural behaviors. We propose ArabPref, which to our knowledge is the first large-scale pan-Arab preference data set for the alignment of cultural behavior conditioned on the country. Our contribution includes two resources in English and Modern Standard Arabic: a culturally grounded preference dataset for training and evaluation, and a multiple-choice benchmark designed to assess culturally aligned behavior across nations. All test data are validated by native speakers to ensure authenticity. To optimize cultural alignment, we experiment with post-training methods across multilingual and Arabic-centric language models, evaluating performance on both generation tasks and our cultural multiple-choice benchmark. By releasing an evaluation benchmark, we provide a foundation for systematically studying and improving culturally aligned behavior in language models.
  </details>

- **[One Head Away from Language Confusion: Single Attention Heads Across Model Families](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25026)**  `arXiv:2610.25026`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiwon Jeong, Anjelo Jann Laroza, Chaewoon Yoon, Arjun Pillai, Pranav Kokati, Christian Hoang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models sometimes reply in an unexpected language, a failure known as language confusion. We ablate individual attention heads in 12 model checkpoints spanning seven model series, using FLORES-200 continuations in five languages and Language Confusion Benchmark (LCB) chat replies. In ten models, zeroing one head causes substantially more departures from the expected language than zeroing any other head in its layer; its LCB pass-rate drop persists, though reduced, under mean ablation. Seven heads move 8.0 to 51.5 percent of all FLORES continuations out of the prompt's language, versus at most 1.4 percent for other same-layer heads; the other three mainly affect explicitly requested languages. These seven heads matter less in base models given plain text. In two models, replacing the values a head reads at the requested-language name with another language's mean makes replies fail and shifts some into that language, unlike a random change of equal size. Replacing a head's output with another language's mean redirects 84 to 99 percent of scored non-English monolingual LCB replies into that language in five models. Yet the requested language's mean improves crosslingual replies beyond every control head only in the two Gemma models, whose baselines are low. Code and results are available in the public repository atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Epi: Towards a Persistent, Self-Improving Agent for Autonomous Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24944)**  `arXiv:2610.24944`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yue Huang, Zhangchen Xu, Xiaonan Luo, Zheyuan Liu, Lichi Li, Yiping Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Épi, an autonomous research agent that sustains investigations across model sessions and evolves its methods through accumulated experience. Épi combines persistent research history, hierarchical memory, branching and independent review to maintain research continuity, learn from experiments, and explore and revisit alternative approaches. Its experiments run across session boundaries, and a fixed verifier evaluates their outputs. Épi produced 120 record-setting results, comprising 20 numerical bound records across 12 families and 100 construction records across eight families. These include an approximately 84-fold narrower interval for the $n$-queens constant and a more than 3.6-million-fold narrower rigorous interval for the quartic Feigenbaum attractor dimension. Exact dual certificates establish that the Cohn-Elkies linear-programming bound cannot be sharp in dimensions 10 and 11. Beyond numerical improvements, Épi gives a computer-assisted proof of Tuza's conjecture for split graphs with an eight-vertex clique part and at most three triangle-active neighborhood types, with arbitrary multiplicities. We extend this research process to algorithmic coding with Épi-turbo, a lightweight configuration that develops executable solvers for tasks including vehicle routing, scheduling and production planning. On 30 FrontierOR tasks, Épi-turbo achieves a 5.4% higher total benchmark score than Codex at 64% of its model cost, using the same underlying model and reasoning effort. Épi is designed to support human understanding by connecting computational results to explicit methods and mathematical arguments that researchers can examine and build upon.
  </details>

- **[What Can't Jev Decide? Probing the Limits of One-Pass Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24929)**  `arXiv:2610.24929`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Wang Yang, Xinpeng Li, Yanyan Zhang, Chuang Ma, Shouren Wang, Jianzhe Wei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Jev and its open replicas choose among the options a caller supplies with a single forward pass, fast enough to decide inside an agent loop. We ask what such a model cannot decide, and find a simple limit: Jev cannot make a decision that an LLM makes only by writing out intermediate steps. On a benchmark that varies one factor at a time, Jev finds and selects records without error up to 20k tokens and 64 options, but once the answer depends on two or more updates of one user, accuracy falls, to 53-80% at sixteen updates, because similar records interfere. Given a stale summary of the state, Jev follows it. On real decision items graded by how many thinking tokens a reference LLM needs, Jev answers 97.5% of the items that one forward pass of that LLM solves, but only 74% of the others. Jev also does not know its limit: its confidence flags the errors it makes itself but not those it inherits from a stale input, and neither an explicit nor a trained hand-off option catches the latter. The open models we evaluate share these limits. Checking the state such models rely on requires a model that reasons.
  </details>

- **[GLOSS-MT: Controllable Low-Resource Machine Translation with an Editable Dictionary](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24730)**  `arXiv:2610.24730`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Mich-Seth Owusu, Stephen E. Moore, Akwasi Asare, Lawrence Adu Gyamfi, Paul Azunre, Joel Budu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Adapting a pretrained machine translation (MT) model to new terminology usually requires new parallel data and another round of fine-tuning, which is costly for low-resource languages. We ask whether controllability can be added to MT, and the fine-tuning burden reduced, without degrading the pretrained model. GLOSS-MT looks up the source's noun phrases in an offline English-Twi dictionary, replaces every phrase found by its Twi translation in place, and fine-tunes a translation model once to translate these mixed inputs. We apply the same data and pipeline to NLLB-200 (600M, 1.3B, 3.3B), OPUS-MT en-tw and TranslateGemma 4B. On a document-level English-to-Asante Twi benchmark (84 documents, four domains), every fine-tune scores above its pretrained base in chrF2 (+2.85 to +5.48), and the three NLLB fine-tunes are the best open systems (50.12-50.80). A direct controllability test on human-translated Bible passages shows that the three fine-tunes tested write 79-97% of the dictionary terms, and that replacing every dictionary entry by a different term changes the output accordingly (70-97% of the new terms appear) with no retraining. We release the models, data and code.
  </details>

- **[DiffuPlan: Editable Planning State for Diffusion Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24717)**  `arXiv:2610.24717`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Qisheng Zhou, Zhen Xiong, Qiaoyu Tan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models (dLLMs) are emerging as an alternative to autoregressive (AR) language models, yet dLLM agents largely inherit an interaction protocol designed for AR generation: once intermediate state is written, later observations can only be incorporated by appending more text. This is a poor match to a defining capability of dLLMs -- reconstructing earlier text conditioned on context that appears both before and after it. We ask whether this capability can support a different agent-state paradigm in which intermediate planning state is directly revisable as evidence arrives. We introduce DiffuPlan, a diffusion search agent that maintains a single editable planning state and reconstructs only the affected steps when later evidence resolves an entity or changes the search route. To support variable-length revisions, we introduce Expansion-Partition Marginalization (EPM), which marginalizes over equivalent expansion paths. We further adapt diffusion trajectory RL to multi-turn search. Across four multi-hop QA benchmarks, DiffuPlan achieves 50.24 average F1, outperforming strong AR search agents. More importantly, under identical data and training, editable planning state improves the diffusion agent by 2.44 F1 versus only 0.47 for its AR source model. These results show that dLLMs need not imitate append-only AR agents: revising committed state is a distinct agentic advantage.
  </details>

- **[Muse Glimmer 30B: An On-Device Agentic Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24628)**  `arXiv:2610.24628`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Arash Ebrahim Abtahizadeh, Muna Aghamelu, Adil Ahmed, Juan C. Angeles Acuna, Alexis Audran-Reiss, Asaf Avrahamy, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Muse Glimmer, an open-source 30B parameter causal language model with perception encoder, purpose-built for autonomous agentic tasks on consumer hardware. Muse Glimmer integrates multi-step reasoning, reliable tool use and failure recovery into a single model that runs locally on consumer devices without requiring cloud infrastructure. The model accepts text and image inputs and produces text outputs, was trained on data from over a hundred languages, and is quantized to approximately 4-bit precision for efficient single-GPU inference. Muse Glimmer achieves state-of-the-art performance for its size class on agentic benchmarks, setting a new standard compared to the models of its size: Gemma4-31B and Qwen3.6-27B on tool use, multi-step planning, and agentic task completion. We release the model under the Apache 2.0 license to foster open-source development of capable, safe, local AI agents.
  </details>

- **[Which Long Documents Matter? A Controlled Study of Data Selection for Context Extension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24469)**  `arXiv:2610.24469`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Junlong Jia, Jiang Zhou, Xing Wu, Chaochen Gao Tinghao Yu, Feng Zhang, Lilin Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Natural long documents are widely used for context extension, but which selection pipelines are effective remains unclear. We conduct a controlled comparison on Qwen2.5-3B at 128K under matched 6B-token continued-training budgets. Random sampling of natural long documents (random-long) outperforms repacking at longer evaluation lengths. Within the same candidate pool, selecting documents with high LongPPL key-token density yields further gains, unlike the evaluated generic-quality and LLM-judge selectors. We introduce truncLongPPL to reduce scoring cost by computing key-token density over a truncated prefix. At 128K, scoring the first half of the reference span yields downstream performance close to full-span scoring with a measured $2.28\times$ end-to-end scoring speedup. Across three training seeds, truncLongPPL achieves the highest mean scores among evaluated pipelines, exceeding random-long by 4.54 points on validation HELMET and 3.28 on held-out RULER, with gains most consistent in retrieval. At a 256K target context, it gains 2.82 average RULER points over random-long on Qwen2.5-3B. In short/long mixed training across Qwen2.5-3B, Qwen3-8B, and OLMo~3-7B, it gains 1.47--7.31 average RULER points over random-long mixing, with seven-task short-context averages within 0.23 points of the corresponding baselines. After supervised fine-tuning (SFT), Qwen3-8B and OLMo~3-7B models trained with selected long documents outperform their random-long counterparts by 1.13--1.67 points on LongBench v2 and 0.62--1.44 points on MRCR.
  </details>

- **[When Opposite Premises Translate Identically: A Source-Conditioned Diagnostic for Multilingual Commonsense Items](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24428)**  `arXiv:2610.24428`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Shuai Wang, Rui Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multilingual reasoning comparisons depend on translated premises preserving the relations in their English sources. Automatic translation scores can rank source-target pairs, but an acceptance decision alone cannot certify a counterfactual benchmark field. We test a source-contrast diagnostic for ACCORD premises: reverse the English polarity, translate both arms independently, count identical target outputs, and score each arm against its own source. Across 71 matched question identities at two containing-item depths, five target languages, and three translation checkpoints, Swahili has 63, 113, and 67 exact collisions among 710 pairs per checkpoint. At a human-reference-calibrated threshold, round-trip NLI passes both arms of 3 of the 113 NLLB-3.3B collisions, while LaBSE passes 78. Each collision proves at least one unfaithful translation, but does not identify its arm. These controlled measurements do not establish screening accuracy on spontaneous translation errors or a language-by-hop reasoning effect; the study has no native equivalence labels or answer-model evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Analyzing Repetition and Interference Effects in Memory-Constrained User Simulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24232)**  `arXiv:2610.24232`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Cara Su-Yi Leong, Nicholas Tomlin, Tal Linzen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  User simulators can help evaluate and train AI systems to interact with humans at scale. To do so, evaluation of user simulators must measure behavioral parity with people. We evaluate whether memory-constrained user simulators are sensitive to two factors known to affect human information retention: repetition of a fact and interference from competing information. We find that some simulators achieve human-like overall accuracy, and replicate the magnitude of repetition and interference effects, but none match people's accuracy on particular questions: while humans misremember facts, models forget them, leading to different error patterns. We release this data as a challenge benchmark for future work on user simulators.
  </details>

- **[OR-License: Evaluating LLM Agents as Real-World Operations Research Practitioners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24087)**  `arXiv:2610.24087`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Peisong Wang, Feixue Liu, Yize Meng, Bowen Liu, Ruoyu Chen, Gaoyang Dai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can large language model (LLM) agents deliver expert-level operations research (OR) solutions directly from original client materials, without prior expert reformulation? Existing OR benchmarks typically begin with self-contained descriptions or predefined optimization tasks, underrepresenting the translation of original business materials into professionally validated solutions. We introduce OR-License, an end-to-end benchmark of 31 instances across 10 industrial scenarios from paid OR engagements totaling USD 3.68 million. It preserves original requirements, input data, deliverable formats, and information boundaries after de-identification. To study information acquisition, we introduce ORSim, a controlled client simulator that discloses business facts without supplying OR expertise. Hidden evaluation checks feasibility and compares objective quality against original expert solutions accepted by clients before benchmark construction. Deployment-Adjusted Quality (DAQ) averages expert-relative quality across instances, assigning zero to infeasible deliveries. Across 11 frontier LLMs using coding-agent harnesses, several exceed the expert reference in conditional objective quality, but none matches its DAQ; the strongest primary Claude Code configuration achieves 88.39% strict feasibility and a DAQ of 0.87 against the expert reference of 1. Client information acquisition extends beyond clarification into solution development and validation, with some later stages showing higher confirmed issue-resolution proportions. Trajectory analysis identifies formulation and implementation failures, while task-equalized research-chain completion rates range from 24% to 45% across models on evaluable tasks. These findings motivate sustained information-gap resolution, faithful problem formulation, and evidence-linked self-improvement for reliable long-horizon agents.
  </details>

- **['Your AI Text is not Mine': Redefining and Evaluating AI-generated Text Detection under Realistic Assumptions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04906)**  `arXiv:2606.04906`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Nils Dycke, Marina Sakharova, Nico Daheim, Iryna Gurevych_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Although it is generally agreed that AI-generated text poses a broad societal risk, there is no common understanding in the AI-generated text detection literature on what constitutes harmful use. Rather, existing datasets and approaches often define their own criteria and make their own assumptions, sometimes implicitly, and often only loosely related to real-world needs and applications. To address this gap, we here systematically define various notions of AI-generated text and their characteristics. To study these, we collect AITDNA - a new benchmark of human-machine co-constructed texts that is annotated with detailed genesis information, such as the entire edit and AI-interaction history. We benchmark various machine-generated text detectors and find that they often only perform well for specific notions but not as broad detectors. We release code and data publicly.
  </details>

- **[Latent Abstraction for Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17866)**  `arXiv:2604.17866`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ha Lan N.T, Minh-Anh Nguyen, Dung D. Le_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has become a standard approach for enhancing large language models (LLMs) with external knowledge, mitigating hallucinations, and improving factuality. However, existing systems rely on generating natural language queries at each hop and maintaining a strict architectural separation between retriever and generator, preventing them from leveraging the full representational capacity of the LLM. We propose \textbf{LAnR} (Latent Abstraction for RAG), a unified framework in which a single LLM jointly performs encoding, retrieval, and generation entirely within its own latent space. Rather than generating textual queries, LAnR produces dense retrieval vectors from the hidden states of a designated \texttt{[PRED]} token and uses them to match against encoded document representations from the same model. Furthermore, LAnR adaptively decides when sufficient evidence has been retrieved using a lightweight MLP control head over those same hidden states, eliminating both the separate retriever and explicit token-level stopping reasoning. This design is motivated by our empirical observation that answer token entropy reliably signals retrieval sufficiency. Extensive experiments on six QA benchmarks spanning single-hop and multi-hop settings demonstrate that LAnR outperforms existing RAG methods, while achieving improved inference efficiency through reduced number of retrieval calls and tighter model integration.
  </details>

- **[KEEP-Bench: Evaluating Evidence Preservation in Context Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26827)**  `arXiv:2610.26827`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ziqing Man, Di Wu, Jia-Chen Gu, Junyi Zhang, Kai-Wei Chang, Nanyun Peng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Context compression improves long-context efficiency but may introduce faithfulness issues through an information bottleneck. Existing evaluations rely on QA metrics, but a reader can still answer correctly from an unfaithful compression, so these metrics underestimate such loss. To bridge this gap, we introduce KEEP-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark that directly assesses compression faithfulness with 4,102 cases across three challenging settings that require keeping evidence for plausible reference resolutions, explicit qualifiers, and minority perspectives. Experiments show that even with tailored prompting, the strongest models remain unreliable: despite context windows exceeding 100K tokens and inputs averaging only 3.1K tokens, the best open-weight model (31B) preserves all required evidence in only 66.8% of cases, and the best proprietary model in only 61.6%. Performance also varies sharply across evidence structures, so conclusions from limited structures may be biased. We further introduce a metric, Evidence Preservation Return (EPR), to relate evidence recall to inference FLOPs, and find that it peaks at moderate scales (4B-8B), where recall remains low. Additional output budget mainly benefits already strong models and widens capability gaps, yet the stronger preservation of these models comes at a steep computational cost. These findings highlight the importance for future work to investigate compressors with strong information preservation under a small computation budget, rather than relying on a large output budget or model scaling.
  </details>

- **[ASTOR: Benchmarking and Improving Autoformulation for Soft-Constraint Optimization with a Formal Grammar](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26819)**  `arXiv:2610.26819`  `cs.CL`  
  _Amer Goel, Yeganeh Alimohammadi, Andrew Daw_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world decision problems combine two qualitatively different kinds of constraints: requirements that must be satisfied, called hard constraints, and preferences that can be violated, called soft constraints. Because these problems are often posed to decision-makers in natural language, large language models offer a natural tool for assisting with their solution. This has given rise to autoformulation, which focuses on using LLMs to translate natural-language descriptions of decision problems into executable mathematical optimization models. However, this research often neglects soft constraints like those proposed by consumers, managers, and planners, despite the fact that many practical problems require modeling them explicitly. We introduce ASTOR, an inference-time framework that maps a natural-language decision problem to a grammar-guided abstract syntax tree (AST) before deterministically compiling the resulting representation into executable optimization code. We also introduce SoftOR, a unified benchmark of natural language problems which focuses on soft constraint formulations. On SoftOR, ASTOR achieves the best aggregate accuracy among comparable single-request and multi-agent LLM methods while remaining competitive on standard autoformulation benchmarks that include only hard constraints. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[RESIN: Multi-Chain Reasoning with Entity-Structured Indexing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26782)**  `arXiv:2610.26782`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xuanyu Meng, Jash Rajesh Parekh, Jiawei Han_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-chain reasoning is a challenging task not only because the multi-chain evidence is dispersed, but also because different parts of reasoning may require distinct, interacting chains of support. Flat retrieval can flood a model with loosely related text, whereas early selection of a single reasoning path can discard evidence that becomes useful only after other facts are considered. We introduce RESIN (Reasoning with Entity Structured Indexing), which uses structure to locate evidence while keeping the original source as the basis for reasoning. RESIN decomposes a question into focused one-hop needs, retrieves a small set of source-grounded units for each hop, and places only this relevant evidence in a compact reasoning buffer. The buffer preserves alternative supports until final composition, making intermediate decisions explicit without forcing a brittle path. On the full HolisQA benchmark, RESIN achieves 82.92 context recall and 76.70 answer relevancy over all questions, with higher reported CR and AR across five domains and higher F1 in four domains than the published baselines. Ablations show that both typed evidence diversity and hop-wise decomposition are essential, supporting a clear conclusion: Precise evidence organization and delayed commitment make multi-chain reasoning effective and auditable. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Monsoon: Scalable Spontaneous Speech Collection for Low-Resource ASR in 50 Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26776)**  `arXiv:2610.26776`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shobhit Banga, Manas Dhir, Aaditya Pareek, Amritansh Walecha, Bhaskar Singh, Hanuman Sidh, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  For most languages, automatic speech recognition (ASR) is limited less by model architecture than by a lack of accurately transcribed spontaneous speech. We present Monsoon, a framework for collecting such data at scale. It elicits unscripted conversations between paired contributors, screens recordings with automated classifiers, corrects transcripts over five levels of human review, and selects segments for lexical and acoustic coverage. It has collected speech in 50 languages; we analyse seven 3,500-hour corpora. At equal volume, its quality stages reduce semantic word error rate (WER) by 10-39% relative to a conventional protocol. Compared with an architecture-matched baseline trained on public corpora, Whisper fine-tuned on Monsoon performs comparably on the baseline's own test sets and reduces semantic WER by 20-44 points on conversational benchmarks. The gains transfer to a second architecture and grow with data volume. We release the framework, its quality-control models, and the fine-tuned models.
  </details>

- **[HoloCosm: Beyond Single-Metric Benchmark Compression Across Multiple Metrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26714)**  `arXiv:2610.26714`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yupei Lin, Zhongzhan Huang, Junxin Li, Guoming Ling, Shanshan Zhong, Hefeng Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmark compression reduces evaluation cost by selecting a small set of samples. Existing methods typically guide selection using a single metric, although benchmarks often assess models along multiple dimensions. We find that model rankings are often weakly correlated across metrics, indicating that different metrics capture distinct aspects of model performance. Samples selected for one metric do not reliably preserve scores and rankings on others when used for compressed evaluation.
