At 80\% compression, each of the five single-metric baselines yields higher reconstruction error than equal-size random sampling in over 70\% of cross-metric comparisons. To address this challenge, we present HoloCosm, which jointly minimizes normalized score-reconstruction error across source models and metrics while constraining ranking distortion. We evaluate HoloCosm across multiple compression rates on 15 language, multimodal, VLA, and agent benchmark configurations with 85 component metrics. With 80\% compression, HoloCosm outperforms Random, the strongest baseline overall: average NMAE drops by 35.3\% to 0.236, worst-component NMAE drops by 39.5\% to 0.330, and average Spearman correlation rises from 0.884 to 0.915. In addition, HoloCosm remains competitive with existing baselines in single-metric evaluation. We will release our code and experimental results.
  </details>

- **[ReAnchor: Reusing Rejected Verifier States for Diffusion Drafting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26531)**  `arXiv:2610.26531`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sanghyun Lee, Wonjin Nam, Seungho Jang, Jaeho Lee, Jiho Park, Jaewoon Lee, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Block diffusion drafters accelerate speculative decoding by generating multiple draft tokens in a single forward pass. However, each pass produces only a fixed-length block from a single target-generated anchor, limiting how far the draft can extend. Meanwhile, target verification computes hidden states even at positions beyond the first rejection, but these states are typically discarded rather than reused. We propose ReAnchor, a training-free method that reanchors a pretrained drafter at shifted positions using discarded verifier states to generate parallel draft blocks. These blocks improve predictions within the original block and extend coverage beyond its endpoint. At sampling temperature of T=0, ReAnchor achieves its largest average gains on Gemma 4-12B, improving acceptance length by 19.4% and decoding speed by 17.9% across eight benchmarks. Averaged equally over four target models and eight benchmarks, it improves acceptance length by 15.2% and decoding speed by 13.4% with pretrained DFlash drafters.
  </details>

- **[SNO-PI: Label-Scarce SNOMED CT Entity Linking with Pre-trained Encoders and Curated Terminology Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26425)**  `arXiv:2610.26425`  `cs.CL`  
  _Piyawoot Songsiritat_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present SNO-PI, the top-performing submission on SNOMED International's SNOMED CT Entity Linking Benchmark as of 18 October 2026. SNO-PI achieves a macro character IoU of 0.5387, outperforming the organisers' reference system (0.4582; based on the 2024 challenge winner's approach) by 18% relative. Linking clinical mentions to SNOMED CT is a zero- and few-shot problem over a very large label space: the 272 annotated MIMIC-IV discharge summaries cover only 6,595 of the roughly 219,000 eligible concepts, and a third of those occur only once. This motivates a design built on transfer from pre-trained models and human-curated knowledge. SNO-PI combines high-recall span proposal, SapBERT candidate retrieval and listwise ensemble reranking with three BERT-family encoders, each further pre-trained with masked language modelling on unlabelled MIMIC-IV notes and then on SNOMED CT synonyms and hierarchy before task fine-tuning. We find that curated knowledge helps most on concepts never seen in training, yet these concepts remain the main source of error.
  </details>

- **[Self-Consistency of Entity Sets and Citations in a Deep Research System under Repeated Prompting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26421)**  `arXiv:2610.26421`  `cs.CL`  
  _Lauri Pykälä, Kimmo K. Kaski_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep research agents (DRA) are commonly used for open-ended synthesis tasks where closed-world QA benchmarks are poorly suited to characterize relevant aspects of performance. We study the self-consistency of one commercial deep research system under repeated identical prompting, representing each report with the set of named entities it mentions, categorised by a custom schema, and the set of citations it retrieves. Across repeated runs of a single open-ended cybersecurity related prompt, we quantify pairwise overlap to characterize how stable the retrieved and synthesized output structure is under repeated identical prompts. In ten runs, the pairwise entity Jaccard results in a median of 0.12 and the pairwise citation Jaccard is below 0.02, with discovery curves that do not visibly saturate. The magnitude and structure of (dis)agreement between entity sets varies by entity class, which suggests that self-consistency in a DRA is better understood as a function of the underlying entity space than as a stable property of the system.
  </details>

- **[How Benchmark Graph Construction Shapes Multi-Hop KG Navigation: A Controlled Audit of PathQuestion and PathQuestion-Large](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26399)**  `arXiv:2610.26399`  `cs.CL`  
  _Eduin E. Hernandez, Sergio A. Diaz, Luis F. Garcia, Nurassyl Askar, Stefano Rini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-hop knowledge-graph question answering (KGQA) benchmarks are often treated as fixed language-and-reasoning tasks, but for navigation-based methods, difficulty also depends on the graph in which those tasks are instantiated. This makes graph construction a consequential but often entangled part of the navigation task. To isolate this effect, we audit PathQuestion (PQ) and PathQuestion-Large (PQL) as graph-navigation benchmarks under a controlled intervention: we hold questions, topic entities, released answers, released path annotations, and family-level splits fixed while replacing each released task-filtered graph with the larger source KG distributed with the benchmark. Source restoration expands the effective navigation search space and graph-consistent answer and path multiplicity while sharply reducing unguided reachability to released answers. We evaluate whether these structural changes persist across navigation policies using structural references, frozen small-language-model navigation policies, and trained MINERVA policies. Across both frozen and trained policies, restoration substantially reduces released-answer accuracy and trajectory fidelity, while graph-consistent evaluation recovers only part of the loss. These results show that benchmark graph construction is part of the navigation task specification rather than merely an implementation detail and can materially shape measured behavior even when the released QA task is held fixed.
  </details>

- **[Heartbeat Thinking: Speculative Reasoning and Tool Execution While Listening in Realtime Voice Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26233)**  `arXiv:2610.26233`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhenyu Zhang, Kewei Fu, Yongqi Tong, Chenzhi Zheng, Wei Wu, Jiang-Ming Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-using voice agents often leave users waiting after speech ends, even when actionable information arrives earlier in the request. Speculative approaches begin work during speech, but chunk-bounded or interrupt-on-update generation can truncate a tool call before its arguments are complete. This couples computation time to speech updates, limiting useful work completed in advance. We introduce Heartbeat Thinking, which handles updated input through new Thinker invocations while allowing ongoing invocations and dispatched tools to finish. Periodic invocations read the latest partial transcript and a shared record of completed reasoning and tool results. These results help resolve missing arguments and initiate dependent calls, allowing preparation to progress across speech updates. Across SpeechFCEval, FDBench v3, and AudioCRAG, Heartbeat improves aggregate speculative tool coverage or retrieval reuse over a SHANKS-style windowed baseline, with benchmark-dependent effects on final task quality. On FDBench v3, it increases tool coverage by 16 percentage points and reduces mean final-response latency by 36.4\%. The results show that heartbeat thinking paradigm preserving ongoing computation during speech can turn listening time into reusable preparation for faster and more capable voice-agent responses.
  </details>

- **[DeepSEA: A Multilingual Evaluation Benchmark for Southeast Asia](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26155)**  `arXiv:2610.26155`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jiuzhou Han, Abhishek Bijay Mishra, Trevor Cohn, Shubham Mittal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While language models have demonstrated remarkable capabilities, their performance remains disproportionately skewed towards high-resource languages. This disparity is particularly acute for the 700 million inhabitants of Southeast Asia, where vast linguistic diversity remains under-represented in existing benchmarks, often suffering from limited language coverage or narrow task scopes. To address this, we introduce DeepSEA, a comprehensive benchmark constructed from established datasets through professional human translation into 12 Southeast Asian languages, spanning both high-resource (e.g., Indonesian, Thai, Vietnamese) and low-resource (e.g., Burmese, Khmer, Lao) tiers across five distinct tasks: mathematical reasoning, scientific reasoning, question answering, abstractive summarization, and machine translation. Extensive evaluation of frontier proprietary and open-weights models reveals that while frontier models like Gemini-3.7-Flash maintain robustness across resource tiers, smaller models face severe performance degradation on low-resource languages. Crucially, models perform better in reasoning and summarization when pivoted through English rather than target languages, exposing a critical dependency on English representations, especially for low-resource languages. Beyond these observations, we provide a cross-lingual error analysis and a cross-script tokenizer fertility analysis. We hope these findings shed light on the development of next-generation inclusive multilingual models.
  </details>

- **[Balance-Aware Pivot Selection for Parallel Diffusion LLM Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26040)**  `arXiv:2610.26040`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yejin Lee, Hyundong Jin, Yo-Sub Han_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models enable parallel token generation, offering a promising alternative to sequential autoregressive decoding. Existing acceleration methods use predictive confidence to enable parallel token commitment while preserving generation quality. However, confidence-based selection prioritizes reliable token predictions without explicitly considering how token positions shape the remaining decoding schedule. Moreover, such ordering does not always reflect inter-token dependencies, suggesting that confidence need not be the sole criterion for token selection. Motivated by this limitation, we propose Balance-Aware Pivot Decoding (BPD), which favors central pivots within contiguous masked intervals and decodes the selected positions in parallel. Balanced pivots yield logarithmic decoding depth in the interval length. Our practical method accounts for model uncertainty by combining confidence-based prefilling with confidence-thresholded center selection. We evaluate the resulting quality-efficiency tradeoff on LLaDA and Dream across five mathematical reasoning, code generation, and instruction-following benchmarks. Our implementation is available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Scaling Synthetic Pre-training Data and Evaluation for Indic LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26006)**  `arXiv:2610.26006`  `cs.CL`  
  _Abhinav Khattar, Aishwarya Chhabra, Ameya Velingker, Anusha Kamath, Brandon Norick, Kanishk Singla, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Building capable language models for India requires scalable multilingual data and robust evaluation. We introduce Nemotron-Pretraining-Indic-v1, a synthetic pretraining corpus of approximately 2.83 trillion tokens covering all 22 scheduled Indian languages in native script and twelve in romanized form. It is the largest openly released Indic pretraining corpus. Using our GPU-accelerated pipeline, we translate curated English documents, assess translation quality, and repair low-quality outputs while preserving technical content and document structure and maintaining source traceability. Through continued-pretraining experiments across twelve languages, we show that combining our synthetic corpus with natural Indic text improves knowledge and reasoning over a public-corpus baseline. Additional experiments show how data volume, script coverage, and Indic training share affect English performance. We also introduce the Indic-LLM-Eval-Suite, a multilingual benchmark suite for evaluating Indic language models across chat, instruction following, reasoning, knowledge, code, and translation. The suite combines existing Indic benchmarks with task-aware translations of English benchmarks, using automated quality assessment, targeted correction, and native-speaker review in a human-in-the-loop pipeline. We use the suite to comprehensively benchmark contemporary open-weight Indic and multilingual models across capabilities and languages, revealing substantial variation across tasks and languages. Together, these resources provide a scalable foundation for developing and evaluating more capable Indic language models.
  </details>

- **[LEAPS: Long-context Evaluation of Adaptive Pattern Learning and State Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25919)**  `arXiv:2610.25919`  `cs.CL`  
  _Howard Yen, Nitya Mani, Alex Renda, Christopher S. Hardin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-context language models (LCLMs) power agents that act over long trajectories, from improving a codebase to running research experiments to forecasters that condition on lengthy histories. These applications draw on three distinct long-context capabilities: recall, where the answer is a fact present in the context; state tracking, where the outcome can be computed by performing a series of transformations to a specified state; and pattern learning, where the goal is to make a prediction by sampling from a distribution inferred from many observations. Existing benchmarks primarily focus on the first capability. In this work, we introduce LEAPS (Long-context Evaluation of Adaptive Pattern learning and State tracking), a collection of 12 novel datasets, spanning agentic workflows, behavioral modeling, and forecasting of market and world events. By construction, LEAPS is resistant to data contamination, extends to millions of input tokens, and uses reliable metrics. We evaluate 30 API and open models from 8K to 512K input tokens. LEAPS distinguishes models that perform near perfectly on recall tasks, finding that such models' state tracking and pattern learning abilities can vary significantly and degrade much more dramatically with increasing input lengths--even for pattern learning, where more observations should make learning a theoretically easier task. Moreover, thinking models often underperform their base counterparts, exhausting their generation budget or oversimplifying the task. We hope LEAPS can guide the development of the next generation of LCLMs.
  </details>

- **[FisherEdit: Fisher Information Guided Knowledge Editing for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25914)**  `arXiv:2610.25914`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yizhou Yang, Jiahong Fu, Zhiwu Wang, Kailong Fan, Haokun Lin, Yichen Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sequential knowledge editing in Large Language Models (LLMs) often leads to a degradation of general capabilities due to cumulative parameter interference. In this paper, we revisit prominent projection-based methods like AlphaEdit and reveal a surprising mismatch: their robustness is primarily driven by update regularization rather than the intended null-space projection. Based on this insight, we propose FisherEdit, a theoretically grounded method built on Fisher geometry, which replaces coarse activation-space projections with importance-aware regularization in parameter space. It penalizes changes to sensitive parameters more strongly while retaining flexibility for new facts, and admits an efficient row-wise closed-form solution. Experiments on Llama-3, Qwen2.5, and Olmo-3 across multiple benchmarks demonstrate that FisherEdit achieves state-of-the-art performance while better preserving model stability and downstream task accuracy.
  </details>

- **[CALM: Consistency-Aware Logit Masking in Decoding Space for Mitigating Overthinking of Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25908)**  `arXiv:2610.25908`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shengjing Liu, Feiyang Li, Yuxuan Yang, Yile Wang, Hui Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large reasoning models (LRMs) have achieved strong performance on challenging mathematical and scientific tasks through long chain-of-thought reasoning, but they often suffer from overthinking with unnecessary reasoning steps. Existing truncation-based methods typically detect consecutive trial-answer agreement and force early exit, yet such local stopping signals do not necessarily indicate final-answer stability and can disrupt useful subsequent reflection or verification, potentially leading to performance degradation. We propose consistency-aware logit masking (CALM), a training-free decoding intervention for mitigating overthinking in LRMs. CALM first monitors trial answers at step boundaries and then applies logit masking to action transition point (ATP) tokens such as "Wait", "Alternatively", and "Hmm", suppressing redundant transitional reasoning while allowing the model to continue its thought process more naturally. Experiments across four advanced LRMs and five benchmarks show that CALM reduces token consumption by 18.6%-25.0% compared with full CoT reasoning while maintaining performance, and can even improve accuracy by 0.07%-1.48% by reducing redundant reasoning. LLM-as-a-Judge results also demonstrate that CALM provides more coherent and concise results.
  </details>

- **[Arabic Financial NLP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25854)**  `arXiv:2610.25854`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mo El-Haj_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Financial communication connects numerical accounts of economic activity with explanations of performance, responsibility and future expectations. Arabic financial natural language processing offers methods for studying these connections across corporate reports, market disclosures, journalism and banking interactions. Its contribution to digital humanities depends on retaining the relationship between a computational representation and the document from which it was made. This chapter follows that relationship from source collection and Arabic text processing to the analysis of vocabulary, evaluation, entities, causal attribution and multilingual reporting. It then examines what financial summaries and retrieval-grounded answers preserve or lose, and how dialectal intent detection changes the perspective from institutions speaking to institutions listening. Published Arabic resources are discussed in relation to the questions they were designed to answer, with a distinction between financial pretraining corpora, annotated datasets and evaluation benchmarks. A running constructed example connects the methodological discussion without presenting illustrative analysis as empirical findings. The chapter argues for an account of Arabic financial NLP in which linguistic context, documentary provenance and interpretive judgement remain part of the evidence throughout the analytical process.
  </details>

- **[CoMem: Learning to Anticipate Failures from Retrieved Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25790)**  `arXiv:2610.25790`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yun Shen, Ming Shen, Tianjun Yao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reflection lets LLM agents improve from their own attempts, but the memory it builds is local to a single task, so an agent reflecting on its own tends to revisit the same explanation for failure. Cross-task reflective memory addresses this in two forms: retrieved memory keeps past experience explicit and looks up related cases, which is specific but bounded by the bank, and parametric memory internalizes experience into parameters, which generalizes but is grounded in nothing beyond the task description. When both are used, existing agents combine them additively, feeding their outputs to the solver side by side, so retrieved experience never changes what the learned memory produces. We introduce CoMem (Compounding Memory), which makes retrieved experience an input to the learned memory itself, at training and at inference. The agent builds a bank of its own successful and failed reflections, learns from retrieved cases to write failure hypotheses, candidate explanations of how an attempt is likely to go wrong, and at test time reasons from newly retrieved cases to guide each attempt. Varying the retrieved cases gives the memory different perspectives, yielding reflective diversity that is not merely sampled but also grounded in retrieved experience. Across two backbones and five benchmarks in math, code, and multi-hop QA, CoMem achieves strong overall performance. Additional evaluations show that it generalizes to out-of-distribution tasks and enables smaller models to provide effective guidance to stronger solvers.
  </details>

- **[Truncated Reasoning Under a Budget: Evaluating System-1 Models as Decision-makers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25768)**  `arXiv:2610.25768`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhongyin Wang, Zhaoqian Xue, Junting Mu, Haozheng Liu, Ye Yuan, Haolun Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) often generate more reasoning tokens than needed. Limiting the reasoning budget reduces cost, but forcing an answer from an incomplete trace can hurt accuracy. System-1 decision models select among candidate options without producing a reasoning response. We evaluate Jev as a decision reader of truncated reasoning across seven LLM reasoners and five multiple-choice benchmarks (35 pairs). Jev achieves higher mean accuracy than forced answering at tested budgets from 500 to 16,000 tokens, including the estimated costs of reasoning and answering. At a 1,500-token budget, it improves accuracy by 4.1 pp overall and 8.6 pp on truncated questions, with significant gains in 17 pairs under Holm-corrected bootstrap tests. Offline replay estimates 34.5% of the full-run cost, with a 1.13 pp accuracy loss. A policy selected on training folds saves 71.3% of the estimated full-run cost on held-out folds, with a 0.58 pp accuracy loss. We also find that Jev shows strong question-only accuracy, exceeding 500-token forced answering on average (68.0% vs. 65.5%), although this advantage varies by benchmark. These findings suggest that System-1 decision models can complement LLM reasoning and reduce the need for costly reasoning completion.
  </details>

- **[Self-Distillation for Supervised Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25497)**  `arXiv:2610.25497`  `cs.CL`  
  _Wenxuan Zhu, Bang An, Shuming Liu, Wenqing Cui, Tianqi Xu, Bing Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Supervised fine-tuning treats the expert demonstration or gold response as a one-hot target, suppressing valid alternative continuations that the model would otherwise assign probability to. We propose Gold-Conditioned Distillation (GCD), a method that turns expert demonstrations into model-native supervision. GCD uses one model as both teacher and student: the teacher conditions on a prompt containing the gold response in context, the student conditions on the original prompt as at inference, and the student is trained to minimize the forward KL divergence to the teacher's next-token distribution at each gold response token. This lets the model use its prior knowledge to teach itself the new task: correctness is anchored by the gold response, while the soft target preserves plausible alternatives. Across various training tasks on Qwen and Llama models, GCD improves new-task accuracy over SFT and generally reduces degradation on general benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Design Considerations in Low-Resource Archival OCR: A Case Study in Lakota](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25440)**  `arXiv:2610.25440`  `cs.CL`  
  _Téa Wright, Laura X. Ma, Claudia Iron Hawk, Hannah J. Haynie, Alexis Palmer, Alane Suhr, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Building and expanding access to digital archives supports language reclamation and creates lasting resources for community revitalization. Designing effective tools to support these efforts requires taking into account the community-, language-, and document-specific context. We investigate how small ($\leq$3B) vision-language models (VLMs) can be adapted as optical character recognition (OCR) tools for Lakota, a North American Indigenous language. In addition to benchmarking modeling approaches, we inspect four practical facets of low-resource archival OCR that arise in previous work and conversations with community members: constrained training budgets, segmentation noise, scan degradation, and orthographic variance. We demonstrate that fine-tuning VLMs for OCR post-correction on 35 pages of transcribed data decreases character error rate (CER) from 17.1 to 3.9\% on a bilingual corpus, and find that training only with nine pages increases CER by only 1.1\% over this. However, evaluating under realistic automatic segmentation nearly doubles the CER of the best-performing models, stressing the importance of taking realistic use cases into account. We hope this work encourages future research on developing language technologies grounded in the workflows and constraints of ongoing revitalization efforts.
  </details>

- **[Towards Figurative Language Understanding in Moroccan Darija: An Evaluation of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25406)**  `arXiv:2610.25406`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jihad R'baiti, Youssef Hmamouche, Emmanuele Chersoni, Chu-Ren Huang, Amal El Fallah Seghrouchni_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Figurative language remains a major challenge for Machine Translation (MT) systems, as the intended meaning of such expressions often goes beyond literal translation. Although recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) improved the translation of Arabic dialects, benchmark resources for evaluating figurative language translation in Moroccan Darija remain scarce. To this end, we introduce the Moroccan Darija Figurative Language Corpus (MDFC), a benchmark dataset for MT and multiple-choice question (MCQ) selection covering idioms, metaphors, and proverbs. Using MDFC, we evaluate 19 open- and closed-weight language models and MT systems at both the expression and contextual levels under zero-shot and six-shot settings. We also conduct layer-wise similarity-based probing to determine how model representations distinguish figurative from literal meanings. Our findings reveal that closed-weight models achieve the strongest translation performance, while the benefits of contextual information vary across models and tasks. Differences between translation performance and MCQ accuracy highlight the distinction between generating an appropriate figurative translation and recognizing the intended meaning among candidate translations.
  </details>

- **[From Unverifiable Domain Text to Cross-Lingual RLVR via Code-Switched Task Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25399)**  `arXiv:2610.25399`  `cs.CL`  
  _Cheonbok Park, Jihwan Kim, Shinyoung Joo, Joosung Lee, Hwiyeol Jo, Jeonghoon Kim, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) can improve reasoning in large language models, but specialized non-English domains expose a data mismatch. Raw English domain text is often available, while target-language RLVR tasks with verifiable rewards are scarce. We introduce code-switched task synthesis for cross-lingual RLVR, a data-centric approach that derives target-language and code-switched RLVR task variants from otherwise unverifiable English domain text. Using fill-in-the-middle multiple-choice QA as a seed task format, we synthesize automatically verifiable tasks from domain passages and reshape them into cross-lingual variants. These variants preserve the same evidence-answer relation but change how English and target-language evidence are presented during RLVR. On Korean medical benchmarks and cybersecurity benchmarks across four target languages, code-switched training outperforms matched monolingual and bilingual-mixture baselines on average target-language performance while maintaining comparable English performance. Additional analyses provide evidence of better target-language input understanding and stronger cross-lingual alignment in both model outputs and internal representations. These results suggest that code-switched training helps target-language inputs access domain reasoning learned more strongly in English.
  </details>

- **[SPARC: Spoken Profile Authorship Recognition Corpus](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25265)**  `arXiv:2610.25265`  `cs.CL`  
  _Efthymios Tsaprazlis, Bowen Yi, Tiantian Feng, Junghwan Kim, David Jurgens, Shrikanth Narayanan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speech anonymization has focused on the acoustic signal, leaving open how much speaker information the transcribed words themselves retain. In this work, we study pairwise speaker and demographic-attribute verification from transcribed speech. We introduce SPARC (Spoken Profile Authorship Recognition Corpus), a benchmark built from two podcast corpora comprising over 12,500 speakers, 1.5M quality-filtered utterances, and speaker-level inferred or source-provided labels for sex, age, and accent, and evaluate five attribution models and three LLMs on identity- and attribute-verification trials with episode- and metadata-based topic proxies. LLM adversaries distinguish same-speaker pairs from text alone. Attribute-label agreement is harder to verify, but LLMs perform sex- and accent-label verification above chance. Finally, releasing only short probe texts (50 words) degrades the strongest adversaries the most, offering a partial reduction in observed linkage.
  </details>

- **[A Thousand Faces: A Persuasion Benchmark with Experience-Grounded Persuadees Informed by Psychology and Pragmatics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25206)**  `arXiv:2610.25206`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ziqing Zhuang, Dingyi Zhang, Deyu Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  People can express the same beliefs and desires yet need different reasons to change their minds. What makes an argument relevant depends on how those views arose: experiences shape what a person finds credible, what matters to them, and what could prompt reconsideration. These personal grounds may also be expressed indirectly, through contextual clues in conversation. Benchmarks that specify beliefs and desires without these connections can reward arguments that match a description while overlooking whether they address the person's reasons. We introduce A Thousand Faces (ATF-Persu), with 1,000 personas across 200 topics and nine domains. Drawing on personality and attitude theories and conversational implicature, we construct five personas per factual context, linking experiences to judgments, expression, and grounds for revision. Evaluations show lower persuasion success than three comparison benchmarks under their respective protocols. Complete backgrounds yield broader use of supplied personal grounds and higher persona faithfulness than simplified specifications; blinded reviewers also rate ATF-Persu above CToMPersu on identifiable personal reasons, their influence on responses, and contextual expression. The dataset is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Morpheus: a fully local, defense-in-depth de-identification engine for Greek clinical text - a benchmark evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25155)**  `arXiv:2610.25155`  `cs.CL`  
  _Filippos-Paschalis Rorris, Nikoleta Stanitsa, Tatiana Rorri, Foteini Rorri, Constantine N. Antonopoulos_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Background and Objective: Clinical free text cannot leave the hospital without de-identification, yet no de-identification system or benchmark exists for Greek clinical text. We evaluated Morpheus, a fully local de-identification pipeline for Greek clinical documents, on 500 synthetic discharge summaries. Methods: The notes (300 cardiac surgery, 100 cardiology, 100 nephrology) were generated by a frontier large language model (Gemini 3.1 Pro) constrained to authentic Greek hospital document layouts. Notes were pre-annotated at generation; two clinicians then independently reviewed and corrected every note, blinded to each other, with disagreements arbitrated. Primary outcomes were recall over must-redact identifiers and over-redaction of clinical content, measured over must-survive probes. Secondary outcomes: residual leaks flagged by an independent egress-review layer, processing time and reproducibility. Results: Inter-annotator agreement was F1 = 0.998. The de-identification rate was 96.59% (7,666/7,937 identifier instances; 95% CI 96.16-96.96): 98.49% for direct and 93.42% for quasi-identifiers. No patient's own complete identifier survived in any note; of 271 residual instances, 199 (73.4%) concerned institutional identity, chiefly the hospital letterhead. The over-redaction rate was 2.82% (190/6,747 probes; 95% CI 2.45-3.24). The egress guard flagged 2 of 271 residuals, both complete telephone numbers. Two independent runs gave identical verdicts. Conclusions: A fully local, deterministic pipeline removed 98.5% of direct patient identifiers from Greek clinical text while altering under 3% of protected clinical content; most residuals concerned the institution, not the patient. To our knowledge this is the first benchmark evaluation of clinical-text de-identification for Greek and the first quantified evaluation of an independent egress-guard layer.
  </details>

- **[MemBubble: Benchmarking How Agent Memory Shapes LLM Response Diversity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25127)**  `arXiv:2610.25127`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jiajun Tan, Fei Sun, Xinyu Ma, Shixuan Zhang, Du Su, Qi Cao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory serves as the core component of LLM-based agents, enabling personalized responses via extracting and using user preferences from past interactions. However, its use may reduce response diversity, thereby narrowing the range of information available to users. Existing evaluations rely largely on synthetic interactions and cover few memory systems, leaving the effects of memory on response diversity insufficiently evaluated in real-world scenarios. We introduce MemBubble, a benchmark of 1000 instances built on preferences extracted from real user-assistant conversations. To measure the extent to which memory concentrates response distributions, we construct test queries in scenarios distinct from user preferences while preserving topical relevance through shared preference categories. Across multiple LLMs and memory mechanisms, entropy decreases by 34.7% on average compared with directly providing the full interaction history, highlighting the important role of memory processing in diversity loss. We also explore simple user-side interventions and changes in information availability but find that these do not effectively close the diversity gap. Our findings highlight the need to consider preserving diversity and user access to information alongside improvements in personalization when designing and using agent memory systems.
  </details>

- **[Behave Like Experts: Fusing Data with Semantic Knowledge for Effective and Efficient Semantic Map Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25122)**  `arXiv:2610.25122`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhu Liu, Zhen Hu, Yuxuan Liu, Ying Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Semantic map models (SMMs) construct a graph that reflects conceptual similarity by comparing cross-lingual form-meaning mappings. Experts build such models not only by obeying the data constraint, i.e., the connectivity hypothesis, which states that conceptual nodes belonging to the same form must be connected, but also by incorporating their prior semantic knowledge. Current automatic methods ignore this knowledge, which harms both accuracy and efficiency by enlarging the search space of candidate graphs. In this paper, we fuse the distributional semantic knowledge hidden in the internal representations of language models into a top-down, data-driven algorithm. This expert-like behavior re-sorts the candidates in a huge search space, guided by conceptual semantics similarity. Experiments on a self-collected benchmark of 10 datasets and comprehensive metrics show that our method builds SMMs both effectively and efficiently.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Factual Accuracy: A Benchmark for Evaluating Perspective Alignment in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25087)**  `arXiv:2610.25087`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sunghoon Kim, Sangyeop Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models often generate factually correct responses that fail to maintain user-requested perspectives, blending multiple perspectives and forcing users to filter through unwanted information---a perspective control failure that factual accuracy metrics cannot detect. We introduce PerspectiveBench, a benchmark quantifying perspective alignment by constructing scenarios where multiple valid reasoning paths coexist, and propose the Perspective Alignment Score (PAS) to measure adherence to specified perspectives. Experiments reveal that state-of-the-art models successfully include target content but fail to exclude non-requested perspectives. Providing documents through RAG improves factual grounding but does not resolve perspective drift, as models prioritize comprehensive coverage over strict user constraints. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[P2N: Reusing Deep Representations for Greater Effective Depth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25076)**  `arXiv:2610.25076`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yiqin Hao, Boyi Zeng, Zitong Wang, He Li, Shixiang Song, Feichen Song, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mathematical problem solving and autonomous agents are increasingly important applications of language models, demanding stronger reasoning capabilities. Computational depth is a key resource for such reasoning, but adding model layers increases both training and inference costs, while test-time scaling incurs substantial additional inference computation. We introduce P2N (Previous to Next), which reuses the previous token's deep representations, already available during generation, to increase effective computational depth with negligible decoding overhead. Specifically, P2N adds the previous token's deep hidden state to the current token's shallow representation, allowing subsequent layers to build on previously completed deep computation. This recurrence extends effective computational depth across tokens, while introducing only a single vector addition per decoding step. We train the recurrence using parallel Jacobi updates restricted to a middle-layer core. Experiments with Qwen-style models show that, at 1.5B parameters, P2N improves GSM8K accuracy by over 10 percentage points under both zero-shot and few-shot prompting, with nearly unchanged decoding latency. The benefits also extend to language modeling and average performance on general downstream benchmarks. on general downstream benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[When Does Better Acceptance Pay? A Controlled Study of Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24993)**  `arXiv:2610.24993`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yongchao Long, Chunrui Zheng, Yuxuan Zhou, Guangkun Nie, Tian-Chi Ma, Chao Pang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The throughput gains of speculative decoding depend on the trade-off between acceptance length and execution latency. We ask when additional acceptance is worth the cost of obtaining it. We establish a comparison protocol spanning training, inference, and evaluation, remeasuring released checkpoints and retraining six drafter recipes on shared target-generated data across ten benchmarks and two target models. Alignment changes the picture: gaps that appear large in published comparisons shrink substantially under shared training and inference conditions. This narrowing motivates two deeper questions: how is acceptance improved at different costs, and when do these gains improve throughput? We find that acceptance can be improved through nearly free objective and context changes or more expensive token-to-token conditioning. DCore illustrates the former route as a strong baseline with little additional measured drafting overhead. The payoff is conditional: task uncertainty and serving conditions affect acceptance returns, execution costs, and the preferred drafter and budget. These findings inform future drafter design and deployment choices.
  </details>

- **[A Measurement Perspective on Benchmarking LLM-Generated Text Detectors using Item Response Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24972)**  `arXiv:2610.24972`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yanbin Zhao, Zhipeng Chen, Guangsheng Bao, Guandong Zhang, Yan Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmarking large language model (LLM)-generated text detectors commonly relies on aggregate detector-level metrics such as accuracy, F1 score, and AUC. Although useful for summarizing overall performance, these metrics provide limited insight into how benchmark composition shapes detector comparisons or which texts distinguish detectors with different response profiles. We propose a measurement-based benchmarking framework for jointly characterizing benchmark heterogeneity and detector behavior using multidimensional item response theory (IRT). By treating benchmark texts as measurement items and detectors as respondents, the framework estimates text-level discrimination and difficulty-location parameters alongside multidimensional detector abilities. We apply the framework to a benchmark of 32K human-written and machine-generated texts across four domains, evaluated by 14 detectors. The estimated IRT item-parameter space reveals distinct but overlapping human-machine distributions, with substantial variation across domains and text lengths. The human-machine label shows the strongest multivariate association with this space, while domain and text length further contribute to benchmark heterogeneity. Detector ability profiles reveal multidimensional and domain-dependent response patterns that are not fully reflected in aggregate performance rankings. As a practical application, clustering texts in the estimated IRT space yields a compact subset of 2,088 texts (6.5\% of the full 32K benchmark) that largely preserves the comparative detector-performance patterns observed on the full benchmark. Overall, the proposed framework provides a principled and interpretable methodology for benchmarking heterogeneous detection technologies while jointly characterizing the measurement properties of the texts used for their evaluation.
  </details>

- **[BanglaBBQ: A Bias Benchmark for Question Answering in the Bangladeshi Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24764)**  `arXiv:2610.24764`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sumit Anthony Gomes, Yasin Rahman, Taeeba Tasnia, Faiza Nooren Suhita_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper introduces BanglaBBQ, a question-answering bias benchmark designed to capture stereotypes grounded in the Bangladeshi context, in both Bangla and English. Following KoBBQ and PakBBQ, BanglaBBQ adapts the original Bias Benchmark for Question Answering (BBQ) by classifying its templates into three groups: directly translated (DT), modified to align target groups or contexts with Bangladesh (TM), and removed when deemed unsuitable for the Bangladeshi context (SR). This process retains 140 templates across six categories, comprising 76 DT and 64 TM templates. Five newly authored (NA) categories, based on stereotypes common in Bangladeshi society, are then added: marital status, age and political perception, regional origin, educational background, and mental-health status. Native Bangladeshi annotators judged all 466 newly authored templates, and 372 were retained because a strict majority of English annotators identified the intended target and rated the attitude as common enough. Each newly authored template is linked to a documented source and a verbatim quote from it. Each evaluated template also has a counter-stereotypical version of its disambiguated context. The dataset was evaluated using two open-weight models (Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B) and two closed models (GPT-4.1-mini and Gemini 2.5 Flash). All four lean towards the stereotyped person when the context is ambiguous, in both languages, and the effect of language differs by model, so English results do not predict behaviour in Bangla. On the adapted BBQ templates, both Qwen3 models are also less accurate when the context contradicts a stereotype than when it confirms it. On the newly authored templates, the closed models follow counter-stereotypical evidence almost perfectly, and all four models are more biased on the templates that annotators validated than on those they rejected.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Recall Saturation: Scaling Hard-Negative Reranker Training for Industrial Legal Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24478)**  `arXiv:2610.24478`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yunhan Li, Mingjie Xie, Zeyang Shi, Gengshen Wu, Min Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Industrial retrieval systems often improve quality by enlarging embedding candidate pools or adopting complex training pipelines. We show that, under a sufficiently large but practical candidate pool, embedding recall exhibits diminishing returns, motivating a closer examination of reranker quality. On Chinese legal retrieval benchmarks, embedding top-300 reaches a high-recall operating region, while relevant evidence may still be ranked below the output cutoff. We compare hard-negative strategies for reranker training, including embedding-mined, self-mined, and dynamic-negative variants, under aligned retrieval protocols that evaluate reranking after candidate retrieval. Among the evaluated staged configurations, an embedding-to-self-mined pipeline provides a favorable trade-off; additional dynamic stages do not improve all held-out metrics. The selected pipeline scales from a 0.6B to a 4B reranker. Relative to the native 4B reranker, the trained 4B Stage2 model improves reported metrics on the STARD and CSAID benchmarks and on an internal development set, and is preferred in 72.4\% of non-tied comparisons on 410 de-identified, reconstructed internal operational-query inputs in a latency-sensitive offline preference evaluation. These results support evaluating the retrieval and reranking stages separately when selecting an optimization target for industrial retrieval systems.
  </details>

- **[Ben-Misandry: A Bengali Dataset and Late-Fusion Transformer Framework for Detecting Anti-Male Gender Hate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24474)**  `arXiv:2610.24474`  `cs.CL`  
  _Md Mehedi Hasan Nipu, Chinmoy Mitra, Tarannum Ahmed Nowshin, Israt Moyeen Noumi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Gender based hate speech research in Bengali has focused almost exclusively on misogyny, leaving anti male hate, or misandry, entirely unstudied for this language and creating a systematic blind spot in moderation systems trained to catch attacks on women but not on men. We introduce Ben-Misandry, the first Bengali misandry detection dataset: 5,000 comments across six taxonomy classes, including a novel category for mocking male emotional vulnerability with no direct analogue in existing misogyny taxonomies. The corpus is taxonomy-controlled synthetic data generated with GPT-4.1, spot-checked by four human reviewers on a 500-comment sample who confirmed the large majority of assigned labels; a full inter-annotator- agreement study is left to future work. We benchmark it with a classical pipeline (seven classifiers over four text representations) and a transformer pipeline (BanglaBERT, XLM-RoBERTa, and two fusion strategies). The best classical model, a linear SVM over character-level TF-IDF, reaches 91.8% accuracy; a late fusion ensemble averaging BanglaBERT and XLM-RoBERTa softmax outputs reaches 96.6% accuracy and macro ROC-AUC above 99%, nominally ahead of every single model and early fusion, though we have not run significance testing to confirm these margins are meaningful. We relate this to classifier combination theory, under which averaging encoders with decorrelated errors is expected to reduce variance, and corroborate the decision boundary with Shapley explanations that recover the same class- discriminative vocabulary found in our exploratory analysis, while noting this cannot rule out the model partly keying on synthetic-generation artifacts. Our results indicate misandry detection in Bengali is feasible and measurable on this synthetic corpus, offered as an initial public resource pending validation on real-world data.
  </details>

- **[Toward a Universal Local Speech Feature Extractor Through Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24424)**  `arXiv:2610.24424`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jessica Yang, Marcelo Beramendi Caballero, Ju-Chieh Chou, Karen Livescu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-supervised speech models commonly consist of a set of convolutional neural network (CNN) layers used as a local feature extractor, followed by transformer layers. The CNN layers are computationally demanding, and are also similar across models and resemble the much simpler mel spectral features. We hypothesize that the CNN layers can be replaced by a simple, universal model. To test this hypothesis, we learn a two-layer feature extractor via distillation from one or more teacher models (HuBERT, data2vec, and WavLM). Experiments on SUPERB(-SG) benchmark tasks show that our model largely retains the performance of the teacher models and can also be used in an unseen model (wav2vec 2.0). Our feature extractor requires only 3% of the MACs of the CNNs, resulting in a $>$20% overall inference runtime reduction.
  </details>

- **[FlashMap: Flow Map Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24365)**  `arXiv:2610.24365`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jeff Shen, Zhejian Zhou, Ryan Lee, Jonathan May, Shirley Ho_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates large language model (LLM) inference by using a lightweight drafter to propose a block of tokens that the target model verifies in one parallel pass. Autoregressive drafters generate this block one token at a time, whereas block diffusion drafters such as DFlash generate it in one pass, making drafting faster. However, these diffusion drafters predict each token independently, ignoring dependencies between tokens in the block. We introduce FlashMap, a continuous flow drafter that conditions a flow map language model on the target model's hidden states and generates the whole block in one forward pass while capturing inter-token dependencies. On Qwen3-8B, FlashMap is faster than a matched DFlash baseline on every benchmark, with 7% higher average speedup and up to 6.1x lossless acceleration.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Clean Accuracy: The Practical Challenges and Benefits of Pixel-Based Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24290)**  `arXiv:2610.24290`  `cs.CL`  
  _Haowen Tang, Wei Xia, Raoyuan Zhao, Hinrich Schütze, Yihong Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pixel-based language models process rendered text without relying on a fixed subword vocabulary, offering a promising approach to noisy and multilingual inputs. However, their practical value depends on whether they can be reliably adapted to downstream tasks. To this end, we systematically examine this question through fine-tuning behavior, typographical robustness, and zero-shot crosslingual transfer. Under our tested settings, pixel-based models require longer training and broader labelled-data coverage than text-based models, while delayed learning and learning-rate-dependent failures make adaptation less reliable. Our analyses further suggest that task-relevant information is less readily accessible in pretrained pixel representations, placing greater demands on fine-tuning. Once successfully adapted, however, pixel-based models lose substantially less accuracy under typographical noise and achieve higher target-language accuracy than English-pretrained text-based counterparts on two crosslingual benchmarks, despite weaker clean English performance. Together, these findings reveal a central trade-off: replacing tokenization with rendering reduces sensitivity to corrupted and unfamiliar inputs, but shifts a central bottleneck to downstream adaptation.
  </details>

- **[ReaDy: Recurrent States as Dynamic Context for Speculative Drafting in Hybrid Linear Attention Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20503)**  `arXiv:2610.20503`  `cs.CL`  
  _Bohan Sun, Zunhai Su, Xialie Zhuang, Jianjin Li, Ngai Wong, Chuan-Wei Kuo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates autoregressive generation by drafting multiple tokens per step, yet feature-based drafters rely on target hidden features whose contextual emphasis may not align with the needs of subsequent draft positions. Hybrid linear attention (HLA) models maintain compact recurrent states that accumulate historical associations, offering an efficient source of complementary context for speculative drafting. We introduce ReaDy, the first speculative decoding method, to the best of our knowledge, to leverage HLA recurrent states for position-specific context retrieval during drafting. ReaDy equips each draft position with a learned query to retrieve supplementary context directly from the target's committed recurrent states. A lightweight state reader integrates this context into draft representations through residual updates, while low-rank state reads and fused operators keep additional computation and I/O overhead low. Building on DSpark's parallel backbone and sequential prediction head, ReaDy employs a lightweight two-stage adaptation strategy, with reader capacity, state sources, and residual placement examined through ablation studies. Across seven benchmarks spanning mathematics, code generation, and dialogue, ReaDy achieves an average $3.16\times$ decoding speedup over autoregressive decoding with Qwen3.8-27B on a single NVIDIA B200 GPU. It outperforms DSpark on every benchmark, improving throughput by 7.97% and acceptance length by 8.50% on average. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Are We Really Measuring Forgetting? Pitfalls in Evaluating Machine Unlearning for Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19613)**  `arXiv:2610.19613`  `cs.CL`  
  _Oscar Chew, Po-Yi Lu, Yi-Jie Cheng, Kunal Jain, Hsuan-Tien Lin, Zhengzhong Tu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As machine unlearning for multimodal large language models (MLLMs) rapidly advances, rigorous evaluation of its effectiveness becomes increasingly important. However, existing benchmarks largely inherit evaluation protocols from text-only unlearning and overlook the unique challenges of unlearning in multimodal settings. In this work, we identify three major pitfalls that may lead to misleading conclusions: the lack of heldout evaluation to distinguish genuine concept-wise forgetting from instance-wise overfitting, the reliance on synthetic, fine-tuned knowledge rather than real-world knowledge acquired during pre-training, and unfair method comparisons caused by applying the same training settings to different unlearning methods. To address these challenges, we introduce GEMMU (Generalizable Evaluation for Multimodal Machine Unlearning), a benchmark built on real-world knowledge and a principled evaluation protocol. Re-evaluating representative unlearning methods under GEMMU reveals substantial generalization gaps and challenges conclusions drawn from previous benchmarks. Our findings highlight the importance of rigorous evaluation for advancing multimodal machine unlearning and provide guidelines for future benchmark design.
  </details>

- **[HERO: Harnessing Evidence for Test-time Refinement and Prompt Optimization in Program Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19184)**  `arXiv:2610.19184`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mingyu Lu, Timothy Ossowski, Tristan Naumann, Hoifung Poon, Peniel Argaw_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Harness optimization improves LLMs by refining reusable prompts and scaffolding, yet requires further adaptation for program generation tasks such as text-to-SQL and text-to-Python. Existing methods often rely on LLM reflectors to propose harness updates, which can fix observed failures but also disrupt previously correct behavior. Text-to-code tasks offer a natural way to mitigate this issue: we can execute generated outputs, allowing us to evaluate and verify the effects of proposed updates directly. Building on this signal, we introduce HERO Harnessing Evidence for Refinement and Optimization), which combines offline and test-time harness optimization. At the first stage, HERO learns reusable prompt updates from execution-verified successes and failures and validates candidate revisions before adoption. At test time, we treat the generated code as an instance-specific harness and refine it using execution feedback. Across four SQL, Cypher, and Python benchmarks, HERO achieves the best offline performance, improving pass@1 by 7.6 points over the initial prompt, while test-time refinement adds another 2.0 points and improves execution efficiency.
  </details>

- **[Where Words Begin and End](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17782)**  `arXiv:2610.17782`  `cs.CL`  
  _Husein Zolkepli_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Forced aligners locate each word of a known transcript in the audio, and many speech pipelines depend on them. In our own data, a widely used CTC aligner inserts a pause at nearly every word boundary, and we found almost no aligner evaluated on telephone audio. We compare nine aligner configurations against human word boundaries on read, conversational and telephone English. The Montreal Forced Aligner (MFA) is the most precise, while CTC aligners consistently start words late, because most frames inside a word go to the blank token. We then fine-tune Whisper with LoRA to predict each word's start and end as timestamp tokens, training only on boundaries placed by people: 47 hours in 87 languages. The resulting aligner is more precise than MFA on every English test condition, and than MFA's own Korean and Dutch models. With FA-Bench's protocol and code on TIMIT, its word-boundary error is 11.6 ms, against 18.2 ms for the best published system. Two choices matter most on telephone conversations: placing the timestamp slots after the word they time, and training on noisy transcripts, which also makes the aligner nearly insensitive to errors in the transcript. The released model reaches 16.0 ms on real landline calls, against 36.5 ms for MFA. The models are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[AD4EDU: Benchmarking University Lecture Audio Description across Accessibility Standards, Metrics, and Learner Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17042)**  `arXiv:2610.17042`  `cs.CL`  
  _Stylianos Psychias, Yingqiang Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Audio description (AD) aims to enhance the accessibility of visual media for blind and low-vision (BLV) persons. However, in higher education, where slide-based lectures are a default mode of teaching, lecture AD remains largely underexplored. The main reason is that existing production guidelines, evaluation metrics, and benchmark datasets are oriented toward entertainment content and do not transfer to lectures, where ADs must complement the lecturer's speech, resolve deictic references, and fit within narrow pauses. In this work, we propose AD4Edu, consisting of an AD benchmark, AD4Edu-Bench, and a rule-diagnosable evaluation metric, AD4Edu-Eval, for generating and evaluating ADs in higher education. Specifically, we consolidated established accessibility guidance, including WCAG 2.2, the DCMP Description Key, and NCAM's STEM description practices. We further collected silver reference ADs, judge-ordered preference pairs, and pairwise preferences from four BLV raters, and post-trained models with direct preference optimization (DPO) for detailed analysis. Our findings indicate that while the metric reliably detects violations of the standard, ADs that comply with it do not necessarily fulfill the subjective preferences of BLV persons, most notably on description length, underscoring the necessity of active participation of BLV persons in co-designing inclusive AI solutions.
  </details>

- **[Enhancing Biomedical Named Entity Recognition via Multiple Programming Languages Instruction Tuning and Ensemble Method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02949)**  `arXiv:2610.02949`  `cs.CL`  
  _Songtao Li, Yijia Zhang, Jianyuan Yuan, Shidi Zhang, Fengyu Zhang, Hongfei Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Instruction tuning has become a common paradigm for applying large language models (LLMs) to biomedical named entity recognition (BioNER). However, existing instruction-tuning approaches still face two key challenges. First, conventional natural-language instructions typically serialize BioNER annotations as flat textual outputs, providing limited structural constraints for typed entity extraction. Second, high-quality biomedical annotations are limited, and learning from a single serialized output form may restrict structural diversity and reduce model robustness. Although external biomedical knowledge can be introduced to alleviate data scarcity, it often requires costly resource construction. To address these challenges, we propose MITE, a Multiple Programming Languages Instruction Tuning and Ensemble method for BioNER. MITE reformulates BioNER as a structure-to-structure generation task by representing both instructions and entity outputs in code-formatted representations. Specifically, each training instance is transformed into multiple programming-language formats, including Python, C++, and Java, while preserving the same underlying entity semantics. These language-specific representations provide structurally diverse supervision without requiring external biomedical knowledge or additional annotations. During inference, MITE aggregates predictions from different code formats through an entity-level voting strategy, reducing language-specific prediction variance and improving robustness. Experiments on three widely used BioNER datasets demonstrate that MITE achieves competitive performance against representative BERT-based and LLM-based baselines, attaining the best results on two benchmarks. Ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of multi-language representations.
  </details>

- **[Debias It Yourself: Teaching LLMs Cognitive Bias Mitigation Interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40124)**  `arXiv:2609.40124`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chahat Raj, Sina Mansouri, Aylin Caliskan, Antonios Anastasopoulos, Ziwei Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bias has long been studied in social psychology and cognitive science, where decades of research have produced a body of validated interventions that reduce stereotypical thinking and prejudiced responses in humans. We propose Debias It Yourself (DIY), a cognitively grounded framework that translates five such interventions into debiasing procedures for large language models and delivers them through three established paradigms: Show (in-context examples), Train (instruction tuning), and Revise (guided self-revision). Across three models, five bias benchmarks, eleven debiasing baselines, and three reasoning benchmarks, Train+Revise and Revise alone attain the top two average ranks, lead the bias-reasoning tradeoff (mean bias as low as 2% at 90% reasoning accuracy), and reduce bias on unseen dimensions by up to 14.8%. Our code and data are publicly available.
  </details>

- **[ATTUNER: Recomputation-Free Agentic Context KV Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36722)**  `arXiv:2609.36722`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xinghao Chen, Junnan Dong, Cai Ke, Chak Tou Leong, Haocheng Sun, Keyu Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents repeatedly load reusable content, such as skills, documents, and memory entries, into the current context. Re-encoding this content for every request wastes computation. Position-independent caching (PIC) alleviates this by encoding each artifact independently and reusing its key-value (KV) states at arbitrary positions, but it incurs a quality loss relative to full-context prefill. Existing methods repair this loss by restoring global position IDs or recomputing selected tokens. In this work, we isolate the source of the loss, finding that the positional mismatch has minor effect, and independently cached artifacts retain faithful representations: reading a provided artifact stays largely accurate, and performance degrades only when the model must select among multiple artifacts. Moreover, replacing PIC's attention scores with full-prefill scores recovers performance with the cached KV unchanged, localizing the failure to the attention rather than KV recomputation. Motivated by this, we propose \textsc{Attuner}, a query-side adaptation method that learns to read a frozen artifact cache. \textsc{Attuner} inserts low-rank adapters into the query projections and is trained by distilling full-prefill distribution into the student. It trains fewer than 0.05\% of the model parameters and, at inference, requires neither cache recomputation nor a full-context reference. On Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B across seven benchmarks covering skills, documents, memory, and code, \textsc{Attuner} substantially outperforms prior PIC baselines in both in-domain and out-of-domain settings, matches full-context prefill quality while providing up to $3.73\times$ speedup.
  </details>

- **[How to Reduce Whisper Hallucination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32560)**  `arXiv:2609.32560`  `cs.CL`  
  _Husein Zolkepli_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Whisper is still what runs in production: one permissively licensed checkpoint, 99 languages, no per-language tuning. But it writes sentences nobody said. On 42 clips of pure room tone, whisper-large-v3 emits words on 61.9% of them and emits something on 100%. The usual response is to distil a student from Whisper pseudo-labels, filtering the hallucinations out of the corpus first, which deletes the evidence while leaving the behaviour in place: the student is fitted to the subset where the teacher was right, and inherits a failure mode absent from its own training data. The second response, adding non-speech audio so the model learns to stay quiet, already ships and works, but teaches suppression without discrimination. The checkpoint best on every non-speech arm here is also the one that recovers the fewest genuinely spoken phrases and deletes 58% of repeated speech. The teacher has to be fixed, with both halves of the signal. Our benchmark scores both at once: 11,852 clips over eight arms, 6,267 synthetic positives, 8,296 clips of real audio that made a production model fail, and FLEURS in 58 languages. We collect 40,891 hallucination phrases in 100 languages, choose a text-to-speech system by measurement, and synthesise those phrases as positives, so the model meets the same text both as something to suppress and as something to transcribe. Across 33 matched pairs of fine-tunes differing only by those positives, adding them lowers word emission on real voice-free audio in 31 pairs and raises phrase recovery in 32; among the 30 pairs that hold English accuracy it is 30 out of 30. The best checkpoint takes hallucination on silence from 61.9% to 2.4% and words over real voice-free audio from 99.9% to 47.8%, while raising phrase recovery from 69.8% to 82.7%. The benchmark, lexicon and synthetic corpus are released atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Hear2Act: Benchmarking When Prosody Should Change What an Assistant Does](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19515)**  `arXiv:2608.19515`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xinyi Liu, Hooshang Nayyeri, Dilek Hakkani-Tur, Emine Yilmaz, Joo-Kyung Kim, Yifei Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Prosodic cues can carry task-relevant information that words omit, but existing benchmarks rarely test whether it changes downstream decisions. We introduce Hear2Act, a 480-scenario protocol with unstated user needs and verifiable task success. Across matched scenarios, we keep the task and user needs fixed, varying whether an unmet need is stated in words or absent from the transcript while the user's reaction is conveyed through prosody, yielding Explicit lexical and Prosody-mediated conditions. We vary assistant access to transcript, audio, and an indication of whether the recommendation is resolved, and evaluate two audio-capable LLMs and five text LLMs on behavior and task outcomes. Our results show an asymmetry: prosodic information is valuable when the need is absent from the transcript, but adds little when that need is stated in words. Yet neither audio-capable LLM benefits much from raw audio: adding audio to the transcript changes task success only from 14.3% to 15.1%. When models infer from audio whether the recommendation remains unresolved and use that inference with the transcript, success rises to 39.6%, close to 40.5% with ground-truth state. This reveals a perception-action gap: raw audio barely helps, while making the information explicit in text enables much better decisions.
  </details>

- **[Trust Before Fusion: QIMG-7 and Source-Aware Resolution for Polluted Multimodal RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10798)**  `arXiv:2607.10798`  `cs.CL`  
  _Saadeldine Eletter, Owais Aijaz, Preslav Nakov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is often evaluated with clean evidence, yet real retrieval can return topically relevant but unreliable content: false text and misleading images from corrupted metadata, entity swaps, typographic overlays, semantic edits, adversarial patches, blends, or style transfer. We introduce QIMG-7, a controlled benchmark for multimodal retrieval pollution in multi-sentence factual QA, spanning four datasets, seven image-attack families, and 16 paired clean/polluted regimes, for 1,760 evaluation rows per method. Across four generator/gate stacks, naive multimodal fusion is brittle: in the main gpt-4o-mini stack, Full-MM support drops from 0.908 with clean text to 0.490 with polluted text, often making Parametric fallback safer than retrieval. We propose source-aware trust resolution (SATR), a training-free approach that compares Parametric, Text-only, and Full-MM candidate answers and selects among candidate answers or falls back based on source reliability. The Field-Selector variant achieves the best balanced score, 0.816, improving over Full-MM by 11.7 points and over the Cascaded Router by 2.7 points. Ablations show that, in this text-first setting, explicit text-reliability modeling is the dominant driver of these gains. Overall, in text-first factual QA with multimodal retrieval conflict, our results support selective trust rather than unconditional fusion. Artifacts are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Index SLM Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09885)**  `arXiv:2607.09885`  `cs.CL`  
  _Tianjiao Li, Lusheng Zhang, Shien He, Xiaojing Liu, Tianxing Yan, Mengran Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Index-1.9B, a series of open small language models developed at Bilibili. The series comprises four models: Index-1.9B-Base, a foundation model with 1.9 billion non-embedding parameters pre-trained on 2.8 trillion predominantly Chinese and English tokens; Index-1.9B-Pure, a control variant trained with an identical recipe but with all instruction-like data strictly filtered from the corpus; Index-1.9B-Chat, aligned from the base model with supervised fine-tuning and direct preference optimization; and Index-1.9B-Character, which augments the chat model with retrieval-augmented generation for few-shot role-playing customization. Pre-training employs a Warmup-Stable-Decay learning-rate schedule in which the concentration of curated data is raised substantially during the decay phase, together with a Norm-Head output layer that stabilizes training under large learning rates. On a suite of standard benchmarks covering examination, reasoning, mathematics, and code, Index-1.9B-Base attains an average score of 64.92, competitive with or exceeding open models of several times its size. We further report controlled studies on model depth, learning-rate magnitude and scheduling, the interaction between learning-rate decay and data quality, and the effect of including instruction data during pre-training, and we document an unexplained surge in benchmark performance midway through the constant-learning-rate phase. All models, together with evaluation code, are released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Multilingual Reasoning Cascades Need More Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.27306)**  `arXiv:2606.27306`  `cs.CL`  
  _Arnav Mazumder, Dengjia Zhang, Shuyue Stella Li, Yulia Tsvetkov, Niyati Bafna_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Translation cascades for reasoning translate the query from another language to English, reason in English, and translate the answer back to the original language. This is a competitive approach to multilingual reasoning, but structurally lossy, since each stage discards information later stages may need, including cues for cultural grounding, register, and disambiguation. We examine the benefits of a simple and training-free intervention: a context-aware translation cascade, which additionally provides the original question, the English translated question, and the reasoning trace to the context of the final translation module. We evaluate gains across nine multilingual benchmarks including various task types, three backbone models, and 285 high-, mid-, and low-resource languages, and demonstrate strong gains for open-ended generation across models and resource regimes. We show that the original language question carries most of the beneficial context. Our study emphasizes the need to better design information flow in machine translation cascades for mitigating error propagation, and provides a simple and actionable default strategy: preserve the original user question until the end of the pipeline.
  </details>

- **[AGORA: An Archive-Grounded Benchmark for Agentic Workplace Document Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24526)**  `arXiv:2606.24526`  `cs.CL`  
  _Honglin Guo, Qi Zhang, Yu Zhang, Weijie Li, Rui Zheng, Zhikai Lei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly deployed as agents that reason over documents rather than answer from parametric knowledge. We study archive-grounded reasoning: locating sparse evidence across a large, messy collection of workplace files, reconciling inconsistent terminology, units, and time conventions, and computing an answer. Existing benchmarks address only parts of this setting and none jointly stresses archive-groundedness, agentic exploration, and cross-domain coverage. We introduce Agora, a benchmark pairing 362 questions with eight domain collections of 9,664 authentic documents and 372M tokens, far exceeding any model's context window, so agents must explore deliberately rather than scan exhaustively. Agora is built by an agentic pipeline combining cross-document task synthesis, leakage-preventing obfuscation, and difficulty filtering. Evaluating eight models, we find the task far from solved: even the strongest reaches only 59.4% accuracy, with notable variation across domains.
  </details>

- **[EvoBrowseComp: Benchmarking Search Agents on Evolving Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13120)**  `arXiv:2606.13120`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yunhan Wang, Jiaan Wang, Lianzhe Huang, Xianfeng Zeng, Fandong Meng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Search Agents -- large language models augmented with search tools -- have intensified the need for future-proof evaluation benchmarks. Existing benchmarks such as BrowseComp rely on static knowledge, making them vulnerable to test-set contamination and parametric memorization. Consequently, models can achieve high scores through fact recall rather than genuine retrieval, obscuring true browsing competence via reasoning shortcuts.In this paper, we introduce EvoBrowseComp, an evolving benchmark of 400 English and 400 Chinese contamination-free complex questions synthesized via live-web traversal. To collect these questions, we design a three-agent collaborative framework: (1) a QA synthesis agent that retrieves fresh knowledge from the live web to synthesize QA pairs; (2) an information filtering agent that filters retrieved knowledge in terms of credibility and popularity to block parametric shortcuts; and (3) a high-level guidance agent that formalizes questions into reasoning graphs to reduce logical redundancy and shortcuts in synthesized QA pairs. Because the framework supports fully automated synthesis, EvoBrowseComp can be regularly updated to prevent data contamination and maintain temporal freshness. Extensive experiments confirm its great difficulty, requiring broad horizontal search. It establishes a scalable paradigm for auto-updatable, high-difficulty benchmarking that keeps pace with both evolving world knowledge and advancing agent capabilities.
  </details>

- **[ClinSeekAgent: Automating Multimodal Evidence Seeking for Agentic Clinical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20176)**  `arXiv:2605.20176`  `cs.CL`  
  _Juncheng Wu, Letian Zhang, Yuhan Wang, Haoqin Tu, Hardy Chen, Zijun Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) and agentic systems have shown promise for clinical decision support, but existing works largely assume that evidence has already been curated and handed to the model. Real-world clinical workflows instead require agents to actively seek, iteratively plan, and synthesize multimodal evidence from heterogeneous sources. In this paper, we introduce ClinSeekAgent, an automated agentic framework for dynamic multimodal evidence seeking that shifts the paradigm from passive evidence consumption to active evidence acquisition. Given a clinical task and access to raw patient data, ClinSeekAgent queries longitudinal EHR records and invokes imaging analysis tools when relevant; refines its hypotheses as new information emerges; and integrates the collected evidence into grounded clinical decisions. We validate ClinSeekAgent in two roles: as an inference-time agent for frontier LLMs and as a training pipeline for open-source models. For inference-time validation, we construct ClinSeek-Bench, which covers 13 text-only EHR task categories and 6 multimodal EHR--chest X-ray task categories, and pairs each question with both a Curated Input of pre-selected evidence and the raw patient data for automated evidence seeking.
