Without any benchmark-specific curation, ClinSeekAgent significantly outperforms curated input for both Claude Opus 5 and GPT-5.6 Sol, improving overall F1 from 66.5 to 68.8 and from 60.0 to 62.5 on multimodal tasks. The ablations show that the performance gain come from multi-round evidence seeking rather than from access to more of the patient record. For training-time validation, we fine-tune Qwen3.5-35B-A3B on ClinSeekAgent trajectories generated by GPT-5.6 Sol. The resulting ClinSeek-35B-A3B reaches 35.6 average F1 on AgentEHR-Bench, outperforming all evaluated open-source models, including ones more than ten times larger, as well as Claude Opus 5.
  </details>

- **[CompactAttention: Accelerating Chunked Prefill with Block-Union KV Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16839)**  `arXiv:2605.16839`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jiwon Song, Dongwon Jo, Beomseok Kang, Jae-Joon Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chunked prefill has become a widely adopted serving strategy for long-context large language models, but efficient attention computation in this regime remains challenging. Existing sparse attention methods are primarily designed for one-shot prefill and do not translate efficiently to chunked prefill: block-sparse kernels lose efficiency when the query length is limited by the chunk size, while fine-grained pattern search becomes costly when repeated over the accumulated KV cache at every chunk. QUOKA, a recent method that directly targets chunked prefill, avoids sparse-kernel overhead but relies on query-subsampled, token-level KV selection, which can miss query-specific KV entries and introduce explicit KV-copy overhead. To address these limitations, we propose CompactAttention, a chunked-prefill attention mechanism based on Block-Union KV Selection. CompactAttention treats 2D block-sparse masks as KV-selection signals rather than direct sparse-kernel execution plans, and converts them into GQA-aware per-group KV block tables through Q-block union and intra-group union. This construction produces block tables that preserve all KV blocks selected by the input masks under paged execution constraints, enabling selected KV blocks to be accessed in place without explicit KV compaction. On LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct, CompactAttention maintains accuracy close to dense attention on the RULER benchmark while delivering up to 2.79$\times$ attention speedup over dense attention and 1.25$\times$ over FlashPrefill at 128K context length under chunked prefill.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Perspective Evidence Synthesis and Reasoning for Unsupervised Multimodal Entity Linking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.20283)**  `arXiv:2604.20283`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mo Zhou, Jianwei Wang, Kai Wang, Helen Paik, Ying Zhang, Wenjie Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Entity Linking (MEL) is a fundamental task in data management that maps ambiguous mentions with diverse modalities to the multimodal entities in a knowledge base. However, most existing MEL approaches primarily focus on optimizing instance-centric features and evidence, leaving broader forms of evidence and their intricate interdependencies insufficiently explored. Motivated by the observation that human expert decision-making process relies on multi-perspective judgment, in this work, we propose MSR-MEL, a Multi-perspective Evidence Synthesis and Reasoning framework with Large Language Models (LLMs) for unsupervised MEL. Specifically, we adopt a two-stage framework: (1) Offline Multi-Perspective Evidence Synthesis constructs a comprehensive set of evidence. This includes instance-centric evidence capturing the instance-centric multimodal information of mentions and entities, group-level evidence that aggregates neighborhood information, lexical evidence based on string overlap ratio, and statistical evidence based on simple summary statistics. A core contribution of our framework is the synthesis of group-level evidence, which effectively aggregates vital neighborhood information by graph. We first construct LLM-enhanced contextualized graphs. Subsequently, different modalities are jointly aligned through an asymmetric teacher-student graph neural network. (2) Online Multi-Perspective Evidence Reasoning leverages the power of LLM as a reasoning module to analyze the correlation and semantics of the multi-perspective evidence to induce an effective ranking strategy for accurate entity linking without supervision. Extensive experiments on widely used MEL benchmarks demonstrate that MSR-MEL consistently outperforms state-of-the-art unsupervised methods. The source code of this paper was available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Language Models as Higher-Order Planning Formalizers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23844)**  `arXiv:2603.23844`  `cs.CL`  
  _Owen Jiang, Cassie Huang, Ashish Sabharwal, Li Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work provides overwhelming evidence that LLMs, even those trained to scale their reasoning trace, quickly deteriorate at planning as problems become more complex. LLM-as-Formalizers aim to address this by employing LLMs as a bridge to translate natural language descriptions into structured planning representations such as PDDL, which are then fed to a programmatic solver. We observe that its success may be overstated because planning problem descriptions in standard benchmarks often have a one-to-one mapping to PDDL, which departs from real use cases. To address this, we introduce the notion of unraveling problems where a natural yet succinct description translates into a very large PDDL representation. Using unraveling variants of four standard planning domains, we demonstrate that LLM Formalizers also do not always scale. We tackle this challenge by introducing a new paradigm, LLM-as-Higher-Order-Formalizer, where the LLM generates a high-level program that captures the recurrent logic within the description and in turn generates the larger PDDL representation. This decouples token output from the combinatorial explosion of the underlying formalization and search space, leading to improved performance for complex problems.
  </details>

- **[Overton Pluralistic Reinforcement Learning for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.20759)**  `arXiv:2602.20759`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yu Fu, Seongho Son, Ilija Bogunovic_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing alignment paradigms remain limited in capturing the pluralistic nature of human values. Overton Pluralism (OP) addresses this gap by generating responses with diverse perspectives from a single query. This paper introduces OP-GRPO (Overton Pluralistic Group Relative Policy Optimization), a reinforcement learning framework for implicit Overton Pluralism that enables a single LLM to produce pluralistic responses without explicit prompting or modular orchestration. Our workflow consists of two main steps: 1) Similarity estimator training, which fine-tunes a Sentence Transformer for OP tasks to provide a more accurate coverage evaluation of the given responses; and 2) OP-GRPO training, which incorporates this similarity estimator into a carefully designed dual-reward system to ensure both broad coverage of genuine human perspectives and the uniqueness of each perspective, thereby promoting diversity. Empirical results demonstrate that OP-GRPO achieves a "Small models, Big perspective coverage" effect: our trained Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct surpasses the GPT-OSS (20B) baseline with a 37.4% relative accuracy gain on the Natural Language Inference (NLI) benchmark. It also outperforms a modular-architecture baseline with a 19.1% relative improvement. Evaluations with GPT-5.4 as the LLM judge and OP-Benchmark further confirm the robustness of our approach.
  </details>

- **[PaperAudit-Bench: Benchmarking Error Detection in Research Papers for Critical Automated Peer Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.19916)**  `arXiv:2601.19916`  `cs.CL`  
  _Songjun Tu, Yiwen Ma, Jiahao Lin, Qichao Zhang, Xiangyuan Lan, Junfeng.Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can generate fluent peer reviews but often miss sparse, subtle, and document-level scientific errors. We introduce PaperAudit-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating paper-level error detection and error-aware automated review. It comprises (1) PaperAudit-Dataset, 220 high-quality AI/ML papers containing more than 2,200 controlled synthetic errors across eight categories, and (2) PaperAudit-Review, an evidence-aware framework that links structured error detection to review generation. Experiments across models, error categories, paper sections, and analysis depths show that sparse long-context auditing remains challenging for current LLMs. Conditioning review generation on explicit, manuscript-grounded error evidence produces more conservative and discriminative assessments that align more closely with human judgments than representative automated-review baselines. The dataset also supports post-training compact detectors through SFT and RL; Qwen3-14B achieves 55.8% F1 at Detection@1 under a fixed held-out protocol. A PubPeer transfer study and deployment in ScienceOne, a scientific foundation-model platform led by the Chinese Academy of Sciences, further illustrate practical applicability.Code and Dataset Page:this https URLScienceOne Manuscript Review:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Reasoning for Hierarchical Text Classification: The Case of Patents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.07167)**  `arXiv:2510.07167`  `cs.CL`  
  _Lekang Jiang, Wenjun Sun, Stephan Goetz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hierarchical text classification (HTC) assigns documents to multiple levels of a pre-defined taxonomy. Automated patent subject classification represents one of the hardest HTC scenarios because of professional difficulties and extensive labels. Prior approaches only output a flat label set, which offers little insight into the reason behind predictions. Therefore, we propose Reasoning for Hierarchical Classification (RHC), a novel framework that reformulates HTC as a step-by-step reasoning task to sequentially deduce hierarchical labels. RHC trains large language models (LLMs) in two stages: a cold-start stage that aligns outputs with chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning format and a reinforcement learning (RL) stage to enhance multi-step reasoning ability. RHC demonstrates four advantages in our experiments. (1) Effectiveness: RHC surpasses previous baselines and outperforms the supervised fine-tuning counterparts by approximately 3% in accuracy and macro F1. (2) Explainability: RHC produces natural-language justifications before prediction to facilitate human inspection. (3) Scalability: RHC scales favorably with model size with larger gains compared to standard fine-tuning. (4) Applicability: Beyond patents, we further demonstrate that RHC achieves state-of-the-art performance on other widely used HTC benchmarks, which highlights its broad applicability.
  </details>

- **[Enriching Patent Claim Generation with European Patent Dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.12568)**  `arXiv:2505.12568`  `cs.CL`  
  _Lekang Jiang, Chengzu Li, Stephan Goetz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Drafting patent claims is time-intensive, costly, and requires professional skill. Therefore, researchers have investigated large language models (LLMs) to assist inventors in writing claims. However, existing work has largely relied on datasets from the United States Patent and Trademark Office (USPTO). To enlarge research scope regarding various jurisdictions, drafting conventions, and legal standards, we introduce EPD, a European patent dataset. EPD presents rich textual data and structured metadata to support multiple patent-related tasks, including claim generation. This dataset enriches the field in three critical aspects: (1) Jurisdictional diversity: Patents from different offices vary in legal and drafting conventions. EPD fills a critical gap by providing a benchmark of European patents to enable more comprehensive evaluation. (2) Quality improvement: EPD offers high-quality granted patents with finalized and legally approved texts, whereas others consist of patent applications that are unexamined or provisional. Experiments show that LLMs fine-tuned on EPD significantly outperform those trained on previous datasets and even GPT-4o in claim quality and cross-domain generalization. (3) Real-world simulation: We propose a difficult subset of EPD to better reflect real-world challenges. Results reveal that all tested LLMs perform substantially worse on challenging samples, which highlights the need for future research.
  </details>

- **[VLA-Rolling: Fewer Denoising Steps, Better Control via Adaptive Action Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25043)**  `arXiv:2610.25043`  `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Sicheng He, Yi Pan, Qiuting Yu, Jiaming Huang, Qi Lu, Shuyan Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flow-matching vision-language-action (VLA) models generate an action chunk by iteratively denoising Gaussian noise, but typically execute only a short prefix before replanning with updated observations. Under this receding-horizon paradigm, the unexecuted remainder---the action tail---is discarded, and the next chunk is regenerated from fresh noise. We show that this discarded tail retains transferable denoising progress: action-tail initialization can match or outperform a native 10-step Gaussian baseline using fewer denoising steps. However, the optimal denoising budget varies substantially across samples, indicating that tail reuse should be adapted to the updated environment. Based on this observation, we introduce VLA-Rolling, a lightweight inference framework that reuses action tails through three stages: action-tail inheritance, per-action confidence estimation, and confidence-guided refinement through renoising and denoising budget allocation, while the underlying VLA remains frozen. Across $\pi_{0.5}$, SmolVLA, X-VLA, and MolmoAct2 on LIBERO, MetaWorld, LIBERO-Plus, and RoboTwin 2.0, VLA-Rolling reduces action-chunk inference cost in all 16 model--benchmark pairs, achieving up to a $1.48\times$ speedup, while improving success rate in 14 pairs by up to $15.08$ percentage points. These results show that action tails are not merely discarded predictions, but reusable intermediate states that enable more efficient and accurate VLA inference. These results demonstrate that the action tail can support the generation of better actions with fewer denoising steps. Our project is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Autonomous intelligence for mining productivity: a multi-agent AI approach to operational value creation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26369)**  `arXiv:2610.26369`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _N Guerin, A Dehoux, M Nakhla_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Integrated Remote Operation Centres (IROCs) are emerging as the architectural frontier of Industry 4.0 for capital-intensive sectors such as mining, oil and gas, and energy. Yet two intertwined gaps constrain their evolution towards full autonomy. First, no integrated agentic architecture has been proposed for mining IROCs that orchestrates Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition systems, digital twins, predictive analytics and human-machine interfaces under safety-critical constraints. Second, no evaluation framework jointly addresses the real-time, Health-Safety-Environment, regulatory-auditability and cost-per-decision requirements that govern industrial agentic deployments. This paper makes four contributions. It synthesises industry needs and quantified gains documented in analogous industries that motivate an agentic approach to mining IROCs. It introduces EI2ROC, a three-tier multi-agent architecture combining a Large Language Model orchestrator with specialised monitoring and production-capacityprediction agents communicating through a Model Context Protocol-style tool interface. It provides a critical review of agentic evaluation along six orthogonal dimensions and identifies the industrial blind spot of current benchmarks. Finally, it proposes a multi-dimensional benchmarking framework for industrial agentic AI comprising scenario library, harness, metrics and humancalibration protocol. The paper states explicitly which components are implemented in the current prototype, which belong to the proposed reference design and which remain future work, and reports preliminary cost and latency observations from an operating site. Together, EI2ROC and the benchmarking framework constitute a methodological blueprint transferable across CAPEX intensive industries.
  </details>

- **[Explore or Confirm? LLM Agents Misallocate Measurements Under Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25111)**  `arXiv:2610.25111`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Abdul Karim, Matt Keon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As model reasoning gets cheaper, the physical experiment becomes the slow and costly step in discovery. Each experiment returns a noisy reading: a high value can mean a good candidate or a lucky draw. With a fixed budget, an AI agent must keep choosing between exploring a new candidate and confirming a promising one by re-testing it. We measure that choice in Wellocate, a simulated assay campaign with known true values. It is built on a generated landscape and on two real protein datasets, GB1 and PhoQ, scrambled so no model can answer from memory. We test five language models as minimal agents, with no tools and no memory beyond the history in each prompt. They misallocate in both directions. Three over-confirm, spending most of the budget re-testing. On the generated landscape one under-confirms, trusting single readings, and there every model ends with a worse final pick than a basic scripted strategy. Each failure has its own cause. The over-confirmers run short of new candidates worth trying: a list of untried candidates cuts their re-testing and, on real protein data, makes two of them pick much better. A running summary of their readings or a description of the landscape changes it by at most five percentage points. For the under-confirmer, re-testing its top candidates in a final round, replayed on its own campaigns, recovers only about a quarter of what it loses by picking the wrong candidate. This suggests confirmation has to run through the whole campaign. We release Wellocate as a benchmark and reinforcement-learning environment with frozen splits and scripted baselines. With it, anyone can measure how an agent splits its budget before trusting it with real experiments, and train agents to split it better. Code, data and the environment are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[FDE-Bench: Evaluating End-to-End Delivery from Underspecified Real-World Business Requests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14539)**  `arXiv:2610.14539`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Huaiming Li, Can Huang, Chufan Wu, Danyu Liu, Xiaomin Yuan, Qinze Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world business requests often leave key requirements unstated because frontline practitioners struggle to articulate rules they know from experience. Forward-deployed engineers (FDEs) must elicit these requirements through dialogue and iteratively optimize solutions until they meet customer acceptance criteria. Existing optimization benchmarks typically start from explicit objectives, while clarification benchmarks often introduce information gaps into existing tasks. We introduce FDE-Bench, the first benchmark built from real customer projects to evaluate agents on end-to-end delivery tasks that require interactive requirement clarification and iterative optimization. Its 49 cases span combinatorial optimization and machine learning. Clarification targets are distilled from discussions between frontline practitioners and FDEs. Agents elicit missing requirements from a simulated customer, and case-specific evaluators assess final artifacts against acceptance criteria using customer-accepted FDE solutions as quality references. Across 18 agent configurations, the highest mean delivery score is only 0.5510, compared with an FDE reference of 1.0. The highest delivery success rate (DSR), defined as the fraction of planned runs reaching this reference, is only 6.12%. These results show that reliable FDE-level end-to-end delivery remains out of reach for the evaluated agents on underspecified real-world business tasks. The benchmark project page is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[VACS: Value-Aligned Compositional Shielding for Multi-Agent Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26135)**  `arXiv:2609.26135`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Yiyao Zhang, Diksha Goel, Hussain Ahmad_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent reasoning systems in high-stakes domains must be both accurate and safe, yet agents often follow heterogeneous value priorities (e.g., rigor, conciseness, safety), causing conflicting recommendations. Existing methods do not jointly provide: (i) principled inference of each agent's implicit values from behavior, (ii) compositional formal safety guarantees without full online communication, and (iii) value-aware conflict resolution with faithful explanations. We present VACS (Value-Aligned Compositional Shielding), a four-layer framework addressing all three. Layer 1 learns value-dimension rewards from pairwise preferences using Bradley-Terry modeling and infers per-agent value weights via deep MaxEnt IRL. Layer 2 encodes value constraints in a Lean-inspired DSL and synthesizes compositional assume-guarantee shields for runtime safety. Layer 3 resolves disagreement through nucleolus-based credit allocation and Hamiltonian consensus optimization under long-term value constraints. Layer 4 extracts a critical reasoning path from co-state sensitivities and generates formally grounded natural-language explanations. Our contribution is primarily a unified systems design with formalized interfaces and operational guarantees at the verifier-constrained decision level, rather than a complete end-to-end formal proof of all language-model internals. In controlled proof-of-concept evaluations with role-conditioned agent panels on NEJM-AI QA, MathInstruct-Subset, and a cybersecurity incident-response benchmark (CyberSec-Eval), VACS outperforms strong baselines in accuracy (85.4%, 95.0%, and 90.0%) while reducing logical inconsistency rates to near zero.
  </details>

- **[VeriCUA: Learning Proactive Verification for Reliable GUI Agent Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26469)**  `arXiv:2610.26469`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhizhou Wang, Yaxi Lu, Zhong Zhang, Zikun Hu, Xin Cong, Yankai Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning for computer-use agents increasingly relies on proactive reward agents that interact with the post-execution GUI environment to verify what recorded screenshots cannot reveal. The reliability of such verification hinges on two coupled decisions: what to check and when to stop. Existing methods leave these decisions to prompted foundation models. However, neither scaling up the model nor providing few-shot examples reliably improves verification: prompted Judges ranging from 4B to GPT-5.4 all plateau at 68%-70% macro-average agreement. This is because the value of a check depends on the environment rather than the task alone, and becomes apparent only through execution. Learning from execution is itself constrained, as the Actor's terminal state cannot be faithfully replicated during training, allowing only one verification attempt per state. We introduce VeriCUA, a proactive reward agent that learns its verification strategy from these single attempts. A trainable Judge decides what to check and when to stop, while a frozen Probe executes the checks in the live environment. Online reinforcement learning optimizes the Judge for both verdict correctness and efficient Probe use, with returns normalized across terminal states. Trained only on OSWorld and evaluated without further training on MobileWorld and WebArena-Verified, VeriCUA achieves the highest reward agreement across all three benchmarks and lies on the agreement-cost Pareto frontier among proactive verifiers. It surpasses few-shot GPT-5.4 by 5.36 points with 47.6%-59.8% fewer Probe tokens, and even a 1B VeriCUA exceeds every prompted Judge. As the reward for Actor RL, it raises task success by 3.21 points overall and 5.40 points on held-out multi-application tasks. These results identify the verification strategy, not verifier scale, as the key to reliable GUI rewards.
  </details>

- **[CODA: Cohort- and Drift-aware Foundation Model for Multimodal Clinical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25871)**  `arXiv:2610.25871`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Changshuo Liu, Jiaqi Zhu, Wenqiao Zhang, Xiaokui Xiao, Beng Chin Ooi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in multimodal foundation models have demonstrated strong performance across diverse clinical tasks. However, existing approaches predominantly operate at the individual sample level and fail to model the interplay between population-level cohort patterns and patient-specific variation that underlie real-world clinical reasoning. In practice, clinicians routinely reason through patient cohorts sharing similar demographic, physiological, or pathological characteristics, while accounting for patient-specific deviations from the group prototype. Motivated by this gap, we propose CODA, a cohort- and drift-aware foundation model for multimodal clinical reasoning. CODA explicitly integrates cohort structure into the modeling pipeline through two complementary mechanisms: cohort-aware population stratification and drift-aware individual adaptation. In particular, we leverage reinforcement learning to discover clinically meaningful patient cohorts, and further model each patient's deviation from cohort prototypes as a structured drift signal encoding fine-grained individual heterogeneity. These cohort- and drift-aware representations are incorporated into the foundation model to enable reasoning that is simultaneously informed by population-level patterns and sensitive to individual-level variation. Extensive experiments on real-world electronic health record (EHR) benchmarks demonstrate that CODA achieves consistent effectiveness and strong generalizability across diverse clinical tasks, including closed-QA, open-QA, and report generation.
  </details>

- **[Spectral Adaptive Graph Transformer for Efficient and Scalable Traffic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25140)**  `arXiv:2610.25140`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Amirhossein Ghaffari, Susanna Pirttikangas, Ekaterina Gilman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Traffic forecasting models based on spectral graph convolution offer an interpretable account of how information is mixed between sensors, but they rely on an eigendecomposition of the graph Laplacian. It costs cubic time, is fixed by the road network, and provides a symmetric operator that cannot represent the directed propagation of traffic congestion. Attention-based spatio-temporal transformers remove these limitations but generate a dense sensor-by-sensor attention map at each layer and time step, so memory, rather than accuracy, becomes the binding constraint on large networks. We introduce the spectral adaptive graph transformer, whose spatial operator is learned rather than derived from the Laplacian, directed rather than symmetric, and conditioned on the current traffic state rather than a static state, at a cost linear in the number of sensors. A sparse road graph diffusion branch provides the strictly local propagation that a low-rank operator cannot express, and a zero-initialized gate learns the balance between the two. Before spatial mixing, a unit-mean-gain frequency-bin attention module reweights each sensor's short-term periodicities according to its identity and current context. Across six public benchmarks spanning 170 to 1843 sensors, evaluated over three random seeds, the proposed model attains the lowest error of any single end-to-end model on one benchmark and remains competitive with the strongest current attention models on the others, while using about a quarter of their memory and training about twice as fast.
  </details>

- **[MM-LeJEPA: Correlated Gaussian Regularization for Multimodal Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24681)**  `arXiv:2610.24681`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Haowen Bai, Rui Gong$, Meichen Liu, Ming-Xuan Weng, Bihan Wen, Kim-Hui Yap_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LeJEPA derives an isotropic Gaussian embedding target by minimizing, under its task assumptions, a Fisher information criterion that controls the task-averaged squared prediction bias. This enables unimodal self-supervised learning without reconstruction decoders, exponential-moving-average teachers, or contrastive negatives. However, its isotropic task prior does not account for the cross-modal structure of multimodal tasks that read shared content. We introduce MM-LeJEPA by extending LeJEPA's task assumptions to multimodal learning. Specifically, we model multimodal tasks as strength-weighted combinations of components that read independent information from the two modalities and components that read the same content from both. Under explicit gradient-moment, correspondence, and symmetry assumptions, we derive a correlated Gaussian target that minimizes a weighted Fisher information functional under a fixed total variance constraint. An analytic transform adapts Sketched Isotropic Gaussian Regularization (SIGReg) to this target, yielding a two-term objective that combines distributional regularization with local-to-global prediction. The extension adds a single scalar hyperparameter, the target cross-modal correlation, and retains the simplicity of LeJEPA. On public audio-visual classification benchmarks, our results are competitive with representative open-source methods. Code will be released.
  </details>

- **[Good Judgment, Bad Advice: Decision-Model Controllers Raise Agent Cost Without Improving Success](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24649)**  `arXiv:2610.24649`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Naman Vats_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent scaffolds increasingly delegate small decisions (whether to stop, verify, or change approach) to cheaper models. We ask whether a typed decision model, Jev 1.13, consulted only at deterministic decision points, makes a Claude Sonnet 5.5 terminal agent cheaper without making it worse. In a pre-specified study on Terminal-Bench 2.1, we hold one agent loop fixed and vary only the controller: none, deterministic rules, a small LLM (Claude Haiku 5.5) answering the same questions, Jev, and a placebo that samples Jev's development-phase choice frequencies at the same moments. On 21 held-out tasks (315 trials), Jev raised cost per attempt by 19% (geometric-mean ratio 1.19, 95% CI 1.02-1.36) at an identical pass rate (73.0% vs. 73.0%). Jev was cheaper than the small-LLM controller (ratio 0.81) but 22% more expensive than the placebo, and no pass-rate difference between arms was detectable. Offline, Jev ranked finish requests better than the alternatives (AUROC 0.78 vs. 0.62 for rules and 0.60 for the small LLM) but was poorly calibrated: its mean probability that a run was complete was 0.50 when 87% of those runs passed, so it asked the agent to verify work that was already correct. No controller ever chose to abandon a run. Informative decision signals did not become useful interventions; we discuss what that would require.
  </details>

- **[Cortex: A Fixed-Point Theory of Governed Coding Agents - Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24255)**  `arXiv:2610.24255`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Marius-Constantin Dinu, Florian Zeba_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A coding agent combines a large language model (LLM) with a harness that plans, edits, and executes code. We study Cortex, a supervisory layer for pre-execution governance, requirement tracking, and iterative validation and repair. We formalize requirement closure on a finite lattice. For a fixed requirement set and a sound, monotone validation rule with persistent evidence, exhaustive iteration reaches the least fixed point with no more strict increases than there are requirements. Fair consequence scheduling reaches the same closure. A separate stochastic model bounds expected completion time under a uniform positive-progress assumption. These results depend on their stated assumptions; artifact repair, fallible validation, and finite execution budgets do not satisfy them automatically. We define Gauntlet's trajectory-similarity and build-gated scores, attack-success estimators, Wilson intervals, and case-cluster bootstrap summaries. Effective-feedback accounting counts newly certified requirements, rather than estimating their information value or utility. The five evaluation families support raw-versus-governed comparisons. We report retained benchmark observations, including rescoring of existing artifacts; constructed outcomes are excluded. Unequal observation sets and incomplete provenance prevent causal claims about governance or measured long-horizon scaling.
  </details>

- **[Training-Free Prompt Selection for Efficient Reinforcement Learning With Partial Rollouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24229)**  `arXiv:2610.24229`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Lei Jiang, Honghui Bao, Yue Zhou, Zhengyao Gu, Langzhou He, Natalie Parde_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has emerged as a prominent paradigm for training reasoning models, but its computational cost makes efficient data selection increasingly important. Since training examples contribute unequally to learning, selecting a small, informative subset can offer a more efficient alternative to using the entire data pool. The training-free selection method estimates prompt value with hidden states from complete greedy rollouts. However, in our datasets, $26$--$32\%$ of these rollouts fail to terminate within the generation budget. We introduce LIFT, a training-free data selector that identifies impactful prompts using only partial reasoning rollouts. At its core, LIFT uses Partial Rollout Context Lift (PRCL), a score inspired by pointwise $\mathcal{V}$-information that accumulates token-level log-likelihood ratios between a question-conditioned context and a null context with the question removed. This enables prompt scoring from a short rollout prefix rather than a complete trajectory. Across Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B and DeepSeek-R1-Distill-1.5B, prefixes retaining at least 90\% and 95\% of the full-rollout PRCL score, respectively, require only 26\% and 4.7\% of the tokens generated by full rollouts. Compared with SHIFT, LIFT reduces selection cost by 3.7--9.1$\times$ while achieving competitive downstream RLVR performance, with the highest six-benchmark average in three of six settings.
  </details>

- **[Unified Multiparadigm Fusion Intelligence for Rare Disease Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24024)**  `arXiv:2610.24024`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jian Yang, Peiling Liu, Songda Li, Yu Cao, Yuqing Feng, Lan Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rare diseases (RDs) affect an estimated 300 million people globally, leaving patients to navigate a protracted diagnostic odyssey constrained by clinical scarcity and fragmented medical knowledge. Artificial intelligence (AI) has progressively advanced RD diagnostics, from symbolic methods and deep learning to large language model (LLM)-based systems. However, each paradigm carries structural limitations: symbolic methods are bounded by knowledge completeness, neural models sacrifice interpretability, and LLM-based systems are vulnerable to evaluation biases like data leakage. In this study, we introduce FusionRare, a unified multiparadigm fusion framework integrating knowledge-driven symbolic reasoning, deep-learning pattern recognition, case-based patient retrieval, and LLM-based heuristic inference via statistical meta-aggregation and knowledge-enhanced agentic reflection. Evaluated on 2,528 strictly isolated clinical cases, FusionRare achieves state-of-the-art RD prioritization, attaining a macro-averaged Recall @1 of 51% for phenotype-only diagnosis and 79% for phenotype-genotype joint analysis. These results represent decisive advantages over the advanced agentic system DeepRare, frontier LLMs including GPT 5.5, and specialized tools like PhenoBrain and LIRICAL under stringent anti-leakage protocols. A randomized, crossover clinician-AI validation demonstrated that FusionRare elevates the true diagnostic rank in 45% of decisions, improves physician confidence in 62%, and reduces time to diagnosis by 21%. These findings indicate that reconciling these orthogonal AI paradigms significantly enhances diagnostic capabilities, bridging the gap between computational benchmarks and actionable clinical reliability. More broadly, this work provides a replicable framework for deploying trustworthy diagnostic AI across complex, high-stakes, and underserved medical domains.
  </details>

- **[How Do Agentic LLMs Decide to Call Tools? A Tool-Call Vector Shaped by Suppression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09624)**  `arXiv:2610.09624`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Xijie Gong, Tingxu Han, Jiahao Zhang, Wei Song, Ziqi Ding, Hanqi Yan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool calling, invoking external tools on demand, is central to agentic LLMs, yet the mechanism that decides whether a model calls a tool or responds directly remains poorly understood. Agentic prompts are long and heavily scaffolded, combining role instructions, tool schemas, format templates, and the user's request across hundreds of tokens, creating a noisy, highly entangled context in which no single controllable variable for mechanistic analysis is obvious. To obtain such a variable, we propose a method that converts complex agentic prompts into minimal contrastive pairs in which a single request verb determines the tool-call decision: replacing an execution-verb (e.g., \textit{write}) with an analysis-verb (e.g., \textit{discuss}) reliably flips the decision, suggesting it is mediated by a compact internal state. We construct 500 such paired prompts across Python, Java, and C++ (300 for mechanistic analysis, 200 held out for evaluation). We trace the decision to a vector, $\mu_\Delta$, that is both causally necessary and sufficient and generalizes beyond the discovery prompts to native multi-turn $\tau^2$-Bench trajectories and verb-free requests. Behavioral ablations show that the scaffold establishes a tool-call prior; Transcoder decomposition then reveals that analysis verbs suppress this prior through features signaling that tool use is unnecessary, whereas execution verbs largely leave it intact. Downstream scaffold-reading attention heads and MLP features read out the resulting state, and the same mechanism recurs across seven models from the Qwen, Mistral, and Granite families. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Stashbird: Episode-Grounded Lifecycle Reconciliation for Conversational Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34242)**  `arXiv:2609.34242`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Chidera Biringa, Lucas Yannul, Xiaowen Wang, Marco Ayala, Nicholas Yi, Alex Moyse, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agents require memory that remains consistent as conversational evidence changes. We present Stashbird, an episode-grounded memory system that uses provenance to reconcile derived graph and retrieval state during updates and deletion. Repeated evidence extends support, corrections supersede prior active relations, and deletion removes unsupported state while preserving relations with remaining support. Under fixed semantic decisions, reconciliation produces correct final states in all 20 update sequences, compared with eight without reconciliation, and satisfies the defined checks across 263 deletion interventions. Across four long-term memory benchmarks, Stashbird achieves competitive question-answering accuracy and strong model efficiency. On LoCoMo, it uses 76.4x fewer ingestion prompt tokens than Graphiti and 8.1x fewer retrieval prompt tokens than reproduced Hindsight
  </details>

- **[LayerRoute: Action-Conditioned Mixture-of-Layers Routing for Vision-Language-Action Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06079)**  `arXiv:2609.06079`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zheng Lu, Haoran Liao, Wanqi Zhong, Yunhe Ni, Lijie Wang, Xingjie Fan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policies leverage pretrained vision-language models (VLMs) to guide action generation for robot control. VLMs provide hierarchical visual-semantic representations that evolve across layers, from local visual geometry to abstract, language-aligned semantics; different manipulation tasks may therefore require different mixtures of layer representations. Meanwhile, the action module maintains intermediate representations that evolve throughout action computation and may provide useful information for subsequent decisions. However, existing VLA interfaces offer limited flexibility in representation access: VLM information is exposed through fixed layer assignments for each action layer, while intermediate action states are only propagated implicitly through residual streams without explicit reuse. We introduce LayerRoute, an action-conditioned representation routing interface that enables adaptive access to VLM layers and action representations. The Layer Mixture Router dynamically forms mixtures of cached VLM representations, while Action-State Reread reuses earlier action representations. Across diverse simulation and real-world benchmarks, LayerRoute consistently improves StarVLA-$\pi$ and $\pi_{0.5}$, achieving up to 7.2 gains on LIBERO Long with only 0.31% / 3.87% additional parameters. Ablation studies validate the benefit of action-conditioned layer routing, while routing analyses reveal structured allocation patterns across action layers and task settings.
  </details>

- **[MSCR: Jointly Balancing Utilization Across Modalities and Discovering Synergistic Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24395)**  `arXiv:2610.24395`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Xinyu Chen, Liangjian Wen, Jiang Duan, Dongkai Wang, Yong Dai, Jiayu Bai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In multimodal learning, modality imbalance often lets one modality dominate optimization and keeps the model from fully exploiting cross-modal interactions. Balance-oriented methods such as the Multimodal Competition Regularizer (MCR) perform well by alleviating this imbalance during training. These dynamic balancing methods, however, mainly adjust modality contributions and do not consider how imbalance affects the formation of synergistic information during fusion. Synergy is a major source of multimodal advantage, because it is predictive gain that cannot be recovered from unimodal evidence alone. We propose the Multimodal Synergistic Competition Regularizer (MSCR), a fusion method that jointly promotes balanced modality utilization and the discovery of synergistic information. MSCR has two complementary objectives. An anti-dominance objective suppresses excessive unimodal influence and leaves room for synergistic information, and a synergy objective encourages the fused predictor to outperform a non-synergistic unimodal reference. An adaptive gate further concentrates the regularization on samples that need stronger collaborative modeling. Experiments on five benchmarks show that MSCR consistently improves performance over MCR and strengthens synergistic capability. It raises conditional recovery, the accuracy of the fused prediction on samples that the unimodal predictors misclassify, as well as the share of the model's output that InterSHAP attributes to cross-modal interactions. Its gain in conditional recovery persists when the fusion module is Transformer-based. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Self-Improving Video-to-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24177)**  `arXiv:2610.24177`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Hyunwoo Ryu, Eric Ming Chen, Max Wilcoxson, Vincent Sitzmann_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video generative models are an emerging tool for embodied agents: they can serve as scalable visual planners, generating videos that depict how to complete previously unseen tasks. However, executing these plans typically requires a video-to-action or "inverse dynamics" model trained on large, human-collected video-action datasets, severely limiting scalability. We introduce Mirror, a self-supervised approach that learns video-to-action mappings through environment interactions, without prior action data or hand-crafted rewards. Mirror learns by attempting to follow a visual plan by executing its best guess of the underlying actions in the environment. Mirror then trains on the resulting video-action pairs, incrementally self-improving and following the visual planner more closely. Across Atari, OGBench, and DeepMind Control, Mirror outperforms leading learning-from-observation methods with learned video plans, and achieves near-expert performance with oracle plans. On Minecraft BASALT, it outperforms methods with access to expert actions or hand-engineered task knowledge. In Minecraft survival, it is the first method to acquire an iron pickaxe through self-improvement alone, without rewards or expert action labels. In the offline setting, with expert action labels but no environment interaction, it sets a new state of the art in obtaining diamonds. Our results suggest that advances in video models can translate into increasingly capable agents without a corresponding need for hand-crafted rewards or large collections of expert action data.
  </details>

- **[Omni-Interactive Universal Embedder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27044)**  `arXiv:2608.27044`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Wei-Yao Wang, Kazuya Tateishi, Shuyang Cui, Christian Simon, Takashi Shibuya, Shusuke Takahashi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal representation learning has been shifting from traditional two-tower architectures to large language model (LLM)-based embedders due to their strong instruction-following capabilities. Despite this progress, existing approaches primarily focus on language and image modalities, which also remain the dominant modalities for user-conditioned interactions in current embedders. In this paper, we propose the first Omni-Interactive Universal Embedder (OmniUE), which not only learns a unified embedding space across text, video, and audio by leveraging intermediate-layer representations from dedicated learnable tokens, but also supports omni-interactive querying, enabling users to provide inputs in the form of text, visual regions of interest, and audio spans. Within OmniUE, visual and audio segmenters process diverse user interactions and integrate them with an omni-LLM to produce user-conditioned any-to-any embeddings via context aggregation. To evaluate OmniUE's omni-interactive capabilities, we introduce OmniCHOIR, benchmarking models for omni-interactive compositional audio retrieval based on the given text, video, and audio as well as unimodal or multimodal interaction prompts. OmniUE consistently surpasses state-of-the-art baselines across diverse modalities, with average improvements of 10.4% on textual-interactive video benchmarks (MMEB-v2-video), 1.0% on audio tasks (MAEB), 83.1% on visual-interactive benchmarks (SCaR), and 21.9% on our omni-interactive OmniCHOIR benchmark. We believe that jointly advancing omni-modal representation learning and omni-interactive querying paves the way toward universal embedders. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Neuro-Inspired Inhibitory Message Passing for Accurate and Token-Efficient Multi-Agent Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24718)**  `arXiv:2610.24718`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.MA`  
  _Nima H. Siboni, Magdalena Springer, Vahid Rostami_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent systems promise to solve problems by bringing together information beyond the reach of any individual agent. Retrieval and tool use can distribute this information across agents, making communication essential to collaboration. A central challenge is keeping crucial evidence visible in each agent's context as messages accumulate. Repeated information can crowd out rare, decisive evidence. Models of cortical circuits suggest a principle for addressing this imbalance: inhibition can selectively suppress shared inputs while preserving sensitivity to weaker signals. We adapt this principle into inhibitory message passing, a training-free communication router that cancels redundant evidence separately for each evidence sentence and receiver. Each round, it forwards evidence sentences restated in agents' messages, delivering each at most once to receivers whose own evidence lacks it and excluding the senders' reasoning and answers. Against standard message passing, accuracy in peer groups rose by 39-77 percentage points across three benchmarks, and tokens per run fell by 10-46%. Gains held across seven open models spanning four to thirty-two billion parameters and two commercial models, in groups up to sixty-four agents, and across multi-agent architectures including peer-to-peer, orchestrator-worker, and multilevel hierarchical organizations. These findings support a scalable design pattern for more accurate and token-efficient multi-agent reasoning.
  </details>

- **[ADIAS: Automated Design of Interactive Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06410)**  `arXiv:2608.06410`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.MA`  
  _Lekang Jiang, Bohan Tang, Stephan Goetz, Yiwen Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-evolving agents can improve without updating model weights by iteratively optimizing the harness based on evaluation feedback. Existing methods are largely candidate-centric: they record evolution outcomes (which agent variants have been produced and how they perform), rather than evolution progress (which issues, i.e., recurring failure patterns, have been fixed and which remain). Such methods pose two difficulties for cumulative harness evolution. First, repair context is scattered across candidate records and must be reconstructed every round, which leads to inefficient, redundant, or misdirected revisions. Second, because candidates are evaluated as a whole, partial fixes across different agents are difficult to combine, while ineffective changes persist alongside beneficial ones. To address these problems, we formulate issue-centric agent optimization, which explicitly carries forward evolutionary progress through a persistent issue state. We instantiate this paradigm in ADIAS, a harness-evolution framework with two key components. An Issue Manager maintains the issue state with stable issue identities, lifecycle status, supporting evidence, and intervention-outcome histories. An Issue-Guided Optimizer proposes repair targets from the issue state and performs focused harness revision. On five interactive benchmarks, ADIAS outperforms the strongest baseline by 25.2% on average and achieves consistent gains across four backbone models. Controlled ablations show that removing the Issue Manager or the Issue-Guided Optimizer reduces performance by up to 40.7%.
  </details>

- **[Agent World Model in the Wild: Computer-Use Agent Environment Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26828)**  `arXiv:2610.26828`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhaoyang Wang, Qianhui Wu, Haorui Wang, Simon Yu, Baolin Peng, Canwen Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent learning is shifting from learning from human data toward learning through interaction with the world. However, scaling such learning directly in real-world computer systems is challenging and can introduce substantial security risks. We therefore propose Agent World Model in the Wild (WildAWM), a scalable pipeline for synthesizing controllable environments and tasks for training general computer-use agents (CUAs). Whereas existing approaches typically specialize in a single agent interaction interface, we construct executable environments that jointly support graphical user interfaces, model context protocol services, and command-line interfaces, allowing agents to flexibly combine visual interaction, tool use, and code execution. WildAWM is entirely powered by open-source models, demonstrating that high-quality computer-use environments can be generated without proprietary frontier models. Beyond environments, we address three recurring challenges in task synthesis: ensuring solvability, maintaining sufficient difficulty, and producing reliable verifiers, by introducing execution-based quality gates that combine heterogeneous agent rollouts, cross-judge consistency checks, and counterfactual probes of judgment robustness. The resulting pipeline synthesizes 341 long-horizon environment-task pairs that can require over 100 agent steps to solve and remain challenging even for frontier models, with GPT-6-Astra solving only 60% of them. We train two Qwen models of different sizes exclusively in these synthetic worlds. Both models achieve substantial gains across 8 out-of-distribution benchmarks spanning web, claw, and computer-use agents, demonstrating that learning in WildAWM's synthesized worlds generalizes broadly to unseen computer-use tasks. The code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[When the harness decides success: state that cannot be re-observed](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26632)**  `arXiv:2610.26632`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Raimondas Lencevicius_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent harnesses decide what a language model sees across turns: they drop old turns, summarize them, or replay the model's own earlier reasoning. We ask when these choices change whether an agent succeeds, not only what it costs, and report three findings. First, in a pre-registered synthetic probe, truncating the context window costs a low-cost reasoning model nothing in success when a needed fact can be looked up again (it looks the fact up instead), but drops its success to chance when the fact cannot be re-observed: the two penalties differ by 92.5 percentage points (90% CI [89.5, 95.5]). A pre-registered replication on one model from a second provider showed the same contrast. Once the fact was lost, that model chose no answer rather than guessing. Second, on ARC-AGI-3, where the benchmark's Standard harness trails its Provider Adapter harness by a wide margin, we decompose that gap one component at a time, for one model on four games. Summarizing old turns instead of dropping them, while keeping the Standard harness's invitation to take notes, recovers 86% of a 63-point gap without replaying the model's reasoning. The same harness without the invitation recovers 43%, and adding the provider's native replay of the model's reasoning recovers the remaining 57%. These are point estimates from one counted run per configuration and game, and the replay share depends on a single run. Third, in the benchmark's published runs and in ours, the Adapter's advantage in the probability of clearing a level it has reached is larger at deep levels than at early ones. This largely restates where runs stopped and does not separate a growing advantage from a constant one.
  </details>

- **[RouterWorld: Scaling Conversational AI with Latency-Aware LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26511)**  `arXiv:2610.26511`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Jinyang Li, Denis Emelin, Simone Tedeschi, Weiwei Cheng, Cristina Morariu, Haytham Assem, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conversational AI systems built on Large Language Models (LLMs) require routing mechanisms that balance answer quality and response latency at scale. However, existing LLM routing benchmarks assume full observability of all candidate model outcomes, under-emphasize latency as a first-class metric, and fragment evaluation across heterogeneous protocols misaligned with real-world settings. We introduce RouterWorld, a comprehensive benchmark for LLM routing in conversational GenAI. RouterWorld unifies 371k single-turn responses and 76k multi-turn agentic trajectories, adopts natural user interactions with a standardized LLM-as-a-judge protocol, and decouples efficiency from transient pricing via hardware-grounded latency annotations. Crucially, RouterWorld formalizes routing under partial observability through two setups: Cold-Start, which restricts learning to sparse, static logs, and Voyager, which models continuous data streams where routing decisions shape future training distributions. Evaluating 13 routing baselines, we find that most routers over-emphasize accuracy while ignoring latency under partial observability. Code and benchmark data are publicly available.
  </details>

- **[EvoSkill v2: Fully Agentic Skill Evolution from Benchmark Adaptation to a Stateful Evolver](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25931)**  `arXiv:2610.25931`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Dastin Huang, Baran Nama, Salaheddin Alzubi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  EvoSkill v2 is a three-agent system that edits the instructions (skills) an agent is given, leaving its model and harness intact. The user starts a run by describing a benchmark and supplying a grader, which a benchmark adaptor turns into a benchmark-specific plugin. A solver attempts the tasks, and an evolver reads the solver's training rollouts and rewrites the skills at each epoch. We implement the evolver as one stateful coding agent with a bash tool for the whole run, unlike the two most common designs among the skill optimizers we surveyed: a sequence of single model calls, as in SkillOpt, and an agent that starts afresh each round, as in SkillBoost. We also measure how much the solver's rollouts vary from one attempt to the next, give the evolver several attempts per training task, and accept every epoch's skills instead of selecting among them on noisy scores. With fine-grained ground truth, EvoSkill v2's skills raised the held-out pass rate above the stronger of SkillOpt and SkillBoost by 7.7 percentage points on SpreadsheetBench v2 and by 5.4 on $\tau^3$-bench banking. In both benchmarks and both feedback settings, EvoSkill v2's evolver cost less per percentage point of held-out pass rate gained than either baseline's optimizer. EvoSkill v2 also enables a less capable model to improve its own skills, demonstrated by a gain of up to 21.5 percentage points in the $\tau^3$-bench banking experiment where Claude Haiku 5.5 powers both solver and evolver.
  </details>

- **[Outcome-Anchored Credit Reallocation for Agentic Knowledge Graph Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25384)**  `arXiv:2610.25384`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Mingyuan Li, Yucheng Li, Xiaxia Wang, Gong Cheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic knowledge graph question answering (KGQA) requires large language models (LLMs) to solve complex questions through multi-turn interactions with a knowledge graph (KG). Outcome-supervised reinforcement learning derives verifiable outcome rewards from final answers but uniformly assigns each rollout's outcome-level advantage to all policy-generated tokens, creating a trajectory-to-operation mismatch since KG operations within a rollout can have varying effects on the search process and final answer. To address this, we propose Outcome-Anchored Credit Reallocation (OACR), which employs a training-only KG-state outcome model to estimate outcome-reward changes between adjacent KG search states and converts them into operation credit, from which token-wise weights are derived. These multiplicative weights reallocate token-level update magnitudes while preserving the sign of the outcome-level advantage at every generated token. Across three benchmarks, OACR achieves stronger overall performance than existing KGQA methods while using fewer inference-time LLM calls and tokens.
  </details>

- **[Dialogue Generation for Conversational Recommendation Using User Search Interactions and Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25182)**  `arXiv:2610.25182`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Minsung Hwang, Donggyu Yoon, Gowoon Hwang, Kwanghee Jang, Joyce Jiyoung Whang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large-scale conversational recommendation datasets typically require manual dialogue construction or annotation. We propose SearCl, which automatically generates conversational recommendation dialogues grounded in user search and click behaviors. SearCl segments user interactions into query sessions and derives each recommendation target from content selected after a search query. A configurable probabilistic state machine controls the dialogue flow, while two LLM agents acting as the seeker and recommender generate utterances according to turn-level constraints. To support recommendation, the recommender is provided with knowledge graphs constructed from metadata about the content items used in each dialogue, capturing their attributes and relationships. Using SearCl, we generate 3,538 dialogues from content search and click interaction data, covering 1,731 unique target items. Under an LLM-as-a-judge protocol, the resulting dialogues achieve higher quality scores than dialogues from existing benchmarks. These results demonstrate that SearCl enables controllable and knowledge-grounded dataset construction without human annotations.
  </details>

- **[Revealing what benchmark scores hide: Jointly measuring the breadth and reliability of LLM capabilities using accessibility profiles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24957)**  `arXiv:2610.24957`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Kazem Faghih, Yize Cheng, Shoumik Saha, Mobina Pournemat, Armin Gerami, Soheil Feizi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language model benchmarks assign each question a single outcome from one fixed wording, even though the same underlying request can be expressed in many equivalent ways. We argue that the quantity of interest should therefore not be correctness on one wording, but the accessibility of an inquiry, which we define as the probability that a model succeeds across meaning-preserving formulations. The distribution of accessibility across inquiries reveals a structure that accuracy and existing best-, worst-, or average-case prompt scores collapse: how broadly a model's capabilities extend across inquiries and how dependably those capabilities can be elicited. Two models can have the same average performance while one succeeds across more inquiries unreliably and the other succeeds across fewer inquiries but more consistently, leading to opposite preferences depending on whether the task allows multiple retries until one success or when repeated consistent success is required. Across six benchmarks and more than twenty models, this tradeoff is common: for about half of the model pairs with nearly equal mean accessibility, which model looks better depends on whether one success across five formulations is enough or all five must succeed, quantities we call Access@5 and Reliable@5, respectively. For some pairs the two measures diverge significantly, by up to 10 points in opposite directions. Accessibility also reveals that scaling, reasoning, and prompt optimization can redistribute capability across inquiries in ways aggregate accuracy obscures, sometimes broadening the set of inquiries a model can succeed on while making success on them less repeatable. Accessibility turns formulation sensitivity from a nuisance into a measurable property of capability, distinguishing what a model can sometimes succeed on from what users can actually depend on.
  </details>

- **[WIDE: Boosting Adaptive LLM Inference via Token-level Dynamic Width Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28418)**  `arXiv:2607.28418`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Haozhe Hu, Hao Wu, Peiran Yin, Chao Han, Yunpu Ma, Xiaoyu Shen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pruning is a promising approach for improving the efficiency of large language models (LLMs). Static structured pruning is hardware-friendly, but its input-agnostic computation allocation often degrades model quality at high sparsity. Dynamic pruning adapts computation to individual tokens, but coarse-grained layer-level routing limits allocation flexibility, and finer-grained decisions introduce irregular execution that can offset computational savings. To address these challenges, we present WIDE, the first end-to-end differentiable framework that combines token-wise structured dynamic width pruning with GPU kernel co-design for both prefill and decoding. WIDE uses differentiable routers to select GQA-aligned query-head groups and GPU-tile-aligned FFN-channel groups for each token. To execute these decisions efficiently, it reorders routing masks to cluster active tokens within each group, enabling hardware-agnostic block-level skipping and architecture-aware skipping of inactive loads and tensor-core operations. Experiments on the Llama3 and Qwen3 families demonstrate improved quality retention over representative static and dynamic pruning methods. At 50\% target routing sparsity, WIDE improves Llama3.1-8B average zero-shot accuracy by up to 20.26 points over the strongest dynamic-depth baseline under calibration-only settings, while retaining 97.6\%/88.2\% of dense Qwen3-14B likelihood/generation performance after LoRA tuning. Separate latency benchmarks demonstrate end-to-end speedups of up to $1.68\times$ for prefill and $1.55\times$ for decoding over the dense baseline. These results demonstrate that pruning--kernel co-design pushes the frontier of token-wise dynamic structured pruning, combining improved quality retention with practical acceleration across both prefill and decoding. Our code is avaliable at \url{this https URL}.
  </details>

- **[Nexus : An Agentic Framework for Context-Aware Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14389)**  `arXiv:2605.14389`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Sarkar Snigdha Sarathi Das, Palash Goyal, Mihir Parmar, Nanyun Peng, Vishy Tirumalashetty, Chun-Liang Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language models have emerged as highly capable reasoners, yet their performance in time series forecasting remains uneven across domains and levels of contextual grounding. This is primarily due to their suboptimal time series reasoning and numerical modeling capabilities. To tackle this, we introduce Nexus, a multimodal forecasting framework that bridges qualitative event reasoning with structural numerical modeling through principled statistical synthesis. Nexus follows three structured stages: (1) Temporal profiling and anchor generation, which distills the multimodal history into a timestamped causal-temporal profile and generates a forecast anchor (2) Dual contextual outlook generation, which produces decoupled macro and micro level forecast trajectories, and (3) Bayesian synthesis, which reconciles the forecasting anchor with both contextual forecast trajectories via closed-form Gaussian updating weighted by empirical error covariances. Evaluated across three diverse forecasting benchmarks constituting eight datasets spanning rich event contexts, abrupt shocks, and purely numerical series, Nexus consistently outperforms leading time-series foundation models and language model baselines, while providing interpretable causal reasoning.
  </details>

- **[VibeTraining-Bench: Data-Trainer-Harness Co-Design for Iterative System Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26342)**  `arXiv:2610.26342`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Ruotian Peng, Yuanyi Ren, Shuaiqi Liu, Xin Guo, Fang Zhu, Yangyi Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive self-improvement (RSI) requires agents to diagnose system bottlenecks and coordinate improvements across training and inference. We refer to the agents' capability to jointly optimize training data, the trainer, and the inference harness as cross-component co-design. We introduce VibeTraining-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating this capability across tasks spanning terminal operation, software engineering, multi-turn tool use, and mathematical reasoning. Within each task, research agents start from an identical initial agent system under a fixed resource budget. Guided by validation feedback, they iteratively refine data, trainers, and inference harnesses, then select and submit a final agent system for evaluation on the test set. To prevent reward hacking, we enforce three safeguards: test data isolation, separate research and grading containers, and network restrictions for research agents. Our evaluation results show that frontier research agents substantially improve target agent systems. For example, on SWE-bench Verified, GPT-6-Astra raises the target agent system's performance from 32.9% to 53.7%, comparable to the human-developed instruct model's 55.4%. However, there is still room to improve their ability to co-design and iteratively refine data, trainer, and harness to achieve cumulative performance gains. We hope that VibeTraining-Bench will serve as a unified platform for advancing RSI, deepening our understanding of cross-component optimization for agent systems. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Understanding When Reflective Prompt and Skill Optimization Helps on Locally Served Reasoning Models: A Two-Model Study of GEPA and SkillOpt](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26020)**  `arXiv:2610.26020`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Andrea Giummarra, Carmelo Baglieri, Antonio Di Maria, Andrea Farruggio, Michele Piatto, Giovanni Micale_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reflective text-space optimizers such as GEPA and SkillOpt have reported large gains on frontier and mid-size models. We test replication across two reasoning-native target-serving configurations: DeepSeek V4 Flash through DwarfStar and Qwen3.8-27B through LM Studio. For each target, we evaluate the same held-out tasks and four conditions - minimal baseline, GEPA prompt, SkillOpt skill, and manually engineered prompt - on HotpotQA (n=500 per condition) and MATH-500 (n=250). In the primary Qwen v2 rerun under the same frozen protocol, GEPA selects the HotpotQA seed and exactly reproduces baseline behavior, whereas SkillOpt's validation gain reverses into held-out degradation. On MATH-500, GEPA raises exact match from 0.828 to 0.904 while reducing latency but increasing token consumption, so lower response time does not imply lower token cost. DeepSeek likewise shows its strongest GEPA effect on MATH-500 (0.852 to 0.908). Across both targets, GEPA's strongest gains occur on MATH-500, while neither optimizer improves the HotpotQA baseline. Optimization is therefore task- and method-dependent, and validation gains may reverse on held-out evaluation. An earlier Qwen run is retained as serving-sensitivity evidence. These results suggest that textual headroom for a model-task pair and the observed effect of a text-space optimizer are more informative when decomposed into content/reasoning, answer-realization/evaluator alignment, and serving-completion/reliability. The cross-benchmark pattern further suggests that optimization may benefit from low-ambiguity, actionable evaluation signals.
  </details>

- **[GLOBE: Geometry-Led Online Behavioral Early Exit via Unit-Sphere Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25970)**  `arXiv:2610.25970`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Xinyue Peng, Zhenlin Liang, Meng Cai, Hua Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning can improve problem solving, but models often keep generating after the correct answer is already reachable; the extra steps raise serving costs and may replace a correct intermediate result with an incorrect one. We propose GLOBE (Geometry-Led Online Behavioral Early Exit), an online, training-free algorithm that terminates the model's CoT process early. GLOBE embeds each completed reasoning segment as a point on a unit sphere and reads the shape of the resulting semantic trajectory. GLOBE first applies a trial phase that generates short trial answers in a separate branch, stopping the main stream on an accepted trial after checking confidence against a threshold. If generation continues, a trajectory phase stops the stream once this path repeatedly reverses direction with little net progress. On five benchmarks including mathematical reasoning, tool calling, and agent memory, GLOBE cuts output tokens by 49.7% and active end-to-end wall time (E2E) by 51.0% across two models, and achieves a five-task average of 80.58%, comparable to Full CoT.
  </details>

- **[When Retrieval Partially Conflicts with Skills: Understanding Procedural Hallucination in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25827)**  `arXiv:2610.25827`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Guanqun Jiang, Pengfei Cao, Kang Liu, Jun Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language models increasingly use skills that provide reusable instructions and procedures for task execution. After a relevant skill has been selected, the model may need to apply its governing procedure together with retrieved task context. When the retrieved context is useful but contains cues that violate the skill, the model must retain compatible information while rejecting the conflicting cues. We study failures in which the model adopts such cues and produces a retrieval-supported but skill-invalid answer, which we call procedural hallucination. We introduce SourceClash, a matched benchmark with 1,271 bundles and 6,355 prompts across ten procedural domains. Each bundle fixes the task, skill specification, and answer space while varying the relation between the skill and retrieval. Across nine models, valid-answer rates average 25.7% under conflicting retrieval, compared with 78.2% and 72.8% under two supportive retrieval settings. Mechanistic analysis of open-weight models shows that the answer-preference difference typically reaches its maximum shortly after generation begins. Residual replacement moves the model toward valid answers, while attention decomposition attributes most of the repair to value vectors. Intervention at the identified value pathway also improves final answer validity. These results characterize how partly conflicting retrieval changes skill-guided generation.
  </details>

- **[Standing on the Shoulders of Giants: Enhancing LLM Agents for Automated Fact-Checking with Human Expert Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25777)**  `arXiv:2610.25777`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Xinwen Chen, Haorui He, Yupeng Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated fact-checking (AFC) systems powered by large language model (LLM) agents still face challenges in evidence retrieval and claim verification. Existing approaches improve their capabilities through computationally costly post-training or human-in-the-loop feedback that is difficult to scale. To transfer professional fact-checking expertise to LLM agents efficiently, we distill publicly available guidelines published by Agence France-Presse (AFP) Fact Check into a reusable, modular Professional Fact-Checking Skill (PFC-Skill). We then present SkillCheck, the first AFC system that gives LLM agents dynamic access to PFC-Skill to guide evidence retrieval and claim verification at inference time. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SkillCheck improves upon skill-free DEFAME baselines across multiple LLM backbones, with gains of 2.2--4.8 pp. in Accuracy and 1.5--6.3 pp. in Macro-F1 on the AVeriTeC benchmark. Notably, SkillCheck with GPT-4o-Mini exceeds DEFAME with GPT-4o by 3.4 pp. in Accuracy while cutting inference cost by roughly 61%. The performance gains also extend to open-web retrieval. Ablations show that incorporating the skill during evidence retrieval provides most of the gains, while incorporation into both stages yields the best overall performance. Case studies show that PFC-Skill enables more targeted search and closer scrutiny of evidence. To conclude, our work shows that LLM agents can "stand on the shoulders of giants" by drawing on the best practices of professional fact-checkers, substantially enhancing AFC performance without extra training or continuous human supervision.
  </details>

- **[Auditing Tool Traces in Agentic LLMs: Source Localization and Counterfactual Dependence Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25540)**  `arXiv:2610.25540`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Junhao Liu, Tianxiao Li, Yongxian Wu, Ziheng Duan, Ren Li, Jing Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic LLMs increasingly support scientific prediction by grounding decisions in external evidence. In biology, they promise accurate, interpretable predictions for important problems by integrating complex experimental and computational evidence. However, serialized tool results can contain hidden label shortcuts, such as dataset identifiers or template artifacts, that create apparent success without biological reasoning. To address this risk, we introduce a two-stage audit. Stage 1, Per-Tool TF-IDF Decomposition (PT2D), localizes label-predictive patterns to individual tool results while avoiding signal dilution in the concatenated tool trace. Stage 2, Token-Class Redaction, counterfactually tests whether the frozen LLM depends on the localized shortcut. We apply this audit to TCR-pMHC binding, where tool augmentation substantially outperforms a no-evidence baseline. At face value, this suggests that the LLM has learned to use scientific evidence. The audit overturns this interpretation: PT2D localizes the strongest signal to identifiers in one tool result, and redacting them reduces performance to chance. These findings show how hidden label shortcuts can masquerade as scientific reasoning or discovery, motivating source-level audits before benchmark gains are trusted.
  </details>

- **[System X: An LLM Decision Model That Replies from Its Own KV Cache](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25456)**  `arXiv:2610.25456`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Syed Usama Khalid Bukhari, Syed Safwan Khalid, Muhammad Aamir_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decision models return probabilities over supplied options, but a deployed agent may also need to reply in words. The present study introduces System X, a large language model (LLM) decision model that adds a text-reply option to the LLM-as-Jev readout. When this option wins, or confidence is low, the same model continues from the decision's key-value (KV) cache, avoiding a repeated prompt prefill. Qwen3-0.6B has been fine-tuned with LoRA on Reason-9k and Bitext reply rows. JevBench accuracy shows no statistically significant change (55.4% to 54.5%, p=0.87), while expected calibration error falls from 0.292 to 0.169. Held-out Bitext routing accuracy reaches 98.3%. Cache reuse saves 15-37% of latency on an Apple M4 Pro, with token-identical replies in the FP32 demonstration cases. Scoring options on copies of the prompt cache matches full-sequence scoring to within 1.4e-6 in probability. Adding dialogue, industry and complaint data (System X+) improves support-task results but lowers IFEval accuracy from 55.5% to 45.0%. System X v4 uses Qwen3-1.7B, rebalanced data and teacher-rewritten reply targets. Against its base LLM, JevBench accuracy rises from 55.0% to 61.0% (p=0.04), with no statistically significant IFEval change (p=0.71). A matched latency test on unadapted Qwen3-1.7B saves 17-39%.
