One LLM judge prefers v4 replies to an unadapted 1.7B second call (31 to 11 pairs, p=0.003); the other's preference is not significant. These reply comparisons also differ in prompts and generation limits. State conflicts and unsupported reply details remain failure modes. Further studies with several seeds, human raters and real support data are recommended.
  </details>

- **[CoworkEnv: Synthesizing Training Environments for Long-Horizon Cowork Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24960)**  `arXiv:2610.24960`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zhongwei Xie, Yiming Du, Jiaxin Bai, Baixuan Xu, Chunyang Li, Xiyu Ren, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI coworkers must coordinate tools, communicate across turns, and act on changing workspace state while respecting workflow constraints. Constructing training experience for these capabilities requires coherent requests, service records, tool interfaces, and grading checks. We present CoworkEnv, a capability-guided framework for synthesizing executable training environments for AI coworkers. Workflow requirements guide task construction, and programmatic validation with feedback repair checks generated specifications and grading behavior. Teacher executions yield curated demonstrations for supervised fine-tuning (SFT), while student interactions provide rollouts and rewards for reinforcement learning (RL). Using these resources, we train compact PocketWorker models. For Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct, SFT raises the mean score from 46.6 to 64.1 on Claw-Eval, including a 31.9-point gain on Multi-turn tasks. An SFT-only extension to ClawMark demonstrates applicability across coworker benchmarks. We further compare RL training dynamics across configurations that vary reward processing and credit assignment. Improving training rewards provide preliminary evidence that the constructed environments support policy optimization.
  </details>

- **[A Cross-Domain Evaluation of Prompting Techniques for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24913)**  `arXiv:2610.24913`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Kevin Krazius, Tim Schlippe, Paul Libbrecht_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Prompt engineering has become a central mechanism for guiding the behavior of Large Language Models (LLMs) across a wide range of reasoning and language understanding tasks [1], [2]. However, existing studies frequently evaluate prompting techniques using different LLMs, datasets, or task settings, making direct comparisons across methods and domains difficult [3]. Consequently, in this paper we present a controlled cross-domain evaluation of Zero-Shot prompting, Few-Shot prompting, Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), and automatic prompt optimization based on a PE2-inspired optimization pipeline [4]-[6]. All experiments were conducted with the same LLM and evaluated on six benchmark datasets covering arithmetic reasoning, logical reasoning, commonsense reasoning, and factual verification. The results show that no prompting strategy consistently outperforms the others across all domains, and pairwise statistical comparisons of the highest-performing configurations revealed no significant differences. RAG achieves the highest overall average accuracy of 78.33% when 300 examples are available for retrieval, while Few-Shot Prompting achieves comparatively high accuracies across several structured reasoning tasks. Zero-Shot Prompting remains competitive on tasks with self-contained inputs, whereas the PE2-inspired optimization approach produces stable but not systematically superior results compared to the other evaluated prompting techniques. Furthermore, increasing the number of task-specific examples does not consistently improve performance across prompting techniques, indicating strong domain and task dependency. These findings suggest that prompting techniques should be selected adaptively based on task characteristics rather than treated as universally applicable optimization strategies.
  </details>

- **[HybridResearcher: Bridging the Structured-Unstructured Boundary for Deep Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24899)**  `arXiv:2610.24899`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Soyoung Yoon, Peiran Xu, Han Wang, Ruofan Wu, Xiaodong Yu, Feng Yan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While LLM agents are increasingly capable of gathering and synthesizing evidence from a single homogeneous source, such as structured tables or unstructured data, real-world analytical questions frequently span heterogeneous sources. For example, answering one enterprise question may require both a result computed over an internal database and evidence found on the web. Recent evaluations on benchmarks like HybridDeepResearch show that even frontier agents struggle when evidence must cross this boundary. While these benchmarks have exposed the limitation, methodologies to explicitly teach cross-source reasoning remain absent. To address this, we introduce HybridResearcher, a 4B agent trained to bridge evidence across the structured and unstructured boundary. To build it, we synthesize cross-source bridge trajectories by starting with validated single-source tasks-such as existing text-to-SQL or web search queries-and fabricating the missing source around a bridge entity drawn from the seed's own output. We retain a sample only when strict construction gates confirm that neither side alone suffices, ensuring the data requires true cross-source interdependence. Finally, we train on this curated dataset using supervised fine-tuning followed by reinforcement learning. Evaluated on the existing HybridDeepResearch benchmark, HybridResearcher improves over our base model, Qwen3.5-4B, by 30.0 points pass@8 and surpasses the far larger Qwen3.5-397B-A17B by 8.3 points, while also improving over its base model on established, held-out single-source benchmarks (BrowseComp and BrowseComp-Plus), demonstrating that this complex cross-source reasoning can be taught directly to small models without triggering catastrophic forgetting.
  </details>

- **[Medical Fine-Tuning Reshapes, Not Reduces, Patient-Facing AI Risk: A Rubric-Based, LLM-Judge Evaluation Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24807)**  `arXiv:2610.24807`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Savan Doshi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Patient-facing medical question answering with large language models (LLMs) is moving from research demonstrations to production deployments, yet evaluation remains anchored on factual correctness. Factuality is necessary but not sufficient: a response can be factually grounded and still issue a directive that, if acted on, would harm the patient. We introduce a risk-sensitive evaluation framework that scores responses by the presence and severity of six categories of risk-bearing language (treatment directives, contraindications, dosage expressions, urgency-triage cues, high-alert medication mentions, and overconfident assertions), anchored on published clinical harm taxonomies. Severity is classified by a three-judge LLM ensemble. We apply this framework to six 7-9B instruction-tuned models, three general (Mistral-7B, Phi-3.5-mini, Llama-3.1-8B) and three medically adapted (BioMistral-7B, OpenBioLLM-Llama3-8B, Med42-Llama3-8B), on 200 real patient questions from the K-QA benchmark. Two findings emerge. First, ensemble risk-bearing language is statistically independent of factual hallucination rate (pooled Spearman $\rho = -0.065$, $n = 300$, 95% CI $[-0.18, +0.05]$; distance correlation $= 0.10$, independence test $p = 0.24$). Second, medical fine-tuning does not reduce aggregate risk but reshapes it: continued-pretraining adaptations (BioMistral, OpenBioLLM) consistently suppress high-alert medication mentions while amplifying overconfident assertions, whereas clinical instruction-tuning (Med42) suppresses dosing only, without amplifying overconfidence. Per-category Krippendorff $\alpha$ is fair to good for the four linguistically anchored categories (0.53-0.71) and low for the two clinical-judgment-dependent categories (0.19, 0.31), indicating where automatic safety evaluation is and is not yet reliable. We release the rubric, judge prompts, and evaluation code.
  </details>

- **[Axiomatic World Modeling for Physics Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24519)**  `arXiv:2610.24519`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Xinye Yang, Zhenyang Liu, Yuxuan Wang, Yuanyuan Lei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent research-level physics-reasoning benchmarks for LLMs reveal a failure mode that lies not in knowledge or execution, but in modeling the world being reasoned about. Models often silently alter given quantities, fabricate laws, or inject unstated assumptions to force a problem into familiar patterns, and then reason inside this corrupted world. We define this premise-level divergence as modeling drift: the gap between a model's internal axiomatic world model (AWM) and the true physical world specified in the problem. Although RLVR and PRM have yielded substantial gains in mathematics and code, their reward shapes do not directly supervise world modeling in axiomatic domains such as physics. We propose Reinforcement Learning with Axiomatic World Modeling (RLAWM), which optimizes the policy by penalizing divergence between its inferred axiomatic world model and the true physics world. RLAWM probes physics reasoning at two hierarchical levels (modeling and solving), each scored by a physically grounded reward. The modeling reward targets abstract knowledge formulation, evaluating whether the model captures the true axiomatic world before attempting calculation. The solving reward targets instantiated knowledge reasoning, checking whether the model's concrete derivation remains consistent with the axiomatic world. This two-level structure separates what world the model believes it is reasoning in from how it reasons within that world. The framework operates exclusively during training, with no extra cost during inference. RLAWM improves performance by up to +12.2 points on PhysReason and +5.2 on PHYSICS over the baseline. It also generalizes zero-shot across difficulty, source, and domain, with a 2.45x gain and +10 points over the strongest baseline on olympiad-level physics and math.
  </details>

- **[The Judge Can Debias Itself: Awareness and Self-Debiasing for Reliable LLM-as-a-Judge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24320)**  `arXiv:2610.24320`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Youbing Yin, Jingyu Wu, Anoop Kumar, Scott Novotney, Daben Liu, Erin Babinsky_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-as-a-Judge systems are increasingly employed for automated evaluation, yet remain susceptible to systematic biases that undermine their reliability. We demonstrate that the judge can effectively debias itself without additional training. We propose two lightweight strategies: bias-aware prompting, which explicitly alerts the judge to potential target biases prior to evaluation, and self-debiasing, in which the judge critiques the initial judgment for bias influence. To broaden the evaluation scope, we introduce three novel benchmarks--authority bias (fabricated citations), confidence bias (assertive phrasing), and formatting bias (structured presentation)--and evaluate across seven bias types in total. Across three frontier models, self-debiasing improves performance in 20 out of 21 model-bias combinations with gains up to 15 percentage points, while bias-aware prompting also yields consistent improvements. The recent fine-tuned external critic, however, does not generalize as reliably to the newly-introduced bias types (degradation observed in 5 out of 9 cases). Our findings demonstrate that prompting a judge to reason about bias, either preemptively or retrospectively, is a robust and generalizable approach for enhancing evaluation integrity.
  </details>

- **[Not Every User Says It All: Training Tool-Using Agents Beyond Ideal Simulated Users](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24294)**  `arXiv:2610.24294`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Han Bao, Zheyuan Zhang, Kaiwen Shi, Yanfang Ye_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-using agents are trained and evaluated almost exclusively with strong simulated users who state every detail clearly, yet real users are often weaker. We construct weak users by masking sentences of a simulated user's replies, holding the task and the user's knowledge fixed, and cross-evaluate agents trained with strong and weak users on tau2-bench and ToolWOZ. Weak users lower the success of every policy, and training with them does not help: no policy trained with weak users, through fixed masks, mixtures or a curriculum, serves weak users better than the strong-trained policy beyond run-to-run noise, and prompting the agent to ask more does not help either. We propose StaGPO (State-Grouped Policy Optimization): the agent writes what the task needs, what is missing and how it is being filled, and GRPO uses this state to compare turns of different rollouts that reach the same situation, assigning step-level credit. Within the standard recipe of a small SFT cold start followed by RL, StaGPO makes weak-user training useful: on ToolWOZ, the Qwen3.5-2B policy trained with weak users reaches 76.8% with weak users, the highest of any Qwen3.5-2B policy we trained, 4.8 points above GRPO and 2.8 above the strongest baseline; it is also 5.4 points above GRPO with strong users, and generalises to an unseen mask rate; trained with strong users instead, it serves weak users at least as well as GRPO trained with weak users. On tau2-bench it scores highest with weak users for both models, by a small margin for Qwen3.5-4B.
  </details>

- **[Probing Ambiguity: Adversarial Intent Resolution for Robust Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24248)**  `arXiv:2610.24248`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Youbing Yin, Xujun Peng, Muhammad S. Uddin, Erin Babinsky, Daben Liu, Anoop Kumar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-SQL systems typically assume a one-to-one mapping between natural-language queries and correct SQL translations. In practice, however, many queries are inherently ambiguous, admitting multiple valid interpretations that yield distinct outputs. We introduce Adversarial Intent Resolution (AIR), a multi-agent framework that actively probes and resolves such ambiguity without any task-specific training. AIR first constructs competing interpretations, verifies through SQL execution that they produce genuinely different results, and generates targeted clarification questions grounded in the observed data differences. Evaluating on leading benchmarks for ambiguous questions, AIR demonstrates strong performance, efficiency, and robust cross-benchmark transferability. On the AMBROSIA benchmark under schema-only context, a realistic production setting where exposing the full database is infeasible, AIR outperforms IntentRL, the state-of-the-art reinforcement learning model, across all metrics (e.g., F1 0.679 vs. 0.618). Crucially, AIR exhibits only ~7% performance degradation without the full database, compared to ~20% for IntentRL. On the AmbiQT benchmark, where ambiguity arises from schema design rather than linguistic structure, AIR achieves higher F1 under both context settings without any benchmark-specific adaptation.
  </details>

- **[GardenBench: A lightweight, daily evaluation of LLM capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23866)**  `arXiv:2610.23866`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Benedict Cummins-Mburu, Mariana Garcia Mejia, Oscar Heath, Sabrina Kreyzerman, Ellie Murray, Arusan Surendiran, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce GardenBench, a benchmark with 11 test suites that evaluate the capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) across a range of tasks. By systematically prompting LLMs with various lightweight queries, we document day-to-day changes in model performance, including run costs and times, that other benchmarks miss. This allows us to compare the accuracy and features of responses across model providers and versions. We account for daily variations in model accuracy, capturing both model progress and degradation, for instance, when a model is changed post-deployment or when a new model is released. We base our evaluation on general-use tasks and make our results publicly accessible via a live dashboard, diversifying existing approaches to LLM testing. Our main contribution is an innovative, lightweight, and inexpensive benchmark, designed to be consumer-facing and for everyday tasks.
  </details>

- **[HTN-Orchestrate: A Hierarchical Task Network Framework for Multi-Agent Orchestration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22195)**  `arXiv:2610.22195`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yanchen Deng, Jianguo Huang, Yao Long Teng, Yixuan Li, Handuo Zhang, Ning Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Orchestrating multiple agents on a complex task requires reconciling local subtask execution with global task planning. Dynamic dispatch approaches provide execution flexibility but do not explicitly model subtask hierarchies or dependencies, while predefined workflows impose this structure but fix the decomposition before execution begins. Therefore, we introduce HTN-Orchestrate, a two-layer framework that separates these responsibilities through a hierarchical task network (HTN). The Planner maintains subtask hierarchies and dependencies, deferring decomposition until prerequisite results become available, while local Orchestrators schedule workers to execute each leaf subtask. Results propagate upward under \textbf{AND} semantics, and accumulated findings inform a rebuilt task network when the current decomposition cannot resolve the task. On five benchmarks spanning web research, scientific reasoning, and research workflows, HTN-Orchestrate outperforms ReAct, Agent Team, and DeepSeek Harness using the same base model, achieving 89.73% accuracy on BrowseComp and 73.44% on SUPERChem, exceeding Agent Team by 2.45 and 4.69 percentage points, respectively.
  </details>

- **[SwarmRSI: Recursive Self-Improvement through Evolving Scientific Agent Swarm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20431)**  `arXiv:2610.20431`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Qianru Meng, Xi Zhang, Konstantinos Papakostas, Congfeng Cao, Wenxiang Hu, Tianyi Lu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive self-improvement (RSI) in scientific research aims to improve agents' research capabilities through their own experimental experience. Scientific agent swarms can combine evidence and implementations from different research directions to guide their agents' subsequent experiments. However, poor experimental results can leave unclear whether to revise a hypothesis or its implementation, while overall program scores can hide components useful to other agents. To address these challenges, we introduce SwarmRSI, a framework that evolves scientific agent swarms for recursive self-improvement. We devise Feedback-Guided Swarm Mutation to refine hypotheses and their implementations using experimental feedback from other agents. We also propose Complementary Swarm Recombination to reuse retained hypotheses and complementary program components across agents, allowing partial results to support new research. Recursive Swarm Evolution integrates both mechanisms into a feedback loop, using new experimental evidence to guide subsequent research. Experiments on OpenProblems, NatureBench and MLE-Bench Lite demonstrate SwarmRSI's state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance. Further evaluations show that accumulated research experience improves performance on held-out tasks. Biologists also reproduce its programs for follow-up biological analyses.
  </details>

- **[MedEvoEval: Evaluating Continual Evolution of Doctor Agents through Simulated Clinical Episodes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.28900)**  `arXiv:2606.28900`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Hui Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Doctor agents are moving beyond single-turn answer generation toward evolving clinical decision systems that acquire evidence within episodes and reuse experience across episodes. Fixed-input medical QA and many interactive benchmarks provide limited support for evaluating how episode-level decisions interact with cross-episode experience. We introduce MedEvoEval, an executable longitudinal evaluation framework based on action-gated simulated outpatient episodes. Each case is converted into role-specific patient, examination, and manager views; evidence is revealed through valid actions, and structured traces link observations, actions, final outputs, scores, and optional experience write-back. Experiments show that these traces expose process costs hidden by final-answer scoring, characterize how MDT-style consultation reallocates resources, and support analyses of memory maturation, held-out transfer, update-stage response, and backward retention. On 200 held-out cases, the mature-memory condition with sequential write-back reduces interaction turns by 0.915 and improves composite utility by 0.0483 after Holm correction. MedEvoEval provides a concrete basis for evaluating experience-driven improvement, transfer, and retention. The accompanying artifact provides 700 processed episode records with provenance, role-specific schemas, and execution, scoring, and analysis code.
  </details>

- **[Herculean: An Agentic Benchmark for Financial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14355)**  `arXiv:2605.14355`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Xueqing Peng, Zhuohan Xie, Yupeng Cao, Haohang Li, Lingfei Qian, Yan Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As AI agents advance, the central question is no longer whether they can solve isolated financial tasks, but whether their capabilities generalize across complete professional workflows. Existing benchmarks cannot answer this question because they evaluate either static competencies or agents within a single interaction structure. We introduce Herculean, a benchmark of end-to-end agentic financial intelligence that crosses agent architecture, backbone model, and four structurally distinct workflows: Trading, Hedging, Market Insights, and Auditing. Each workflow is implemented as an MCP skill that preserves its native state, actions, constraints, and outcome criteria, allowing intact agent systems to be evaluated under matched external conditions. Across 30 architecture--backbone configurations, we find that agent capability is not globally ordered. Workflow rankings are weakly aligned, changing the architecture frequently reverses performance under the same backbone, and each architecture concentrates failure in different forms of workflow control. These results establish that agent performance is an interaction between architecture and workflow demand, rather than an intrinsic property of the underlying model. Consequently, success on a single financial workflow provides little evidence of dependable, general financial intelligence.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Cooperative Simulators: Generating Realistic User Personas for Robust Evaluation of LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12894)**  `arXiv:2605.12894`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Harshita Chopra, Kshitish Ghate, Aylin Caliskan, Tadayoshi Kohno, Chirag Shah, Natasha Jaques_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM) agents are increasingly deployed in settings where they interact with diverse users, including those who are unclear, impatient, or reluctant to share information. However, collecting real interaction data at scale remains expensive. The field has turned to LLM-based \emph{user simulators} as stand-ins, but these simulators inherit the behavior of their underlying models: cooperative and homogeneous. As a result, agents that appear strong in simulation often fail in real human interactions. To narrow this gap, we introduce Persona Policies (PPol), a plug-and-play control layer that induces realistic behavioral variation in user simulators while preserving original task goals. Rather than hand-crafting personas, we employ an evolutionary coding agent to discover persona generation programs optimized for human-likeness and behavioral coverage over real user conversations. The evolved program generates diverse, human-like personas for any task in the domain. Across 4 benchmarks--including $\tau^2$-bench Retail and Airline, ColBench, and WildChat--evolved PPol yield 28-72% absolute gains in fitness score over the baseline simulator. In blinded evaluations, annotators judged PPol users as 'human' 80.4% of the time, nearly 2x more than the baseline simulators. Training agents with PPol also improves real-world performance: our user study with live human-agent interactions showed that fine-tuning with our method boosted task success by +23% over default baselines. PPol thus offers a novel approach to strengthen simulator-based evaluation and training without changing underlying tasks.
  </details>

- **[Distinguishing Performance From Competence in Evaluations of Humanlike Abstract Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.02125)**  `arXiv:2510.02125`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Claas Beger, Ryan Yi, Shuhao Fu, Kaleda Denton, Arseny Moskvichev, Sarah W. Tsai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI reasoning models have exceeded human performance on the ARC-AGI-1 benchmark, but does that mean state-of-the-art models have the underlying competence (humanlike abstract reasoning) that the benchmark was designed to test? Here we investigate the abstraction abilities of AI models using the closely related but simpler ConceptARC benchmark. Our evaluations vary input modality (textual vs. visual), use of external Python tools, and reasoning effort. Beyond output accuracy, we evaluate the natural-language rules that models generate to explain their solutions, enabling us to assess whether models recognize the abstractions that ConceptARC was designed to elicit. We show that the best models' rules are frequently based on less abstract, more domain-specific concepts, capturing intended abstractions considerably less often than humans. In the visual modality, AI models' output accuracy drops sharply; however, our rule-level analysis reveals that a substantial share of their rules capture intended abstractions, even as the models struggle to apply these concepts to generate correct solutions. In short, we show that using performance (accuracy) alone can substantially overestimate AI competence in textual modalities while underestimating it in visual modalities, an illustration of the risk of mistaking performance for competence.
  </details>

- **[Empirical Analysis of Decoding Biases in Masked Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13021)**  `arXiv:2508.13021`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Pengcheng Huang, Tianming Liu, Zhenghao Liu, Yukun Yan, Shuo Wang, Tong Xiao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Masked Diffusion Models (MDMs) have recently emerged as a promising non-autoregressive paradigm for sequence generation. However, their performance is highly sensitive to the choice of decoding strategy. In this work, we reveal that prevalent uncertainty-based decoding strategies induce two decoding biases in MDMs: rigid boundary bias and trivial token bias. These biases limit the model's reasoning ability and ultimately degrade generation quality. To address these challenges, we propose UNmasking Calibration for DecOding DEbiasing (Uncode), a decoding calibration framework that regularizes uncertainty-based decoding by incorporating two complementary priors to shape global decoding trajectories and promote content informativeness. Extensive experiments on three advanced MDMs across seven reasoning- and planning-intensive benchmarks demonstrate that Uncode consistently outperforms existing decoding strategies by more than 7%, while achieving performance comparable to autoregressive models of similar parameter scales. The code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AnyGoal: On World Model Generalization Beyond Expert Rollouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26844)**  `arXiv:2610.26844`  `cs.AI`  
  _Heejeong Nam, Divyat Mahajan, Quentin Le Lidec, Randall Balestriero, David Klindt_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The goal of a world model is to capture the dynamics of an environment. This has the great appeal that it allows agents to plan and simulate a course of actions with the help of a world model. For these uses to be reliable, however, the learned dynamics must behave well beyond the specific state-action combinations observed in expert rollouts. Despite this, the relationship between data coverage, dynamics generalization, and downstream planning performance remains underexplored. We study this relationship through the lens of compositional generalization: can a world model recombine familiar state and action factors into valid dynamics? To answer this, we introduce AnyGoal, a benchmark of goal-shifted task variants built from widely used world-model environments, together with an open-source library. Each task is designed to be within reach of a world model that has learned the underlying dynamics: individual state factors and actions remain within their training support, but their combinations are underrepresented in expert trajectories. Across multiple environments and world model families, we find that current models consistently exhibit failures under these recombinations. By examining dynamics and representation failures across model families, interaction regimes, and evaluation metrics, we study where current world models generalize and where challenges remain for goal-shifted prediction and planning. Together, this framework, benchmark, and analysis provide a basis for evaluating progress toward world models that generalize to new planning tasks.
  </details>

- **[LG-Bench: A Graph-Structured Evaluation Benchmark for Life Sciences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26787)**  `arXiv:2610.26787`  `cs.AI`  
  _Lu Sun, Xiangyi Zhang, Xiangyang Zhu, Kaiyuan Ji, Zijian Chen, Qiang Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Traditional evaluation benchmarks reduce inherently interconnected scientific knowledge in life sciences into flat lists of questions, disregarding the underlying topological structure of the knowledge. We introduce, the first graph-structured benchmark for life sciences, featuring over 10,000 high-quality multiple-choice questions across medicine, biology, and chemistry. Our approach constructs a weighted evaluation graph using bidirectional matching and semantic similarity algorithms, where nodes represent questions and edge weights capture their semantic relationships. Leveraging this graph topology, we design two novel evaluation metrics. The Global Coherence Score (GCS) measures a model's consistency within semantically related neighborhoods, while Knowledge Balance Score (KBS) analyzes how model errors are distributed across the graph to reveal conceptual blind spots. LG-Bench facilitates fine-grained comparison of LLMs by surfacing differences in conceptual coherence and patterns of knowledge organization across models. Our framework shifts the evaluation paradigm from flat accuracy metrics to structure-aware analysis, offering a new lens for diagnosing and improving LLM performance in the life sciences domain.
  </details>

- **[StebBench: Diagnosing Real-World Procedural Understanding in AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26679)**  `arXiv:2610.26679`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hossein A. Rahmani, Bin Wu, Chaeeun Lee, Fanghua Ye, Sahan Bulathwela, Sujay Kumar Jauhar, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating an agent's planning ability remains challenging for open-domain tasks, where verifying procedures through execution is often impractical. Existing evaluations typically assess only the final plan and do not distinguish procedural errors such as missing, incorrect, or misordered actions. We introduce STEPBENCH, a benchmark of 6,885 verified procedures crawled from wikiHow across 81 open-domain categories. From each procedure, we derive 5 diagnostic tasks: four with deterministic answers involving withheld, incorrect, or shuffled steps, and one requiring next-step generation. We additionally evaluate full-plan generation to examine how diagnostic performance correlates with end-to-end plan validity. Using the same procedures across tasks lets us directly compare different planning capabilities. For generation, STEPEVAL identifies critical failures that prevent goal completion. Across 14 configurations of 12 models, we find substantial differences across planning capabilities, with step ordering proving the most difficult: the strongest model correctly orders only 63.0% of procedures, dropping to 33.1% for those with nine or more steps. Incorrect-step detection also predicts judged plan validity across models, suggesting a lower-cost alternative for evaluating generation quality. On STEPBENCH-Val, comprising 214 procedures published in 2026, comprehension scores and rankings remain stable, suggesting limited influence from memorisation.
  </details>

- **[AllPath: Layered Verification for a Deployed, Self-Revising LLM Trading Agent under Human-Gated and Autonomous Regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26662)**  `arXiv:2610.26662`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tian Zhang, Meng Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM trading agents are evaluated almost exclusively in backtests and autonomous benchmark harnesses. Deployment, where actions are irreversible, raises a different question: who verifies the agent's actions, and which failures can each verifier structurally catch? We report on AllPath, a personal LLM trading agent deployed against a live paper-brokerage account. The system watches positions through a deterministic rules engine, and each night it reviews its own day and proposes revisions to its own trading strategies. Verification is split across three layers. Code verifies what must not depend on judgment: deterministic gates enforce risk limits and block any strategy revision that fails an integrity check. The agent verifies itself within fixed bounds: its nightly review has an iteration cap and no tools that can act. A human verifies judgment, through approval screens that show the current state of each proposal. We also compared two verification policies for the agent's self-revisions on the live system: two and a half weeks with every self-revision human-gated, then five weeks with reflection revisions auto-applied behind the same code gates, while the operator's chat channel stayed human-gated throughout. Each layer caught a different kind of failure. The code gates rejected no proposal; their role was keeping revisions consistent. The human rejected two proposals that passed every code check, both over a disagreement about the market, and later removed 11 of the 19 rules the agent had added on its own, at a median lag of eight days. The most instructive production incidents, such as strategies silently left unmonitored, passed every gate; the operator found them by hand, and the fix each time was an interface change that makes the condition visible. We contribute the layered architecture, the two-regime account, and a production incident taxonomy, none observable in a backtest.
  </details>

- **[BetaMem: Learning to Remember a Changing World for Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26554)**  `arXiv:2610.26554`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mai Liu, He Sun, Siru Zhong, Kerun Chen, Hao Yan, Xiaohui Lv, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon embodied agents face temporal confusion and incomplete or distorted records, recurring errors from fixed memory rules, and growing storage and retrieval costs. We present BetaMem, a framework for building and evolving traceable long-term memory through six linked layers: working, spatial, entity, episodic, experiential, and procedural memory. A dual-level write contract constrains operation executability and source support for content: supervised fine-tuning learns compliant memory operations, while reinforcement learning combines repeated sampling with teacher verification of the original observations to reward complete, accurate records and penalize omissions, distortion, and unsupported content. We formulate failure-driven policy recursive self-improvement through diagnosis along the causal sequence of memory operations and local rule repairs gated by independent replay. Atomic tools frame storage, updates, retrieval, and forgetting as cost-utility allocation under storage and read budgets. Using the open-source Qwen3.5-9B model for memory generation, BetaMem achieves overall scores of 48.08% on M3-Bench-robot, 92.08% on LoCoMo, 72.6% on OpenEQA EM-EQA, and 62.4% on EgoLifeQA, the highest among the listed memory methods under their respective evaluation protocols. Training the model for memory generation raises the contract pass rate from 50.9% to 86.2% while reducing output tokens per stored action by 47.3%. A separate Qwen3-VL-8B-Instruct evaluation on EMem-Bench achieves an average success rate of 38.4% and an average error recurrence rate of 24.9%. We perform best among evaluated methods using comparably sized open-source models. Category results show that we have advantages in integrating information across observations, recalling events over long histories, and inferring personal habits.
  </details>

- **[Agentic Orchestration of EDC Data Space Retrieval and Natural Language Driven Data Processing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26548)**  `arXiv:2610.26548`  `cs.AI`  
  _Marla Grunewald, Fin Gentzen, Iulisloi Zacarias, Admela Jukan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Participating in a data space still requires the Dataspace Protocol (DSP) negotiation flow to be hand-coded against each provider's access policy. We present a multi-agent architecture that automates this process using natural language requests. Built on LangGraph over an Eclipse Dataspace Components (EDC), the design separates concerns into cooperating agents. The orchestrator's role is to route and plan. The data extraction agent's role is to expose the DSP protocol's three composable tools (catalog discovery, contract negotiation and download). The agent also gates negotiations on the consumers' verifiable credentials, refusing assets rather than fabricating them. Furthermore, there are downstream Machine Learning (ML) and Visualization agents, as well as a final Verification agent, which confirms that the request was actually fulfilled and rechecks the intended operation against the contract's usage policy. In a primary benchmark involving 600 runs, the system achieved 89% task success with zero policy violations when evaluating multi-dimensional policy reasoning with a locally hosted open-weight model. It failed safely through refusal rather than misrouting. The reliability of the system is largely dependent on the phrasing of requests: technical and expert requests succeed up to 98% of the time, whereas casual and under-specified requests succeed 72% of the time. Furthermore, in a secondary evaluation of 600 runs isolating hierarchical geographic access control, the framework successfully blocks 100% of unauthorized regional requests. While the verification agent hallucinates that 1% of these requests, the system logs confirm that no data is ever extracted and the framework simultaneously achieves an end-to-end verified delivery rate of 86% for eligible consumers.
  </details>

- **[Remembering Has a Price: Learning Memory Worth under Resource Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26453)**  `arXiv:2610.26453`  `cs.AI`  
  _Guannan Lai, Zi-Yang Bai, Jun-Peng Jiang, Han-Jia Ye_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Lifelong agents continually accumulate and reuse experience to improve after deployment, yet in practice they often operate under tight generation, interaction, and memory budgets. Under such stringent joint constraints, we find that existing lifelong-memory methods can become ineffective, with several sophisticated approaches offering little advantage over simple retrieval or even no-memory agents. This reveals a more fundamental problem: resource scarcity makes each memory decision more consequential. When only a small amount of past experience can be retained or reused and opportunities for reasoning and recovery are limited, retrieving a relevant but ineffective memory can consume scarce context and hinder downstream execution. The key challenge is therefore not simply to retrieve relevant experience, but to determine which experiences are actually worth retaining and reusing. To address this challenge, we propose TokenWorth, a lightweight online memory framework that learns query-dependent memory worth from sparse execution feedback and applies it consistently to memory retrieval and bounded memory management. Across three LifelongAgentBench environments and both local and API-based deployments, TokenWorth achieves the highest task success rate while using the fewest output tokens among the evaluated methods. These results suggest that effective lifelong adaptation under resource constraints depends not on remembering more, but on learning what is worth remembering. Our code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[BOSS: Token-Budgeted Online Sampling and Selection for LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26383)**  `arXiv:2610.26383`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yupeng Chang, Yahan Li, Chenlu Guo, Diana Inkpen, Yuan Wu, Yi Chang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-time scaling can improve LLM reasoning, but existing approaches typically adapt generation or apply answer selection after a predetermined generation schedule. We study within-instance allocation: under a hard per-instance prompt-plus-completion token cap, when should inference stop, generate another candidate, or spend residual tokens adjudicating candidates already observed? BOSS is a training-free policy for this three-way decision, with worst-case budget checks before every generation and judge call. Its key contribution is online generation-adjudication allocation under one shared token ledger. Across two Llama-3.1 models and four reasoning benchmarks, BOSS attains the highest accuracy in 22 of 24 model-task-budget cells under the common hard cap. At Llama-3.1-8B and B=4k, it reaches 60.5 macro-average accuracy versus 58.6 for Adaptive-Consistency, 59.1 for TALE-EP, and 58.9 for fixed-schedule League. On identical Stage-I candidate pools, randomized A/B judging with repeated voting improves plurality from 58.4 to 60.3, with the full BOSS selector reaching 60.5. Approximately matched-cost controls further characterize how the benefit of adjudication changes with available inference budget. Together, these results establish generation-adjudication allocation as a distinct design axis for reasoning under strict per-instance caps.
  </details>

- **[GAIDA: Generative AI for Human-in-the-Loop Document Annotation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26362)**  `arXiv:2610.26362`  `cs.AI`  
  _Islam Mesabah, Ali Elhosary, Priyabanta Sandulu, Darko Obradovic, Sebastian J. Vollmer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Document annotation is a fundamental yet labor-intensive step in building document information extraction systems. While Optical Character Recognition (OCR) and Large Language Models (LLMs) can automate parts of this process, we argue that annotation systems should be judged not by how completely they remove human effort, but by how effectively they redirect it toward review and correction. We propose a modular human-in-the-loop architecture that integrates OCR-based extraction, LLM-based semantic pre-annotation, and human validation in a single editable workflow, and present \textbf{GAIDA}, a research prototype serving as its reference implementation. GAIDA supports interchangeable OCR engines, LLM backends, and prompt configurations, and enables stage-specific measurement of annotation effort. In an initial single-annotator, stage-specific study on the FUNSD scanned-form and WildReceipt retail-receipt benchmarks, OCR assistance reduced region-creation and transcription time by up to \textbf{80.9\%}, and reviewing LLM pre-annotations reduced semantic-labeling time by up to \textbf{73.7\%}, despite imperfect pre-annotations of 78.86\% and 86.98\% Micro F1 on FUNSD and WildReceipt, respectively. Finally, we reuse the same implementation, adapting only OCR and prompt configuration, to build and openly release \textbf{AraReceipt}, a manually annotated dataset of 100 Arabic receipt images labeled with 25 key-information classes. The results indicate that imperfect automated outputs provide practical value when presented as editable suggestions under human control.
  </details>

- **[StrucChunk: Structure-Aware Retrieval for Legal Document RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26313)**  `arXiv:2610.26313`  `cs.AI`  
  _Surya Deep Singh, Ashutosh Tripathi, Gaurav Deep Singh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) can ground LLM outputs in domain-specific knowledge, but legal documents are hard: standard chunking fragments hierarchical argument structure, and generic embeddings struggle with both precise citation matching and semantic understanding. We propose StrucChunk (Structure Aware Chunking) with hybrid dense-sparse retrieval for hierarchically structured legal documents. It treats documents as directed acyclic graphs of provisions, preserves clause boundaries, resolves cross-references before embedding, and combines BM25 with BGE-M3 via Reciprocal Rank Fusion. We evaluate on five corpora, three legal traditions and two languages. StrucChunk reaches 100% boundary precision (vs. 7.7% for recursive splitting on CrPC), preserves 97.6% of documented cross-references (vs. 0%), and matches section coverage with 40.3% fewer chunks on GDPR. On LegalBench-RAG (6,858 query-passage pairs), it achieves Char-Recall@8 of 48.18% vs. 23.59% for the best baseline (+104%), surpassing the published state of the art by +83%. On 501 queries each for CrPC and CPC (Indian law), Recall@5 improves from 0.557 to 0.804 (+44.4%) and from 0.158 to 0.259 (+64.6%); chunking accounts for 88% and 73% of the gain. On 501 GDPR queries, Recall@5 is 0.868 with R@1 improving +59%. On NitiBench-CCL (3,730 Thai financial law queries), HR@5 is 0.856 vs. 0.594 for naive chunking (+44.1%), showing that section-boundary alignment is the dominant factor across languages, and cross-reference augmentation adds +2.3% on cross-reference queries. We release the implementation, 5,256 evaluation queries across five legal corpora, annotation methodology and chunking quality metrics. Code:this http URL
  </details>

- **[Selective Transfer through Sequence Distillation: From Benchmark Knowledge to Backdoor Behaviour](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26311)**  `arXiv:2610.26311`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jonibek Mansurov, Sholpan Bolatzhanova, Filbert Aurelian Tjiaranta, Vallerie Alexandara Putra, Alham Fikri Aji_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge distillation is widely used to transfer capabilities from teacher models to student models, but recent work shows that it can also transfer unintended teacher properties. We study whether different hidden teacher properties survive the same distillation channels. Specifically, we compare two properties: benchmark-associated knowledge and refusal backdoors, and show that distillation treats them very differently. Teachers fine-tuned on benchmark data successfully pass that knowledge to students through all four distillation channels we test, including when students are trained only on teacher-generated number sequences containing no natural language or domain content whatsoever. Refusal backdoors tell the opposite story: backdoored teachers achieve near-perfect triggered refusal, yet sequence-distilled students inherit essentially none of this behaviour. Logit-based distillation transfers it substantially, reaching up to 85.5\% triggered refusal in students that saw zero backdoor examples. Distillation is therefore selective, and the selection criterion is not what data the student sees, but what structure the teacher's property has. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[REFLEX: RNA Ensemble Generation via Flexibility-Calibrated Stochastic Bridge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26261)**  `arXiv:2610.26261`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wanli Ma, Tianmeng Hu, Ke Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  RNA molecules often function through multiple experimentally observed conformational states. A central challenge is to capture ensemble diversity without sacrificing structural fidelity: samples may collapse toward averaged representative structures or drift into unsupported conformational regions. We introduce REFLEX (RNA Ensemble generation calibrated by FLEXibility), a sequence-conditioned generator for RNA backbone-frame ensembles based on a heteroscedastic stochastic bridge on SE(3). During training, experimental B-factors calibrate residue-wise bridge widths, exposing each observed conformer through local perturbation neighborhoods with residue-specific scales while keeping constrained regions sharper. A learned flexibility condition guides the bridge-field network to recover target conformer-specific frames from these calibrated noisy states. At inference, REFLEX uses only the input sequence, requiring no MSA-subsampling heuristics. On a multi-conformer RNA benchmark, REFLEX achieves the best precision-recall trade-off for ensemble coverage across evaluation thresholds, while recovery-stress ablations indicate gains in target-frame recovery under stronger perturbations.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Static Leaderboards: Capability-Focused Evaluation with Structural Diversity for Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26251)**  `arXiv:2610.26251`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hongyin Zhang, Mingyi Xiang, Rongzhao Chen, Yankai Wang, Jialin Qiao, Xiangdong Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Static public benchmarks for time series foundation models (TSFMs) typically have fixed and narrow test distributions. Their repeated reuse encourages benchmark-specific tuning, making reported performance reflect adaptation to the fixed test set rather than genuine forecasting generalization. In addition, a single aggregate score cannot fully characterize model capabilities across distinct temporal structures and input relationships. We propose CaFE (Capability-Focused Evaluation), a refreshable evaluation extension grounded in original benchmark data that enriches static benchmarks with structural diversity and enriches single-score black-box evaluation with multi-dimensional, capability-oriented assessment. CaFE first profiles the original benchmark and defines eight Structural Features (SFs) that characterize its temporal structure. For each SF, it resamples the original data according to its structural support, removes edge-of-distribution samples, and augments the retained samples by varying the SF's salience, producing samples with different structural profiles. After filtering out out-of-distribution samples while preserving diversity, CaFE yields a refreshed evaluation set. TSFMs are then evaluated on this set using both conventional forecast-error metrics and intervention-response metrics, giving each model a per-feature capability profile alongside endpoint accuracy. Experiments on GIFT-Eval and FEV-Mini20 show that CaFE yields stable evaluation results and reveals per-feature strengths and weaknesses that a single aggregate score hides. Moreover, on genuine data filtered by structural condition, models selected by CaFE outperform those chosen by the aggregate leaderboard. CaFE therefore makes model selection conditional on the structural response of interest.
  </details>

- **[CoRS: Fast Neural Flexible Job-Shop Scheduling via Composite Interval State Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26239)**  `arXiv:2610.26239`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiang Sutong, Zhao Peng, Wu Xuan, Wang Yan, Wang Jiaqi, Zhang Rui, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural solvers for the Flexible Job-Shop Scheduling Problem (FJSP) typically refresh graph representations and update feasibility after every operation--machine decision. However, only feasibility must remain exact at every step, whereas graph encoding is substantially more expensive and can potentially be refreshed less frequently. We present CoRS, which decouples them: an interval-level graph representation is reused across multiple sequential decisions, while a stepwise feasibility state is updated after every action. This two-timescale design amortizes graph encoding without parallelizing action generation or relaxing scheduling constraints. CoRS further develops a feasibility-preserving composite-interval decoding scheme with dynamic constraint tracking and boundary state synchronization, which reduces representation refresh frequency while maintaining continuous feasible schedule construction. Furthermore, to alleviate representation staleness caused by interval-level state reuse, CoRS introduces enhanced static features as a compensation mechanism. By providing more comprehensive structural information of the scheduling problem, these features improve the model's perception of overall problem characteristics. Extensive experiments on synthetic and multiple public FJSP benchmarks demonstrate that CoRS significantly reduces inference time while maintaining competitive solution quality, achieving favorable speed-quality trade-offs under tight wall-clock budgets and low-latency scenarios.
  </details>

- **[MoveBench: A Rubric-Based Benchmark for Evaluating Large Language Models in Sports and Health Coaching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26185)**  `arXiv:2610.26185`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yitong Li, Jiang Bian, Jingling Wang, Pan Ding, Chengying Gu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite rapid advances in large language models (LLMs), their deployment in the sports and health domain remains high-risk due to the absence of domain-specific evaluation benchmarks. Existing general-purpose benchmarks (e.g., MMLU and HELM) primarily assess broad knowledge and reasoning capabilities, but fail to capture critical domain requirements such as exercise safety, physiological appropriateness, personalization, and actionable coaching quality. This gap is particularly concerning in fitness and health applications, where incorrect or unsafe recommendations can lead to physical harm. To address this limitation, we introduce MoveBench, the first comprehensive benchmarking framework specifically designed for evaluating LLMs in sports and health coaching. The proposed benchmark integrates heterogeneous task formats, including multiple-choice questions (MCQs) and open-ended question answering (OpenQA), grounded in real-world user scenarios and paired with structured rubrics emphasizing safety compliance, scientific validity, and training logic. Through large-scale evaluation of 15 diverse LLMs on 809 questions with 5,012 rubric-level criteria, our results reveal substantial performance variability, particularly in safety awareness, structured program design, and personalization capability, highlighting a critical gap between knowing and doing. Our work establishes a foundational evaluation standard for sports and health LLM applications and provides a reproducible framework for advancing safe, reliable, and context-aware AI-driven coaching systems. Open resources are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Reliable Before Profitable: QuantHarness for Governing Financial Strategy Development with LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26101)**  `arXiv:2610.26101`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiasheng Mao, Jiacheng Liu, Hengwei Ye, Zheng Tian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents can turn natural-language financial research requests into executable and backtested strategies, but a successful run may still implement the wrong rule, use information unavailable at decision time, or obscure how repairs and candidate selection changed the strategy. Because these failures arise throughout development, final code and performance metrics alone are insufficient to establish either fidelity to the request or the validity of the development process. We therefore address the reliability of financial strategy development at the trajectory level: the system must control how intermediate outputs become downstream state and preserve the evidence needed to justify the terminal outcome. We introduce QuantHarness, a two-layer agent harness in which a finance-specific Workflow structures specification, data grounding, construction, testing, and review, while Harness Core enforces stage-specific information, action, and commitment boundaries. We also introduce FinRelBench, a benchmark that evaluates strategy-development reliability through execution success, requirement satisfaction, and runtime evidence. QuantHarness achieves the highest Task Success at 90.72\%, compared with 74.23\%--84.54\% for the comparison systems. Controlled stress tests show that its boundaries contain targeted violations, while structured run records improve evidence retrieval and support decision replay under the evaluated protocol.
  </details>

- **[From Code to Playable Systems: CEGame-Bench for Long-Horizon Game Creation Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26098)**  `arXiv:2610.26098`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zixuan Dong, Yanghai Wang, Hanyan Bian, Jiaxi Tao, Junze Li, Jiadong Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A game ships when a player can launch it, finish it, and return to a saved world, none of which a successful build guarantees, yet game-creation agents are mostly tested on one engine or on one-prompt browser games. We present CEGame-Bench, the first long-horizon benchmark for complete-game creation across five production engines: Defold, Godot, Roblox Studio, Unity, and Unreal Engine. Agents build each game from an empty project and a design document in runs averaging up to 295 steps and 65M tokens; the document doubles as a contract, and a hidden evaluator accepts a game only by real-input play. Under four scaffolds, agents earn most integration points but usually under half the gameplay points, and their games reach the ending in only 16% of runs, while references built by engineers with coding agents score near the maximum, which places this artifact-to-play gap in the agents. No two engines rank configurations alike (Kendall's tau 0.14-0.62), and the same game usually scores higher on Unity and Unreal Engine, which check more before play. We release Game Completion Boost, an agent skill for requirement-driven playtesting and repair that raises the execution-based score of three models by 14.1-24.3 points.
  </details>

- **[CUA-Lite: An Open Platform for Computer Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26071)**  `arXiv:2610.26071`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhanhui Zhou, Weichen Zhang, Haoran Liu, Lingjie Chen, Tianneng Shi, Kevin Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open resources for computer use agents (CUAs) -- environments, datasets, and models -- remain scattered: without a standard, these resources cannot be integrated and scaled; additionally, evaluation, RL, and SFT cannot share one infrastructure. We present CUA-Lite, an open platform for developing CUAs, built around three standardized abstractions: (1) Unified environment interface, under which we integrate 12 benchmarks across desktop, browser, and mobile, plus lightweight VM-free sandboxes that replicate OSWorld's desktop at much lower cost, providing over 30,000 verifiable tasks. (2) Unified supervised data format, into which we convert the major public CUA datasets, from GUI grounding and understanding to full rollouts, and record our fresh rollouts from frontier CUAs on these verifiable environments. (3) Model harnesses shared across evaluation, RL, and SFT, one per model and implemented for 14 model families. Each harness runs its model in these computer environments, producing the rollouts that evaluation and RL consume, or renders stored supervised data into the model's own training format for SFT. We release reference scores across the integrated benchmarks, and show that RL and SFT through the same harness improve open-weight models. We release our dataset, code, and models, and the platform is open to contributions.
  </details>

- **[ClueFlow: Learning Long-Horizon Search through Attributed Research-State Transitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25980)**  `arXiv:2610.25980`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yicheng Wang, Wentao Tan, Yidan Huang, Yifei Liu, Qiong Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon search requires agents to acquire relevant evidence and integrate it into an evolving research state. Existing methods maintain research states to guide exploration or assign turn-level progress signals, yet grounding these signals in explicit, attributable state changes remains underexplored. We introduce ClueFlow, which learns long-horizon search from attributed research-state transitions. ClueFlow constructs Source-State-Final attribution chains that connect evidence-acquiring browser actions, Evidence and Candidate updates, and the final answer supported by those updates. During search, ClueFlow records typed state changes and potentially supporting browser actions, then applies gold-conditioned semantic validation and source tracing after execution. We use this attribution to guide both supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning. During SFT, attribution selects which decisions receive supervision with their original interaction contexts. During Turn-PPO, the same attribution augments outcome-based turn advantages with credit assigned along the Source-State-Final chains. Transition-level validation also recovers useful supervision from gold-consistent state changes in trajectories with incorrect final answers. We evaluate ClueFlow across two Qwen backbones and four search benchmarks spanning live-web and fixed-corpus settings, observing consistent gains from SFT and further improvements from PPO. With Qwen3.5-35B-A3B, ClueFlow achieves 60.0% accuracy on BrowseComp and 72.0% on BrowseComp-Plus. In controlled BrowseComp-Plus experiments, attribution-guided SFT matches all-turn exact-context SFT using about 30% of the training tokens, while attribution-guided PPO outperforms outcome-only PPO by 4.1 pp. These results demonstrate that state-transition attribution enables efficient supervision and effective credit assignment for long-horizon search agents.
  </details>

- **[ABACUS: Program-Guided Multistep Reasoning over Frozen Judgment Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25963)**  `arXiv:2610.25963`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuhao Wang, Zelin Zang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Judgment models answer semantic questions by scoring a finite set of candidate outcomes. Can reusable programs turn these local decisions into powerful multistep reasoning? We introduce \method, a framework that jointly constructs judgment schemas and composition programs for frozen judgment models. A generative teacher designs the procedure offline; the judgment model resolves local semantic questions online, and the saved program composes its decisions into an answer. We study three allocations of design effort between humans and the teacher, focusing on construction through a shared interface. We instantiate the framework with Kev. On three selected BIG-Bench Hard tasks, nine independently constructed programs solve all 450 executions of 150 development inputs. A separate deployment study freezes three programs after development-set reference-agreement refinement and evaluates them on 600 held-out questions. These programs achieve near-perfect 99.67\% macro accuracy, versus 64.00\% for direct Kev and 68.80\% for final-answer-only Qwen3.5-4B. They attain comparable observed accuracy to Qwen's unrestricted response policy (99.07\%) with an \textbf{8.43-fold online speedup}: 0.255 versus 2.150 seconds per question. These results show how reusable programs can substantially expand the reasoning capability of a compact judgment model while keeping its parameters fixed.
  </details>

- **[CXBench: A Benchmark for Process-Compliant LLM Agents in Regulated Customer-Experience Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25954)**  `arXiv:2610.25954`  `cs.AI`  
  _Boxi Yu, Yuzhong Zhang, Zhenfeng Su, Linfu Xi, Yinghan Yao, Shitao Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-using language agents increasingly perform regulated customer-service workflows, where a correct final state is insufficient: agents must also provide disclosures, obtain consent, and follow required procedures. Existing tool-agent benchmarks largely score final business states, accepting trajectories that reach correct outcomes through non-compliant processes.We introduce cxbench, a benchmark that evaluates outcome and process compliance separately across six regulated domains. Each task combines a customer request, a tool-based environment, and explicit process requirements. Rule cards link cited regulatory obligations to observable trace evidence. A hybrid grader combines deterministic checks for ordering and authority with an LLM judge for semantic requirements. We define a false-pass as an outcome-pass run that fails process checks.Across seven models and 120 tasks, 55.5% of the 840 retained executions are false-passes, representing 80.1% of outcome-passing runs; only 13.8% pass both checks. Process-inclusive evaluation changes the observed model ordering and exposes differences hidden by outcome-only scoring. cxbench makes these failures inspectable through trace-linked criteria.
  </details>

- **[When Should AI Chime In? Benchmarking Timely Contribution of Proactive Agents in Multi-Party Chat](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25896)**  `arXiv:2610.25896`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ruotong Wang, Brian S. Yu, Xinyi Zhou, Joseph Chee Chang, Pao Siangliulue, Jonathan Bragg, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Proactive agents can support group collaboration by surfacing useful information without waiting to be asked, but even relevant contributions can be disruptive if poorly timed. In this work, we isolate the timing question: given a fixed candidate contribution such as a clarification or relevant fact, when should an AI agent chime in? We introduce PITCH (Proactive Intervention Timing in CHat), to our knowledge the first benchmark with large-scale human judgments of proactive agent timing collected from participants in the same unscripted multi-party conversations they took part in. To collect high-quality judgments, we design a novel interface in which 2-4 people chat synchronously, author candidate AI contribution messages, place their own and others' contributions into the chat history, and then deliberate as a group about the best placement for each message. PITCH contains 384 candidate contributions and 1,091 judgments, each marking an ideal turn, an acceptable window, or that a contribution should not appear, from 89 participants engaged in 35 conversations. Evaluating 15 models across 28 configurations, we find that while models can place contributions well when given the entire conversation history, they systematically chime in too early when asked to decide live as the conversation unfolds: best-turn accuracy falls from 57.8% to 19.0%, with nearly half of placements occurring before every participant's acceptable window. In comparison, lay humans deciding live are more accurate (23.7% vs 19.0%), and fewer of their placements are premature (39.7% vs 46.4%). Model reasoning traces show that 93% of intervention justifications cite relevance, while almost none consider waiting. By providing a human-grounded benchmark, our work reveals gaps in agents' ability to integrate into group settings and points towards proactive agents that can reason explicitly about appropriate timing.
  </details>

- **[Minimum-Cost Robust Evidence Certificates for Horn Reasoning under Correlated Source Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25865)**  `arXiv:2610.25865`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yukun Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A small justification for a rule-based conclusion may disappear when a source is withdrawn, and multiple derivations need not provide independent evidence. We study minimum-cost certificates that preserve a Horn conclusion under every withdrawal of at most k source domains. Evidence records remain distinct from asserted facts, supporting replicated attestations and overlapping failure dependencies. Transversals of minimal-support footprints characterize robustness and explain why disjoint-proof requirements can reject valid certificates. An exact procedure alternates weighted evidence selection with a source-failure oracle. A rule-closed counterinterpretation yields a globally valid selection cut without enumerating supports or failure scenarios. We prove correctness, finite termination, and cost optimality, establish coNP-completeness of unrestricted verification and NP-completeness of synthesis for every fixed budget, and identify a tractable reachability restriction. Experiments on controlled benchmarks, reachability graphs, and ontology-derived instances demonstrate that our counterinterpretation-guided synthesis achieves up to 92% optimum coverage within practical resource bounds, outperforming scenario expansion and general abduction while confirming the necessity of transversal-based reasoning over proof-packing heuristics.
  </details>

- **[MASIF: Implicit Failures for Process Observability in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25852)**  `arXiv:2610.25852`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yanwei Huang, Arpit Narechania, Atanu Sinha_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent systems (MAS) can complete a task despite local execution errors that are corrected, bypassed, or carried forward without individually determining its outcome. We call these implicit failures (IFs) and study them as signals for process observability beyond final task outcome. Across 1,828 traces from nine MAS-benchmark configurations, IFs occur in 54.3% of successful and 75.2% of failed traces. We develop taxonomies of error types and subsequent absorption mechanisms, supported by human and cross-model annotation audits. Counterfactual replay further probes how repairing an IF changes the final outcome. To examine the practical value of IFs, we evaluate IF-based best-of-N trace selection against existing process reward models, and introduce a novel RL algorithm, IF-GRPO, that uses IF costs to refine preferences among successful trajectories in GRPO. Experiments show that IF-GRPO exhibits some gains in accuracy and process metrics. Importantly, it exposes trade-offs between task performance, IF count and cost, and token-normalized IF density. Overall, our results show that IFs provide complementary process observability into how MAS reach their outcomes, including error recovery, propagation, and IF-based process differences that final task success alone does not expose.
  </details>

- **[A Cue's Hidden Price: Auditing Seller Influence on AI Shoppers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25843)**  `arXiv:2610.25843`  `cs.AI`  
  _Felix Buecheler, Gerardo Berbeglia, Mingyu Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly asked to shop on a user's behalf, which lets seller-controlled content such as badges, endorsements, and framing steer what they recommend. That poses the question at the core of generative engine optimization (GEO): not whether seller content shifts an agent's choice, but how much. The usual gauge of that influence, how often a cue wins the choice, has a blind spot: once a cue is strong enough to be picked almost every time, the choice rate can no longer show how much stronger one cue is than another. We introduce price-equivalent (PE) auditing, which removes this ceiling by pricing the cue in money. In a counterbalanced, position-controlled forced choice, we raise the cued product's price until it is chosen only half the time, and report that premium as the cue's price-equivalent: a formally defined, policy-conditioned parity threshold that makes very different cues comparable. Priced this way, cues that look equally strong at equal price differ by a wide margin, so choice-rate audits substantially under-resolve seller influence. We run the audit over 113,472 trials, seven models across families, and two product categories. To our knowledge, we also run the first price-equivalent audit of a decision-native model, Jev, which outputs a choice probability directly instead of generating text; strikingly, its profile is unlike any text model we tested, resisting most cues yet remaining susceptible to the strongest social-proof claims. We release the stimuli, logs, and code as a benchmark that reports each model's cue-price profile rather than a single score, giving developers and auditors a common, reproducible way to measure how content moves AI shopping agents.
  </details>

- **[FrontierRefactor: Benchmarking Multi-Scale Performance-Oriented Codebase Refactoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25834)**  `arXiv:2610.25834`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shijian Wang, Fanqing Meng, Jiajun Chen, Qiguang Chen, Linxin Song, Runhao Fu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous codebase refactoring requires coding agents to restructure working software while strictly preserving externally observable behavior and prioritizing execution performance and compute efficiency over stylistic preference. Existing benchmarks: refactoring benchmarks assess functional correctness while overlooking runtime regressions, whereas code performance benchmarks target isolated micro-kernels that ignore codebase-scale complexity. We observe that refactoring scale is governed by the scope of the observable contract, the caller-visible interface fixing behavioral invariants while freeing private implementations for optimization. Building on this insight, we introduce FrontierRefactor, a benchmark of 60 qualified engineering tasks spanning four scales, from computational kernels to full repositories, across in-place optimization and cross-language rewriting. Evaluating frontier coding agents through a reference-free cascaded protocol across 1,080 execution runs reveals that correctness and speedup are largely decoupled: 41.2% of submissions satisfy functional equivalence yet fail to deliver verified performance improvements, with 19.3% suffering performance regressions and 21.9% trapped in measurement noise. Refactoring paradigms reveal orthogonal engineering bottlenecks: while cross-language rewrites gain speedups from native compilation after passing functional checks, agents struggle with contract fidelity, with pass rates dropping from 65.0% on modules to 32.6% at package scale; conversely, in-place optimization reliably preserves behavioral contracts at 86.5% but rarely achieves acceleration without physical performance profiling. Trajectory analysis shows that agents fall victim to uncalibrated micro-benchmarking illusions, an operational blind spot that multi-turn execution-timing feedback directly resolves, raising strict success from 16.0% to 40.0%.
  </details>

- **[When to Stop Asking: Factor Coverage Bias and LLM Determinability Judgments with Underspecified Information in Medicine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25819)**  `arXiv:2610.25819`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yusuke Watanabe, Yohei Kobashi, Taisei Tosaki, Takeshi Kojima, Yusuke Iwasawa, Yasushi Okuno, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical decision-making often begins with underspecified information and requires not only gathering missing information but also knowing when to stop. This calls for judging determinability (whether the available information is sufficient for a decision) by considering whether missing information could change the conclusion, not simply the amount of available decision-relevant information. Reliance on this amount as a cue for determinability, which we term \textit{factor coverage bias}, could undermine the trustworthiness of large language models (LLMs) through premature conclusions or excessive information gathering, such as unnecessary invasive tests. Yet whether LLMs exhibit this bias remains insufficiently studied. To address this gap, we construct a controlled pairwise benchmark in which LLMs identify the determinable case within each determinable--undeterminable pair. We add a decision-relevant factor to either side while keeping each case's determinability unchanged, and similarly add irrelevant text to distinguish this bias from context-length effects. Adding a relevant factor made LLMs more likely to select the augmented case as determinable even when it remained undeterminable, an effect that persisted with increased reasoning effort, while adding irrelevant text made them less likely to select the augmented case, an effect that reasoning substantially reduced. These findings identify factor coverage bias as a failure mode distinct from context-length effects and a potential risk to LLM trustworthiness. Our work provides an evaluation framework for LLM trustworthiness under underspecification.
  </details>

- **[TimelineAgent: An LLM-based Framework for Query-Centric Event Timeline Extraction Across Multiple Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25733)**  `arXiv:2610.25733`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qingkai Min, Yunxin Bao, Yifeng Lu, Zhenyu Feng, Yulong Chen, Andreas Vlachos, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Query-centric cross-document event timeline extraction aims to construct event timelines from multiple documents under diverse query focuses. The task is very challenging due to the heterogeneous event modeling and reasoning requirements arising from both the intrinsic complexity of cross-document timeline construction and the diversity of query focuses. In this paper, we propose TimelineAgent, an LLM-based multi-agent framework featuring a multi-stage reasoning workflow, where heterogeneous event features are organized into reusable skills and orchestrated through a goal-oriented protocol. To facilitate evaluation, we construct a benchmark covering diverse query focuses. Experimental results show that TimelineAgent consistently outperforms existing methods, offering a practical framework for complex real-world timeline construction.
  </details>

- **[CVaR-RMD: CVaR Selection Pressure for Robust Metaheuristic Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25697)**  `arXiv:2610.25697`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ruodai Cui, Li Niu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models have recently been used to discover heuristics through iterative program search, offering a promising approach to automated algorithm design. However, candidates selected by average performance may degrade across instance families, random seeds, and problem scales because mean-based objectives can obscure rare but severe failures. We propose CVaR-RMD, a risk-sensitive evolutionary framework that uses an LLM to mutate executable heuristic programs and ranks candidates by empirical conditional value-at-risk over heterogeneous evaluation conditions. Across three combinatorial optimization domains, CVaR-based selection improves upper-tail performance and reduces failure rates while maintaining competitive mean performance. These gains persist on larger unseen instances and independently curated within-domain benchmarks
  </details>

- **[ToolOPD: Credit-Aware On-Policy Distillation for Tool-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25693)**  `arXiv:2610.25693`  `cs.AI`
