  _Chu Zhao, Lei Tang, Yanming Wang, Jianzhe Zhao, Guibing Guo, Zhengzong Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) enhances the capabilities of small language agents by providing dense, token-level supervision from a teacher model on student-generated trajectories. However, standard OPD implicitly assumes that credit is uniformly distributed across long-horizon trajectories, assigning the same strength of teacher supervision to different actions. Through credit-distribution analysis and outcome-reversal counterfactual experiments, we find that trajectory credit is concentrated on a small number of critical actions. Moreover, even when the trajectory is held fixed, reversing only the final outcome substantially changes the credit assigned to multiple interaction turns. These findings show that the uniform-credit assumption fails to identify critical decisions and ultimately degrades the model's reasoning accuracy. To address these challenges, we propose ToolOPD, an outcome-aware on-policy distillation framework designed for long-horizon tool use. ToolOPD first summarizes each completed trajectory and its outcome into a reusable hindsight skill. It then measures how this skill changes the teacher's probability of each student action token and converts the change into outcome-aware credit. The resulting credit preserves supervision that supports successful decisions or discourages failed ones, while suppressing irrelevant and conflicting signals. ToolOPD uses these credits to reweight the action-level distillation loss and jointly optimizes it with reinforcement learning. Extensive experiments across multiple long-horizon tool interaction benchmarks demonstrate that ToolOPD significantly outperforms existing reinforcement learning and self-distillation baselines.
  </details>

- **[Think, Compress, Answer: Reinforcement Learning for Multimodal Grounding via Reasoning Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25640)**  `arXiv:2610.25640`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jinbae Seo, Hyeongjun Kwon, Jiyoung Lee, Hyunsung Jang, Namkoo Ha, Jungin Park, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Extended reasoning has substantially improved multimodal large language models (MLLMs) on tasks requiring compositional and relational inference. However, multimodal grounding imposes a distinct requirement: reasoning must ultimately converge on a target-specific compact representation that supports precise localization. As reasoning trajectories grow longer, they often expand into broad scene narratives and peripheral relational details, which can dilute grounded perceptual evidence and lead to ambiguous or incorrect predictions. Motivated by the information bottleneck principle, we propose Think-Compress-Answer (TCA), a reinforcement learning framework that decomposes multimodal grounding into three phases: free-form reasoning, target-aware context compression, and answer generation conditioned on the compressed context. TCA is optimized through a multi-stage Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with progressive rewards that establish the structured output protocol, minimize reasoning-specific information while preserving target-relevant content, and directly improve localization accuracy. This staged design enables MLLMs to retain the benefits of extended reasoning while distilling it into the minimal context required for grounding. Experiments across six multimodal grounding benchmarks show that TCA attains substantial zero-shot performance gains, demonstrating the effectiveness of reasoning compression.
  </details>

- **[Design and Cross-Domain Evaluation of an Agent Execution Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25568)**  `arXiv:2610.25568`  `cs.AI`  
  _Saurav Joshi, Manas Gupta, Ashwin Venkatesh Raman, Akshay Budhkar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Completing complex tasks with language-model agents requires coordinating planning, execution, and review so the final result addresses the user's original objective. We present an agent execution harness that keeps the original objective separate from a revisable plan and an execution record. A planner assigns scoped tasks, workers produce outputs and artifacts, and a reviewer examines results against the original request before submitting or requesting additional work. Reusable domain-specific instructions guide these roles and are refined during construction using task inputs and execution trajectories, without official grading feedback. We evaluate complete harness configurations across terminal tasks, clinical responses, application building, and scientific questions. On Terminal-Bench 2.1, the harness passes 83 of 89 instances (93.26%); on HealthBench Professional, a Fable-configured system achieves a length-adjusted rubric score of 69.63 across 525 tasks. In a 53-task paired HealthBench comparison, the Fable-configured harness achieves an aggregate score 19.89 points higher than direct completion, despite a $-1.46$-point median task-level change; mean inference cost rises from \$0.29 to \$2.13 per task. We combine these outcomes with latency, documented instruction revisions, and reviewer decisions. Evaluating outcomes alongside execution trajectories and resource use clarifies how coordination supports task completion, where it introduces regressions, and at what cost.
  </details>

- **[Symbolic Constraint Agents: Mining Formal Tool-Call Constraints to Prevent Agent Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25552)**  `arXiv:2610.25552`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jinu Lee, Shraddha Barke_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Symbolic constraints (programmatic, deterministic checks for tool calls) enable deterministic verification of LLM agents' tool calls, but existing approaches primarily enforce predefined safety policies rather than tool dependencies in complex tool environments. We explore whether symbolic constraints can be mined from environments and observations beyond predefined policies, and whether they can improve end-to-end task performance. We present Symbolic Constraint Agents (SCA), a framework that mines symbolic constraints from API documentation, refines them against execution traces, and checks the agent's tool calls with an SMT solver at runtime. A violation triggers a natural-language warning or, when the valid replacement is uniquely determined, an automatic repair. On AppWorld, refinement raises error-detection F1 from 37.0 to 58.3 on GPT-5.5 traces and from 39.3 to 61.9 on Qwen3.5-27B traces. At runtime, repair raises GPT-5.4-mini's task success by 5.4 points under a 40-call budget and 9.8 points when environment errors are hidden, while removing 59--87\% of failed calls across models, and transfers to $\tau^2$-bench with gains of up to 5.6 points. These findings show that automatically acquired symbolic constraints can improve tool-calling reliability and task completion, particularly when failures are costly or difficult to diagnose.
  </details>

- **[Editing as Language: Teaching LLM Agents a Grammar for Native Context Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25550)**  `arXiv:2610.25550`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yunlong Deng, Yan Li, Xing Hu, Yida Zhu, Sean Du, Weiyang Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A synopsis in novels folds a story into a few lines; a function in code gathers many steps with a single execution. Context editing is native to language itself. In this paper, we propose the Native Context Editing (NCE) framework, which gives language model agents a grammar for natively editing their own context. By encoding context-editing operations in a grammar of dedicated special tokens, we teach the agents to invoke edits through the language itself. We train agents on iteratively constructed trajectory segments, with each teacher edit conditioned on the evolving context. This establishes context editing as an atomic and inherent capability of LLM agents, enabling them to dynamically modify their context as they generate responses over long-horizon reasoning and interaction. Compared with context-editing methods that learn to generate tool-use code within specific external harness environments, our approach integrates context editing as an internal mechanism of the model's native generation process, enabling better generalization across diverse real-world workflows. On four long-horizon agentic benchmarks (Terminal-Bench 2.0, SWE-bench Verified, SWE-bench Multilingual, and SWE-bench Pro), NCE improves the average task success rate over the base model by 5.6 percentage points for LongCat-Flash-Lite-Sparse and by 4.5 for Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, while reducing average token usage by 36% and 15%, respectively.
  </details>

- **[StopLoss: Teaching Agents to Predict Failure and Stop Early](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25525)**  `arXiv:2610.25525`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiarong Liang, Xueguang Ma, Dongfu Jiang, Yuxuan Zhang, Ping Nie, Wenhu Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents keep working on runs that will fail verification, and asking a frontier model to watch for this is expensive in its own right. We introduce StopLoss, a small external monitor that learns failure recognition from completed trajectories and their verifier outcomes. The name is inspired by the stop-loss order in finance: StopLoss ends a run early when the run is likely to fail, which saves resources. Final outcomes label earlier local windows, so a fine-tuned language model can score the most recent tool steps in a single forward pass, and a threshold calibrated on successful runs turns that score into an abort decision without changing the executor. Trained on GPT-family SWE-rebench trajectories and evaluated on SWE-bench Verified and a SWE-bench Pro subset with GPT and Claude executors, StopLoss is compared against six general-purpose judges reading identical windows. Outcome supervision lifts failure prediction from near chance to a useful signal at the first check, at one to two orders of magnitude lower cost per check than any tested judge. This translates into savings: relative to completing every task, StopLoss lowers cost per retained solve by 4.5\% on Verified while preserving 99\% of successful runs, and by up to 31\% on the costlier Pro subset, reaching 48\% with a Qwen3-8B backbone, and in all four settings its cost per retained solve is below that of every judge policy. Outcome-trained small monitors can make failure recognition cheap enough for early stopping to pay for itself.
  </details>

- **[When Empathy Misses the Goal: A Benchmark for Goal Displacement in LLM Advice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25522)**  `arXiv:2610.25522`  `cs.AI`  
  _Dean Ariel, Guy Laban_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conversational large language models can respond with warmth and reassurance while failing to advance the user's stated goal. We conceptualize this failure as $\textbf{goal displacement}$, the substitution of persona-maintenance content for goal-relevant substance. To measure it, we introduce $\textbf{GDB}$ (Goal Displacement Benchmark), comprising 230 scenarios across nine domains and $4{,}134$ human-scored responses from 18 models. A $2 \times 2$ taxonomy crosses hard constraints with emotional distress, distinguishing failures to confront infeasible goals or combine empathy with practical guidance from reverse displacement, where unsolicited problem-solving replaces requested emotional support. A deletion-test rubric assesses whether substantive help remains after purely supportive sentences are removed. Independent raters achieve weighted $\kappa = 0.82$ against author scores for displacement severity. Trap displacement ranges from $0.0\%$ to $37.7\%$ and is strongly negatively correlated with Chatbot Arena Elo ($\rho = -0.706$, $n = 15$). Reverse displacement reaches $41.9\%$, including substantial rates in models with negligible trap displacement. A factorial probe supports a distinction from social sycophancy. Human-scored responses in the high-complexity, low-pressure condition exhibit $35.0\%$ sycophancy but $7.5\%$ displacement, with little association between the constructs. Matched OLMo-2 checkpoints show that mixed empathy-with-constraints displacement remains at $52.3\%$ after supervised fine-tuning and falls to $13.8\%$ after direct preference optimization. GDB provides a reproducible diagnostic of whether responses advance the operationalized user goal, supporting model comparison, post-training evaluation, and regression testing. We release the dataset, annotations, and rubric under CC-BY-4.0 and the evaluation code under MIT.
  </details>

- **[Bud ContextCompact 2.0: Context Compaction for Agent Success and Accuracy with Small Language Models and Constrained Context Windows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25516)**  `arXiv:2610.25516`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jithin VG, Ditto PS_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agents that work for many turns outgrow the context window of the model that serves them, and on small language models (SLMs) and constrained inference systems that window is small. Context compaction replaces older turns with a summary so a session can continue, but it is lossy, costs a model call, and can help or hurt depending on when it runs. We describe Bud ContextCompact 2.0, the compaction built into the Bud agent runtime, and evaluate it in two settings. On recall questions over recorded agent sessions, compaction to a 16K budget raised a 1.2B-parameter model's accuracy to 0.335, from 0.247 with truncation and 0.176 with its full 32K history (p < 0.001), and raised a 9B model from 0.515 (truncation) to 0.577 (p = 0.04). On 100 SWE-bench Verified tasks run end to end by a 27B reasoning model on a 49K-token window (556 graded sessions), compaction raised the resolve rate on long sessions from 41% to 53% (+12.3 points, 95% CI +1.6 to +23.0, McNemar p = 0.043), left short sessions unchanged, and used 13% fewer tokens per solved task at equal total cost. The main failure without compaction was not the history overflowing but a reasoning turn exhausting the room the history left. The gain has limits: it vanished under a 45-minute session budget, because each summary took a median of 161 s, and in a threshold study it appeared only when compaction triggered early enough to leave room. An 8B model submitted after about ten turns at 5-8K tokens and never needed compaction. We argue that the accuracy results transfer across hardware, including CPU inference, for an equivalent model and window, while the time cost scales with the hardware and must be budgeted for.
  </details>

- **[BrandEval: A Two-Track Multi-Agent Benchmark for Risk-Sensitive LLM Crisis Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25208)**  `arXiv:2610.25208`  `cs.AI`  
  _Haotian Gan, Weidong Tang, Hanbin Sun, Yi Luo, Xintian Li, Jiayong Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating LLMs for crisis communication requires more than judging isolated responses, because public feedback and competitor actions can reshape trust over time. Existing benchmarks remain largely static and single-turn, offering limited evidence about long-horizon risk-sensitive communication. We introduce \textbf{BrandEval}, a two-track benchmark for product-crisis communication, focusing on public brand responses to product-related safety, quality, service, and trust incidents. BrandEval-Static evaluates stakeholder-aware strategic analysis, while BrandEval-Dynamic stress-tests response policies in \textbf{BrandPolis}, a controlled multi-agent sandbox with consumer feedback, competitor responses, and trust-risk trajectories. We also construct \textbf{BrandSRD}, a Chinese crisis-response dataset, and instantiate a reference Strategic Agent using \textbf{Strategic Rationale (SR)}. Across proprietary and open-source models, we find that static strategic quality is informative but incomplete: it aligns with some end-state outcomes but does not reliably predict volatility and delayed recovery; dynamic stress tests further expose tail-risk failures under manipulative or weakly grounded policies. The code and dataset will be available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CorrectRTL: Boost RTL Generation Quality Through Automated Self-Correction with Instruction Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24977)**  `arXiv:2610.24977`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jing Wang, Shang Liu, Hangan Zhou, Wenji Fang, Yuchao Wu, Yugao Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong promise in specification-to-RTL generation. Among the explorations, the self-correction paradigm has significantly advanced LLM-based RTL generation. However, two challenges limit further progress. Existing benchmarks are saturated by advanced LLMs, leaving little room to evaluate the agentic self-correction framework. The monolithic self-correction paradigm rewrites the entire design at once, where LLMs are susceptible to error confusion and corruption of originally correct code. To address the benchmark challenge, we contribute ScaleBench, a more challenging benchmark of 200 complex RTL designs. To address the challenge in methodology, we propose CorrectRTL, an agentic framework that realizes a new decompose-then-correct paradigm, in which the LLM agent explicitly decouples scaffold construction from detailed implementation. CorrectRTL contributes three core techniques: (1)~\emph{Structure-oriented decomposition}, (2)~\emph{Snippet-based RAG}, and (3)~\emph{SVA-based self-correction}. On ScaleBench, CorrectRTL improves Pass@1 from 20.5% to 49.0% (+28.5%). On VerilogEval, CorrectRTL achieves up to 96.1% Pass@1, demonstrating consistent improvements across both simple and complex designs.
  </details>

- **[MemPrice: Online Memory Allocation via Adaptive Pricing for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24955)**  `arXiv:2610.24955`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yiming Zong, Shiyuan Piao, Jiashuo Jiang, Fugee Tsung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon LLM agents face a fundamental allocation problem as interaction histories grow: which information to retain, how much context to devote to it, and at what granularity. Existing memory mechanisms largely focus on relevance, retrieval, or compression, leaving this allocation trade-off implicit. We introduce MemPrice, which treats active context as a shared resource and dynamically allocates it across memories and representation levels. MemPrice turns accumulated memory pressure into an adaptive price on context, encouraging detail only when its estimated retention value justifies the additional footprint. This pressure persists across memory refreshes, allowing the contents of memory to evolve while preserving long-run control over context usage. We show that irreversible compression can force linear worst-case regret against hindsight following its own memory trajectory, and introduce a same-state benchmark to evaluate allocation on MemPrice's realized memory states. MemPrice enforces the hard context budget at every round and regulates long-run occupancy through adaptive pricing, without prior knowledge of the interaction horizon. Under priced-frontier regularity, its average allocation-regret bound against this benchmark vanishes when accumulated estimation error is sublinear. We evaluate the framework under matched memory budgets on LongMemEval, LoCoMo, AppWorld, and WebShop across three foundation models, spanning both query-blind memory construction and goal-directed agent context management.
  </details>

- **[IndustrialCAD-1400: Benchmarking Detailed Industrial Assemblies from Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24789)**  `arXiv:2610.24789`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hebao Zhu, Kang An, Zhuoke Liao, Faqiang Qian, Ziliang Wang, Mingang Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Industrial assemblies require more than a recognizable shape: functional parts, interfaces, dimensions and service features must satisfy a detailed request together. We introduce IndustrialCAD-1400, a text-to-CAD benchmark for this industrial setting. Its 1,400 requests combine 400 equipment categories across 40 domains with 250 four-case design families. Family requests specify a median of 17 named component or configuration roles and six explicit numeric targets; controlled variants test whether dimensions change while shared structure is retained. Task construction draws on documented manufacturer information, owner specifications and engineering guidance. Models generate geometry programs from requests, and a common Blender environment executes them to produce scenes, optional measurements, and standardized views for evaluation. A request assessment of front, isometric, and top views measures visible component, arrangement, and interface fulfillment without requiring a single reference geometry. Five stratified panels preserve complete families while covering the benchmark exactly once, enabling balanced comparisons and separating industrial breadth from controlled numerical responsiveness. In conclusion, IndustrialCAD-1400 extends text-to-CAD from recognizable part geometry to industrial assembly synthesis under joint component, interface, spatial, and numerical specifications.
  </details>

- **[DialogMem: Evaluating Dialogue-Grounded Embodied Memory in 3D Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24767)**  `arXiv:2610.24767`  `cs.AI`  
  _SeongJun Jeong, Junghyun Kim, Gi-Cheon Kang, Byoung-Tak Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied agents in human-centered environments require memory that integrates what they observe with what humans tell them over time. However, existing embodied memories are built primarily from visual experience, leaving open how dialogue with humans can be grounded in the objects encountered throughout that experience. We introduce DialogMem, a two-phase benchmark in which agents build memory from time-aligned streams of posed RGB-D observations and dialogue, and later use it for instance-level object navigation and question answering (QA). DialogMem provides 142 episodes across 27 real-world 3D scenes, with 14,193 QA pairs and 11,890 navigation instructions. We evaluate nine memory representations within a unified framework of memory formation, evidence retrieval, and reasoning for task outputs. In experiments, appending dialogue to visual memory at evaluation time substantially improves QA but yields negligible navigation gains, suggesting that relevant dialogue is present in memory but not grounded to specific objects. Controlled diagnostics show that navigation improves with link quality but falls short of ground-truth object-level memory even under perfect linking. Retrieving the correct object entry and recovering its spatial location remain further bottlenecks. These results identify three bottlenecks for dialogue-grounded embodied memory: grounding and organizing dialogue at object-level entries during formation, retrieving the correct entry, and localizing it accurately.
  </details>

- **[ReTRACE: Feedback-Guided LLM Agent in a Chemistry Harness for Retrosynthesis Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24744)**  `arXiv:2610.24744`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jinxiang Xie, Yijian Zhang, Qingyuan Yi, Jianyi Wang, Hao Dong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Computer-aided retrosynthesis has advanced through single-step prediction, tree search and route scoring, but complex targets still require judgement over the whole route, where stereochemistry, functional-group compatibility, cost and greenness interact. Here we present \textbf{\mbox{ReTRACE}} (\textbf{RE}trosynthesis with \textbf{T}ool-orchestrated \textbf{R}easoning \textbf{A}nd \textbf{C}ontext-aware \textbf{E}vidence), an agent that places a large language model (LLM) in a chemistry harness that integrates molecular tools, complementary single-step prediction engines, a purchasable starting-material library, a route memory and a multi-criterion route evaluator. Routes are judged by a new metric that combines programmatic checks with adversarial LLM review, and its verdicts are compared with a synthetic chemist's. The agent learns continually from evaluator feedback during search, diagnosing weak routes and backtracking to revise them. With Claude Opus 4.8, at least one of the top 10 routes proposed by ReTRACE passed the audit for 87.4\%, 95.0\% and 86.7\% of targets on the three benchmarks. Two prospective wet-lab syntheses show that ReTRACE route proposals can be converted into executable procedures after expert revision.
  </details>

- **[Are We Evaluating VLM Token Pruning Right? Looking Beyond Accuracy and Token Count](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24740)**  `arXiv:2610.24740`  `cs.AI`  
  _Geng Li, Guohao Chen, Shilin Shan, Jianfei Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual token pruning aims to reduce inference cost while preserving the capabilities of the original VLM. Yet existing evaluations rely on aggregate task accuracy and retained token count as proxies for pruning quality and actual efficiency. We reveal two limitations: 1) Aggregate accuracy can obscure whether a pruning method truly identifies critical visual tokens; many examples remain solvable under arbitrary token removal and models can exploit textual shortcuts when visual evidence is degraded. 2) Retained token count is an unreliable proxy for efficiency, as identical token budgets can incur different end-to-end costs depending on where and how pruning is performed. Consequently, comparisons based on aggregate accuracy and retained token counts fail to recover the global performance-efficiency Pareto frontier across methods. We introduce PAA-Bench, a plug-and-play evaluation framework for existing benchmarks, to evaluate whether pruning succeeds through meaningful token selection and whether this success translates into real speedup. To determine when token choice actually matters, we propose Random-Pass@k to measure how often an example remains correct under random pruning, enabling comprehensive evaluation across different levels of selection difficulty. To compare efficiency fairly, we introduce Preservation-Constrained Speedup (PCS) to report the maximum end-to-end speedup each method achieves under a shared preservation target. Experiments across 8 methods, 3 VLM backbones, and 9 benchmarks show that stronger token compression does not necessarily yield greater speedup, method differences widen on examples where token selection matters, and efficiency rankings change with the preservation target.
  </details>

- **[Toward Collective Model Intelligence: Characterizing and Coordinating Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24739)**  `arXiv:2610.24739`  `cs.AI`  
  _SinapisAI Team, Jian-Dong Liu, Qun Dai, Zhendong Du, Guannan Lai, Hao-Yu Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Independently developed AI models accumulate diverse and complementary capabilities, but a growing model collection does not automatically realize Collective AI. Addressing this gap requires understanding model capabilities with limited evaluation, organizing complementary models to collaborate on diverse tasks, and executing model calls efficiently under resource constraints. We present a systematic approach to pursuing Collective Model Intelligence through three connected components. First, Model Eval constructs evidence-based capability profiles by combining query-level performance estimation with cross-domain prediction, reducing the need for exhaustive evaluation. Second, Mio (Models in One) organizes complementary model candidates and supports task-conditioned, cost-aware selection and collaboration as task requirements evolve. Third, Mos (Model Serving OS) separates model selection from execution and provides endpoint scheduling and shared model deployment and distribution. Experiments show lower capability-estimation errors under limited evaluation. Across nine public benchmarks, Mio 1.0 Lite achieves the highest average score and lowest average cost among compared methods, with per-request costs 73.7-97.5% lower than those of the best-scoring alternatives. A prewarmed shared cache accelerates model-file retrieval by up to 23.3 times. This work lays a foundation for Collective Model Intelligence by characterizing and coordinating the diverse capabilities of an expanding model universe.
  </details>

- **[Can LLM Agents Resolve Service Failures? Simulating Multi-Turn Complaint Handling under Information Asymmetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24733)**  `arXiv:2610.24733`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wailing Ng, Mengze Hong, Chen Jason Zhang, Shiqi Fan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents are widely adopted in service chatbots for rule-guided support and information access, yet emotionally demanding complaint handling still relies heavily on human agents. We introduce ServiceBench, to our knowledge the first benchmark dedicated to complaint handling, with 2,500 scenarios spanning 184 complaint categories and five service industries. We propose Asymmetric Progressive Complaint Simulation (APCS) to simulate realistic complaint handling, together with a justice-based evaluation framework. Our experiments reveal that even capable LLM agents struggle to handle complaints effectively. They lack the ability to proactively gather information and strategically guide conversations toward resolution, often resorting to premature compensation. We further show that targeted post-training with SFT and DPO substantially improves these capabilities, enabling a smaller model to match or surpass much larger models on several outcomes.
  </details>

- **[Selm: Self-Evolving Library Management for Multi-Skill Agent Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24630)**  `arXiv:2610.24630`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mengxi Hu, Yuxuan Hu, Jinbo Su, Ke Wang, Honghao Shi, Wenli Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly use skills as a lightweight way to acquire task-specific knowledge. Recent work enables skills to evolve from task trajectories. However, these methods often lack systematic management of evolved skills, making it difficult for agents to reuse and accumulate skills across domains, thus limiting performance on subsequentthis http URLreliably accumulate, reuse, and maintain useful skills across tasks, we present SELM (Self-Evolving Library Management), a framework that formulates skill evolution as library-level management. SELM jointly manages skill retrieval, updating, and lifecycle by maintaining skill confidence and utility signals. It introduces utility-aware retrieval to select useful skills, a Map-Reduce framework to generate targeted updates from task-specific experience, and lifecycle controls to retain or remove skills based on their effectiveness. These mechanisms enable the skill library to continuously accumulate useful experience while preserving and pruning skills across tasks, improving agent performance on complex tasks across diverse domains. We evaluate SELM on four agent benchmarks covering diverse tasks and execution environments, and SELM consistently outperforms in most settings.
  </details>

- **[HSPR: Recovering Software-Agent Benchmark Scores via Hierarchical Posterior Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24611)**  `arXiv:2610.24611`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wanyi Chen, Wanqin Shen, Yuanchen Shi, Zhensu Sun, Fang Kong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating software agents on a full benchmark is expensive. A small task subset can preserve agent rankings while yielding inaccurate scores. Historical responses provide useful structure, but tasks with identical source responses can have different outcomes for a new target agent. We introduce Hierarchical Score Posterior Recovery (HSPR), which combines source-fitted response factors with uncertain group success rates. Observed target outcomes update the posteriors over the target latent state and group rates; posterior-guided acquisition selects subsequent tasks. Its score-based criterion equals the expected one-step reduction in full-score posterior variance. Observed successes and posterior-predictive counts for unqueried tasks yield a full-score posterior. The final estimate uses its median with source-selected calibration. At a 10% task budget on four task banks from the SWE-bench family, HSPR achieves the lowest mean absolute error (MAE) on three banks and the lowest maximum absolute error (MaxErr) on all four among the methods compared. Removing group uncertainty or replacing adaptive queries increases macro MAE in the respective controls. Macro MAE decreases across 5-20% budgets.
  </details>

- **[HABIT-Bench: Evaluating Habit Induction from Longitudinal Weak Evidence in Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24520)**  `arXiv:2610.24520`  `cs.AI`  
  _Junming Zhang, Rubin Wei, Xu Cheng, Hao Sun, Ruoxi Cheng, Xiquan Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In long-term interactions with large language model (LLM) agents, users reveal habits through their choices and actions. These habits capture implicit user information that agents must infer across conversations. However, individual observations may provide only partial evidence about a habit or when it applies. Retaining past interactions alone does not ensure that agents can infer habits and apply them appropriately. We introduce HABIT-Bench to evaluate how well agent memory systems infer habits from weak evidence and apply them to later requests. We construct synthetic user histories from task-oriented dialogue data and controlled habit graphs. The histories span hundreds of sessions across food, finance, software, and travel. Four-choice tasks assess agents' ability to infer user habits and apply them appropriately. They include temporary exceptions, changes in habits, and suggestions that users have not accepted. Sampled tasks undergo independent human review. We evaluate eight memory methods with Qwen3-235B-A22B and GLM-5.3-Flash. The best-performing memory method achieves 33.61\% macro accuracy across the four domains. Performance remains limited even when annotated evidence is supplied directly. These results expose a persistent gap between access to past interactions and the ability to infer and appropriately use user habits.
  </details>

- **[ConCI: A Controlled Counterfactual Benchmark for Contextual Integrity in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24494)**  `arXiv:2610.24494`  `cs.AI`  
  _Juhyeon Park, Sumin Yu, Taesup Moon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Contextual integrity (CI) holds that the appropriateness of sharing personal information depends on the context of the information flow, not on the information alone. However, existing CI benchmarks often use independent scenarios that vary multiple CI parameters simultaneously and are dominated by inappropriate flows, making it unclear whether correct predictions reflect sensitivity to context or a general refusal tendency. We present ConCI, a controlled-counterfactual benchmark in which an anchor flow is paired with two counterfactuals that each modify a single CI parameter. The resulting triplet is scored jointly, with credit given only when all three flows are judged correctly. Built from six existing benchmarks, ConCI contains 1,592 flows in 556 triplets, is approximately label-balanced, and is validated by seven human annotators per flow. Across seven LLMs, many correct anchor predictions fail on at least one counterfactual, and even the strongest model errs on about a fifth of triplets. Furthermore, models perform near ceiling on inappropriate flows but weaker on appropriate ones, often continuing to judge a flow inappropriate even when a contextual change should make it appropriate. Larger models narrow this bias toward non-disclosure but do not eliminate it, and reasoning yields only modest gains.
  </details>

- **[MELD: Recursive Self-Improvement via Hierarchical Coordination for Multi-Agent Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24454)**  `arXiv:2610.24454`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sizhe Tang, Zuyuan Zhang, Zeyu Fang, Tian Lan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent scientific discovery with large language models raises a coupled learning problem: research assignments determine both which experiments are pursued and the experiences from which agents adapt. We introduce MELD, a unified hierarchical test-time training framework that jointly adapts a team-level Coordinator policy and agent-level individual Scientist policies within a single discovery process. The Coordinator generates a joint portfolio of search branches and research directives over a shared derivation tree, while Scientists develop hypotheses and execution plans implemented by a shared frozen Executor. Both levels receive alternating GRPO-style parameter updates under a shared smooth best-of-budget objective, allowing team organization and individual research behavior to co-evolve in a recursive self-improvement loop. Our analysis establishes objective alignment for the hierarchy, characterizes conditions for coordination gains over independent parallel discovery and the first-order benefit of private adaptation, and derives a finite-budget regret bound for adapted models. On 22 MLGym and MLE-bench tasks, MELD is best on 20 of them and achieves the highest aggregate normalized scores on MLGym, using a 4B backbone for coordination and research. Most baselines use proprietary Scientists, and all systems share one frozen Executor.
  </details>

- **[Hippo: Training-Free Agent Self-Evolution via Hippocampal Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24453)**  `arXiv:2610.24453`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yutong Song, Jiang Wu, Yuxuan Ren, Tony Qin, Baoyuan Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Motivated by recursive self-improvement (RSI), we study how LLM agents can learn through external memory without updating model parameters. Extracting useful experience from a single attempt is difficult: flawed reasoning can produce success, while poor execution can obscure a sound strategy. Preserving its value over time is also difficult: our accumulation baseline without consolidation initially improves but eventually performs below a no-memory baseline. Our key idea is to compare attempts at the same task to identify reusable decisions and refine when they apply. We introduce Hippo, a memory framework inspired by selective hippocampal encoding and replay that learns from these comparisons when shortcomings are diagnosed. It compares the resulting experience with existing memory to merge overlaps and resolve conflicts through explicit applicability conditions. This allows prior guidance to be revised without storing every lesson as a separate entry. Experiments on SWE-bench Verified, WebArena, and Mind2Web show performance gains in all three settings and substantially fewer retained experiences than the accumulation baseline. The gains extend across the evaluated base models of varying capabilities, indicating that memory-based adaptation remains useful even for highly capable models.
  </details>

- **[Trust the Edge, Doubt the Arrow: Auditing Validation Signals for Text-Mined Clinical Causal Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24390)**  `arXiv:2610.24390`  `cs.AI`  
  _Su Lan, Shicheng Zhou, Yiqi Wang, Yanming Zhu, Xuefei Yin, Alan Wee-Chung Liew_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical causal retrieval-augmented generation systems increasingly use knowledge graphs (KGs) mined from notes, yet extracted assertions remain candidates that require audit for patient-level EHR support and direction. We construct a directed candidate graph from 328,839 MIMIC-IV discharge summaries using LLM extraction and UMLS normalization, and evaluate three signals against SemMedDB: within-admission co-occurrence lift, cross-admission diagnosis-coding temporal precedence, and literature corroboration. Lift filters unsupported co-mentions and provides useful EHR support, but not causal proof. Coding order is a weak direction signal: among the 686 candidates where it commits, it reaches 54.2% agreement with SemMedDB, below the extractor's 69.4% on the same subset. Literature corroboration supplies independent direction evidence but covers only 30.4% of candidate relations. A MIMIC-III replication yields the same pattern: extractor direction remains stable (70.5%), whereas coding-order direction is near chance (50.9%). Thus, text-mined clinical causal candidate KGs are best treated as EHR-supported but direction-uncertain. In a leakage-controlled causal QA benchmark, lift-only retrieval achieves 35.7% accuracy; conservative causal demotion achieves 37.2% while reducing evidence-unsupported answers from 17.3% to 6.1% at fixed retrieval recall. We release code, aggregate summaries, and scripts to regenerate restricted outputs.
  </details>

- **[Train Locally, Learn Together: Distributed Multi-Agent Training via ADMM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24380)**  `arXiv:2610.24380`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yiwei Chen, Changsheng Wang, Yicheng Lang, Qing Qu, Alfred O. Hero, Sijia Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent systems (MAS) can outperform individual language-model agents by combining specialized roles, diverse reasoning processes, and collaborative decision-making. However, collaborative MAS training is typically centralized, requiring a common trainer to aggregate role-local trajectories and learning signals and maintain model and optimization states. This motivates a distributed multi-agent learning paradigm in which agents improve system-level capabilities through role-local learning and decentralized communication, paving the way toward scalable, resilient, and self-organizing MAS. To this end, we introduce, to the best of our knowledge, the first optimization-grounded framework for distributed MAS training based on consensus alternating direction method of multipliers (ADMM). We formulate collaborative MAS learning as a consensus-constrained finite-sum optimization problem, naturally lending itself to distributed optimization via ADMM. The proposed approach alternates role-local learning with inter-agent consensus using distributed communication protocols such as ring all-reduce, enabling joint optimization while keeping each agent's data and training state local. We evaluate our framework on distributed safety alignment and general reasoning tasks. Under compromised-agent settings, ADMM can reduce HarmBench attack success rate (ASR) by up to 26.4%, while achieving reasoning gains comparable to centralized training. These results show that distributed MAS training retains the benefits of centralized joint optimization without requiring a centralized trainer.
  </details>

- **[SteerSearch: Interactive Search Reasoning with Mid-Response Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24287)**  `arXiv:2610.24287`  `cs.AI`  
  _Udit Thakur, Baohao Liao, Matteo Nulli, Stefan Vasilev, Seyyed Hadi Hashemi, Shahram Khadivi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Search-augmented language models increasingly serve as long-horizon research agents, yet most open training methods assume a fixed user goal throughout search. We study mid-response steering, where users redirect a model during ongoing reasoning or retrieval. We identify answer-target anchoring, a failure mode in which fixed-query search agents retrieve evidence for the updated request but still answer an earlier question. We introduce SteerSearch, an RL framework that trains on interactive search trajectories with injected interventions to enable context-preserving target reassignment, and SteerSearch-Bench, a benchmark adapted from multi-hop QA datasets. We also investigate Contrastive Context Reward (CCR), an auxiliary signal for aligning post-intervention reasoning with the updated goal. Without CCR, SteerSearch improves average steering accuracy over Search-R1 from 11.1% to 31.3% on Qwen2.5-3B and from 36.6% to 44.6% on Qwen3-4B, while preserving standard single-query performance. Gains also extend to larger Qwen2.5 models and natural multi-turn conversations.
  </details>

- **[From SEC Filings to Financial Signals: Evaluating LLMs on Stock Market Reactions and Analyst Forecasts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24268)**  `arXiv:2610.24268`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shengkun Wang, Taoran Ji, Linhan Wang, Sattar Mansi, Chang-Tien Lu, Yinlin Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Form 10-Q MD&A sections combine long-form management narrative with accounting and forward-looking information. Benchmarks for financial filings commonly evaluate question answering or numerical reasoning, leaving unclear whether outputs from large language models (LLMs) align with market and analyst outcomes. We evaluate prompted, zero-shot outputs from 13 general-purpose and finance-specialized LLMs on 45,964 Form 10-Q filing events from 2001 to 2024, linking MD&A text to cross-sectional fundamental ranks, pre-filing analyst forecasts, and realized abnormal returns. The evaluation covers return ranking under five controlled information views and next-quarter EPS forecasting under direct and consensus-anchored interfaces. Relative to disclosure alone, adding cross-sectional fundamental ranks raises mean effective-date rank correlation (RankIC) by 0.0089 for effective-event-date returns and 0.0098 for following-day returns, whereas analyst-summary context provides no comparable average gain. Across the four disclosure-containing views, finance-specialized models show an effective-event-date group advantage but no statistically reliable following-day advantage, while general-purpose Qwen models produce the strongest individual return-ranking results. Under the full-information view, direct LLM forecasts average the 43.4th percentile of the analyst distribution and do not consistently beat individual analysts. Consensus anchoring raises the mean inside-range share from 41.1% to 59.5% but increases reported-EPS error.
  </details>

- **[AM-Bench: A Unified Taxonomy and Evaluation Suite for Agentic Misalignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24264)**  `arXiv:2610.24264`  `cs.AI`  
  _Eric Zhang, Terry Jingchen Zhang, Chijioke Ugwuanyi, Jerick Shi, Bernhard Schölkopf, Zhijing Jin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As LLM-based agents are deployed with increasing autonomy in real-world environments, a critical failure mode emerges: agentic misalignment, where agents choose harmful actions over failure. However, existing safety benchmarks primarily evaluate whether agents comply with explicitly harmful instructions, leaving the conditions that cause agents to choose harmful actions poorly understood. To address this limitation, we propose AM-Bench, a unified taxonomy and 35 scenario evaluation benchmark spanning seven industries for agentic misalignment grounded in the fraud triangle, a well-established criminological framework explaining goal-directed misconduct. We adapt this framework to agentic environments and construct our evaluation suite and detection systems along three dimensions: perceived threat, perceived opportunity, and reasoning. We operationalize each dimension into structured subcategories rooted in prior theory: five threat categories derived from the instrumental convergence thesis, seven opportunity categories drawn from the Emergent Strategic Reasoning Risks (ESRR) taxonomy, and a novel four-question binary reasoning framework synthesized from prior literature. Our evaluation of 10 frontier models reveals a 62-percentage-point gap in misalignment across scenarios, with Gemini 3 Flash and Gemini 3.1 Pro exhibiting the highest levels of agentic misalignment while Claude Sonnet 4.6 displays near-zero rates across all conditions. Our reasoning framework further reveals that models may exhibit harmful behaviours without explicit situational awareness or explanation, complicating both mitigation and detection. AM-Bench scenarios and code are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Doing Less with More: A First-Principles Exploration of the Suitability of Agentic Computing over Alternative Architectural Choices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23921)**  `arXiv:2610.23921`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ritvik Garimella, Biplav Srivastava, Amit Sheth_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  There is growing interest in automating business activities with Agentic Artificial Intelligence (AI) due to latter's seeming ease of use. Never has it been easier, or costlier, to do less with more. However, little is known about when agents are preferable to established alternatives such as local computation, Representational State Transfer (REST), the Simple Object Access Protocol (SOAP), and the Model Context Protocol (MCP), particularly when development speed, performance, and operational cost are considered. We investigate this question using a controlled mathematical task that compares seven methods on a benchmark of 1,000 arithmetic expressions where semantics of operator precedence has to be preserved. We ran this setup across a native Function Calling baseline (Python library), REST, SOAP and MCP microservice architectures, direct responses from large language models (LLMs) using a Groq cloud API and five locally deployed Ollama models, and agentic systems in which five local LLMs invoke MCP tools. The deterministic methods achieve 100% accuracy with negligible latency. Direct LLM evaluation achieves at most 92.9% accuracy at over 22,000x the latency of a native function call. With MCP tools, the best agent reaches 99.9% accuracy, but incurs nearly 1,000,000x the baseline latency. These results demonstrate that LLMs approaches are poorly suited for exact arithmetic evaluation and motivate an informed architectural decision framework (ADF) for choosing an architecture based on accuracy and throughput requirements. We recognize that an actual interaction with an agent may span both reasoning and linguistic tasks. Hence our recommendation is to explore multiple architectural choices (based on task type) rather than one-size-fits-all solution (LLMs).Code is available at -this https URL
  </details>

- **[Improving Environment-Agnostic Agents: The Role of Scaffolding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22289)**  `arXiv:2610.22289`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sergey Rodionov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agents built on stronger language models may increasingly construct useful procedures for themselves, reducing the advantage of developer-supplied scaffolding. We investigate why this advantage persists and whether an environment-agnostic agent can be improved without environment-specific engineering. We evaluate a Codex-based agent in two nearly identical prompt variants on ARC-AGI-3, AGI Maze, and Terminal-Bench. Each variant is deployed with unchanged code, prompts, and general-purpose tools across environments, receiving environment-specific information as input. Changing only an instruction to encourage reporting difficulties and returning control for automatic continuation improves mean scores on both ARC-AGI-3 and AGI Maze, while Terminal-Bench shows small, mixed changes. Yet the general agent remains below an ARC-AGI-3-specific reference, including with GPT-5.6-sol. The reference prescribes organization and control rather than supplying new game rules or solutions. To investigate this gap, we transfer reset decisions to a scripted controller that follows a convention already given to the agent. This small change recovers part of the specialized agent's advantage. A complementary AGI Maze experiment shows that additional tutorial experience can reduce performance. Together, these findings highlight the distinction between having information and tools available and using them effectively. Supplied procedures still matter for the models tested here. The performance gains also come with higher token-based cost. The experiments do not separate better use of information and procedures from additional or differently allocated inference. We provide the EnvTraverse framework and an evaluated baseline agent for testing whether further environment-independent changes improve performance in some environments without degrading it in others.
  </details>

- **[Realtime-Bench: Can Frontier Models Solve Real-time Interactive Problems?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22155)**  `arXiv:2610.22155`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jingyu Tang, Xu Yang, Zhehao Zhang, Zheng Wang, Qi Cheng, Haozhe Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-time interaction requires agents to perceive evolving environments and act under temporal constraints, yet existing evaluations of real-time streaming models focus on conversational quality and streaming perception, leaving effective action under strict temporal constraints untested. We introduce Realtime-Bench, a suite of carefully curated dynamic tasks where delayed actions directly affect success. Under Wall Clock, where environments advance according to actual inference latency, the best success rate reaches only 44% and the best Pass^3 is only 36%, demonstrating that real-time interaction remains challenging. To separate serving latency from models' real-time streaming capability, we compare Wall Clock with Paused Clock, which removes inference delays as an idealized reference, and introduce Token Clock, which advances simulated time according to generated token counts, testing temporal awareness independently of physical serving speed. We find that temporal costs limit success in evolving environments and that higher reasoning effort has mixed effects under time pressure, reflecting a trade-off between decision quality and timely execution. These findings suggest directions for improving real-time multimodal agents, including adaptive reasoning with temporal awareness and continuous perception and action.
  </details>

- **[When Agent Memory Needs to Compute: Evidence Access and Executable Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19474)**  `arXiv:2610.19474`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zerui Yang, Quan Xue, Chaorui Zhang, Xueyan Niu, Xuanyu Zheng, Xiyuan Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Questions about an agent's history often require aggregating events, which retrieval alone does not resolve. We combine source-preserving evidence access with executable aggregation. Trajectory Memory (TM) retains the original trajectory and constructs a source-addressable reading view at question time. TallyMem augments it with a reusable event ledger, model-generated event specifications and deterministic execution. With a commit-specific adaptation on 776 questions from 99 held-out MINTEval repositories, TallyMem raises Qwen3-32B exact match from 35.2% to 49.0%, a 13.8-point gain over TM; gains span 12.8-13.9 points across three readers. A controlled probe with event sets parsed independently from raw logs shows an 11.0-point gain, from 82.8% to 93.8%, without routing. On full AMA-Bench, the routed system yields a smaller 1.3-point gain, from 69.8% to 71.1%. These results support executable aggregation on tasks that require combining events, with benefits dependent on the task and event specification, the largest residual system-error category in our diagnostic analysis.
  </details>

- **[Empowering Edge Agents: A Systematic Analysis of Edge-Cloud Collaboration in LLM Agent Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17816)**  `arXiv:2610.17816`  `cs.AI`  
  _Pengcheng Huang, Zhenghao Liu, Shixin Ren, Shi Yu, Sijia Yao, Yukun Yan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Edge-cloud collaboration combines the lower inference overhead and greater data locality of edge-side execution with the stronger capabilities of cloud-hosted models. However, as LLM agents increasingly operate in long-horizon scenarios, cloud participation becomes interleaved throughout execution rather than a one-shot decision for each query. Such interleaved participation introduces trajectory-level effects that remain insufficiently understood. To address this gap, we introduce EC2-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating edge-cloud collaboration in long-horizon agent execution. It provides executable digital-workspace agent tasks and a unified evaluation framework covering task performance, resource usage, and privacy exposure. Using this benchmark, we empirically study diverse forms of edge-cloud collaboration. Our study reveals three findings. First, collaboration achieves a favorable balance among task performance, resource usage, and privacy exposure compared with edge-only and cloud-only execution. Second, as edge models become stronger, collaborative execution approaches cloud-only performance, and performance differences across strategies shrink, while privacy exposure remains strongly strategy-dependent. Third, stronger edge capability shifts preferred collaboration from externally controlled cloud participation and direct cloud execution toward more edge-controlled collaboration. Further analyses identify cloud-invocation decision-making and instruction following as important edge-side abilities for effective collaboration. These findings provide a systematic understanding of edge-cloud collaboration in long-horizon agent execution and insights for designing future edge agents that effectively collaborate with cloud models. Code and resources are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Recursive Environment Scaling for General Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16397)**  `arXiv:2610.16397`  `cs.AI`  
  _Lingxiao Jing, Zhengbo Jiao, Yichen Xiang, Bofan Zhu, Lingbei Meng, Yan Ni, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study environment scaling as a way to increase agent-benchmark difficulty while holding core task objectives fixed. Our framework, Recursive Environment Scaling (RES), transforms existing benchmark environments while preserving their objectives and completion requirements. A proposer changes information access, resource relations, and execution dependencies; execution-grounded checks validate the resulting candidate, and accepted changes are inherited across rounds. This produces related environments for paired evaluation and training-trajectory collection. Experiments on 294 initial tasks from three benchmarks show lower task success for all six evaluation models, with decreases of 57.14--77.14 percentage points on Claw-Eval-Live. With equal trajectory counts and identical optimization settings, fine-tuning on RES trajectories outperforms original-trajectory SFT in all nine model--benchmark comparisons by 7.00--14.61 percentage points. Ablations identify recursive inheritance and dependency composition as important contributors to increased difficulty. Together, these results support environment scaling for both agent evaluation and training.
  </details>

- **[HyWe: Unifying Search and Operation for Real-World Hybrid Web Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16319)**  `arXiv:2610.16319`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zijian Cai, Haowei Liu, Kailai Chen, Lin Peng, Cong Wan, Ziqi Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world hybrid web tasks need both information gathering and page interaction, yet search-based and operation-based agents are developed and evaluated separately. To bridge this gap, we introduce HyWe, a framework that unifies both capabilities within one model and trajectory. Its two modes use dedicated prompts and tool sets to sustain sub-goal execution; explicit switching selects the needed capability, and shared history lets each mode build on the other's findings. To provide demonstrations of these behaviors, we develop HyWeCollector, a fully automatic pipeline that uses task prototypes, four switching patterns, and multi-agent synthesis to produce verified hybrid trajectories. We train HyWe-35B on these demonstrations to unify efficient information gathering and browser interaction in a single 35B web agent. To evaluate hybrid task completion, we introduce HyWeBench: 100 human-written tasks on websites distinct from training, with human-written sub-goal checklists and an outcome-based evaluation pipeline. Hybrid training enables HyWe-35B to achieve 47.0% success on this benchmark, improving on the untrained base model by 19.0 percentage points and outperforming existing search-based and operation-based agents. Even before training, the same base model solves 28.0% of tasks with HyWe, versus 11.0% with all tools under one prompt. Beyond hybrid tasks, HyWe-35B achieves 59.0% and 88.3% success on Online-Mind2Web and WebVoyager, outperforming existing open-source baselines and cutting action cost per successful task by 48.3% and 51.9% compared with Fara-7B.
  </details>

- **[Attention Sinks in VLMs Are Borrowed, Not Seen](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15855)**  `arXiv:2610.15855`  `cs.AI`  
  _Dengjia Zhang, Alexander Martin, Pengyu Yan, Xihan Sun, Jiaqi Li, Benjamin Van Durme, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study how to detect and mitigate visual attention sinks in vision-language models (VLMs). Visual attention sinks are image tokens that absorb a large share of attention over the image regardless of the query or task, and they mislead methods that treat attention maps as a record of where the model ``looked.'' Prior work has detected sinks through massive activation dimensions and proposed redistributing their attention, but none have addressed what makes a token become a sink. Comparing six candidate detectors on ten VLMs from four families, we find that only the centered cosine similarity between an image token's key and the key of the language model's text-sink detects visual sinks. Thus, a visual sink is an image token whose key aligns with the text-sink's key. Moving a sink's key component along this direction to the median of the image keys sends its attention back to the text-sink (\textbf{Are Borrowed}) while leaving model answers unchanged~(\textbf{Not Seen}). Building on this, we propose a training-free, entropy-triggered re-look that first cleans the attention map, then crops the most-attended region of the cleaned map and inserts it into the reasoning trace. Cleaning adds negligible computation to the forward pass, and the re-look improves accuracy with a mean gain of $+4.3$ points on fine-detail VQA benchmarks across model families.
  </details>

- **[MedZERO: Self-Evolving Agents for Open-Ended Medical Reasoning Through Controlled Knowledge Accumulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08327)**  `arXiv:2610.08327`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xilin Dang, Weilin Ruan, Xue Yang, Jinghao Wang, Xiaowei Hu, Jinpeng Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have shown promise in medical question answering and clinical reasoning, yet their improvement remains constrained by static parametric knowledge and costly expert supervision. Self-evolving agents offer a promising alternative by enabling models to improve through iterative task generation and problem-solving. However, most existing self-evolving methods are designed for easily verifiable domains such as mathematics and coding, where solutions can be checked by exact answers or executable programs. Medical reasoning is fundamentally different: it is open-ended, knowledge-intensive, and often only partially verifiable. We present MedZERO, a self-evolving framework for open-ended medical reasoning. MedZERO couples an Examiner that generates frontier medical question-option pairs with a Reasoner that solves them through evidence-grounded multi-turn reasoning with external knowledge tools. To support reliable, continual improvement, MedZERO adopts controlled knowledge accumulation, which maintains temporary exploratory knowledge and curated persistent knowledge in reasoning. We evaluate MedZERO on five public medical reasoning benchmarks using 4B- and 8B-scale base models under open-ended evaluation. At both model scales, MedZERO achieves the highest average accuracy among the evaluated methods, with gains of up to 13.7 percentage points over the next-best self-evolving baseline. Code available at \url{this https URL}.
  </details>

- **[FineSID: Scalable and Efficient Semantic Identifier Learning for Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36670)**  `arXiv:2609.36670`  `cs.AI`  
  _Song-Li Wu, Weinan Gan, Zhaocheng Du, Xianquan Wang, Jingyi Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A critical prerequisite of generative recommendation is designing semantic identifiers (SIDs) that are both scalable to large item sets and efficiently learnable. Existing SID learning methods fundamentally rely on Top-1 hard assignment during vector quantization. While heuristic strategies -- such as clustering-based initialization or forced post-hoc collision resolution -- can artificially inflate codebook coverage, they often disrupt end-to-end semantic alignment and fail to address the underlying optimization bottleneck: sparse gradient propagation. In standard Top-1 assignment, gradients concentrate on a narrow subset of frequently selected codewords, leaving the majority inherently under-trained and causing severe SID collisions. To overcome this limitation natively without relying on complex initialization priors, we propose FineSID, a unified quantization framework that moves beyond Top-1 assignment by enabling fine-grained gradient propagation across the entire codebook. Instead of updating only a single selected codeword, FineSID distributes learning signals to all codewords in a soft, differentiable manner. This design promotes globally balanced codebook optimization while strictly preserving semantic consistency, effectively alleviating SID collisions and stabilizing training in large, high-dimensional codebooks. Extensive experiments on multiple public benchmarks demonstrate that FineSID is robust to initialization configurations and consistently improves both codebook utilization and recommendation accuracy. Our work provides a principled, initialization-agnostic solution for semantic identifier learning, advancing the practicality of generative recommendation.
  </details>

- **[LSTMem: Hierarchical Long Short-Term Online Memory for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33268)**  `arXiv:2609.33268`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xianglong Shi, Ruijie Yang, Sirui Zhao, Shukang Yin, Zihao Bian, Tinghao Yi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models increasingly serve as long-horizon assistants and agents, where they must both accumulate information across interactions and make the relevant parts available when later requests depend on them. Existing compact online memories typically use a single persistent state both to accumulate history and to serve readout, so what the memory stores cannot be controlled separately from what it exposes to the current computation. We propose LSTMem, an LSTM-inspired online memory that instead equips each layer of a frozen LLM with two matrix-valued states: a cell state that accumulates history and a hidden state whose readouts correct the backbone's attention. Input and forget gates control what the cell stores, while an output gate separately controls what the cell exposes through the hidden state. LSTMem further connects memory across depth through forward hidden-state propagation and block-end feedback, and uses higher-layer reconstruction gradients to refine lower-layer cell states before rebuilding hidden states from shallow to deep layers. Across memory benchmarks on Qwen3-4B-Instruct, LSTMem consistently improves MemoryAgentBench, LoCoMo, and HotpotQA over the plain backbone. Comparisons further show that the LSTM-based memory formulation outperforms an associative-memory counterpart, while removing cross-layer hidden-memory propagation degrades performance. These results demonstrate the benefits of separating memory accumulation from memory expression and organizing memory hierarchically across model depth. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ActiveMem: Dynamic Latent Memory Trees for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33244)**  `arXiv:2609.33244`  `cs.AI`  
  _Song-Li Wu, Jingyi Wang, Zhaocheng Du, Weinan Gan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on external memory to support long-horizon reasoning and decision making. Existing memory systems typically retrieve historical trajectories or summaries as independent context fragments, overlooking the procedural dependencies underlying multi-step execution. As memory scales, such flat retrieval introduces context fragmentation and cross-task interference, leading to structurally inconsistent reasoning trajectories. We propose ActiveMem, a hierarchical memory framework that recursively organizes agent experiences into dependency-aware latent execution trees. ActiveMem abstracts trajectories into reusable subtask nodes while explicitly preserving execution transitions, enabling coherent reasoning-path retrieval conditioned on the current execution state. To support continual adaptation, ActiveMem further learns dynamic memory expansion, retrieval, and pruning policies through reinforcement learning. Experiments across various agent benchmarks demonstrate that ActiveMem consistently improves task completion, reasoning stability, and memory efficiency over existing memory-based agents. Moreover, ActiveMem enables compact open-weight models to achieve competitive performance with substantially larger proprietary systems.
  </details>

- **[CodeSkill: Latent Skill Abstraction for Long-Horizon Code Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33243)**  `arXiv:2609.33243`  `cs.AI`  
  _Song-Li Wu, Jingyi Wang, Zhaocheng Du, Weinan Gan, Weiwen Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Code agents require long-horizon decision-making over complex interaction trajectories. However, existing reinforcement learning (RL) approaches typically optimize behavior at the token level, creating a mismatch between low-level generation and high-level behavioral reasoning. This limitation leads to inefficient exploration and weak credit assignment under sparse rewards. Moreover, while large-scale agent trajectories often contain recurring multi-step behavioral patterns, their noisy token-level representations hinder effective experience reuse. To address these challenges, we propose CodeSkill, a framework that adapts hierarchical latent skill modeling to the code agent domain. CodeSkill first leverages a teacher model to distill both successful and failed trajectories into multi-level textual abstractions. It then integrates temporal variational inference with reinforcement learning to map these discrete semantics into continuous latent variables, while an adaptive boundary mechanism dynamically gates skill transitions based on execution feedback. The learned skills are injected into a frozen LLM policy as latent semantic prefixes, enabling optimization in a compact semantic space rather than over raw token sequences. By shifting RL from token-level exploration to experience-level reasoning, CodeSkill improves optimization efficiency and long-horizon behavioral coherence. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CodeSkill achieves highly competitive performance against strong open-weight baselines across diverse general and industrial coding benchmarks. Furthermore, the learned skills exhibit strong transferability and robust cross-domain generalization, highlighting the effectiveness of explicit behavioral abstraction for scalable agentic code generation.
  </details>

- **[PotARCin: Multi-Dimensional Evaluation of Skill Acquisition in Abstract Reasoning Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27288)**  `arXiv:2609.27288`  `cs.AI`  
  _Claas Beger, Ryan Yi, Melanie Mitchell_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus (ARC) has become a prominent benchmark for evaluating general abstract reasoning and fluid intelligence in AI models. Yet standard ARC evaluation considers only a single capability: producing the correct output grid for a test input. We argue that this narrow format fails to evaluate the diversity of abilities that genuine abstract skill acquisition should enable. We introduce PotARCin, a benchmark that extends ARC by assessing understanding of a task's underlying abstract rule across five dimensions: Definition, Classification, Constrained Generation, Editing, and Inversion. PotARCin employs programmatic methods to generate new task instances and transform given inputs for a given ARC task, enabling dynamic generative sampling beyond fixed input-output pairs. Across five state-of-the-art models evaluated on the ARC-AGI-1 training set, we observe a 25-52 percentage-point performance gap between standard ARC evaluation and evaluation on PotARCin, and find that multi-dimensional evaluation reorders models that standard accuracy ranks alike. We further investigate effects of generative sampling, difficulty of corruption types, and questions of self-consistency, showing that models frequently contradict their own formalized rule even where they have stated it correctly. We also introduce P-ARC, a held-out hand-crafted test set, on which models achieve 1-8% accuracy across all five dimensions, underscoring the importance of more holistic evaluations of abstract reasoning capabilities.
  </details>

- **[Taming CoT Obfuscation in VLMs: From Mechanistic Evidence to Activation-Level Enforcement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24243)**  `arXiv:2609.24243`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xutao Mao, Jianing Zhu, Jinman Zhao, Tongliang Liu, Xiaowen Chu, Cong Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) has substantially improved the reasoning capabilities of vision-language models (VLMs), yet it can induce chain-of-thought (CoT) obfuscation, an operational, non-intentional regime where models achieve high accuracy while producing reasoning traces that are less grounded and harder to monitor. While prior work documents this decay at the behavioral level, the underlying representational correlates of such drift remain poorly understood. In this work, we find that obfuscation is accompanied by a representational shift, where RL-induced optimization makes task-agnostic template features less separable from visually grounded content in the model's activation space. Based on this insight, we propose TAME, an activation-level intervention framework that leverages Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) to regularize VLM internal features during RL training. Specifically, TAME utilizes an anti-obfuscation feedback loop to reduce behavioral obfuscation and applies an asymmetric SAE constraint to specific internal directions, penalizing template activations while preserving visual evidence. By directly intervening on the features associated with obfuscation, our method improves the transparency and visual grounding of reasoning traces. Experiments on the VIRL-39k and SPA-VL benchmarks across two model families show that TAME improves CoT monitorability (by up to +30.9 points on VIRL and +16.7 points on SPA-VL over GRPO), while largely preserving task accuracy and general-capability performance across four standard VLM benchmarks, with task-specific trade-offs. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[How Good Are Frontier Models at Physics? Expert Re-Grading Reveals Broken Evaluations and Near-Saturation of Leading Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13009)**  `arXiv:2609.13009`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ali Ansari, Haoran Sun, Andy Zeyi Liu, Mark Jabbour, Yongshan Ding, Steven Girvin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low reported scores on leading physics benchmarks, including those featured in the Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index (2026), suggest that frontier language models still struggle with advanced physics, a demanding test of their scientific reasoning and quantitative problem-solving abilities. Yet this impression does not always align with domain experts' experiences using these models in their work. We revisit these reported findings by evaluating frontier models on six widely used physics benchmarks and auditing them with experts, focusing on text-only problems with verifiable final answers. For each subfield of physics, faculty and graduate researchers with relevant expertise carefully review problem statements, reference solutions, and model responses to distinguish genuine model errors from grader errors, incorrect reference solutions, and ambiguous or underspecified questions. Most audited cases initially evaluated as incorrect reflect these benchmarking issues rather than errors in the models' physics reasoning. We then ask experts to address these benchmarking issues by correcting erroneous reference solutions and repairing or excluding flawed questions. We find that GPT-5.6-Sol's measured mean@4 rises from 47.3% to 78.7% on HLE-Physics and from 61.0% to 87.2% on CMT-Benchmark, while its corrected pass@4 reaches 94.4% on the 54 retained CritPt challenges. Corrected scores are computed on the retained evaluation subsets following expert review. Scores on the audited subsets of UGPhysics, PRISM-Physics, and PHYBench also rise substantially after correction. These findings suggest that current benchmarks substantially understate frontier models' ability to solve well-posed physics problems. Near-saturation on these closed-ended tasks highlights the need for more demanding, expert-validated evaluations.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Agent Agentic Graph Learning via Structural Signatures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09565)**  `arXiv:2609.09565`  `cs.AI`  
  _Liang Qu, Jianxin Li, Hua Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic graph learning (AGL) has recently achieved promising results on graph reasoning tasks, where an agent powered by a large language model (LLM) sequentially samples the graph as evidence to support its final prediction. Existing methods either employ a single agent or orchestrate multiple role-based agents to reason and learn over the entire graph, but both essentially rely on a shared reasoning policy across different graph regions, which can be suboptimal for graphs with heterogeneous structural and semantic patterns. Inspired by the progress of multi-agent collaboration on complex reasoning tasks, a natural remedy is to let multiple agents own different memory and collaborate; however, applying this paradigm to graphs directly faces two challenges. First, existing AGL methods typically verbalize graph structures into natural-language descriptions for LLM agents, making the reasoning process sensitive to the ordering of structural information and thereby breaking the permutation-invariant nature of graphs. Second, incorporating increasingly large sampled neighborhoods leads to rapidly growing contexts. To address these challenges, this paper introduces a multi-agent agentic graph learning (i.e., MAAGL) framework. MAAGL partitions the graph into communities and assigns an independent agent to each community for region-specific specialization. MAAGL represents structural and semantic evidence separately. Structural evidence is summarized by a dynamically updated structural signature that is permutation-invariant and fixed in size, while semantic evidence is filtered to the top-k nodes ranked by relevance. Based on historical trajectories with similar signatures, agents estimate their confidence and trigger debate-style collaboration when needed. Extensive experiments on four benchmark datasets show that MAAGL outperforms SOTA AGL methods.
  </details>

- **[MemForest: Efficient Agent Memory Management via EventTree Partitioning and Progressive Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08273)**  `arXiv:2609.08273`  `cs.AI`  
  _Junxi Wang, Te Sun, Jiayi Zhu, Chen Zhang, Siyuan Li, Xuyang Liu, et al._
